# Majmu' al-Fatawa - Tome 24

## Page 12074

VOLUME TWENTY-FOUR
THE BOOK OF JURISPRUDENCE
PART FOUR:
From Prayer for Those with Valid Excuses to Zakat

In the name of Allah, the Most Merciful, the Bestower of Mercy.

CHAPTER: PRAYER FOR THOSE WITH VALID EXCUSES

Shaykh al-Islam Ahmad ibn Taymiyyah—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

An elderly man’s limbs have grown so weak that he cannot eat, drink, move, or cleanse himself with water after relieving himself. If he prostrates, he cannot raise himself again. How should he pray?

He replied:

As for prayer, he should do whatever he is able. If he cannot stand, he should pray sitting and gesture with his head as his condition allows. If he prostrates on his thigh, that is permissible. He should wipe himself with a cloth after relieving himself, and someone else should perform ablution for him if possible.

He may combine the two prayers: someone should perform ablution for him at the end of the time for the noon prayer, and he should then offer the noon and afternoon prayers without shortening them. Then, when the time for the sunset prayer begins, he should offer the sunset and night prayers. Someone should also perform ablution for him for the dawn prayer.

If he cannot pray sitting, he should pray lying on his side with his face toward the qiblah,

---

## Page 12075

and if there is no one with him to perform ablution or dry ablution for him, he should pray as his condition allows, whether lying on his back with his feet toward the qiblah or on his side with his face toward the qiblah. If there is no one with him to turn him toward the qiblah, he should pray facing whatever direction he is turned, east or west. And Allah, glorified and exalted is He, knows best.

Shaykh al-Islam was asked:

Is it permissible for a woman to pray sitting when she is able to stand?

He replied:

SECTION

An obligatory prayer offered sitting when one is able to stand is invalid, whether offered by a man or a woman. Indeed, the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—said:

“Pray standing; if you cannot, then sitting; and if you cannot, then lying on your side.”

However, it is permissible to offer voluntary prayers sitting. It is also permissible to offer voluntary prayers on a mount while travelling, facing whatever direction it takes its rider, for the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—used to

---

## Page 12076

pray on his mount, facing whichever direction it took him, and offer Witr on it, but he would not offer the obligatory prayers on it. A sick person may pray sitting if standing causes him hardship. If he cannot sit, he should pray lying on his side. Likewise, a man who cannot dismount to the ground should pray on his mount, as should someone who fears danger from his enemy if he dismounts. And Allah knows best.

Shaykh al-Islam was asked:

Is shortening the prayer while travelling a sunnah or a binding obligation? He was also asked about the authenticity of the hadith narrated by al-Shāfi‘ī from Ibrāhīm ibn Muhammad, from Talhah ibn ‘Amr, from ‘Atā’ ibn Abī Rabāh, from ‘Ā’ishah, who said:

“The Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—did all of that: he shortened the prayer and offered it in full.”

He replied:

Shortening the prayer while travelling is the sunnah of the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—and the sunnah of his Rightly Guided Caliphs. The Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—only ever prayed two rak‘ahs while travelling. Abū Bakr and ‘Umar did likewise, as did ‘Uthmān during the year

---

## Page 12077

that was the first of his caliphate. In the second year, however, he offered it in full at Minā for reasons mentioned elsewhere.

As for the hadith mentioned, there is no doubt that it is wrongly attributed to ‘Ā’ishah. Ibrāhīm ibn Muhammad is Ibn Abī Yahyā al-Madanī, the Qadarī. Both he and Talhah ibn ‘Amr al-Makkī are weak narrators by the consensus of hadith scholars; neither can be relied upon as evidence even in matters less significant than this.

It is authentically reported in the Sahīh that ‘Ā’ishah said:

“Prayers were prescribed as two rak‘ahs each. The prayer during travel was left unchanged, while the prayer when not travelling was increased.”

‘Urwah was asked, “Why, then, did ‘Ā’ishah offer the prayer in full?”

He replied, “She acted on an interpretation, just as ‘Uthmān did.”

Here, then, ‘Ā’ishah herself states that the prayer during travel consists of two rak‘ahs. Her nephew ‘Urwah, who knew her better than anyone else, explains that she offered it in full on the basis of an interpretation; she had no Sunnah report supporting that practice.

It is likewise authentically reported that ‘Umar ibn al-Khattāb said:

“The prayer during travel is two rak‘ahs; the Friday prayer is two rak‘ahs; the Eid al-Fitr prayer is two rak‘ahs; and the Eid al-Adhā prayer is two rak‘ahs—complete, not shortened, as declared by your Prophet.”

Moreover, it is established through mass transmission among the Muslims that the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—never prayed anything but two rak‘ahs while travelling. No one has ever reported that he prayed four. What is established from him, however, is that he both fasted and refrained from fasting while travelling; likewise, some of his Companions fasted and others did not.

---

## Page 12078

As for shortening the prayer, all the Companions did so—including those from Mecca and elsewhere—at Minā, ‘Arafah, and other places. Scholars have differed over offering four rak‘ahs: is it forbidden, disliked, merely less preferable, recommended, or equal to shortening the prayer? There are five views:

First: offering the prayer in full is preferable. This is one view held by al-Shāfi‘ī.

Second: the two are equally acceptable. This is the view of some scholars of Mālik’s school.

Third: shortening the prayer is preferable. This is al-Shāfi‘ī’s correct view and one of the two positions reported from Ahmad.

Fourth: offering the prayer in full is disliked. This is one of the two positions reported from Mālik and the other position reported from Ahmad.

Fifth: shortening the prayer is obligatory. This is the view of Abū Hanīfah and one position reported from Mālik.

The strongest view is that shortening the prayer is Sunnah and offering it in full is disliked. Accordingly, most scholars do not require a specific intention to shorten the prayer. These include Abū Hanīfah, Mālik, and Ahmad, according to one of the two positions transmitted from him within his school.

---

## Page 12079

He was asked:

“Has the Lawgiver (peace and blessings be upon him) prescribed a fixed distance for shortening the prayer?”

He replied:

The Sunnah is for the traveller to shorten the prayer, offering two rak‘ahs instead of four. This is what the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him) and his Companions did on all his journeys. He never offered four rak‘ahs while travelling. The report that “he offered four rak‘ahs while travelling during his lifetime” is a false hadith according to the leading hadith scholars.

Scholars have differed concerning a traveller offering four rak‘ahs. Some maintain that this is impermissible, just as it is impermissible to offer four rak‘ahs for the dawn, Friday, or Eid prayers. Others hold that it is permissible, although the overwhelming majority consider shortening preferable, with only an anomalous dissenting view.

Shortening the prayer requires no specific intention—even if one begins the prayer intending to offer four rak‘ahs—in keeping with the Sunnah of the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him).

“When he (peace and blessings be upon him) led the Muslims on the Farewell Pilgrimage, he would lead them in prayers of two rak‘ahs each until he returned.”

He combined the two prayers at ‘Arafah and Muzdalifah, with the Muslims praying behind him. The people of Mecca and others followed him in both combining and shortening the prayers. He did not instruct anyone to form a specific intention either to combine or to shorten them.

---

## Page 12080

He also stayed at Minā on the day of Eid and in front of Minā, leading the Muslims in prayers of two rak‘ahs each. The people of Mecca and others prayed behind him. Abū Bakr and ‘Umar did likewise after him. Neither the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him), nor Abū Bakr, nor ‘Umar instructed any of the people of Mecca to offer four rak‘ahs, whether at Minā or elsewhere.

Accordingly, the sounder of the two scholarly views is that the people of Mecca combine prayers at ‘Arafah and Muzdalifah and shorten them there and at Minā. This is the position of the majority of the jurists of the Hijaz, including Mālik and Ibn ‘Uyaynah. It is also the position of Ishāq ibn Rāhawayh and the preferred view of a group of scholars from the schools of al-Shāfi‘ī and Ahmad, including Abū al-Khattāb in his treatment of acts of worship.

Another view holds that they combine prayers but do not shorten them. This is the position of Abū Hanīfah and the position explicitly reported from Ahmad. Yet another view holds that they neither shorten nor combine prayers, as maintained by some scholars from the schools of al-Shāfi‘ī and Ahmad. This is the weakest of these views.

The unquestionably correct position is that the people of Mecca shorten and combine prayers there, just as they did there with the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) and his caliphs. It has not been reported that any Muslim told them there, “Complete your prayers, for we are travellers.” It is, however, reported that the Prophet said this during the campaign for the conquest of Mecca, when he led them in prayer within Mecca itself. Likewise, ‘Umar would instruct the people of Mecca to complete their prayers when he led them within the city, but he did not instruct them to do so at Minā.

---

## Page 12081

Scholars have differed over why the people of Mecca shortened their prayers behind him. Some said that this was because of the pilgrimage rites, and therefore someone making a short journey there does not shorten his prayers. Others said that it was because they were travelling. Both views have been held by scholars of Ahmad’s school. The second view is correct: they shortened their prayers because they were travelling. This is why they did not shorten their prayers within Mecca, even though they were in a state of consecration for pilgrimage. Shortening prayer depends on whether one is travelling: only a traveller prays two rak‘ahs, and every traveller prays two rak‘ahs. As ‘Umar ibn al-Khattāb (may Allah be pleased with him) said:

“The traveller’s prayer is two rak‘ahs, the Eid al-Fitr prayer is two rak‘ahs, the Eid al-Adhā prayer is two rak‘ahs, and the Friday prayer is two rak‘ahs—complete, not reduced, meaning not shortened—as declared by your Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him).”

An authentic report relates that ‘Ā’ishah (may Allah be pleased with her) said:

“Prayer was originally prescribed as two rak‘ahs for each prayer. Then the prayers offered when not travelling were increased, while the traveller’s prayer was left as it was.”

Scholars have also differed over whether shortening prayer is restricted to certain journeys or is permissible on every journey. The stronger of the two views is that it is permissible on every journey, whether short or long, just as the people of Mecca shortened their prayers behind the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) at ‘Arafah and Minā. The distance between Mecca and ‘Arafah is approximately one barīd, or four farsakhs.

Moreover, neither the Quran nor the Sunnah distinguishes one journey from another with regard to shortening prayer, breaking the fast, or dry ablution. The Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) set no limit, whether in time or distance, for shortening prayer. The views put forward on this matter are conflicting

---

## Page 12082

and unsupported by any evidence. They are mutually contradictory, and no sound limit can be set. For in most journeys, distances are not measured precisely, and the traveller’s pace varies. What the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) left unrestricted must be left unrestricted, and what he restricted must be restricted. Thus, a traveller may shorten his prayers on every journey. The same applies to all rulings connected with travel, including shortening prayers, praying on one’s mount, and wiping over leather socks. Anyone who divides journeys into short and long, assigns some rulings to one and others to the other, and makes them dependent on a long journey has no evidence that must be accepted. And Allah, Glorified and Exalted, knows best.

Shaykh al-Islām (may Allah have mercy on him) was asked:

If someone undertakes a journey of three days or three farsakhs, is he permitted to combine and shorten his prayers or not?

He answered:

Regarding combining and shortening prayers on a short journey, there are three views—indeed, four, or even five—in Ahmad’s school.

---

## Page 12083

The first view: Neither combining nor shortening prayers is permitted.

The second: Combining prayers is permitted, but not shortening them.

The third: A resident of Mecca is permitted to combine prayers specifically at ‘Arafah and Muzdalifah, even though his journey is short.

The fourth: Both combining and shortening prayers are permitted at ‘Arafah and Muzdalifah.

The fifth: Both are permitted without restriction.

As for someone who combines prayers on account of travel, may he combine them without restriction, or only when travelling? There are two reports from Ahmad on this—whether resident or travelling. Hence, Ahmad explicitly stated that a person may combine prayers when he has something to attend to. Judge Abū Ya‘lā stated that every excuse permitting absence from Friday prayer and congregational prayer also permits combining prayers. Thus, prayers may be combined because of rain, mud, or a strong, cold wind, according to the apparent position of Imam Ahmad’s school. A sick person, a woman experiencing non-menstrual bleeding, and a nursing mother may also combine prayers.

When a traveller presses on with his journey, he may combine prayers, whether his journey is long or short, as established by the Sunnah of the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him). People combine prayers at ‘Arafah and Muzdalifah, whether they are residents of Mecca or not, although the residents of Mecca make only a short journey. Likewise, the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) and his Rightly Guided Caliphs combined prayers at ‘Arafah and Muzdalifah. Whenever they shortened their prayers, those praying behind them, whether residents of Mecca or not, would also shorten theirs. The distance from Mecca to ‘Arafah is

---

## Page 12084

one barīd—four farsakhs.

For this reason, Mālik and some of Ahmad’s followers, such as Abū al-Khattāb in The Five Acts of Worship, held that the residents of Mecca may shorten their prayers at ‘Arafah and Muzdalifah. This is the correct view, even though it differs from the explicitly reported positions of the three imams: Ahmad, al-Shāfi‘ī, and Abū Hanīfah.

Another group of Ahmad’s followers, along with other scholars, therefore held that prayers may be shortened on both long and short journeys, because the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) prescribed neither a minimum distance nor a duration for shortening prayers. Moreover, the residents of Mecca shortened their prayers behind him at ‘Arafah and Muzdalifah. This is the view of many earlier and later scholars, and it has the strongest support in the evidence. Nevertheless, the journey must be one that is customarily regarded as travel—for example, one for which a person takes provisions and sets out into the open country.

By contrast, someone in a place such as Damascus who moves from one of its villages amid the groves to another, as one goes from al-Sālihiyyah to Damascus, is not a traveller. Similarly, the Prophet’s city (peace and blessings be upon him) resembled a collection of neighbouring villages, with each community having its own palm groves, burial grounds, and mosques, in Qubā’ and elsewhere. Going out to Qubā’ was not considered travel. Hence, the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) and his Companions did not shorten their prayers on such outings. Allah Most High says:

“Among the Bedouins around you are hypocrites, and among the people of Medina as well.”

Thus, all its built-up areas fall within what is called Medina, while those outside its settled population belong to the Bedouins, the tent-dwellers. Someone moving from one part of Medina to another is not a traveller and does not shorten his prayers. These, however, are matters open to scholarly judgment.

---

## Page 12085

so anyone who follows the view of one of the scholars on these matters should neither be censured nor shunned. Similarly, scholars differed over whether an intention is required for combining and shortening prayers. The majority, including Mālik and Abū Hanīfah, do not require it. This is also one of the two views within Ahmad’s school and is what his explicit statements imply.

The second view requires it. This is the position of al-Shāfi‘ī and many of Ahmad’s followers, including al-Khiraqī and others. The first view is stronger, but anyone who follows either view should not be censured.

He was asked:

On a journey lasting one day during Ramadan, is it permissible to shorten the prayers and refrain from fasting?

He replied:

Scholars differ on this matter. The stronger view is that he may shorten his prayers and refrain from fasting on a one-day journey during Ramadan, just as “the residents of Mecca shortened their prayers behind the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) at ‘Arafah and Muzdalifah.” ‘Arafah is a journey of one barīd from the Sacred Mosque. Moreover, travel is mentioned without qualification in the Quran and Sunnah.

---

## Page 12086

He was asked:

A man is travelling to a town where he intends to stay for a month or more. Should he offer his prayers in full or not?

He replied:

If he intends to stay in the town for four days or less, he shortens his prayers, as the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) did when he entered Mecca: he stayed there for four days, shortening his prayers. If the intended stay is longer, scholars differ, and the more cautious course is to offer the prayers in full.

However, if he says, “I shall leave tomorrow or the day after tomorrow,” without intending a settled stay, he may continue shortening his prayers indefinitely, for “the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) stayed in Mecca for ten-odd days, shortening his prayers, and stayed in Tabūk for twenty nights, shortening his prayers.” Allah knows best.

He was asked:

A man went out to al-Kharibah on account of fever, knowing that he would stay for two months. May he shorten his prayers? If shortening is permissible, is it better to offer them in full or to shorten them?

---

## Page 12087

He replied:

Praise be to Allah. Scholars differ on this issue: some hold that offering the prayers in full is obligatory, while others hold that shortening them is obligatory. The correct view is that both are permissible. Whoever shortens them is not to be censured, and whoever offers them in full is not to be censured.

They also differ as to which is preferable. For someone who doubts whether shortening is permissible and wishes to err on the side of caution, offering the prayers in full is preferable. But someone to whom the Sunnah has become clear, and who knows that the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) prescribed only two units of prayer for the traveller, set no temporal or geographical limits on travel, and likewise set no fixed limit on a stay—whether three, four, twelve or fifteen days—should shorten his prayers.

This was the practice of a number of the early Muslims. Indeed, Masrūq was appointed to an office he would not have chosen, and he stayed for years, shortening his prayers. The Muslims stayed at Nahāwand for six months, shortening their prayers. They would shorten their prayers even though they knew that their business would not be concluded within four days or even longer. Likewise, after the conquest of Mecca, the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) and his Companions stayed for nearly twenty days, shortening their prayers. They stayed in Mecca for ten days during Ramadan without fasting. When the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) conquered Mecca, he knew that he would need to stay there for more than four days.

Since setting such a limit has no basis, a traveller continues to shorten his prayers for as long as he remains a traveller, even if he stays in one place for months. Allah knows best.

Written by:
Ahmad ibn Taymiyyah.

---

## Page 12088

He was also asked:

Is combining two prayers while travelling preferable, or shortening them? What are the scholars’ views on this? What evidence does each hold, and which view is strongest?

He replied:

Praise be to Allah. Rather, offering each prayer at its appointed time is preferable when there is no need to combine them. When the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) prayed while travelling, he usually offered the prayers at their appointed times. He combined them only on a few occasions.

Many people have distinguished between combining and shortening prayers, regarding shortening as an established Sunnah and combining as a concession for particular circumstances. This is because, on all his journeys, the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) offered the four-unit prayers as two units. No one has reported that he offered a four-unit prayer as four units while travelling; his Companions likewise prayed two units with him. The hadith reporting from ʿĀ’ishah that “she offered the prayers in full while with him and did not fast” is weak.

Rather, it is authentically reported from her in the Sahīh:

“When prayer was first prescribed, each prayer consisted of two units. Then the prayer for those not travelling was increased, while there was no change to

---

## Page 12089

the prayer for travellers.”

It is also authentically reported that ʿUmar ibn al-Khattāb said:

“The traveller’s prayer is two units; the Friday prayer is two units; the Eid al-Adhā prayer is two units; and the Eid al-Fitr prayer is two units—complete, not shortened, as declared by your Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him).”

As for the words of Allah Most High:

“When you travel through the land, there is no blame upon you for shortening the prayer if you fear that the disbelievers may attack you,”

the declaration that there is no blame clarifies the ruling and removes misgivings; it does not preclude shortening the prayer from being the Sunnah. Likewise, He said:

“Indeed, Safā and Marwah are among the symbols of Allah. So whoever performs Hajj to the House or performs ʿumrah, there is no blame upon him for going between them.”

He declared that there was no blame because of the misgivings they had about going between them, arising from the aversion some of them had to doing so during the pre-Islamic era. Going between them is prescribed by agreement among the Muslims, whether it is considered an essential pillar, an obligation, or an emphatically recommended Sunnah.

He, glorified is He, mentioned both fear and travel because shortening encompasses both reducing the number of prayer units and curtailing the essential acts of prayer. Fear permits curtailing the essential acts, while travel permits reducing the number of units. When both occur together, both forms of shortening are permitted. If travel occurs without fear, one of the two forms of shortening is permitted.

The scholars disagree concerning the traveller: is his prescribed prayer two units, with no need for an intention to shorten it, or may he shorten it only with that intention? There are two views:

---

## Page 12090

The first is the view of most scholars, including Abū Hanīfah and Mālik. It is also one of the two views within Ahmad’s school, adopted by Abū Bakr and others.

The second is the view of al-Shāfi‘ī and the other view within Ahmad’s school, adopted by al-Khiraqī and others. The first is the correct view, supported by the Sunnah of the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him). He would lead his Companions in shortened prayers without informing them beforehand that he would shorten the prayer or instructing them to intend to shorten it. Thus:

“When he inadvertently ended the prayer with the salutation after two units, Dhū al-Yadayn asked him, ‘Has the prayer been shortened, or did you forget?’ He replied, ‘I did not forget, nor has it been shortened.’ He said, ‘But you did forget.’”

Another narration states:

“Had anything changed, I would have told you.”

He did not say, “Had it been shortened, I would have instructed you to intend to shorten it.” Similarly, when he led them in combining prayers, he did not inform them before beginning that he would combine them. Indeed, they did not know that he would combine the prayers until he had completed the first. This also establishes that combining prayers does not require forming that intention when beginning the first prayer, as the majority hold. Ahmad’s explicitly stated position agrees with this.

The scholars have disagreed about praying four units while travelling: is it forbidden, disliked, contrary to what is preferable, or itself preferable?

The position of Abū Hanīfah, and one view within Mālik’s school, is that shortening the prayer is obligatory and that the traveller may not pray four units.

---

## Page 12091

and the position of Mālik according to the other report, and of Ahmad according to one of his two views—indeed, their explicit ruling—is that performing the prayer in full is disliked.

Ahmad’s position according to the other report, and al-Shāfiʿī’s according to the more authoritative of his two views, is that shortening the prayer is preferable, while praying four units is contrary to what is preferable. Al-Shāfiʿī also has a view that praying four units is preferable, but this is the weakest of these views.

Some of the Khārijites held that shortening the prayer is permissible only in circumstances of fear. This is also cited as a view of al-Shāfiʿī, but I do not think it is authentically reported from him. For it is established through the mass-transmitted Sunnah:

“The Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) would lead his Companions at Minā in prayers of two units each, when the people were in complete security.”

Abū Bakr did likewise after him, as did ʿUmar thereafter. Given this, how can combining prayers and shortening them be treated alike? All the imams hold that offering each prayer at its prescribed time is preferable when there is no need to combine them. This is the position of Abū Hanīfah and Mālik, and the apparent position in the schools of al-Shāfiʿī and Ahmad.

Indeed, they differed over the permissibility of combining prayers, holding three views. Abū Hanīfah’s position is that prayers may be combined only at ʿArafah and Muzdalifah. The position of Mālik, and of Ahmad according to one of the two reports, is that a traveller may not combine prayers while stopped, but only while travelling; indeed, Mālik permits it only when he is pressing on with his journey. The position of al-Shāfiʿī, and of Ahmad according to the other report, is that a traveller may combine prayers even while stopped.

---

## Page 12092

The reason for this disagreement lies in the hadiths about combining prayers that had reached them, for such hadiths are few. Combining prayers at ʿArafah and Muzdalifah is unanimously accepted and established through mass transmission, so they did not dispute it.

Abū Hanīfah did not allow combining prayers elsewhere because of the hadith of Ibn Masʿūd recorded in the Sahīh, in which he said:

“I never saw the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him) offer a prayer outside its time except the Fajr prayer at Muzdalifah and the Maghrib prayer on the night of Jamʿ.”

By describing Fajr as being offered “outside its time,” he meant outside the time at which the Prophet habitually offered it. For the Sahīh records from Jābir:

“He offered Fajr at Muzdalifah after dawn had broken.”

Muslims unanimously agree that Fajr may not be offered before dawn breaks, whether at Muzdalifah or elsewhere. At Muzdalifah, however, he offered it very early, while it was still quite dark.

As for most of the imams, authentic hadiths about combining prayers had reached them, such as those narrated by Anas, Ibn ʿAbbās, Ibn ʿUmar and Muʿādh, all of which are authentic.

The two Sahīh collections record from Anas:

“When the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) set out before the sun passed its zenith, he delayed Zuhr until the time of ʿAsr, then stopped and offered both prayers together. When he set out after the sun had passed its zenith, he offered Zuhr and ʿAsr, then mounted.”

Another wording in the Sahīh states:

“When the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) intended to combine the two prayers while travelling, he delayed Zuhr until the beginning of the time of ʿAsr, then combined them.”

The two Sahīh collections record from Ibn ʿUmar:

“When the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) was pressed to travel quickly, he combined Maghrib and ʿIshāʾ.”

Another wording in the Sahīh states “that

---

## Page 12093

Ibn ʿUmar, when pressed to travel quickly, would combine Maghrib and ʿIshāʾ” after the twilight had disappeared. He would say:

“When the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him) was pressed to travel quickly, he would combine Maghrib and ʿIshāʾ.”

Muslim’s Sahīh records from Ibn ʿAbbās:

“The Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) combined prayers during a journey he undertook for the expedition to Tabūk, combining Zuhr with ʿAsr and Maghrib with ʿIshāʾ.”

Saʿīd ibn Jubayr said: “I asked Ibn ʿAbbās, ‘What prompted him to do that?’ He replied, ‘He wanted to avoid imposing hardship on his community.’”

Likewise, Muslim’s Sahīh records from Abū al-Tufayl, from Muʿādh ibn Jabal:

“The Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him) combined Zuhr with ʿAsr and Maghrib with ʿIshāʾ during the expedition to Tabūk.”

Abū al-Tufayl said: “I asked, ‘What prompted him to do that?’ He replied, ‘He wanted to avoid imposing hardship on his community.’”

Indeed, it is established that he combined prayers in Medina, as recorded in the two Sahīh collections from Ibn ʿAbbās:

“The Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him) led us in Zuhr and ʿAsr together, without fear or travel.”

Another wording in the two Sahīh collections, narrated from Ibn ʿAbbās, states:

“The Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) prayed seven and eight units of prayer in Medina, combining Zuhr with ʿAsr and Maghrib with ʿIshāʾ.”

Ayyūb said: “Perhaps it was on a rainy night.” The people of Medina would combine Maghrib and ʿIshāʾ on rainy nights, and ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿUmar would join them. This has also been narrated as a report traced back to the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him). This was a practice of the Companions.

---

## Page 12094

Their statement, “He wanted to avoid imposing hardship on his community,” makes clear that combining prayers does not mean delaying the first prayer until the end of its time and performing the second at the beginning of its time, for observing such precise timing entails great hardship. Moreover, this is permissible for anyone at any time, whereas hardship is relieved when a need arises. He must therefore have granted those with valid excuses a concession that relieves their hardship, unlike those without such excuses.

This rests on a principle observed by the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him): there are three prayer times for those with valid excuses and five for everyone else. Allah Most High says:

“Establish prayer at the two ends of the day and at the approach of the night.”

He thus mentions three prayer times. The second end of the day encompasses Zuhr and ʿAsr, while the approaching hours of the night encompass Maghrib and ʿIshāʾ.

Likewise, He says:

“Establish prayer at the decline of the sun until the darkness of the night.”

According to the more correct of the two views, the sun’s decline means its passing the zenith. The expressions used for this describe the sun as declining, passing its zenith, inclining, or slanting. Thus, He mentions the sun’s decline and the darkness of night. Zuhr and ʿAsr are performed after the sun’s decline, and Maghrib and ʿIshāʾ during the darkness of night. He mentions the beginning of the time—the sun’s decline—and its end—the darkness of night. This darkness means the full onset of night and its gloom.

For this reason, Companions such as ʿAbd al-Rahmān ibn ʿAwf and others said: If a menstruating woman becomes pure before dawn, she performs Maghrib and ʿIshāʾ; if she becomes pure before sunset, she performs Zuhr and ʿAsr. This is the position of the majority of jurists, such as Mālik and al-Shāfiʿī

---

## Page 12095

and Ahmad. Moreover, the Prophet’s combining of prayers at Arafah and Muzdalifah indicates that prayers may also be combined elsewhere for a valid excuse. He could have performed Zuhr and delayed Asr until its time began, but Asr was brought forward to accommodate the pilgrimage rites and allow him to devote himself to standing at Arafah.

For this reason, the position accepted by the overwhelming majority of scholars is that prayers may be combined at Muzdalifah and Arafah both by those whose homes are far enough away to permit shortening prayers and by those whose homes are not. When the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) prayed, all the Muslims prayed with him, including the people of Mecca and others. He did not instruct any of them to delay Asr or to bring Maghrib forward. Thus, the view held by some followers of al-Shāfiʿī and Ahmad that the people of Mecca may not combine prayers is exceedingly weak and contrary to the clear, unequivocal Sunnah about which there is no doubt. Their excuse is that they believed a long journey to be the reason for combining prayers. The correct view, however, is that combining prayers is not restricted to long journeys. Rather, prayers may be combined because of rain or illness, as established in the Sunnah concerning a woman experiencing non-menstrual bleeding: the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) instructed her to combine prayers in two hadiths.

Furthermore, scholars hold two views on whether combining prayers is restricted to long journeys, both of which are represented within Ahmad’s school. One view—the well-known position—is that prayers may be combined on a short journey, whereas al-Shāfiʿī’s position is that they may not. The former is more correct for the reasons already given. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 12096

He was asked:

What was the Prophet’s practice regarding combining prayers (peace and blessings be upon him)?

He replied:

As for combining prayers, he did so only on certain occasions, when pressing on with his journey and when he had a valid excuse under Islamic law. Thus, he combined prayers at Arafah and Muzdalifah, and sometimes during the expedition to Tabūk. If he set out before the sun passed its zenith, he would delay Zuhr until the time of Asr and then offer both together. This is established in the Sahīh. If he set out after the sun passed its zenith, it is reported that he would offer Zuhr and Asr together, as he did at Arafah. This is well known in the Sunan collections. This applied when he would not halt until the time of Maghrib, just as at Arafah he would not depart until sunset. If, however, he halted at the time of Asr, he would offer it at its appointed time.

Shortening prayers is therefore not like combining them. Shortening is a regularly observed Sunnah, whereas combining is an occasional concession. Any layperson who treats combining and shortening prayers as equivalent is ignorant of the Sunnah of the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him) and of the statements of Muslim scholars. For the Sunnah of the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him) distinguishes between the two, and the scholars

---

## Page 12097

agree that one of them is a Sunnah, while differing over whether it is obligatory, whereas they dispute the permissibility of the other. How, then, can the two be equated?

The school that allows the greatest latitude in combining two prayers is that of Imām Ahmad. He explicitly stated that combining prayers is permissible because of hardship or other commitments, citing a hadith reported concerning this.

Al-Qādī Abū Ya‘lā and other scholars of our school explained: “This means that if someone has a commitment that permits him to miss the Friday prayer and congregational prayer, he may combine prayers.”

According to Ahmad, Mālik, and a group of scholars from al-Shāfi‘ī’s school, combining prayers is permissible because of illness. All three permit combining Maghrib and Isha because of rain, but they disagree concerning the two daytime prayers. According to the apparent position of Ahmad’s and Mālik’s schools, combining prayers is permissible because of mud, strong cold winds, and similar conditions. A nursing mother may also combine prayers if washing her garment at the time of every prayer causes her hardship. Ahmad explicitly stated this.

The scholars also differed over whether combining and shortening prayers require an intention to do so. The majority held that no such intention is required. This is the position of Mālik and Abū Hanīfah, and one of the two positions within Ahmad’s school; it is also what his explicit statements and underlying principles indicate.

Al-Shāfi‘ī and a group of scholars from Ahmad’s school held that an intention is required. The majority’s position, however, is the one supported by the Sunnah of the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him), as has been explained at length in the appropriate place. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 12098

He was asked—may Allah have mercy on him:

“Regarding combining Maghrib and Isha because of rain: is it permissible because of severe cold or strong winds, or is it permissible only because of rain?”

He replied:

Praise be to Allah, Lord of all worlds. It is permissible to combine Maghrib and Isha because of rain, strong cold winds, or deep mud. This is the sounder of the two scholarly opinions and is the apparent position of the schools of Ahmad, Mālik, and others. And Allah knows best.

He was asked—may Allah have mercy on him:

“A man leads a congregation in prayer. After rain and snow had fallen, he was about to lead them in Maghrib when they said to him, ‘Combine the prayers.’ He replied, ‘I will not.’ May the members of the congregation pray in their homes or not?”

He replied:

Praise be to Allah. Yes, it is permissible to combine prayers because of deep mud, strong cold winds on a dark night, and similar conditions, even if the rain is not

---

## Page 12099

falling, according to the sounder of the two scholarly opinions. This is preferable to their praying in their homes. Indeed, refraining from combining the prayers while praying at home instead is an innovation contrary to the Sunnah, for the Sunnah is to perform the five daily prayers in congregation in the mosques. This is preferable to praying at home, by the consensus of the Muslims.

Combining the prayers in the mosques is preferable to praying them separately at home, by the agreement of the imams who permit combining prayers, such as Mālik, al-Shāfiʿī, and Ahmad. And Allah Most High knows best.

He said—may Allah have mercy on him:

Section

As for prayers performed in exceptional circumstances—such as the obligatory prayers in times of fear, illness, and travel; prayers to ward off affliction when its causes arise, such as prayers occasioned by signs like eclipses and similar events; prayers to seek blessings, such as the prayer for rain; and the funeral prayer—the jurists of the hadith tradition, such as Ahmad and others, follow the general body of established reports from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and his Companions on this subject. Thus, they permit all the forms of prayer in times of fear that have been reliably transmitted from the Prophet

---

## Page 12100

, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. They prefer shortening prayers while travelling, following the Sunnah of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, for he never performed a four-rakʿah prayer during travel without shortening it. They do not, however, consider the prayer of someone who performs four rakʿahs invalid, because the Companions accepted this from those among them who did so. Some regard it as disliked, while others do not, although they consider shortening preferable. Two positions are reported from Ahmad on this matter.

This differs from combining two prayers, which the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, did only on a few occasions. They therefore recommend refraining from combining unless there is a need, following the example of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, who combined when pressing on with his journey. Indeed, differing positions are reported from Ahmad on whether a traveller who has stopped and is not on the move may combine prayers. Thus, Ahl al-Sunnah unanimously agree that shortening prayers during travel is permissible, but differ over the permissibility of performing them in full. They also unanimously agree that performing the two prayers separately is permissible, but differ over the permissibility of combining them.

They permit all the forms of the “eclipse prayer” established from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. The most authentic and best-known form has two bowings in each rakʿah. Authentic reports also mention three and four bowings in each rakʿah. They permit omitting the additional bowing, as reported from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. They prolong the prostrations in this prayer, as authentically reported from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and recite aloud during it, as likewise authentically established from him.

---

## Page 12101

Likewise, regarding “seeking rain,” they permit going out to open ground to perform the prayer for rain and supplicate, as is established from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. They also permit going out and supplicating without performing a prayer, as ʿUmar, may Allah be pleased with him, did in the presence of the Companions. They permit supplicating for rain in conjunction with the regular prayers, as during the Friday sermon and similar occasions, as the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, did.

Likewise, regarding the “funeral prayer,” their preferred practice is to pronounce four takbīrs (declarations that Allah is the Greatest), since it is established that this was the usual practice of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and his Companions. According to the well-known position reported from Ahmad, it is permissible to pronounce five takbīrs and to follow the imam in doing so, since it is established that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, pronounced five, and several Companions, including ʿAlī ibn Abī Tālib, did likewise. According to the sound position in his school, it is also permissible to pronounce seven takbīrs and to follow the imam in doing so, since it is established that the Companions sometimes pronounced seven after the death of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and because there is also a narration to this effect from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace.

---

## Page 12102

Shaykh al-Islām Ahmad ibn Taymiyyah—may Allah have mercy on him—also said:

All praise is due to Allah. We seek His help and forgiveness, and we seek refuge in Allah from the evil within ourselves and from our evil deeds. Whoever Allah guides, none can lead astray; and whoever He leads astray, none can guide. I bear witness that none is worthy of worship except Allah alone, with no partner, and I bear witness that Muhammad is His servant and Messenger. May Allah bless him and his family and grant him peace.

To proceed:

This is “A Principle Concerning Rulings That Differ between Travel and Residence,” such as shortening the prayers, not fasting during the month of Ramadan, and similar matters.

Most jurists among the followers of al-Shāfiʿī, Ahmad, and others have divided these rulings into two categories: those specific to long journeys, namely shortening the prayers and not fasting; and those applicable to both long and short journeys, such as dry ablution and praying on one’s mount. Eating carrion also belongs to this second category. As for wiping over leather socks and combining two prayers, these belong to the first category. There is disagreement concerning this.

The discussion has two parts:

---

## Page 12103

The first is the distinction between long and short journeys. On this, we say:

This distinction has no basis in the Book of Allah or the Sunnah of His Messenger, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. Rather, Allah has made the rulings connected with travel applicable without qualification, as in His words in the verse on purification:

“If you are ill, or on a journey, or one of you has relieved himself”;

and His words in the verse on fasting:

“Whoever among you is ill or on a journey, then an equal number of other days”;

and His words:

“When you travel through the land, there is no blame upon you for shortening the prayer if you fear that the disbelievers may harm you.”

Likewise, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Allah has relieved the traveller of fasting and half the prayer.”

‘Ā’ishah said: “Prayer was prescribed as two rak‘ahs. The prayer during travel was left unchanged, while the prayer during residence was increased.”

‘Umar said: “The Eid al-Adhā prayer is two rak‘ahs, the Eid al-Fitr prayer is two rak‘ahs, the prayer during travel is two rak‘ahs, and the Friday prayer is two rak‘ahs—complete, not shortened, as declared by your Prophet.”

He, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, also said:

“A resident may wipe for one day and one night, and a traveller for three days and their nights.”

And the statement of Safwān ibn ‘Assāl: “

---

## Page 12104

The Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, instructed us, when we were travelling or on a journey, not to remove our leather socks for three days and their nights, except because of major ritual impurity, but not because of defecation, urination, or sleep.”

The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, also said:

“When a servant falls ill or travels, the deeds he used to perform while healthy and resident are recorded for him.”

And he, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Travel is a portion of torment: it deprives you of your sleep, food, and drink. So when one of you has accomplished the purpose of his journey, let him hasten back to his family.”

These texts, and others from the Book and Sunnah, make no distinction between long and short journeys. Whoever distinguishes between them has thus separated what Allah has treated alike, making a distinction with no basis in the Book of Allah or the Sunnah of His Messenger.

This practice—whereby the Lawgiver attaches a ruling to whatever falls under an unqualified term, while some people distinguish between its different kinds without any evidence from revelation—has parallels. One is that the Lawgiver attached purification to whatever is called water, in His words:

“and find no water, then seek clean earth.”

He did not distinguish between one kind of water and another, nor did He divide water into two categories: merely pure and purifying.

Another is that the Lawgiver attached the ruling on wiping to whatever is called a leather sock, without distinguishing between one kind and another. This therefore includes those that are split or have holes, and other kinds, without any specified restriction. Nor did the Lawgiver stipulate that they must remain in place by themselves.

---

## Page 12105

Another example is that He attached the right to take one’s wife back to divorce after consummation, without dividing divorce after consummation into irrevocable and revocable divorce.

Another is that He recognized a third divorce following two divorces and a separation secured by compensation. Such a separation is obtained in return for compensation, and He made it entail an irrevocable separation without a divorce being counted toward the three. This ruling is attached to whatever falls under this designation, without distinguishing between one wording and another.

Another is that He attached expiation to whatever is termed an oath sworn by a Muslim, in His words, Exalted is He:

“That is the expiation for your oaths when you have sworn.”

And His words:

“Allah has prescribed for you a way to release yourselves from your oaths.”

He did not distinguish between one oath sworn by a Muslim and another. Dividing Muslims’ binding oaths into those that can be expiated and those that cannot therefore contradicts this.

Another is that He attached prohibition to whatever is termed an intoxicant, and explained what this encompasses through the words of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace:

“Every intoxicating substance is khamr, and every intoxicating substance is forbidden.”

He did not distinguish between one intoxicating substance and another.

Another is that He attached the ruling to whatever is termed residence, just as He attached it to whatever is termed travel, without distinguishing between one resident and another. Thus, there is no basis for dividing residents into two categories: those who must attend Friday prayer when others establish it, but who do not themselves count toward the congregation required to establish it; and those who do count.

Rather, it must be held that, since the Lawgiver attached these rulings to whatever is termed travel, they

---

## Page 12106

apply to every journey, whether long or short. There are, however, certain matters that are not specific to travel but are permitted both while travelling and while at home. Allah did not restrict the ruling concerning someone compelled to eat carrion to travel, although such necessity most often arises during travel. In this regard, there is no distinction between being at home and undertaking a long or short journey. This ruling should therefore not be made dependent on travel.

As for combining two prayers, is it permissible on a short journey? There are two views within Ahmad’s school. The first is that it is not permissible, as in al-Shāfiʿī’s school, by analogy with shortening prayers. The second is that it is permissible, as Mālik held, because combining prayers is also prescribed while at home on account of illness or rain. It is therefore like eating carrion: its operative cause is need, not travel. This is the correct view, for combining two prayers is not dependent on travel; rather, it is permitted because of need, unlike shortening prayers.

As for praying on a mount, this is established in authentic hadith. Indeed:

“It is widely reported that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, would pray on his mount while travelling, facing whichever direction it took him, and would offer the witr prayer on it. However, he would not offer the obligatory prayers on it.”

Is this permissible while at home? There are two positions within Ahmad’s school and those of others. Thus, if this is permitted

---

## Page 12107

while at home, then it is all the more permissible on a short journey. If, however, it is prohibited while at home, then distinguishing it from shortening prayers and breaking the fast requires evidence.

The Second Issue:
The extent of the journey upon which the Lawgiver has made permission to break the fast and shorten prayers dependent.

There has been considerable disagreement over this. Some have said three days; others, two days at a moderate pace; and others, less than that—even as little as one mile.

Among those who defined it by distance, some specified forty-eight miles; others, forty-six, forty-five, or forty. These are views reported from Mālik. Abū Muhammad al-Maqdisī said: “I know of no basis for the position adopted by the imams.” It is just as he said—may Allah have mercy on him—for fixing such a limit is not established by any scriptural text, consensus, or analogy.

Most of these scholars distinguish between long and short journeys, making this the threshold for a long journey. Others do not call anything a journey unless it reaches this threshold, and do not regard anything shorter as a journey.

Those who specified three days cited his statement:

“A traveller may wipe over his footwear for three days and their nights.”

Yet it is authentically reported from him in the two Sahīhs that he said:

“A woman must not travel a journey of three days unless accompanied by a mahram (a male relative whom she is permanently forbidden to marry).”

It is also authentically reported from him in the two Sahīhs that he

---

## Page 12108

said: “A journey of two days.” It is also established in the Sahīh: “A journey of one day,” and in the Sunan collections: “One barīd (postal stage).” This shows that all these distances constitute travel. His permission for the traveller to wipe over his footwear for three days merely permits this for someone travelling that long; it does not imply that this is the minimum duration of travel. Likewise, his permission for a resident to wipe for a day and a night does not imply that this is the minimum duration of residence.

Those who stipulated two days relied on statements of Ibn Umar and Ibn Abbās. Yet disagreement on this matter is well known among the Companions, with differing statements reported even from Ibn Umar and Ibn Abbās themselves. The report, “People of Mecca, do not shorten your prayers over a distance of less than four barīds, from Mecca to Usfān,” is merely a statement of Ibn Abbās. Its attribution to the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—in the narration of Ibn Khuzaymah and others is unquestionably false according to the leading hadith scholars. How could the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—have addressed the people of Mecca with such a limit, when he stayed there only briefly after his emigration, while in Medina he set no comparable limit for its inhabitants? And why would such a limit be specified for the people of Mecca alone, rather than the rest of the Muslims?

Moreover, setting a limit in miles and parasangs requires knowledge of land measurement, something known only to a select few. Those who cite such measurements merely report them from others, relying on their authority; this is not something established with certainty. The Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—never measured the land at all. How, then, could the Lawgiver prescribe for his community a limit that had never

---

## Page 12109

been mentioned in his words, when he was sent to all mankind? The distance that constitutes travel must therefore be common knowledge. Measuring the land is not practicable; indeed, it is either impossible or exceedingly difficult. Even if kings and others like them can survey a road, they can only measure it along a straight line or along precisely defined curves. Yet travellers may know other routes and may take them, and the route may include uphill stretches. Some people’s journeys take longer because they move slowly, while others’ take less time because they move quickly. The operative cause is travel itself, not the measured extent of the land.

What we find in the words of the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—and the Companions is the measurement of distances in terms of time, as in his statement about the Cistern:

“Its length is a month’s journey, and its breadth is a month’s journey.”

And his statement:

“Between heaven and earth are five hundred years.”

Another hadith states:

“Seventy-one, seventy-two, or seventy-three years.”

It has been said that the first refers to the usual pace of travel, on camelback or on foot, while the second refers to the pace of postal couriers, who ordinarily cover seven times the distance covered at the usual pace. Likewise, the Companions would speak of “a full day” or “two days.” Hence, those who set the limit at forty-eight miles described it as two days’ travel at a moderate pace, on camelback or on foot. But there is no evidence for this.

That being so, we say: whenever a term has no defined limit in either the language or the revealed law, customary usage is the point of reference. Thus, whatever counts as travel in people’s customary usage is

---

## Page 12110

the travel to which the Lawgiver attached the ruling. An example is the journey of the people of Mecca to Arafah. This distance is one barīd, and the Sunnah establishes that shortening and combining prayers are permissible on such a journey. A barīd is half a day’s travel on camelback or on foot, and a quarter of the distance of two days and two nights. This is what is sometimes called the distance permitting shortened prayers, and someone travelling that distance can return the same day.

As for shorter distances—if the distance permitting shortened prayers is defined by measurement—it has been said that one may shorten one’s prayers over a distance of one mile. Ibn Umar is reported to have said:

“If I travelled one mile, I would shorten my prayers.”

Ibn Hazm said:

“We have found no one who shortened his prayers over a distance of less than one mile, while Ibn Umar and others are known to have shortened theirs over this distance. The Lawgiver set no limit for travel, so we adopted this position in adherence to the unrestricted wording of the Sunnah. We have found no one who shortened his prayers over a distance of less than one mile.”

Yet, according to his own principles, this does not constitute a consensus. If the apparent meaning of the text includes shorter distances, it should not matter to him that he knows of no one who adopted that view, as is his usual approach in similar cases. Furthermore, Ibn Umar’s statement does not say that he would not shorten his prayers over a shorter distance. It is also established that Ibn Umar would not shorten his prayers on a journey of one or two days. Either his statements conflict, or they must be understood as referring to different circumstances.

The discussion concerns two points:

“The First Point”

Whoever undertakes a journey like that of the people of Mecca to Arafāt

---

## Page 12111

may shorten his prayers. If, however, it is held that no fixed distance applies, and that what matters is whether the journey constitutes travel, then whoever undertakes what is called travel may shorten his prayers; otherwise, he may not. A man may ride out a parasang to investigate some matter, covering several miles and returning within an hour or two, without being considered a traveller. Another person covering the same distance may be considered a traveller because he journeys by camel or on foot in such a way that he does not return to his starting point that day. There are several lines of evidence for this.

First, authentic reports, accepted by agreement among hadith scholars, establish that during the Farewell Pilgrimage the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, shortened his prayers at Arafah, Muzdalifah and during the days at Minā. Abū Bakr and Umar did likewise after him. The people of Mecca prayed behind them, and they did not instruct them to complete their prayers in full. No one has transmitted, through either a sound or a weak chain, that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace—when he led the Muslims at Batn Uranah in two units of the Zuhr prayer followed by two units of the Asr prayer, shortening and combining them—said to the people of Mecca, “People of Mecca, complete your prayers in full.” Nor did he instruct them to delay the Asr prayer.

Nor has anyone reported that any of the pilgrims, whether from Mecca or elsewhere, prayed behind the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, differently from the way he led the Muslim congregation as a whole. Whoever reports that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, or Umar said on that day, “People of Mecca, complete your prayers in full, for we are travellers,” has erred. What has been reported is only that

---

## Page 12112

the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said this to the people of Mecca within Mecca itself in the year of the conquest. It is also established that Umar ibn al-Khattāb said it to the people of Mecca when he prayed within Mecca itself.

It is clear that, had the people of Mecca stood up to complete their prayers as four units, doing so at Arafah, Muzdalifah and Minā during the days spent there, this would necessarily have been widely reported, given the abundant reasons and motives for transmitting it. Indeed, had they delayed the Asr prayer and then stood up separately from the other pilgrims to pray it in shortened form, that too would have been reported. How much more so had they completed Zuhr as four units, unlike the rest of the Muslims! Furthermore, if they had begun completing Zuhr while the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, had already begun Asr, he would either have had to wait for them by prolonging his standing, or they would have missed part—indeed, most—of Asr with him. What, then, if they were completing all their prayers in full?

This argument is binding on everyone, but it is even more compelling against those who say that the people of Mecca combined their prayers with him. The scholars have differed over whether the people of Mecca may shorten and combine their prayers at Arafah, holding three views. The first is that they may neither shorten nor combine them. This is the well-known position among the followers of al-Shāfiʿī and a group of Ahmad’s followers, including al-Qādī in al-Mujarrad and Ibn Aqīl in al-Fusūl, because they believe that this depends on a long journey, whereas this journey is short.

---

## Page 12113

The second view is that they may combine their prayers but not shorten them. This is the position of Abū Hanīfah and a group of Ahmad’s and al-Shāfi'ī’s followers. The statements transmitted from Ahmad accord with this, for on several occasions he answered that they should not shorten their prayers; he did not say that they should not combine them. This is the view that Abū Muhammad al-Maqdisī favoured regarding combining prayers, and he was right to do so.

The third view is that they may both combine and shorten their prayers. This is the position of Mālik and Ishāq ibn Rāhawayh, and the view of Tāwūs, Ibn 'Uyaynah and other early scholars. It is also the view of a group of Ahmad’s and al-Shāfi'ī’s followers, including Abū al-Khattāb in al-'Ibādāt al-khams (The Five Acts of Worship). This is the view favoured by Abū Muhammad al-Maqdisī and others among Ahmad’s followers. Abū Muhammad and those who agreed with him favoured permitting a Meccan to combine prayers at Arafah. As for shortening them, Abū Muhammad said: “The evidence supports those who permit every traveller to shorten the prayer, unless a consensus is established to the contrary.”

It is known that no consensus has been established to the contrary. This is also the position chosen by a group of scholars among Ahmad’s followers; some of them would shorten their prayers on a journey of one barīd. This is the correct view, and anyone who has understood and reflected upon the Sunnah may not maintain otherwise. Whoever carefully considers the hadiths concerning the Farewell Pilgrimage and their context will know with certainty that those who accompanied the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, whether from Mecca or elsewhere, prayed with him, shortening and combining their prayers, and did not do otherwise. No one has ever reported that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said, either at Arafah or

---

## Page 12114

Muzdalifah or Minā:

“People of Mecca, complete your prayers, for we are travellers.”

It is only reported that he said this within Mecca itself, as the compilers of the Sunan collections narrated from him. His saying this within Mecca, but not at Arafah, Muzdalifah or Minā, indicates a distinction between them. It has been reported through the Iraqis that 'Umar used to say at Minā, “People of Mecca, complete your prayers, for we are travellers,” but this report has no chain of transmission.

If that is established, it may be argued that combining the two prayers is permitted on account of the pilgrimage rites, as the Hanafīs and a group of Ahmad’s followers maintain. This is also what Ahmad’s explicit statement implies: he prohibits a Meccan from shortening prayers at Arafah but does not prohibit him from combining them. Regarding a traveller’s combining prayers, he said that this is permitted on a long journey, just as shortening prayers is, in his view.

If it is held that prayers are combined on account of the pilgrimage rites, there are two views. The first is that prayers may be combined only at Arafah and Muzdalifah, as the Hanafīs maintain. The second is that they may also be combined for other reasons warranting it, even when no travel is involved. This is the position of the three imams: Mālik, al-Shāfi'ī and Ahmad.

Alternatively, it may be argued that this is because it is a short journey, and combining prayers is permissible on short journeys. Both explanations have been advanced by some jurists among the followers of Mālik, al-Shāfi'ī and Ahmad. For combining prayers is not restricted to travel, and the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, did not

---

## Page 12115

combine prayers during his pilgrimage except at Arafah and Muzdalifah. He did not combine them at Minā or on his outward or return journey, although he had previously combined them during the expedition to Tabūk. The correct view is that he did not combine prayers at Arafah merely because of travel, as he shortened them because of travel. Rather, he combined them because maintaining an uninterrupted standing at Arafah kept him from stopping to pray, and because he was occupied with travelling to Muzdalifah.

Thus, he combined prayers at Arafah for the sake of worship, and at Muzdalifah because he was pressing on with his journey there. He would do likewise on his travels: when pressing on with his journey, he would delay the earlier prayer until the time of the later one, then stop and pray them together, as he did at Muzdalifah.

Nothing in his law is an exception to legal analogy. Rather, combining prayers as he did there is also prescribed in comparable circumstances, as the majority maintain. Abū Hanīfah, however, holds that this is an exception to legal analogy. Yet it is known that an exception to an effective cause, unless due to a missing condition or the presence of an impediment, demonstrates that cause’s invalidity. There is neither inconsistency nor contradiction in what comes from Allah. Rather, like cases receive like rulings, and when a ruling is established on the basis of an effective cause, it also applies wherever an equivalent cause is present.

As for “shortening prayers,” there is no doubt that it is specific to travel and has no connection with the pilgrimage rites. The only justification for the people of Mecca shortening prayers at Arafah and elsewhere is that they were travelling. Arafah is one barīd from the Sacred Mosque, as stated by those who measured the distance and by al-Azraqī in “Akhbār Makkah” (Accounts of Mecca). Thus, this was shortening prayers on a journey of one barīd. When they returned to Minā, they were on the return leg of their journey, and the furthest they had intended to travel was only

---

## Page 12116

one barīd. What difference is there between the people of Mecca travelling to Arafah and other Muslims travelling an equivalent distance from their own towns? Allah has permitted only travellers to pray two units of prayer, so it follows that they were travellers. A resident praying behind a traveller must pray four units, as the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, told the people of Mecca while in Mecca:

“Complete your prayers, for we are travellers.”

This is the position of the four imams and other scholars, although there is disagreement within Mālik’s school.

The second proof:

He prohibited a woman from travelling without a mahram—a male relative whom she can never lawfully marry—or her husband. Sometimes he specified a limit, and sometimes he left the prohibition unrestricted. The shortest distance reported in narrations specifying a limit is one barīd. This indicates that travelling one barīd can constitute a journey, just as travelling for three days, two days, or one day can constitute a journey. These hadiths do not imply that other journeys are exempt; rather, he prohibited each of these.

The third proof:

The Lawgiver has not defined a limit for travel, nor does the language assign it a fixed limit. Its meaning therefore rests on what people recognize and customarily regard as travel: whatever they consider travel is travel. A traveller intends to reach his destination and return home. The minimum is a day’s journey, half spent going and half returning; the one-way distance is thus one barīd. Jurists have used this distance as a threshold in matters of “testimony concerning another witness’s testimony,” “a judge’s letter to another judge,” “summoning a litigant,” “child custody,” and other matters explained in their respective discussions. This is one of the two positions within Ahmad’s school. Thus, if the distance were

---

## Page 12117

subject to a fixed limit, setting that limit at one barīd would be more appropriate. The correct view, however, is that travel is not defined by a fixed distance; rather, it varies. A person may be a traveller over a distance of one barīd, yet cover a greater distance without being a traveller.

The fourth proof:

Allah has granted the traveller permission not to fast in Ramadan, and the minimum period of not fasting is one day. A person can travel a distance of one barīd and return in a day, and so needs to refrain from fasting in Ramadan and to shorten his prayers. This differs from a shorter distance, over which he may need neither to shorten his prayers nor to refrain from fasting if he sets out early in the day and returns before noon. If his outward journey takes a day and his return journey takes a day, he needs to shorten his prayers and refrain from fasting. This may imply that he may be permitted to shorten his prayers and refrain from fasting over a distance of one barīd, although he may not be permitted to do so over a greater distance if he is not considered a traveller.

The fifth proof:

Setting the distance at three days’ travel is no more justified than setting it at two days, nor are two days more justified than one day. It follows that there must be no fixed limit; rather, whatever is called travel makes [these concessions] lawful (1). The Sunnah has established the shortening of prayers over a distance of one barīd, so we know that some journeys may be only one barīd, and that the shortest distance called travel in the language of the Lawgiver is one barīd. As for distances shorter than one barīd, such as one mile, it is established in the two Sahīh collections “that the Prophet

> **Footnotes:**
> (1) The text in square brackets is absent from the printed edition, and I could not find it in Siyānat Majmū' al-Fatāwā min al-Saqt wa-l-Tashīf (Safeguarding the Collected Fatwas against Omissions and Scribal Errors).
> Usāma ibn al-Zahrā’ — book coordinator for the Shamela Encyclopedia.

---

## Page 12118

—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—used to visit Qubā every Saturday, sometimes riding and sometimes walking.” There is no doubt that the people of Qubā and others from al-ʿAwālī, the upland settlements, used to visit the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, in Medina. Neither he nor they shortened their prayers on these visits. They would come to Friday prayer from distances of about a mile or a farsakh without shortening their prayers. Friday prayer is obligatory for anyone who hears the call, and the call may be heard from a farsakh away. Yet not everyone obliged to attend Friday prayer is permitted to shorten their prayers.

Some of al-ʿAwālī forms part of Medina, although the name “Medina” encompasses all the settlements, as Allah Most High says:

“Among the Bedouins around you are hypocrites, as are some of the people of Medina, who persist in hypocrisy.”

And He says:

“It was not for the people of Medina and the Bedouins around them to remain behind the Messenger of Allah.”

As for what has been reported from Ibn ʿUmar, its authenticity must first be examined. If it is authentic, the reports from him differ, and other Companions disagreed with him. Perhaps he meant once one has covered a mile of the journey. There is no doubt that Qubā is more than a mile from Medina, yet neither Ibn ʿUmar nor others used to shorten their prayers when they went to Qubā.

Thus, the fact that the people of Mecca shortened their prayers at ʿArafah, whereas the people of Medina did not shorten theirs when going to Qubā and similar places around Medina, demonstrates the distinction. And Allah knows best.

If praying on one’s mount is specific to travel, it may only be done on a journey that is called travel. For this reason, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, did not pray on his mount during

---

## Page 12119

his visits to the mosque of Qubā, although he went there both riding and on foot. Nor did the Muslims coming in from al-ʿAwālī do so. This is because the distance was short, like a distance travelled within a town. The name “Medina” encompasses all its settlements, and the people there were either inhabitants of Medina or Bedouins, as the Quran indicates. Whoever was not a Bedouin was therefore an inhabitant of Medina. Accordingly, travelling to Qubā was like travelling within Medina itself. If that were permitted, praying on one’s mount within the town would also be permitted; otherwise, there is no distinction between the two.

When the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, led his Companions in combined and shortened prayers, he did not instruct any of them to intend to combine or shorten them. Rather, he travelled from Medina to Mecca, offering two-unit prayers without combining them. Then he led them in the noon prayer at ʿArafah without telling them that he intended to offer the afternoon prayer immediately afterwards. He then led them in the afternoon prayer, although they had not intended to combine the prayers. This was combining prayers at the time of the earlier prayer.

Likewise, when he departed from Medina, he led them in the afternoon prayer at Dhū al-Hulayfah, offering two units, without instructing them to intend to shorten the prayer. It is reported in al-Sahīh:

“When he offered one of the two afternoon prayers and ended it with the salutation after two units, Dhū al-Yadayn asked him, ‘Has the prayer been shortened, or have you forgotten?’ He replied, ‘I have not forgotten, nor has it been shortened.’ He said, ‘Indeed, you have forgotten.’ The Prophet asked, ‘Is it as Dhū al-Yadayn says?’ They replied, ‘Yes.’ He then completed the prayer.”

If shortening prayers were permissible only when they intended it, he would have explained this, and they would have known it. To my knowledge, it has not been reported that Imam Ahmad stipulated an intention for either combining prayers or

---

## Page 12120

shortening them; however, some of his followers, such as al-Khiraqī and al-Qādī, stipulated this. As for Abū Bakr ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz and others, they adopted the position consistent with the unrestricted wording of his statements: no intention is required for either combining or shortening prayers. This is the view of the majority of scholars, including Mālik, Abū Hanīfah and others. Indeed, Ahmad expressly stated that a traveller may offer the night prayer before the twilight disappears, explaining that this is because he is permitted to combine prayers. Abū Tālib and al-Marwadhī transmitted this from him, and al-Qādī mentioned it in al-Jāmiʿ al-Kabīr. It is therefore clear that an intention is not a condition for combining prayers.

Nor is “close succession” a condition. Since he permitted the night prayer to be offered before the twilight disappears, explaining that this is because the traveller may combine prayers, he cannot have meant the white twilight. His position, transmitted from him through numerous reports, is that a traveller offers the night prayer after the red twilight disappears. According to him, that marks the beginning of its time and the end of the sunset prayer’s time. Thus, the traveller would be offering the night prayer within its own allotted time, not during the time of the sunset prayer.

When not travelling, however, he recommended delaying the night prayer until the white twilight disappears. He explained: “Walls may conceal the redness, so a person may think that the red twilight has disappeared when it has not. Once the whiteness disappears, he can be certain that the redness has disappeared.”

Thus, in his view, twilight means the redness in both situations. However, because walls may conceal the twilight when one is not travelling, creating uncertainty, he exercised caution by waiting for the onset of the white twilight. This is his position, transmitted through his many explicit statements.

---

## Page 12121

Some have reported a statement from him that twilight means the white twilight when not travelling and the red twilight when travelling. The true meaning of this report is as explained above. Otherwise, neither Ahmad nor any other Muslim scholar said that twilight itself differs according to whether one is travelling or not. Ahmad explained the reason for the distinction. Thus, if an ambiguous statement is reported from him, his explicit explanation clarifies it. Some have also reported from him that twilight means the whiteness without qualification. I believe this is simply a mistaken attribution to him.

His position is that, when travelling, the disappearance of the first phase of twilight marks the end of the sunset prayer’s time and the beginning of the night prayer’s time. Yet he permits a traveller to offer the night prayer before twilight disappears, explaining that this is because the traveller may combine prayers. It follows that he was referring to offering it before twilight disappears, not after the red twilight disappears, since in the latter case permission to combine prayers could not serve as the explanation.

Second: His statement indicates that, in his view, combining prayers means offering them within the same prayer time, even if they are not performed consecutively. This is like combining them during the second prayer’s time, according to the well-known position of his school and that of others. Thus, where combining is permitted, one may offer the sunset prayer at the beginning of its time and the night prayer at the end of the sunset prayer’s time.
