He also explicitly stated something comparable: “If he offers one of the two prayers being combined at home and the other in the mosque, there is nothing wrong with that.” This is an explicit statement from him that combining prayers means combining them within one prayer time, without requiring them to be performed consecutively. Some of his followers interpreted this as applying only when the interval is short, but that contradicts his explicit wording. Furthermore, because the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace,

---

## Page 12122

when he led them in Medina in eight units of prayer together and seven together, is not reported to have instructed them at the outset to intend to combine the prayers; nor is this reported of the early generations after him.

This is the view of the majority, including Abū Hanīfah, Mālik and others. In the case of shortening prayers, the issue rests on what is obligatory for the traveller. Thus, scholars hold three views on performing the two prayers consecutively.

The first is that consecutive performance is not obligatory, whether during the time of the first prayer or the second. Ahmad explicitly stated this, as we have mentioned, regarding travel and combining prayers because of rain.

The second is that consecutive performance is obligatory during the first prayer’s time, but not during the second’s. This is the well-known position among most of his later followers, and the apparent position of al-Shāfiʿī’s school. If the prayers are combined during the first prayer’s time, they must be performed together. If they are combined during the second prayer’s time, however, the first is offered during the second’s time, while the second is offered within its own time. His performance of the second prayer is therefore valid even if he delays it, and he incurs no sin by doing so. According to this view, consecutive performance is required during the first prayer’s time but not during the second’s.

The third is that consecutive performance is required in both cases, just as observing the proper sequence is required. This is a position within the schools of al-Shāfiʿī and Ahmad. It means that if someone offers the first prayer and delays the second, he sins even though the second prayer is valid. For, having postponed the first prayer, he was only permitted to do so on condition that he offer the second together with it. If he does not do so, he is like someone who

---

## Page 12123

delays it until the period of necessity, thereby offering it within its time while nevertheless incurring sin.

The correct view is that consecutive performance is not a condition in any case, whether during the first prayer’s time or the second’s. The religious law sets no limit for this, and observing such a requirement defeats the purpose of the concession. It resembles the view of those who interpret combining prayers as merely performing them back to back: concluding the first with the salutation at the very end of its time and beginning the second with the opening declaration of Allah’s greatness at the very beginning of its time. A group of scholars, including followers of Abū Hanīfah and others, interpreted the Prophet’s combining of prayers in this way.

Observing this is exceedingly difficult and burdensome. A person would have to begin when only enough time remains for four units of prayer, or three in the case of Maghrib. He would also have to avoid prolonging the prayer, even though beginning with the intention of prolonging it is prescribed when the time allows. If, after beginning the prayer, he decides to prolong it or to wait for someone so that the latter can catch the bowing and join the congregational prayer, this would not be prescribed. He would have to strive to conclude with the salutation before the time expires.

It is well known that observing all this is exceedingly difficult, both to ascertain and to carry out. It also distracts the worshipper’s heart from the purpose of prayer. Combining prayers was prescribed as a concession and to relieve the Muslim community of hardship. How, then, could it be prescribed only under conditions of severe hardship and in a manner that defeats the purpose of prayer?

It is thus clear that when the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, delayed Zuhr and brought ʿAsr forward, or delayed Maghrib and brought ʿIshāʾ forward, he did so in a manner that brought ease and removed hardship for himself and his community. Nor did he insist that the first prayer must be concluded

---

## Page 12124

before the time allotted specifically to it expires. Yet how can a worshipper know this while praying? The precise end of Zuhr’s time and beginning of ʿAsr’s time can only be determined by the shadow, and someone engaged in prayer cannot observe the shadow. The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, had neither instruments for calculating the time nor a timekeeper who determined it using such instruments.

The end of Maghrib’s time is known only by the disappearance of twilight. A person would therefore need to look west to see whether the red or white twilight had disappeared, yet someone engaged in prayer is forbidden to do such a thing. If he is praying in a house, a tent, or something similar that blocks his view westward, making it impossible to look west while praying, he cannot, in these circumstances, aim to give the concluding salutation at the very end of Maghrib’s time. Rather, he must conclude sufficiently before the time expires to be certain that his salutation precedes its expiry.

Then, according to their view, he cannot begin the second prayer until he knows that its time has begun. This requires effort and trouble of a kind that neither the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, nor his Companions are reported to have undertaken.

Thus, according to these scholars, combining prayers would, for most people on most occasions, be possible only with an interval between the two prayers. According to the others, however, combining prayers requires performing them consecutively. These understood combining to mean performing the two prayers consecutively, whether within one prayer time or across two, while the others said that there can be no

---

## Page 12125

combining except across two prayer times, which requires an interval between the prayers. Both views are weak. The Sunnah allows greater latitude than either view and imposes neither requirement. Combining prayers is permissible within their shared period: sometimes they are combined at its beginning, as the Prophet combined them at ‘Arafah; sometimes during the second prayer’s time, as he combined them at Muzdalifah and on some of his journeys; and sometimes between these points, midway through the two prayer times. Both prayers may be offered near the end of the first prayer’s time, both at the beginning of the second prayer’s time, or each within its own time. All of this is permissible, because the underlying principle is that, when there is a need, the time is shared. Whether the prayers are combined early or midway depends on need and benefit. At ‘Arafah and in similar circumstances, combining them early is the Sunnah.

The same applies to combining prayers because of rain: the Sunnah is to combine them during Maghrib’s time. Indeed, there are two views within Ahmad’s school as to whether it is permissible to combine them because of rain during the second prayer’s time. It has been said that the apparent meaning of his words is that they should not be combined then. There is also a third view that delaying is preferable, but this is an error contrary to the Sunnah and the early consensus. The proponent of this view assumed that delaying is always preferable when combining prayers, because a prayer may be performed after its time if one has slept through it or forgotten it, whereas it may never be performed before its time. Indeed, if one offers it before the sun passes its zenith or before dawn, one must repeat it. This is mistaken, for the prescribed way of combining prayers at Muzdalifah is only to delay Maghrib until

---

## Page 12126

the time of ‘Ishā’, as established by the mass-transmitted Sunnah and the agreement of the Muslims. I know of no scholar who permitted someone in those circumstances to pray ‘Ishā’ on his way there. They differed only over whether he may pray Maghrib on the way, holding two views on this question.

As for delaying a prayer beyond its time, it is like performing it before its time; indeed, the one who delays it is more deserving of blame. If someone sleeps through a prayer or forgets it, its time for him is when he wakes up or remembers it. That is when he is commanded to perform it, and for him it has no other time. Thus, he has prayed it only within its time.

As for someone who prays before the sun passes its zenith or before the dawn that marks the beginning of the prayer’s time, if he does so deliberately, he has done something he was not commanded to do. If, however, he is unable to determine the time—like a prisoner who has no means of knowing it—scholars hold two views on whether his prayer fulfils his obligation. The same applies to his fasting when he cannot determine when Ramadan falls: if, for example, a captive fasts according to his best judgment, then discovers that he fasted before the proper time, scholars hold two views on whether this fulfils his obligation.

As for someone in a town who mistakenly prays before the proper time, he has not done what he was commanded to do. Does his prayer nevertheless count as a voluntary prayer, or is it invalid? There are two views within the school of Ahmad and elsewhere.

The point is that Allah has not permitted anyone, under any circumstances, to delay a prayer beyond its time, just as He has not permitted anyone, under any circumstances, to perform it before its time. Thus, combining prayers at the later prayer’s time is not inherently preferable to combining them at the earlier prayer’s time. Rather, this depends on need and benefit: sometimes one is preferable, and sometimes the other. This is the position of the majority of scholars, and it is

---

## Page 12127

the evident position of Ahmad, as explicitly reported from him, and of other scholars. Any of his followers who declares either form of combining invariably preferable has misrepresented his position.

The authentic hadiths concerning the Prophet’s combining prayers—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—are transmitted from Ibn ‘Umar, Ibn ‘Abbās, Anas, Mu‘ādh, Abū Hurayrah and Jābir. Those who deny combining prayers have interpreted these hadiths as referring to delaying the first prayer until the end of its time and performing the second at the beginning of its time. Yet authentic reports establish that prayers were combined both at the time of the second prayer and at the time of the first. Other reports mention combining without specifying how; the detailed reports clarify the unspecified ones.

In the two Sahīh collections, Sufyān narrates from al-Zuhrī, from Sālim, from his father:

“When the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, needed to press on with his journey, he would combine Maghrib and ‘Ishā’.”

Mālik narrates from Nāfi‘, from Ibn ‘Umar, who said:

“When the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, needed to hasten his journey, he would combine Maghrib and ‘Ishā’.”

This was narrated by Muslim. Muslim also narrates through Yahyā ibn Sa‘īd, who said: ‘Ubayd Allāh narrated to us, saying: Nāfi‘ informed me, from Ibn ‘Umar, that when he needed to press on with his journey, he would combine Maghrib and ‘Ishā’ after the twilight had disappeared. He would mention:

“When the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, needed to press on with his journey, he would combine Maghrib and ‘Ishā’.”

Al-Tahāwī said:

“Ibn ‘Umar’s hadith mentions combining after the twilight had disappeared only as his own practice. He reported that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, combined the two prayers

---

## Page 12128

and did not mention how he combined them; this report mentions delaying the prayer only as Ibn ‘Umar’s own practice, not as part of what he narrated from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace.”

Those who affirm combining prayers then cited the report narrated by Muhammad ibn Yahyā al-Dhuhlī: Hammād ibn Mas‘adah narrated to us, from ‘Ubayd Allāh ibn ‘Umar, from Nāfi‘:

“‘Abd Allāh ibn ‘Umar hastened his journey and combined Maghrib and ‘Ishā’. I asked Nāfi‘, and he replied: ‘An hour after the twilight had disappeared.’ Ibn ‘Umar said: ‘I saw the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, do that when he needed to press on with his journey.’”

Sulaymān ibn Harb also narrated it: Hammād ibn Zayd narrated to us, from Ayyūb, from Nāfi‘, that Ibn ‘Umar received an urgent summons concerning Safiyyah bint Abī ‘Ubayd while he was in Mecca and she was in Medina. He set out and continued travelling until the sun had set and the stars appeared. A man accompanying him said: “The prayer! The prayer!” But Ibn ‘Umar continued travelling. Sālim then said to him: “The prayer!” He replied:

“When some matter required the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, to hasten during a journey, he would combine these two prayers.”

He continued travelling until the twilight had disappeared, then combined them. He covered the distance between Mecca and Medina in three days.

Al-Bayhaqī narrated these two reports with a well-known, authentic chain of transmission. He stated that Ma‘mar also narrated it from Ayyūb and Mūsā ibn ‘Uqbah, from Nāfi‘, with the following wording in the hadith:

“He delayed Maghrib beyond that twilight until a considerable part of the night had passed. Then he dismounted and prayed Maghrib and ‘Ishā’. He said: ‘The Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, would do that when he needed to press on with his journey or when some pressing matter arose.’”

He also stated that Yazīd ibn Hārūn narrated it from Yahyā

---

## Page 12129

ibn Sa‘īd al-Ansārī, from Nāfi‘, who mentioned that Ibn ‘Umar travelled for nearly a quarter of the night, then dismounted and prayed. He also narrated it through al-Dāraqutnī: Ibn Sā‘id and al-Naysābūrī narrated to us: al-‘Abbās ibn al-Walīd ibn Yazīd narrated to us: ‘Umar ibn Muhammad ibn Zayd informed me: Nāfi‘, the freedman of ‘Abd Allāh ibn ‘Umar, narrated to me, from Ibn ‘Umar:

He was travelling from Mecca when news reached him concerning Safiyyah bint Abī ‘Ubayd, so he hastened his journey. When the sun had set, one of his companions said to him: “The prayer!” He remained silent and continued travelling for an hour. His companion again said to him: “The prayer!” Then the man who had said “The prayer!” remarked: “He must know something about this that I do not know.”

He continued travelling until an hour after the twilight had disappeared, then dismounted and gave the call to commence the prayer. He did not give the call to prayer for any prayer while travelling. He gave the call to commence the prayer and prayed Maghrib and ‘Ishā’ together, combining them. He then said:

“When the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, needed to press on with his journey, he would combine Maghrib and ‘Ishā’ an hour after the twilight had disappeared. He would also offer voluntary prayer on his mount while travelling, whichever way it carried him.”

He would report that the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, used to do that.

Al-Bayhaqī said:

“The reports of Yahyā ibn Sa‘īd al-Ansārī, Mūsā ibn ‘Uqbah, ‘Ubayd Allāh ibn ‘Umar, Ayyūb al-Sakhtiyānī and ‘Umar ibn Muhammad ibn Zayd agree that ‘Abd Allāh ibn ‘Umar combined the two prayers after the twilight had disappeared. They were contradicted by those whose accuracy in preserving Nāfi‘’s hadiths did not approach theirs.”

He also mentioned that Ibn Jābir narrated it from Nāfi‘

---

## Page 12130

with the following wording:

“He continued until the twilight was nearly gone, then dismounted and prayed Maghrib. He then gave the call to commence the prayer, by which time the twilight had disappeared, and led us in prayer. Then he turned to us and said: ‘When a matter required the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, to hasten, he would do this.’”

He said:

“Fudayl ibn Ghazwān and ‘Ittāf ibn Khālid narrated a report with the same meaning from Nāfi‘. However, the account transmitted by those of Nāfi‘’s companions renowned for their mastery of hadith is more likely to be correct.”

Sālim ibn ‘Abd Allāh, Aslam—the freedman of ‘Umar—‘Abd Allāh ibn Dīnār, and Ismā‘īl ibn ‘Abd al-Rahmān ibn Dhu’ayb narrated from Ibn ‘Umar accounts similar to theirs. As for Sālim’s hadith, ‘Āsim ibn Muhammad narrated it from his brother ‘Umar ibn Muhammad, from Sālim. As for Aslam’s hadith, he cited its chain through Ibn Abī Maryam: Muhammad ibn Ja‘far informed us: Zayd ibn Aslam informed me, from his father, who said:

“I was with Ibn ‘Umar when news reached him that Safiyyah was suffering severe pain. He hastened his journey until, after the twilight had disappeared, he dismounted and prayed Maghrib and ‘Ishā’, combining them. He said: ‘I saw the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, delay Maghrib and combine the two prayers when he needed to press on with his journey.’”

Al-Bukhārī narrated it in his Sahīh from Ibn Abī Maryam.

He also cited a chain from the book of Ya‘qūb ibn Sufyān: Abū Sālih and Ibn Bukayr informed us, saying: al-Layth narrated to us, saying: Rabī‘ah ibn Abī ‘Abd al-Rahmān said: ‘Abd Allāh ibn Dīnār—one of the upright Muslims, truthful and devout—narrated to me, saying:

“The sun set while we were with ‘Abd Allāh ibn ‘Umar, and we continued travelling. When we saw that he had continued into the evening, we said to him: ‘The prayer!’ He remained silent until the twilight had disappeared and the stars had begun to sink. Then he dismounted and offered both prayers together. He then said:

---

## Page 12131

‘I saw the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, pray as I have just prayed when he needed to press on with his journey’—meaning that he combined the two prayers after night had fallen.”

As for the hadith of Ismā‘īl ibn ‘Abd al-Rahmān, it was related with a chain through al-Shāfi‘ī and Abū Nu‘aym, from Ibn ‘Uyaynah, from Abū Najīh, from Ismā‘īl ibn ‘Abd al-Rahmān ibn Dhu’ayb, who said:

“I accompanied Ibn ‘Umar. When the sun set, we were too apprehensive to tell him to rise for prayer. When the whiteness along the horizon and the early darkness of night had passed, he dismounted and prayed three units of prayer, followed by two. Then he turned to us and said: ‘This is how I saw the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, act.’”

As for the hadith of Anas, it appears in the two Sahīhs, from Ibn Shihāb, from Anas, who said:

“When the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, set out before the sun had passed its zenith, he would delay Zuhr until the time of ‘Asr, then dismount and combine the two prayers. If the sun had passed its zenith before he set out, he would pray Zuhr and then mount.”

This is the wording describing his practice, transmitted by ‘Aqīl from him. Muslim also narrated it through Ibn Wahb, who said: Jābir ibn Ismā‘īl narrated to me, from ‘Aqīl, from Ibn Shihāb, from Anas, who reported concerning the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace:

“When he needed to press on with his journey, he would delay Zuhr until the time of ‘Asr and combine them. He would also delay Maghrib so as to combine it with ‘Ishā’ when the twilight disappeared.”

Muslim also narrated it through Shabābah: al-Layth ibn Sa‘d narrated to us, from ‘Aqīl, from Ibn Shihāb, from Anas, who said:

“The Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace,

---

## Page 12132

when he wished to combine Zuhr and ‘Asr while travelling, would delay Zuhr until the beginning of the time for ‘Asr, then combine them.”

It was also related through al-Ismā‘īlī: al-Firyābī informed us; Ishāq ibn Rāhawayh informed us; Shabābah ibn Sawwār informed us, from al-Layth, from ‘Aqīl, [from Ibn Shihāb] (*) from Anas:

“When the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, was travelling and the sun passed its zenith, he would pray Zuhr and ‘Asr together, then set out.”

I say: This is how this version reads, but it differs from the well-known version of the hadith of Anas. As for the hadith of Mu‘ādh, Muslim alone, of the two compilers of the Sahīhs, narrated it. He related it through Mālik, Zuhayr ibn Mu‘āwiyah and Qurrah ibn Khālid. The following is Mālik’s wording, from Abū al-Zubayr al-Makkī, from Abū al-Tufayl ‘Āmir ibn Wāthilah:

“Mu‘ādh ibn Jabal informed them that they had set out with the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and he combined Zuhr with ‘Asr and Maghrib with ‘Ishā’. One day he delayed the prayer, then came out and prayed Zuhr and ‘Asr. He then went inside, before coming out again and praying Maghrib and ‘Ishā’.”

I say: Combining prayers falls into three categories. If a traveller is on the move during the time of the first prayer and does not stop until the time of the second, this is the combining established in the two Sahīhs through the hadiths of Anas and Ibn ‘Umar. It is comparable to combining the prayers at Muzdalifah.

If he is to be on the move or riding during the time of the second prayer, and combines them during the time of the first, this is comparable to combining the prayers at ‘Arafah. This has been narrated in the Sunan collections, as we shall mention, Allah willing.

As for someone who remains encamped throughout the times of both prayers, so far as I know, no report has been transmitted that could be cited

> **Footnotes:**
> (*) Shaykh Nāsir ibn Hamad al-Fahd stated (p. 196):
> The copyists omitted the text enclosed in square brackets. It appears in al-Bayhaqī’s Sunan—the source of this passage—(3/162), as well as in the chains of transmission preceding this hadith.

---

## Page 12133

as evidence for this except this hadith of Mu‘ādh. Its apparent meaning is that he was staying in a tent while travelling, and that he delayed Zuhr, then came out and prayed Zuhr and ‘Asr together. He then went back inside his dwelling before coming out and praying Maghrib and ‘Ishā’ together. Entering and leaving apply to a dwelling; someone on the move is not described as entering and leaving, but as dismounting and mounting.

Tabūk was the Prophet’s last military expedition, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. He did not travel after it except for the Farewell Pilgrimage, during which he is only reported to have combined prayers at ‘Arafah and Muzdalifah. As for Minā, no one reported that he combined prayers there. Rather, they reported that he shortened the prayers there. Nor did they report that he delayed the first prayer until the end of its time or brought the second forward to the beginning of its time. This shows that he sometimes combined prayers while travelling and sometimes did not. More often than not, he did not combine them on his journeys. This makes clear that combining prayers is not a Sunnah of travel in the way that shortening them is. Rather, it is done when needed, whether travelling or at home, for he also combined prayers while at home so as not to impose hardship on his community.

Thus, when a traveller needs to combine prayers, he may do so, whether because he is on the move during the time of the second prayer, or during the time of the first and stopping is difficult for him, or because he has some other need even while encamped. For example, he may need sleep and rest at the times of Zuhr and ‘Ishā’. He may stop at the time of Zuhr exhausted, having been awake through the night, hungry and in need of rest, food and sleep, and so delay Zuhr until the time of ‘Asr

---

## Page 12134

and then needs to bring ‘Ishā’ forward to pray it with Maghrib and sleep afterwards, so that he can wake in the middle of the night to resume his journey. In such circumstances and others like them, he may combine prayers.

As for someone staying for several days in a village or town, whose circumstances are like those of its residents, although he shortens his prayers because he is a traveller, he does not combine them, just as he does not pray on his mount, perform dry ablution for prayer, or eat carrion. These concessions are permitted when needed, and he has no need of them. Shortening prayers is different, for it is the Sunnah for prayer during travel.

Combining prayers at the time of the earlier prayer, as the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, did at ‘Arafah, is reported in the Sunan collections. One example is the hadith narrated by Abū Dāwūd, al-Tirmidhī and others through al-Mufaddal ibn Fadālah, from al-Layth ibn Sa‘d, from Hāshim ibn Sa‘d, from Abū al-Zubayr, from Abū al-Tufayl, from Mu‘ādh ibn Jabal:

“During the expedition to Tabūk, if the sun had passed its zenith before the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, set out, he would combine Zuhr and ‘Asr. If he set out before the sun passed its zenith, he would delay Zuhr until he stopped for ‘Asr. He would do likewise with Maghrib: if the sun had set before he set out, he would combine Maghrib and ‘Ishā’. If he set out before sunset, he would delay Maghrib until he stopped for ‘Ishā’, then dismount and combine the two.”

Al-Tirmidhī said: “The hadith of Mu‘ādh is sound and singular.”

I say: Qutaybah also narrated it from al-Layth, from Yazīd ibn Abī Habīb, from

---

## Page 12135

Abū al-Tufayl; however, the hadith critics objected to Qutaybah’s narration. Al-Bayhaqī said: “Qutaybah alone narrated it from al-Layth.” He related that al-Bukhārī said: “I asked Qutaybah, ‘With whom did you write down al-Layth ibn Sa‘d’s narration from Yazīd ibn Abī Habīb, from Abū al-Tufayl?’ He replied, ‘I wrote it down with Khālid al-Madā’inī.’” Al-Bukhārī said: “This Khālid used to insert hadiths into the shaykhs’ narrations.”

Al-Bayhaqī said: “Their objection concerned only Yazīd ibn Abī Habīb’s narration from Abū al-Tufayl. As for Abū al-Zubayr’s narration from Abū al-Tufayl, it is well established and authentic.”

I say: The combining of prayers detailed by Hishām ibn Sa‘d, narrating from Abū al-Zubayr, and mentioned by Mālik falls within the general account of combining prayers given by al-Thawrī and others who narrated from Abū al-Zubayr, from Abū al-Tufayl, from Mu‘ādh:

“The Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, combined Zuhr with ‘Asr and Maghrib with ‘Ishā’ during the year of Tabūk.”

This combining at the earlier prayer’s time is not mentioned in the well-known version of Anas’s hadith. If a traveller sets out after the sun has passed its zenith and has not stopped when ‘Asr becomes due, this is not a situation that requires combining prayers; rather, he prays ‘Asr at its proper time. His journey may, however, continue uninterrupted until sunset, in which case he needs to combine prayers. This resembles combining prayers at ‘Arafah: since the standing there continued until sunset, he prayed ‘Asr together with Zuhr, for prayers were combined according to need. Understood in this way, the Prophet’s hadiths, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, are consistent with one another.

Otherwise, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, would not treat two equivalent situations differently. No one has reported that he combined prayers at Minā

---

## Page 12136

or in Mecca, whether in the year of the Conquest or during the Farewell Pilgrimage, although he stayed there for more than ten days, shortening his prayers. No one has said that he combined prayers during his pilgrimage except at ‘Arafah and Muzdalifah. Thus, it is clear that his combining prayers was not due to his shortening them.

Combining prayers at the earlier prayer’s time while in a town has also been reported in a hadith of Ibn ‘Abbās, in agreement with the hadith of Mu‘ādh. Abū Dāwūd mentioned it, saying: “Hishām ibn ‘Urwah narrated from Husayn ibn ‘Abd Allāh, from Kurayb, from Ibn ‘Abbās, from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, a report similar to the hadith of al-Fadl.”

I say: This hadith is known through Husayn. His narrations may be considered alongside other reports and used as corroborating evidence, but they cannot be relied upon independently. ‘Alī ibn al-Madīnī and al-Nasā’ī criticised him. Al-Bayhaqī narrated it through ‘Uthmān ibn ‘Umar, from Ibn Jurayj, from Husayn, from ‘Ikrimah, from Ibn ‘Abbās, from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace:

“When the sun passed its zenith while the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, was at his stopping place, he would combine Zuhr and ‘Asr. If he set out before the sun passed its zenith, he would travel until ‘Asr became due, then stop and combine Zuhr and ‘Asr. When the sun set while he was at his stopping place, he would combine Maghrib and ‘Ishā’. If he set out before sunset, he would travel until ‘Ishā’ became due, then stop and combine Maghrib and ‘Ishā’.”

Al-Bayhaqī said that Hajjāj ibn Muhammad also narrated it from Ibn Jurayj, who said: “Husayn informed me, from Kurayb.” Husayn had heard it from both of them. Al-Bayhaqī cited ‘Abd al-Razzāq’s narration from Ibn Jurayj as corroboration of this. That narration is well known and has been transmitted by al-Dāraqutnī and others,

---

## Page 12137

and it comes from the books of ‘Abd al-Razzāq.

‘Abd al-Razzāq narrated from Ibn Jurayj, who said: “Husayn ibn ‘Abd Allāh ibn ‘Ubayd Allāh ibn ‘Abbās narrated to me, from ‘Ikrimah and from Kurayb, from Ibn ‘Abbās, that Ibn ‘Abbās said: ‘Shall I tell you about the prayer of the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, while travelling?’ We said, ‘Yes.’ He said:

‘When the sun passed its zenith while he was at his stopping place, he would combine Zuhr and ‘Asr before mounting. If it had not passed its zenith while he was at his stopping place, he would travel until ‘Asr became due, then stop and combine Zuhr and ‘Asr. When Maghrib became due while he was at his stopping place, he would combine it with ‘Ishā’. If it had not become due while he was at his stopping place, he would mount and travel until ‘Ishā’ became due, then stop and combine the two.’”

Al-Dāraqutnī said: “‘Abd al-Majīd ibn ‘Abd al-‘Azīz narrated it from Ibn Jurayj, from Hishām ibn ‘Urwah, from Husayn, from Kurayb.” It is therefore possible that Ibn Jurayj first heard it from Hishām ibn ‘Urwah, from Husayn, as ‘Abd al-Majīd reported from him, and that Ibn Jurayj subsequently met Husayn and heard it directly from him, as ‘Abd al-Razzāq and Hajjāj reported from Ibn Jurayj.

Al-Bayhaqī said: “It was also narrated through Muhammad ibn ‘Ajlān, Yazīd ibn al-Hādī and Abū Ruways al-Madanī, from Husayn ibn ‘Abd Allāh, from ‘Ikrimah, from Ibn ‘Abbās.” The corroborating reports already mentioned strengthen it.

He also cited the hadith that al-Bukhārī mentioned without a full chain of transmission: the report of Ibrāhīm ibn Tahmān, from al-Husayn, from Yahyā ibn Abī Kathīr, from ‘Ikrimah, from Ibn ‘Abbās:

“The Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, would combine Zuhr and ‘Asr while travelling when he was on the road, and he would combine Maghrib and ‘Ishā’.”

Al-Bukhārī included it in his Sahīh, saying: “It was also stated by

---

## Page 12138

Ibrāhīm ibn Tahmān,” and then cited the report.

I say: His words, “while he was on the road,” may mean that he was travelling during the time of the first prayer. There is no doubt about this. They also include travelling during the time of the second prayer, as was explicitly reported from Ibn ‘Abbās.

Al-Bayhaqī said: “Ayyūb also narrated from Abū Qilābah, from Ibn ‘Abbās—we know it only as a report traced back to the Prophet—with a meaning similar to al-Husayn’s narration.” He then cited what Ismā‘īl ibn Ishāq narrated: Sulaymān ibn Harb narrated to us; Hammād ibn Zayd narrated to us, from Ayyūb, from Abū Qilābah, from Ibn ‘Abbās: “I know it only as a report traced back to the Prophet; otherwise, it is from Ibn ‘Abbās: ‘When he stopped somewhere while travelling and found the place agreeable, he would remain there until he combined Zuhr and ‘Asr.’”

Ismā‘īl said: “‘Ārim narrated to us; Hammād narrated to us,” and he related the report. ‘Ārim said: “This is how Hammād narrated it. He said: ‘When he travelled and stopped somewhere, if he found the place agreeable, he would remain there until he combined Zuhr and ‘Asr.’”

Hammād ibn Salamah narrated it from Ayyūb as a statement of Ibn ‘Abbās himself. Ismā‘īl said: “Hajjāj narrated to us, from Hammād ibn Salamah, from Ayyūb, from Abū Qilābah, from Ibn ‘Abbās, who said: ‘If you are travelling and a stopping place does not suit you, continue until you find somewhere to combine the two prayers. If you are encamped and something requires you to leave promptly, combine the two prayers and then depart.’”

I say: The hadith of Ibn ‘Abbās concerning combining prayers in Medina is authentic and is among the well-known authentic reports, as will be explained, Allah willing.

---

## Page 12139

As for the hadith of Jābir, it is recorded in Sunan Abī Dāwūd and elsewhere through ‘Abd al-‘Azīz ibn Muhammad, from Abū al-Zubayr, from Jābir:

“The sun set while the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, was in Mecca, and he combined the two prayers at Sarif.”

Al-Bayhaqī, who narrated it through al-Himmānī from ‘Abd al-‘Azīz, said: “Al-Ajlah also narrated it in the same way from Abū al-Zubayr.”

Abū Dāwūd said: “Muhammad ibn Hishām, Ahmad ibn Hanbal’s neighbour, narrated to us; Ja‘far ibn ‘Awn narrated to us, from Hishām ibn Sa‘d, who said: ‘There are ten miles between them’—meaning between Mecca and Sarif.”

I say: Ten miles are three and one-third parasangs, while a barīd is four parasangs. This distance cannot be covered, even at a brisk pace, before the twilight disappears. People leave ‘Arafah just after sunset and do not reach Jam‘ until the twilight has disappeared. The distance from ‘Arafah to Mecca is one barīd, and Jam‘ is nearer than that; yet they do not reach it until after the twilight has disappeared. How much more so, then, for Sarif! This agrees with the hadiths of Ibn ‘Umar, Anas and Ibn ‘Abbās: when he was on the move, he would delay Maghrib until the twilight disappeared, then offer both prayers together.

Al-Bayhaqī said: “Combining the two prayers on account of travel was a well-known practice among the Companions and the Successors. This is supported by established reports from the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and then from his Companions, as well as the Muslims’ consensus regarding people’s combining the prayers at ‘Arafah and then at Muzdalifah.” He then cited what al-Bukhārī narrated through Sa‘īd

---

## Page 12140

from al-Zuhrī, who reported: Sālim informed me, on the authority of ‘Abd Allāh ibn ‘Umar, who said:

“I saw that when the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, needed to make haste while travelling, he would delay the Maghrib prayer so as to combine it with ‘Ishā’.”

Sālim said: “‘Abd Allāh ibn ‘Umar would do likewise when he needed to make haste while travelling. He would give the call to commence the Maghrib prayer, pray three rak‘ahs, and conclude with the salām. After only a brief interval, he would give the call to commence the ‘Ishā’ prayer, pray two rak‘ahs, and conclude with the salām. He would not offer even a single voluntary rak‘ah between them, nor any voluntary prayer after ‘Ishā’ until he rose to pray in the middle of the night.”

Mālik narrated from Yahyā ibn Sa‘īd that he asked Sālim ibn ‘Abd Allāh ibn ‘Umar: “What was the latest you ever saw your father, ‘Abd Allāh ibn ‘Umar, delay Maghrib while travelling?”

He replied: “The sun set while he was at Dhāt al-Jaysh, and he prayed it at al-‘Aqīq.”

Al-Bayhaqī said: “Al-Thawrī narrated it from Yahyā ibn Sa‘īd, adding: ‘Eight miles.’ Ibn Jurayj also narrated it from Yahyā ibn Sa‘īd, adding: ‘I asked: What time was that? He replied: A third or a quarter of the night had passed.’”

He said: “Yazīd ibn Hārūn narrated it from Yahyā ibn Sa‘īd, from Nāfi‘, who said: ‘He travelled several miles, then dismounted and prayed.’”

Yahyā said: “Nāfi‘ related this account to me on another occasion, saying: ‘He travelled for nearly a quarter of the night, then dismounted and prayed.’”

He also narrated from the Musannaf of Sa‘īd ibn Abī ‘Arūbah, from Qatādah, from Jābir ibn

---

## Page 12141

Zayd, from Ibn ‘Abbās, who would combine the two prayers while travelling and say: “This is Sunnah.”

In a narration from ‘Alī ibn ‘Āsim:

“Al-Jurayrī and Salmān al-Taymī informed me, from Abū ‘Uthmān al-Nahdī, who said: ‘When Sa‘īd ibn Zayd and Usāmah ibn Zayd needed to make haste while travelling, they would combine Zuhr with ‘Asr and Maghrib with ‘Ishā’.’”

We have also transmitted reports to this effect from Sa‘d ibn Abī Waqqās and Anas ibn Mālik, and it has been reported from ‘Umar and ‘Uthmān.

He also cited what Mālik related in al-Muwatta’ from Ibn Shihāb, who said:

“I asked Sālim ibn ‘Abd Allāh: ‘May Zuhr and ‘Asr be combined while travelling?’

“He replied: ‘Yes, there is nothing wrong with that. Do you not see how people pray at ‘Arafah?’”

He also cited the following from the book of Ya‘qūb ibn Sufyān: ‘Abd al-Malik ibn Abī Salamah narrated to us; al-Darāwardī narrated to us, from Zayd ibn Aslam, Rabī‘ah ibn Abī ‘Abd al-Rahmān, Muhammad ibn al-Munkadir and Abū al-Zinād, that they, together with others like them, travelled to al-Walīd. He had sent for them to seek their legal opinion on a matter. They would combine Zuhr and ‘Asr when the sun passed its zenith.

I say:

Here the early generations used the combining of prayers at ‘Arafah as evidence for analogous cases, showing that the ruling is not exclusive to ‘Arafah. This is combining the prayers at the time of the earlier prayer because of a need while travelling.

As for combining prayers in Medina because of rain or some other reason, Muslim and others narrated through Abū al-Zubayr, from Sa‘īd ibn Jubayr, “from Ibn ‘Abbās, who said: ‘Prayer was offered by

---

## Page 12142

the Messenger of Allah, peace and blessings be upon him—Zuhr and ‘Asr together, and Maghrib and ‘Ishā’ together—without fear or travel.’”

Among those who narrated it from Abū al-Zubayr was Mālik in his al-Muwatta’. He said: “I think that was during rain.”

Al-Bayhaqī said:

Zuhayr ibn Mu‘āwiyah and Hammād ibn Salamah likewise narrated it from Abū al-Zubayr with the wording “without fear or travel,” except that neither mentioned Maghrib and ‘Ishā’, and both said “in Medina.” Ibn ‘Uyaynah and Hishām ibn Sa‘d also narrated it from Abū al-Zubayr with the same meaning as Mālik’s narration.

Al-Bayhaqī set out its chains of transmission. Muslim narrated Zuhayr’s report in his Sahīh: Abū al-Zubayr narrated to us, from Sa‘īd ibn Jubayr, from Ibn ‘Abbās, who said:

“The Messenger of Allah, peace and blessings be upon him, prayed Zuhr and ‘Asr together in Medina, without fear or travel.”

Abū al-Zubayr said: “I asked Sa‘īd: ‘Why did he do that?’ He replied: ‘I asked Ibn ‘Abbās just as you have asked me, and he said: “He wanted not to impose hardship on anyone in his community.”’”

He said: Qurrah differed from them in his narration, saying: “On a journey he made to Tabūk.”

Muslim narrated it through Qurrah, from Abū al-Zubayr, from Sa‘īd ibn Jubayr, from Ibn ‘Abbās, who said:

“The Messenger of Allah, peace and blessings be upon him, combined the prayers during a journey he made on the expedition to Tabūk, combining Zuhr with ‘Asr and Maghrib with ‘Ishā’.”

I asked Ibn ‘Abbās: “What prompted him to do that?” He replied: “He wanted not to impose hardship on his community.”

Al-Bayhaqī said: Qurrah had meant the report of Abū al-Zubayr from Abū al-Tufayl

---

## Page 12143

from Muʿādh, for this is the wording of his report. Saʿīd ibn Jubayr narrated both reports: Qurrah heard one, while those mentioned earlier heard the other.

He said:

This is more likely, for Qurrah also narrated Abū al-Tufayl’s report.

I say:

Muslim likewise narrated it in this manner. Thus, this wording was transmitted both in Muʿādh’s report and in Ibn ʿAbbās’s report, for Qurrah is a reliable narrator with an excellent memory. Al-Tahāwī also narrated Qurrah’s reports from Abū al-Zubayr—the report of Abū al-Tufayl and this report of his from Saʿīd—in the same form as Mālik’s report from Abū al-Zubayr. This indicates that Abū al-Zubayr narrated both versions.

Al-Bayhaqī said:

Habīb ibn Abī Thābit narrated it from Saʿīd ibn Jubayr, differing from Abū al-Zubayr in its wording.

He cited it through al-Aʿmash, from Habīb ibn Abī Thābit, from Saʿīd ibn Jubayr, from Ibn ʿAbbās, who said:

“The Messenger of Allah, peace and blessings be upon him, combined Zuhr with ʿAsr and Maghrib with ʿIshāʾ in Medina, without fear or rain.”

He was asked: “What did he intend by that?”

He replied: “He wanted not to impose hardship on his community.”

In Wakīʿ’s narration, Saʿīd said:

“I asked Ibn ʿAbbās: ‘Why did the Messenger of Allah, peace and blessings be upon him, do that?’ He replied: ‘So as not to impose hardship on his community.’”

Muslim narrated it in his Sahīh. Al-Bayhaqī said:

Al-Bukhārī did not include it, despite Habīb ibn Abī Thābit meeting his criteria. Perhaps he passed over it—Allah knows best—because of the differing versions transmitted from Saʿīd ibn Jubayr.

He said:

The version transmitted by the group from Abū al-Zubayr is more likely to

---

## Page 12144

be the accurately preserved version, for ʿAmr ibn Dīnār narrated it from Abū al-Shaʿthāʾ, from Ibn ʿAbbās, with a meaning close to Mālik’s narration from Abū al-Zubayr.

I say:

There is no basis for preferring Abū al-Zubayr’s narration to that of Habīb ibn Abī Thābit. Habīb ibn Abī Thābit is among the narrators of both Sahīh collections, so his report has a stronger claim to preference than Abū al-Zubayr’s, since the latter is included only by Muslim. Moreover, differing versions of the text have been transmitted from Abū al-Zubayr, from Saʿīd ibn Jubayr: one places the incident during travel, as Qurrah narrated from him, agreeing with Abū al-Zubayr’s report from Abū al-Tufayl; another places it in Medina, as most narrators reported from him, from Saʿīd.

Thus, three reports have been transmitted from Abū al-Zubayr: Abū al-Tufayl’s report from Muʿādh about combining prayers during travel; Saʿīd ibn Jubayr’s similar report from Ibn ʿAbbās; and Saʿīd ibn Jubayr’s report from Ibn ʿAbbās mentioning the combining of prayers in Medina. They have accepted all these as authentic because Abū al-Zubayr had an excellent memory. Why, then, should Habīb ibn Abī Thābit’s report not also be established as authentically transmitted from Saʿīd ibn Jubayr, when Habīb is more reliable than Abū al-Zubayr?

The other authentic reports from Ibn ʿAbbās support what Habīb narrated, for the combining of prayers mentioned by Ibn ʿAbbās was not on account of rain. Moreover, his words “in Medina” indicate that it did not occur during travel. Thus, his statement, “He combined the prayers in Medina without fear or rain,” makes it all the more appropriate to say, “without fear or travel.”

As for the person who said, “I think it was because of rain,” this was merely his conjecture; it is not part of the hadith. Rather, assuming

---

## Page 12145

the narrators preserved the wording accurately, the reports can be reconciled. He said, “Without fear or rain,” and also said, “or travel.” The combining of prayers mentioned by Ibn ʿAbbās was occasioned by neither of these.

Ahmad therefore cited this report as evidence that combining prayers for these reasons is permissible all the more so. The wording indicates that combining prayers in these circumstances is even more warranted. This is an instance of an action indicating what applies with even greater force: if he combined prayers to remove hardship arising without fear, rain, or travel, then hardship arising from these circumstances has an even stronger claim to being removed, and combining prayers because of them is more warranted than combining them for other reasons.

Further evidence that Ibn ʿAbbās did not mean combining prayers because of rain—although combining them because of rain has an even stronger claim to permissibility—is what Muslim narrated through Hammād ibn Zayd, from al-Zubayr ibn al-Khirrīt, from ʿAbd Allāh ibn Shaqīq, who said:

“One day Ibn ʿAbbās addressed us after the afternoon prayer until the sun had set and the stars appeared. People began saying, ‘The prayer! The prayer!’ Then a man from Banū Taym came, repeating incessantly, ‘The prayer! The prayer!’ Ibn ʿAbbās replied, ‘Do you presume to teach me the Sunnah? May you be bereft of your mother!’ Then he said, ‘I saw the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, combine the noon and afternoon prayers, and the sunset and night prayers.’”

ʿAbd Allāh ibn Shaqīq said: “Some doubt about this arose in my mind, so I went to Abū Hurayrah and asked him, and he confirmed what Ibn ʿAbbās had said.”

Muslim also narrated it through ʿImrān ibn Hudayr, from Ibn Shaqīq, who said:

“A man said to Ibn ʿAbbās, ‘The prayer!’ He remained silent. Then the man said, ‘The prayer!’

---

## Page 12146

He remained silent, then said, ‘May you be bereft of your mother! Do you presume to teach us about prayer? We used to combine the two prayers during the lifetime of the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace.’”

Here, Ibn ʿAbbās was neither travelling nor experiencing rain, yet he cited what he had narrated as evidence for what he did. Thus, we know that the combining of prayers he reported was not occasioned by rain. Rather, Ibn ʿAbbās was attending to an important matter concerning the Muslims, addressing them about something they needed to know. He considered that interrupting his address and stepping down would defeat its purpose. In his view, this was therefore one of the needs that permitted combining prayers. For the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, would combine prayers in Medina without fear or rain, but because of a need that arose, as Ibn ʿAbbās said:

“He intended not to subject his community to hardship.”

It is well known that the Prophet’s combining of prayers at ʿArafah and Muzdalifah was not occasioned by fear, rain, or travel either. Had he combined them because of travel, he would have combined them along the way and in Mecca, just as he shortened them there. He would also have combined them when he left Mecca for Minā, where he offered the noon, afternoon, sunset, night, and dawn prayers. Yet he did not combine prayers at Minā before the standing at ʿArafah, nor did he combine them there afterwards, during the days spent at Minā. Rather, he offered each prayer at its appointed time, praying two units in each except the sunset prayer.

Nor did he combine prayers because of the pilgrimage rites. Had that been the reason, he would have combined them from the moment he entered the consecrated state for pilgrimage, since he was in that state from then onwards. Thus, we know that his combining of prayers at ʿArafah and Muzdalifah, which is established by mass transmission, was not occasioned by rain or fear, nor specifically by the pilgrimage rites, nor merely by travel. The same applies to his combining of prayers in Medina as reported by Ibn ʿAbbās. Rather,

---

## Page 12147

he combined prayers to spare his community hardship. Thus, whenever they need to combine prayers, they may do so.

Al-Bayhaqī said:

“In Ibn Shaqīq’s report from Ibn ʿAbbās, transmitted through these two reliably established routes from him, neither rain nor travel is ruled out. It must therefore be understood as referring to one of these circumstances, or according to the interpretation given by ʿAmr ibn Dīnār. Neither report precludes that interpretation.”

The response is:

Glory be to Allah! Ibn ʿAbbās was addressing them in Basra: he was not travelling, and there was no rain. He cited an instance of combining prayers as evidence for doing what he himself had done. Had that instance been occasioned by travel or rain, Ibn ʿAbbās was far too eminent to justify his own combining of prayers by citing prayers combined because of rain or travel. Moreover, it is established in both Sahīh collections, on his authority, that this combining took place in Medina. How, then, can it be said that he did not rule out travel?

Habīb ibn Abī Thābit is among the most trustworthy transmitters, and it has been narrated from Saʿīd that he said:

“Without fear or rain.”

As for his statement that al-Bukhārī did not include this report, the response is that this is among the weakest arguments. Al-Bukhārī did not include the narrations of Abū al-Zubayr, nor did he include reports from everyone who met his criteria.

As for his statement, “The report of ʿAmr ibn Dīnār from Abū al-Shaʿthāʾ is similar to that of Abū al-Zubayr,” he cited what the two imams recorded in their Sahīh collections through Hammād

---

## Page 12148

ibn Zayd, from ʿAmr ibn Dīnār, from Jābir ibn Zayd, from Ibn ʿAbbās:

“The Messenger of Allah, peace and blessings be upon him, prayed seven and eight units of prayer in Medina: the noon, afternoon, sunset and night prayers.”

In al-Bukhārī’s version, narrated from Hammād ibn Zayd, he said to Ayyūb, “Perhaps it was on a rainy night?” He replied, “Perhaps.”

The response is:

This conjecture came from Ayyūb and ʿAmr, not from Mālik. They entertained this possibility because the wording they heard did not rule out rain. Had they heard the narration of Habīb ibn Abī Thābit, a trustworthy and reliable transmitter, they would not have formed this conjecture. Furthermore, this narration of Ibn ʿAbbās is an unqualified account of an action. Why did he mention in it that there was neither fear nor rain? This shows that Ibn ʿAbbās intended to explain the general permissibility of combining prayers in Medina, not to identify one particular reason. Whoever claims that he meant only combining prayers because of rain has therefore misunderstood him.

Moreover, ʿAmr ibn Dīnār sometimes allows the possibility that it was because of rain, agreeing with Ayyūb, while at other times he and Abū al-Shaʿthāʾ say that the prayers were combined within their respective times. Thus, both Sahīh collections record, through Ibn ʿUyaynah, from ʿAmr ibn Dīnār:

“I heard Jābir ibn Zayd say: ‘I heard Ibn ʿAbbās say: “I prayed eight units together and seven together with the Messenger of Allah, peace and blessings be upon him.”’”

ʿAmr said: “I said, ‘Abū al-Shaʿthāʾ, I think he delayed the noon prayer and brought forward the afternoon prayer, and delayed the sunset prayer and brought forward the night prayer.’ He replied, ‘I think so too.’”

The response is:

That is not the case, for Ibn ʿAbbās had far greater understanding and knowledge

---

## Page 12149

than to need—if he had offered each prayer at its own time, something whose permissibility is known to laypeople and scholars alike—to cite this unqualified account of an action as evidence for that, or to say: “He intended thereby not to impose hardship on his community.” He knew that offering the prayers at their respective times had already been prescribed in the hadiths on prayer times. Ibn ʿAbbās himself was among those who narrated the hadiths on prayer times, including the account of “Gabriel leading him in prayer beside the House. On the second day, he offered the noon prayer when the shadow of everything equalled its height, and the afternoon prayer when the shadow of everything was twice its height.”

If the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, combined the prayers only in this manner, what would be remarkable about that? It is well known that, on the second day, he offered both prayers at the end of their respective times and said:

“The time is between these two.”

It would therefore be all the more permissible for him to offer only the first prayer at the end of its time.

How could it be fitting for Ibn ʿAbbās to say, “He did that so as not to impose hardship on his community,” when the ordinarily recognized prayer times allow greater latitude and remove hardship more effectively than the manner of combining prayers they have described? How could he cite this action as evidence against someone who objected to his delaying the prayer, if the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, had merely prayed within its specifically allotted time? Would not the Prophet’s delaying the sunset prayer, when he offered it on its own before the twilight disappeared, and his delaying the night prayer until a third or half of the night had passed have provided him with sufficient evidence without resorting to this?

Rather, Ibn ʿAbbās intended to explain that it is permissible to delay the sunset prayer until the time of the night prayer, thereby showing that there is greater latitude when combining prayers than otherwise. It is through this that hardship is removed from the community. Furthermore, it is authentically reported from Ibn ʿAbbās

---

## Page 12150

in the Sahīh that he mentioned combining prayers while travelling, and that the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, combined the noon and afternoon prayers while travelling when he was on the move. This has already been discussed in detail. Thus, in his customary usage, the expression “combining prayers” meant offering both during the time of one of them. As for combining them within their two separate times, he is not known to have used the expression in that sense. How, then, could he depart from his habitual usage to a different one?

Furthermore, Ibn Shaqīq says:

“Something about that troubled me, so I went to Abū Hurayrah and asked him, and he confirmed Ibn ʿAbbās’s statement.”

Do you suppose his misgivings were that it was impermissible to delay the noon prayer until the end of its time, and impermissible to bring the afternoon prayer forward to the beginning of its time? Could this be unknown even to those with the least knowledge, such that it would trouble him? Is this something he would need to put to Abū Hurayrah or anyone else and ask about? Its permissibility is a matter transmitted so widely among Muslims as to be universally known.

Some of them had doubts only concerning the sunset prayer, yet these people permit delaying it until the end of its time. The hadith therefore constitutes evidence against them in either case. The permissibility of delaying it does not depend on combining prayers; rather, it may be delayed until the end of its time regardless, even when the night prayer is also delayed. This is what the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, did when he demonstrated the prayer times, as related in the hadiths on that subject. Likewise, the authentic hadith states:

“The time for the sunset prayer lasts until the glow of twilight disappears, and the time for the night prayer lasts until midnight.”

Similarly, he said:

“The time for the noon prayer lasts until the shadow of each object equals its height, and the time for the afternoon prayer lasts until the sun turns yellow.”

Thus, this

---

## Page 12151

prescribed time, which he explained through his words and actions, saying, “The time is between these two limits,” is neither exclusive to combining prayers nor connected with it.

If someone were to say that the statement, “He combined the two prayers in Medina without fear or travel,” means combining them within their two separate times—as some of the Kufans maintain—there would be no difference between him and them. Why, then, should someone give short measure, failing to argue for others as he argues for himself, and refusing to accept for himself what he accepts for others?

Furthermore, this has been established through reports other than the hadith of Ibn ʿAbbās. Al-Tahāwī related: Ibn Khuzaymah, Ibrāhīm ibn Abī Dāwūd and ʿImrān ibn Mūsā narrated to us, saying: Al-Rabīʿ ibn Yahyā al-Ashnānī informed us: Sufyān al-Thawrī narrated to us, from Muhammad ibn al-Munkadir, from Jābir ibn ʿAbd Allāh, who said:

“The Messenger of Allah, peace and blessings be upon him, combined the noon and afternoon prayers, and the sunset and night prayers, in Medina as a concession, without fear or any other cause.”

However, the standing of this al-Ashnānī requires examination.

As for reports from the Companions concerning combining prayers because of rain, Mālik related from Nāfiʿ that whenever the governors combined the sunset and night prayers on a rainy night, ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿUmar would combine the prayers with them on that rainy night. Al-Bayhaqī said: Al-ʿUmarī also narrated it from Nāfiʿ, adding, “Before twilight had disappeared.”

Al-Shāfiʿī related in his earlier writings: Some of our companions informed us, from Usāmah ibn Zayd, from Muʿādh ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn Khubayb, that Ibn ʿAbbās combined the two prayers in

---

## Page 12152

the rain, before twilight had disappeared.

[He also mentioned what Abū al-Shaykh al-Asbahānī related through an established chain of transmission from Hishām ibn ʿUrwah, Saʿīd ibn al-Musayyib and Abū Bakr ibn ʿAbd al-Rahmān ibn al-Hārith ibn Hishām: They would combine the sunset and night prayers on rainy nights when the two prayers were combined, and no objection was raised to this] (*).

Through his chain of transmission, he also related from Mūsā ibn ʿUqbah that ʿUmar ibn ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz would combine the sunset and night prayers when it rained, and that Saʿīd ibn al-Musayyib, ʿUrwah ibn al-Zubayr, Abū Bakr ibn ʿAbd al-Rahmān and the elders of that time would pray with them without objecting to this.

These reports show that combining prayers because of rain was an established practice in Medina during the time of the Companions and the Successors. Moreover, no objection to it has been reported from any Companion or Successor. It is therefore clear that its permissibility had been conveyed among them through mass transmission. This does not, however, indicate that the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, combined prayers only because of rain. Rather, if he combined them for a reason less compelling than rain, as well as combining them because of rain, then he did combine them without fear or rain. Likewise, if he combined prayers while travelling and also combined them in Medina, then he did combine them in Medina without fear or travel.

Thus, Ibn ʿAbbās’s statement, “He combined prayers without such-and-such or such-and-such,” does not deny that he combined them for those reasons. Rather, it affirms that he combined them in their absence, even though he also combined them when those reasons were present. Even if it had not been reported that he combined them for those reasons, his combining them for a lesser reason would establish, with even greater force, the permissibility of combining them for those reasons. This therefore indicates the permissibility of combining prayers because of fear and rain. He also combined prayers at ʿArafah

> **Footnotes:**
> (*) Shaykh Nāsir ibn Hamad al-Fahd stated (p. 196):
> A short passage was omitted at the beginning of the report because the copyist’s eye skipped ahead, namely: “From Hishām ibn ‘Urwa: that Abū ‘Urwa, Sa‘īd ibn al-Musayyib, and Abū Bakr…” as found in al-Bayhaqī (3/168).

---

## Page 12153

and Muzdalifah without fear or rain. All these hadiths indicate that he combined prayers within a single prayer time to relieve his community of hardship. Combining prayers is therefore permissible when not doing so would entail hardship that Allah has lifted from this community. This establishes, with even greater force, the permissibility of combining prayers because of an illness that makes performing them separately burdensome. Likewise, someone who cannot complete ritual purification at both prayer times without hardship may combine them, such as a woman experiencing non-menstrual bleeding and others in similar circumstances.

It is reported that ʿUmar ibn al-Khattāb said: “Combining two prayers without an excuse is among the major sins.” Al-Thawrī transmitted this in his Jāmiʿ from Saʿīd, from Qatādah, from Abū al-ʿĀliyah, from ʿUmar.

Yahyā ibn Saʿd also transmitted it from Yahyā ibn Subh, who said: “Humayd ibn Hilāl narrated to me, from Abū Qatādah—meaning al-ʿAdawī—that ʿUmar ibn al-Khattāb wrote to one of his officials: ‘Three things are among the major sins: combining two prayers without an excuse, fleeing the battlefield, and plundering.’”

Al-Bayhaqī said: “Abū Qatādah lived during ʿUmar’s lifetime. If he witnessed him writing this, then the chain is connected. Otherwise, this report becomes strong when supported by the first.” This wording indicates that combining prayers is permissible when there is an excuse, and ʿUmar did not distinguish one excuse from another.

Al-Bayhaqī said: “A hadith on this matter has also been transmitted with a connected chain back to the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, but its chain includes someone whose reports cannot be relied upon as evidence. It is transmitted by Salmān al-Taymī, from Hanash al-Sanʿānī, from ʿIkrimah, from Ibn ʿAbbās.” End quote.

---

## Page 12154

Section: Further Discussion of Shortening the Prayer and Why ‘Uthmān Prayed in Full at Minā

Some views on this matter have already been mentioned. The first two views are reported from al-Zuhrī, and Ahmad mentioned both. ‘Abd al-Razzāq narrated: “Ma‘mar informed us, reporting from al-Zuhrī, who said: ‘ ‘Uthmān prayed four units at Minā only because he had resolved to remain there after the Hajj.’”

Al-Tahāwī favoured this explanation, although he also mentioned the other two explanations. He cited the report transmitted by Hammād ibn Salamah, from Ayyūb, from al-Zuhrī, who said: “‘Uthmān prayed four units at Minā only because the Bedouins had become numerous that year, and he wished to teach them that the prayer consists of four units.”

Al-Tahāwī said:

“This indicates that he acted as he did to teach the Bedouins that the prayer consists of four units. It is possible that, when he wished to demonstrate this to them, he intended to stay, thereby becoming a resident whose obligatory prayer was four units. He therefore led them in four units for the reason Ma‘mar related from al-Zuhrī.

“It is also possible that he did this while still a traveller, for that reason.”

He said:

“The first interpretation is more plausible in our view, because the Bedouins knew less about prayer and its rulings during the time of the Messenger of Allah, peace and blessings be upon him, than they did during the time of ‘Uthmān. They had then only more recently left behind the ways of pre-Islamic ignorance, and during the time of the Messenger of Allah, peace and blessings be upon him, they had greater need to learn about the obligatory prayers than they did during the time of ‘Uthmān. Thus, since the Messenger of Allah, peace and blessings be upon him,

---

## Page 12155

did not perform the prayer in full for that reason, but shortened it so that they would pray the traveller’s prayer with him as prescribed, while he taught them the resident’s prayer as prescribed, ‘Uthmān had even greater reason not to lead them in the full prayer for that reason.”

Al-Tahāwī said:

“Others have said: ‘He performed the prayer in full only because he held that none should shorten it except someone who makes camp and moves on.’”

They cited as evidence what he narrated from Hammād ibn Salamah, from Qatādah, who said:

“‘Uthmān ibn ‘Affān said: ‘Only someone who carries provisions and waterskins, makes camp and moves on may shorten the prayer.’”

It was also narrated through his well-known chain from Sa‘īd ibn Abī ‘Arūbah. Someone else narrated it through an authentic chain from [‘Uthmān ibn Sa‘d] (1), from Sa‘īd ibn Abī ‘Arūbah, from Qatādah, from ‘Ayyāsh ibn ‘Abd Allāh ibn Abī Rabī‘ah, that ‘Uthmān ibn ‘Affān wrote to his officials:

“Take heed: no tax collector, [nor a farmer] (2), nor a merchant is to pray two units. Only someone who has provisions and waterskins with him is to pray two units.”

It was also narrated through Hammād ibn Salamah that Ayyūb al-Sakhtiyānī informed them, reporting from Abū Qilābah al-Jarmī, from his uncle Abū al-Muhallab, who said that ‘Uthmān wrote:

“I have learned that some people go out for trade, to collect taxes, or [to harvest dates] (3), and then shorten the prayer. Only someone who is on the move or facing an enemy may shorten the prayer.”

Ibn Hazm said:

“These two chains of transmission are of the highest authenticity.”

Al-Tahāwī said:

“They said that ‘Uthmān’s position was that none should shorten the prayer except someone who needs to carry provisions and waterskins, and someone who is on the move. As for someone who was

> **Footnotes:**
> Shaykh Nāsir ibn Hamad al-Fahd stated (p. 265):
> (1) Perhaps the intended name is Yahyā ibn Sa‘īd al-Qattān, as in al-Muhallā, 5/2.
> (2) Sharh Ma‘ānī al-Āthār (the original source of the quotation), 1/426, reads “wa-lā nā’ī” (nor someone far away), whereas al-Muhallā, 5/2, reads “wa-lā tān.”
> (3) Al-Muhallā, 5/2, reads “li-jashar” (for pasturing livestock).

---

## Page 12156

in a town where he had no need to carry provisions and waterskins, he would perform the prayer in full.

They said: ‘This is why ‘Uthmān performed the prayer in full at Minā: its population had by then grown so numerous that it had become a town where those staying had no need to carry provisions and waterskins.’”

Al-Tahāwī said:

“We consider this view unsound, because Minā in ‘Uthmān’s time was no more populous than Mecca in the time of the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. Yet the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, used to pray two units there. Abū Bakr subsequently did likewise, as did ‘Umar after Abū Bakr. If the prayer was shortened there, although those staying there had no need to carry provisions and waterskins, then places less developed than Mecca have even greater reason to be treated likewise.”

He continued:

“All these explanations, being unsound, are therefore ruled out as reasons for ‘Uthmān’s shortening the prayer, except for the first explanation, which Ma‘mar related from al-Zuhrī. It is possible that he performed it in full for that reason. The report states that he performed it in full because he intended to stay, as we have narrated concerning this and as we have explained its meaning.”

I say:

Al-Tahāwī’s aim is to reconcile ‘Uthmān’s conduct with his own legal principle, but this is not possible. ‘Uthmān was one of the Emigrants, and the Emigrants were forbidden to remain in Mecca. When they came to Mecca for ‘umrah, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, did not permit them to stay there for more than three days after completing their ‘umrah, as reported in the two Sahīh collections from al-‘Alā’ ibn al-Hadramī:

“The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace,

---

## Page 12157

permitted an Emigrant to remain for three days after completing his rites.” For this reason, at the time of his death in Mecca, Ibn ‘Umar instructed that he be buried outside the Sacred Precinct, not in Mecca. The two Sahīh collections also report:

“When the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, visited Sa‘d ibn Abī Waqqās, who had fallen ill during the Farewell Pilgrimage, Sa‘d feared that he would die in Mecca. He said, ‘O Messenger of Allah, will I be left behind after my emigration?’ The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, gave him the glad tidings that he would not die there. He said, ‘You will not die until some people benefit through you and others are harmed through you. But poor Sa‘d ibn Khawlah!’ The Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, lamented his having died in Mecca.”

It is well known that whenever ‘Uthmān performed ‘umrah, he would make his mount kneel, perform ‘umrah, then remount and depart. How, then, can it be said that he intended to remain in Mecca? Moreover, this is a blatant falsehood: ‘Uthmān never took up residence in Mecca; whenever he performed Hajj, he would return to Medina.

Al-Shāfi‘ī and his followers, together with a group of the later followers of Ahmad, such as al-Qādī, Abū al-Khattāb, Ibn ‘Aqīl and others, interpreted ‘Uthmān’s conduct in accordance with their own position. They said:

“Since a traveller has the choice between performing the prayer in full and shortening it, both are permissible. ‘Uthmān acted as he did because shortening the prayer is permissible and performing it in full is also permissible.”

They interpreted ‘Ā’ishah’s conduct in the same way, citing as evidence reports they transmitted from her. Al-Bayhaqī mentioned the view of those who said that he performed the prayer in full on account of the Bedouins. (He related this from Sunan Abī Dāwūd: Mūsā ibn Ismā‘īl narrated to us; Hammād narrated to us, from Ayyūb, from al-Zuhrī, that ‘Uthmān ibn ‘Affān performed the prayer in full

---

## Page 12158

at Minā on account of the Bedouins, whose numbers had increased [that year] (1). He therefore led the people in four units of prayer to teach them that the prayer consisted of four units. Al-Bayhaqī also transmitted a report through [Mūsā] (2) ibn Ishāq, the judge: [Ya‘qūb ibn Humayd] (3) narrated to us; Sulaymān ibn Sālim, the freedman of ‘Abd al-Rahmān ibn Humayd, narrated to us, from ‘Abd al-Rahmān ibn Humayd, from his father, from ‘Uthmān ibn ‘Affān, that he performed the prayer in full at Minā, then addressed the people, saying: “O people, the Sunnah is that of the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and his two Companions. However, [ignorant common folk] (4) have appeared among the people, and I feared that they would [take this as the established practice] (5).”)

Al-Bayhaqī said: “Other explanations have also been given. The more likely explanation is that he regarded shortening the prayer as a concession and therefore considered performing it in full permissible, as ‘Ā’ishah did.”

I say:

This is far-fetched. ‘Uthmān departed from what the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and his two successors after him had consistently practised, even though it was easier for him and for the Muslims. Yet ‘Uthmān was known for his forbearance, for choosing the easiest course for himself and his subjects, and for avoiding severity and harshness. It is therefore incongruous that he should undertake the more burdensome and demanding course, abandoning what the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and his two successors after him had consistently practised—despite his eagerness to follow their example—merely because this less preferable course was permissible, unless he believed that doing so served an overriding benefit that prompted him to act in this way. Even supposing that he was entitled to pray four units, how could he require those praying behind him to do so? For when they followed him as their imam, they prayed as he prayed

>

The correction is based on Fath al-Bārī, 2:591, where al-Hāfiz, may Allah have mercy on him, quotes this report of al-Bayhaqī as follows: “Then he delivered a sermon, saying: ‘Shortening the prayer is the sunnah of the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and his two Companions. However, taghām (ignorant common folk) have appeared—with a fathah (short a) on the tā’ and the dotted khā’—and I feared that they would adopt this as the established practice.’” See also Nayl al-Awtār, 3:260.

---

## Page 12159

and he would thus oblige the Muslims to undertake the more burdensome course, contrary to the Sunnah, merely because it was permissible. The same applies to ‘Ā’ishah. Others among the early generations, both rulers and those who were not rulers, agreed with ‘Uthmān on this and performed the prayer in full, although the leading Companions did not favour that course. Mālik narrated from al-Zuhrī:

A man informed him, on the authority of ‘Abd al-Rahmān ibn al-Miswar ibn Makhramah and ‘Abd al-Rahmān ibn ‘Abd Yaghūth, that the two were travelling together. Sa‘d ibn Abī Waqqās would shorten the prayer and not fast, whereas they would perform the prayer in full and fast. Sa‘d was asked: “We see you shortening the prayer and not fasting, while they perform it in full.” Sa‘d replied: “We know better.”

Shu‘bah narrated from Habīb ibn Abī Thābit, from ‘Abd al-Rahmān ibn al-Miswar, who said: “We were with Sa‘d ibn Abī Waqqās in a village in Syria. He would pray two units, while we would pray four. When we asked him about this, Sa‘d would say: ‘We know better.’”

Mālik narrated from Ibn Shihāb, from Safwān ibn ‘Abd Allāh ibn Safwān, who said: “‘Abd Allāh ibn ‘Umar came to visit ‘Abd Allāh ibn Safwān during his illness. He led us in two units of prayer and then left, so we completed the prayer on our own.”

I say:

‘Abd Allāh ibn Safwān was a resident of Mecca, which is why they completed the prayer after praying behind Ibn ‘Umar. Mālik narrated from Nāfi‘ that Ibn ‘Umar would pray four units behind the imam at Minā, but when he prayed on his own, he would pray two units.

Al-Bayhaqī said: “The more likely explanation is that ‘Uthmān regarded shortening the prayer as a concession and therefore considered performing it in full permissible, as ‘Ā’ishah did.”

He said: “This has also been reported from a number of the Companions, although they preferred to shorten the prayer.” Then

---

## Page 12160

he narrated the well-known report transmitted by ‘Abd al-Razzāq, from Isrā’īl, from Abū Ishāq al-Sabī‘ī, from Abū Laylā, who said:

“Salmān arrived in a party of twelve riders from among the Companions of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. When the time for prayer came, they said: ‘Step forward, Abū ‘Abd Allāh.’ He replied: ‘We do not lead you in prayer or marry your women; Allah guided us through you.’ A man from the group then stepped forward and led them in four units of prayer. Salmān said: ‘What have we to do with a four-unit prayer? Half of that would have sufficed us, and we have greater need of the concession.’”

He said: “Thus Salmān made it clear, in the presence of these Companions, that shortening the prayer is a concession.”

I say:

This incident took place during the caliphate of [‘Uthmān] (*). Salmān objected to praying four units because it was contrary to the Sunnah known to them: prayer leaders had not been praying four units while travelling. His statement, “We have greater need of the concession,” shows that it is indeed a concession—but a concession we are commanded to take. Likewise, eating carrion in circumstances of extreme hunger is a concession that one is commanded to take; refraining from fasting when ill is a concession that one is commanded to take; and praying after dry ablution is a prescribed concession. Concerning passing between al-Safā and al-Marwah, Allah says:

“So whoever performs Hajj to the House or performs ‘umrah, there is no blame upon him for passing between them.”

Yet this is prescribed, whether as an essential pillar, an obligation, or a recommended practice. The man who led Salmān in four units may have considered shortening the prayer inapplicable to someone in his circumstances—either because he considered his journey short

> **Footnotes:**
> (*) The text in square brackets is absent from the edition of Shaykh ‘Abd al-Rahmān ibn al-Qāsim—may Allah have mercy on him—who indicated a blank in the original. The supplied reading is taken from the edition edited by ‘Āmir al-Jazzār and Anwar al-Bāz.
> Shaykh Nāsir ibn Hamad al-Fahd writes (p. 198):
> “The blank may have contained [‘Uthmān ibn ‘Affān, may Allah be pleased with him], since Salmān—may Allah be pleased with him—died a year before ‘Uthmān—may Allah be pleased with him—was killed. Allah Most High knows best.”

---

## Page 12161

or because, in his view, his journey was not one for which prayer could be shortened. Some Companions held that prayer should be shortened only when travelling for Hajj, ‘umrah, or a military expedition. Many earlier and later scholars disagreed over both the kinds of journey that permit shortening the prayer and the distance required. In this particular incident, the imam’s circumstances are unclear. Salmān’s following him indicates that when an imam acts on the basis of an interpretive judgment, he is to be followed in that action—as when, on such a basis, he recites qunūt (the supplication in prayer), or pronounces “Allah is the Greatest” five or seven times.

The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, once prayed five units, and his Companions followed him, thinking that the prayer had been increased. When he concluded the prayer with the salutation, they mentioned this to him, and he said:

“I am only a human being, and I forget as you forget. So when I forget, remind me.”

Scholars have disagreed about what someone praying behind an imam should do if the imam rises for a fifth unit: should he follow him, cease following him and conclude his prayer with the salutation, cease following him and wait for him, or be given a choice between these courses? There are well-known views on this, all of which have been reported from Ahmad.

Alternatively, Salmān may have considered praying four units disliked, yet followed the imam in doing so. Following the imam is obligatory, and doing something disliked is permissible for an overriding benefit. Undoubtedly, a traveller’s praying four units is not like praying four units for Fajr. If a traveller were to pray behind a resident imam, he would pray four units in order to follow his imam. Thus, this prayer is performed as two units in some circumstances and four in others, unlike Fajr. Following a travelling imam could therefore be treated like a traveller’s following a resident imam, since in both cases the person follows his imam.

---

## Page 12162

This view—that praying four units is disliked—is the most balanced of the positions. It is the position Ahmad explicitly stated in al-Athram’s report. When asked whether a traveller may pray four units, he replied, “I do not favour it; rather, prayer during travel is two units.”

Al-Marwadhī also reported that he said, “If he wishes, he may pray four units, and if he wishes, he may pray two.” Ahmad’s statements consistently hold that shortening the prayer is preferable. Indeed, it is reported that he hesitated to affirm that praying four units discharges the obligation.

Mālik’s position is that praying four units is disliked and that the prayer should be repeated within its prescribed time. Hence, two views are mentioned within his school concerning whether a four-unit prayer is valid. Al-Shāfi‘ī’s position permits either course. There are two views concerning which is preferable; the sounder is that shortening the prayer is preferable, as in one of the two reports from Ahmad. This is also the position chosen by many of his followers.

Ahmad’s hesitation to affirm that it discharges the obligation implies that a ruling on the matter is to be derived from his statements within his school. At most, the person has made a disliked addition, and this does not invalidate the prayer: he has performed what is obligatory and something extra.
