An addition made out of forgetfulness does not invalidate the prayer, by the agreement of the Muslims. The same applies to an addition made in error, in the belief that it is permissible. Such an addition is not made by someone who believes it forbidden, but only by someone who believes it permissible. There is no explicit text declaring it forbidden. Rather, the evidence indicates that it is contrary to the Sunnah, not that it is forbidden—like praying without raising the hands, turning to look around during prayer, and similar disliked acts. We shall discuss this matter fully, Allah willing.

As for ‘Uthmān’s praying the full prayer, his conduct should be understood in light of what he used to say

---

## Page 12163

not on what has not been reliably established from him.

He said:

“I have learned that some people go out for trade, to collect revenue, or to harvest dates, and shorten their prayers. Only someone who is travelling or facing an enemy may shorten his prayers.”

In this statement, he clarified his position: someone staying in a village or town may not shorten his prayers unless he fears an enemy nearby. Only someone who is travelling may shorten them—that is, a traveller carrying provisions and waterskins, meaning food and drink; a waterskin is a vessel for water. His point is that someone staying somewhere where food and drink are available enjoys comforts like those of a resident and therefore may not shorten his prayers. Shortening was prescribed only because of the hardship a person encounters, whereas this person encounters no hardship. In his view, therefore, shortening is for the traveller carrying provisions and waterskins, and for someone in fear.

When Minā became built up and provisions and water became available there, he no longer considered shortening appropriate there, either for himself or for the pilgrims accompanying him. As for his statement in that report, “But something has happened this year,” he did not specify what had happened; this may have been the new circumstance. If ignorant Bedouins and others had come, believing that the prayer consisted of four units, then he feared that they would think it could be performed as four units in a place where provisions and water were available, which he regarded as impermissible. And if he had married in Mecca, this too would be consistent with his position, for he had married in a place where provisions and water were available, and he did not consider shortening appropriate for someone staying with his family in such a place. On this understanding, all the reliably established explanations he gave concerning this matter corroborate one another.

---

## Page 12164

As for al-Tahāwī’s objection that Mecca in the Prophet’s time—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—was more built up than Minā was in ʿUthmān’s time, the response on ʿUthmān’s behalf is as follows:

During the compensatory ʿumrah (lesser pilgrimage), then during the campaign to conquer Mecca, and then during the ʿumrah from al-Jiʿrānah, the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—feared the enemy. ʿUthmān permitted someone in fear to shorten his prayers even when staying somewhere where provisions and water were available, for he permitted shortening both for travellers and for those facing an enemy. During the Farewell Pilgrimage, however, the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—was safe, but he was not staying in Mecca. Rather, he and his Companions were staying at al-Abtah, outside Mecca. They were therefore not staying in a place of settled residence or somewhere where provisions and water were available.

Usāmah asked, “Where shall we stay tomorrow? Will you stay at your house in Mecca?” He replied, “Has ʿAqīl left us any house? We shall stay at Khayf Banī Kinānah, where they swore a mutual oath to uphold disbelief.” This campsite is at al-Abtah, between the cemeteries and Minā.

Likewise, ʿĀʾishah—may Allah be pleased with her—explained that she performed the prayers in full because shortening was prescribed on account of hardship, whereas performing them in full caused her no hardship.

The earlier and later generations of scholars differed over the kind of journey that permits shortening prayers and its length. The position of ʿUthmān and ʿĀʾishah was one of their views on the matter.

There are other views concerning the kind of journey that permits shortening prayers. Moreover, ʿUthmān was opposed in this by ʿAlī, Ibn Masʿūd, ʿImrān ibn Husayn, Saʿd ibn Abī Waqqās, Ibn ʿUmar, Ibn ʿAbbās, and other learned Companions. Thus, it was narrated by Sufyān

---

## Page 12165

ibn ʿUyaynah, from Jaʿfar ibn Muhammad, from his father, who said:

“ʿUthmān fell ill while he was at Minā. ʿAlī arrived and was asked, ‘Lead the people in prayer.’ He replied, ‘If you wish, I will lead you in the prayer of the Messenger of Allah—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—of two rakʿahs (units of prayer).’ They said, ‘No, only the prayer of the Commander of the Faithful’—meaning four rakʿahs. So he refused.” There is also a report from Ibn Masʿūd in the two Sahīh collections (1).

Scholars have differed over performing four rakʿahs while travelling, holding several views:

One view is that this is equivalent to performing the dawn prayer as four rakʿahs. This is the position of a group of earlier and later scholars, including Abū Hanīfah, Ibn Hazm, and other adherents of the Zāhirī school. According to Abū Hanīfah, if the worshipper sits for the time needed to recite the tashahhud (testimony of faith), his prayer is complete, and whatever he performs thereafter is treated as a separate voluntary prayer. If he does not sit for that length of time, his prayer is invalid. Ibn Hazm and others, however, hold that his prayer is invalid, just as they regard a dawn prayer performed as four rakʿahs as invalid.

Saʿīd narrated in his Sunan from al-Dahhāk ibn Muzāhim, who said:

“Ibn ʿAbbās said, ‘Whoever performs four rakʿahs while travelling is like someone who performs two rakʿahs when not travelling.’”

Ibn Hazm said:

“We have also transmitted a report from ʿUmar ibn ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz. When performing prayers in full while travelling, for whoever wished to do so, was mentioned to him, he said, ‘No. Prayer while travelling must be two rakʿahs; no other number is valid.’”

> **Footnotes:**
> (1) A blank in the original.
> Shaykh Nāsir ibn Hamad al-Fahd states (p. 198):
> The blank apparently contained—and Allah knows best—the hadith of Ibn Masʿūd expressing his disapproval of performing prayers in full while travelling. Its wording, as recorded in the two Sahīh collections, is:
> He said: “I prayed two rakʿahs (units of prayer) with the Messenger of Allah—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—at Minā. I prayed two rakʿahs with Abū Bakr—may Allah be pleased with him—at Minā, and I prayed two rakʿahs with ʿUmar ibn al-Khattāb—may Allah be pleased with him—at Minā. If only my share of the four rakʿahs were two accepted ones.

---

## Page 12166

Their argument is that it is established that Allah prescribed only two rakʿahs while travelling. Neither the Quran nor the Sunnah provides for any addition to that number. Every report claiming that the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—prayed four rakʿahs or approved of someone doing so is false.

As for the practice of ʿUthmān and ʿĀʾishah, it reflected their interpretation that prayers should be shortened on some journeys but not others, just as others interpreted this as applying only to journeys for Hajj, ʿumrah (the lesser pilgrimage), or jihad. Moreover, leading Companions disagreed with them and objected to their practice.

They also argued that the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—said:

“It is a charity Allah has bestowed upon you, so accept His charity.”

He thus commanded them to accept it, and a command entails an obligation.

Those who hold that both practices are permissible base their argument principally on the words of the Most High:

“When you travel through the land, there is no blame upon you for shortening the prayer if you fear that those who disbelieve may harm you.”

They said that this wording is used only for what is permissible, not what is obligatory, as in His words:

“There is no blame upon you for laying down your weapons if you are troubled by rain or are ill.”

And His words:

“There is no blame upon you if you divorce women before consummating the marriage or fixing a dowry for them.”

And other similar passages. As evidence from the Sunnah, they cited the previously mentioned report that “the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—approved of ʿĀʾishah’s performing the prayer in full,” as well as the reports that he himself did so. They also argued that ʿUthmān prayed in full

---

## Page 12167

at Minā in the presence of the Companions, who likewise prayed in full behind him. All these arguments are weak.

As for the verse, we say:

It is established through mass transmission that the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—would pray only two rakʿahs while travelling, as did Abū Bakr and ʿUmar after him. This indicates that praying two rakʿahs is preferable, as the overwhelming majority of scholars hold. Since shortening the prayer constitutes obedience to Allah and His Messenger and is preferable to praying in full, the absence of blame cannot be cited as evidence that it is merely permissible and carries no particular merit. Moreover, whatever explanation they offer to reconcile this wording with its being recommended is equally available to others who hold that it is commanded as an obligation. Concerning the walk between Safā and Marwah, the Most High says:

“So whoever makes Hajj to the House or performs ʿumrah, there is no blame upon him for walking between them.”

Walking between Safā and Marwah is the prescribed rite of saʿy, by Muslim consensus; it is held to be either an essential pillar, an obligation, or a recommended practice.

Furthermore, even if shortening the prayer is a concession permitting what would otherwise be prohibited, such a concession may be obligatory—as with eating carrion for someone compelled by necessity, performing dry ablution for someone who has no water, and similar cases.

This is assuming it is conceded that the verse refers to reducing the number of rakʿahs, for there are three views concerning the verse. One view is that it refers only to reducing their number. On this interpretation, restricting it to a state of fear serves no purpose.

---

## Page 12168

The second view is that it refers to reducing the actions performed in prayer: the prayer offered in fear involves fewer actions than the prayer offered in safety, and fear permits this reduction. This is countered, however, by the fact that the prayer of fear is permissible both while resident and while travelling, whereas the verse permits shortening while travelling.

The third view—and it is the soundest—is that the verse refers both to reducing the number of rakʿahs and to reducing the actions performed. This is why it makes shortening conditional upon travel and fear. When travel and fear occur together, shortening in both respects is permitted. Travel alone permits only a reduction in the number of rakʿahs, while fear alone permits only a reduction in the actions performed.

Those who hold that the obligatory prayer in fear and during travel consists of one rakʿah—as in one of the two positions within Ahmad’s school, which is also the position of Ibn Hazm—mean when fear and travel occur together. Thus, travel and fear together permit shortening the prayer to one rakʿah, as Abū Dāwūd al-Tayālisī narrated:

Al-Masʿūdī—namely, ʿAbd al-Rahmān ibn ʿAbd Allāh—narrated to us from Yazīd al-Faqīr, who said:

“I asked Jābir ibn ʿAbd Allāh about the two rakʿahs during travel: ‘Should I shorten them?’ Jābir replied: ‘No. The two rakʿahs during travel do not constitute a shortened prayer. Shortening means praying one rakʿah during combat.’”

In Sahīh Muslim, Ibn ʿAbbās said:

“Allah prescribed prayer through your Prophet: four rakʿahs while resident, two rakʿahs while travelling, and one rakʿah in fear.”

It was stated by

---

## Page 12169

Ibn Hazm:

“We have also transmitted it through Hudhayfah, Jābir, Zayd ibn Thābit, Abū Hurayrah and Ibn ʿUmar from the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, with chains of transmission of the highest authenticity.”

Ibn Hazm said:

“On the basis of this verse, we hold that a person offering the prayer of fear while travelling may pray one rakʿah or two, as he wishes. This is because the Quran uses the wording ‘There is no blame,’ rather than a command imposing an obligation. The people prayed it with the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, sometimes as just one rakʿah and sometimes as two. It was therefore a matter of choice, as Jābir said.”

As for ʿUthmān’s prayer, the leading Companions’ disapproval of his practice is well known. Nevertheless, they would pray behind him. Indeed, Ibn Masʿūd would pray four rakʿahs even when praying alone, saying, “Discord is evil.” Ibn ʿUmar, however, would pray two rakʿahs when praying alone.

This shows that they regarded praying four rakʿahs while travelling as disliked and contrary to the Sunnah. Nevertheless, anyone who does so need not repeat the prayer, and if the imam does so, he is to be followed. This is because the traveller’s prayer is not like the Fajr prayer; rather, it belongs to the same category as the Friday prayer and the two Eid prayers. Hence, in the Sunnah he transmitted, ʿUmar ibn al-Khattāb grouped these four together, saying:

“The Eid al-Adhā prayer is two rakʿahs, the Eid al-Fitr prayer is two rakʿahs, the Friday prayer is two rakʿahs, and the traveller’s prayer is two rakʿahs—complete, not shortened, as declared by your Prophet. Whoever fabricates lies has indeed failed.”

Ahmad and al-Nasāʾī narrated this through ʿAbd al-Rahmān ibn Abī Laylā, from Kaʿb ibn ʿUjrah, who said: “ʿUmar said…” It was also narrated by Yazīd ibn Ziyād

---

## Page 12170

ibn Abī al-Jaʿd, from Zubayd al-Yāmī, from ʿAbd al-Rahmān. These four prayers, then, are not of the same kind as Fajr. It is well known that on Fridays one sometimes prays two rakʿahs and at other times four. Anyone who misses the Friday prayer prays four rakʿahs, not two. The same applies to anyone who fails to catch one rakʿah of it, according to the Companions and the majority of scholars, as authentically reported from the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, who said:

“Whoever catches one rakʿah of the prayer has caught the prayer.”

When the conditions for the Friday prayer are met, the imam delivers two sermons and prays two rakʿahs. If, however, he were to deliver the sermon and pray four rakʿahs of Dhuhr, he would be abandoning the Sunnah. Even so, those praying would not be like someone who prayed four rakʿahs for Fajr. For this reason, a sick person, a traveller, a woman, or anyone else who is not obliged to attend the Friday prayer and may pray four rakʿahs of Dhuhr, may follow the imam in the Friday prayer and pray two rakʿahs. Likewise, a traveller may pray two rakʿahs, or follow a resident imam and pray four rakʿahs behind him.

If it is objected:

“Congregation is a condition for the Friday prayer; hence the ruling for someone praying alone differs from that for someone following an imam.”

This distinction was mentioned by the followers of al-Shāfiʿī and a group of Ahmad’s followers.

The reply is:

Whether congregation is a condition for the five daily prayers is disputed within Ahmad’s school and elsewhere. The stronger view is that it is a condition when one is able. Accordingly, when a traveller prays behind

---

## Page 12171

a resident imam, he joins the obligatory congregation and must therefore follow the imam, just as in the Friday prayer. If it is objected:

“Travellers may form a congregation of their own,”

the reply is:

“They may likewise form a congregation on Friday and pray four rakʿahs.”

As for the Eid prayer, it is established that ʿAlī appointed someone to lead the people in the mosque in four rakʿahs: two in accordance with the Sunnah and two because they had not gone out to the open prayer ground. Thus, Dhuhr on Friday and the two Eid prayers are sometimes offered as two rakʿahs and sometimes as four, like the traveller’s prayer, unlike Fajr. The reports from the Companions support this: they disapproved of the imam praying four rakʿahs, yet prayed behind him, as in the report of Salmān and the report concerning Ibn Masʿūd and others with ʿUthmān. Had they regarded this as equivalent to praying four rakʿahs for Fajr, they would not have considered it permissible to pray four, just as no Muslim considers it permissible to pray four rakʿahs for Fajr.

If someone says:

“Once they had sat for the time required for the tashahhud, they had fulfilled the obligatory prayer, and the remainder was voluntary,”

the reply is:

It is well known that none of them is reported to have said, “We intended the two rakʿahs as a voluntary prayer.” Moreover, doing so is not prescribed: no one may pray two rakʿahs after Fajr. Indeed, the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, rebuked someone who offered the Sunnah prayer after the call to commence the obligatory prayer, saying:

“Is Fajr four rakʿahs?”

That man had prayed before the imam; how much more objectionable would it be to join one prayer directly to another?

It is also established in al-Sahīh:

“The Prophet

---

## Page 12172

—peace and blessings be upon him—forbade joining one prayer directly to another without separating them by speaking or standing up.” The Companions used to rebuke anyone who joined the Friday prayer or another prayer directly to a voluntary prayer. How, then, could they permit joining the two rakʿahs offered while travelling directly to a voluntary prayer, if only two rakʿahs were permissible?

Moreover, why must a resident praying behind a traveller pray four rakʿahs, as is established from the Companions and acknowledged by Abū Hanīfah? Furthermore, a resident may pray four rakʿahs behind a traveller who prays two, as was the practice of the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, and his successors, who would say, “Complete your prayer, for we are travellers.”

This further demonstrates that the traveller’s prayer is of the same kind as the resident’s prayer. The overwhelming majority of scholars have accepted that the one may pray behind the other, just as someone may pray Dhuhr behind someone offering the Friday prayer. This is not like making up a missed Dhuhr prayer behind someone offering Fajr.

As for someone who says that a traveller is obliged to pray four rakʿahs but may omit two by shortening the prayer, his position contradicts the scriptural texts, the consensus of the early generations, and foundational principles. It is also internally inconsistent. A traveller may omit these two rakʿahs without replacing them with an alternative or an equivalent. This contradicts their being obligatory, for it is impossible for something to be obligatory

---

## Page 12173

for a person while he is not required to perform it, its substitute, or its equivalent. Thus, the traveller’s obligation is only two rakʿahs. This is what the statements of Ahmad and his early followers indicate, for he did not require an intention to shorten the prayer. He said, “I do not favor praying four,” and withheld judgment on whether four rakʿahs would discharge the obligation.

No one has reported that Ahmad said, “One may shorten the prayer only with an intention to do so.” Rather, this is the position of al-Khiraqī and those who followed him. Ahmad’s explicit statements and answers impose no such restriction, in keeping with the position of the overwhelming majority of scholars. This was also the view preferred by Abū Bakr, in agreement with the early scholars of the school, such as al-Khallāl and others—indeed, al-Athram, Abū Dāwūd, Ibrāhīm al-Harbī and others—for they required no specific intention either to shorten prayers or to combine them.

Since the traveller’s obligation is two rakʿahs, performing them discharges that obligation, whether or not he intended to shorten the prayer. This is the position of the overwhelming majority, including Mālik, Abū Hanīfah, and the early generations generally. I know of no Companion, or anyone who followed them in righteousness, who required a specific intention either to shorten prayers or to combine them. Even if a traveller intends to offer the full prayer, the Sunnah for him remains two rakʿahs. If he prays four, that is disliked, just as it is without that intention.

No one has ever reported that the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, instructed his Companions to form an intention either to shorten prayers or to combine them. Nor did his successors and Companions instruct those praying behind them to do so, even though those following the imam, or most of them, did not know what he would do. For when the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, set out on his pilgrimage, he led them in prayer

---

## Page 12174

at noon in Medina, offering four rakʿahs, and led them in the afternoon prayer at Dhū al-Hulayfah, offering two rakʿahs. Behind him were multitudes whose number Allah alone could count. All had set out to perform Hajj with him, and many did not know how to pray when travelling, either because they had only recently embraced Islam or because they had not travelled before, particularly the women. They prayed with him, yet he did not instruct them to form an intention to shorten the prayer. Likewise, he combined the prayers for them at ʿArafah without telling them, before offering the afternoon prayer, “I intend to pray the afternoon prayer after the noon prayer.”

Section

In Allah’s Book and the Sunnah of His Messenger, travel is mentioned without restriction in connection with shortening prayers and breaking the fast. Scholars have nevertheless differed over the nature and extent of such travel. As for its nature, they have differed in two respects.

The first is its legal status. Some held that prayers may be shortened only when travelling for Hajj, ʿumrah, or a military expedition. This is the position of Dāwūd and his followers, with the exception of Ibn Hazm. Ibn Hazm said: “This was also the position of a group of the early generations, as we have transmitted through Ibn Abī ʿAdī: Jarīr narrated to us from al-Aʿmash, from ʿUmārah ibn ʿUmayr, from al-Aswad, from Ibn Masʿūd, who said: ‘Only someone performing Hajj or engaged in jihad may shorten the prayer.’

“It is also related that when Tāwūs was asked about shortening the prayer, he would say: ‘When we set out to perform Hajj or ʿumrah, we prayed two rakʿahs.’

---

## Page 12175

and that Ibrāhīm al-Taymī regarded shortening the prayer as permissible only when performing Hajj or ʿumrah, or engaging in jihad.”

Their argument is that we have no text establishing that shortening the prayer applies to travellers generally. The Quran mentions shortening the prayer only when a traveller fears harm from those who disbelieve, and this is travel for jihad. As for the Sunnah, the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) shortened his prayers during his Hajj, his ʿumrahs, and his military expeditions. Its permissibility in these cases is therefore established. The default rule is to offer the prayer in full, and this requirement is waived only where the Sunnah has waived it.

Others held that prayers may be shortened only on a journey undertaken as an act of obedience to Allah, not on a merely permissible journey, such as one undertaken for trade. This is mentioned as one report from Ahmad. The majority, however, permit shortening the prayer on any journey during which breaking the fast is permissible. This is the correct view, for the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) said:

“Allah has relieved the traveller of fasting and half the prayer.”

This was related from him by Anas ibn Mālik al-Kaʿbī, and Ahmad and others transmitted it with a good chain of narration. It is also established in Sahīh Muslim and elsewhere that Yaʿlā ibn Umayyah said to ʿUmar ibn al-Khattāb:

“‘There is no blame upon you for shortening the prayer if you fear that those who disbelieve may harm you.’ Yet people are now safe.”

He replied: “I wondered about the same thing you have wondered about, so I asked the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him) about it. He said: ‘It is a charity that Allah has bestowed upon you, so accept His charity.’”

This makes clear that, when travelling in safety, it is permissible to reduce the number of rakʿahs. And even if this is a charity from Allah to us, we have been commanded

---

## Page 12176

to accept it. Some followers of al-Shāfiʿī and Ahmad have said: “We may accept it if we wish, or decline it if we wish, for accepting charity is not obligatory.” They argue this to set aside the command to pray two rakʿahs. This is mistaken, for the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) commanded us to accept Allah’s charity to us, and a command entails obligation. All His kindness towards us is charity bestowed upon us; if we do not accept it, we will perish.

It is also established that ʿUmar ibn al-Khattāb said:

“The traveller’s prayer is two rakʿahs, complete and not shortened, as declared by your Prophet. Whoever fabricates lies has indeed failed.”

Likewise, he said:

“The Friday prayer is two rakʿahs, the Eid al-Adhā prayer is two rakʿahs, and the Eid al-Fitr prayer is two rakʿahs.”

This reports that the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) prescribed two rakʿahs for Muslims when travelling in general, just as he prescribed the Friday prayer and the two Eid prayers. He did not restrict this to journeys for pilgrimage or jihad.

It is also established in the two Sahīh collections that ʿĀʾishah said:

“Prayer was prescribed as two rakʿahs. The prayer of those not travelling was then increased, while the traveller’s prayer was left unchanged.”

This makes clear that the traveller was never commanded to pray four rakʿahs. Thus, Allah has not obliged the traveller to pray four. Neither the Book of Allah nor the Sunnah of His Messenger contains any wording indicating that four rakʿahs were prescribed for the traveller. Whoever obliges a traveller to pray four has therefore imposed an obligation that Allah and His Messenger did not impose.

---

## Page 12177

If it is objected that his expression “has waived” implies that it was previously obligatory—as in his words, “He has waived fasting for him”—it is well known that fasting during Ramadan was never obligatory for the traveller. Rather, since the grounds for the obligation had been established, exempting the traveller was described as waiving it. Moreover, fasting was obligatory while he was resident, so when he travelled, it was waived on account of his journey. Similarly, one says, “Whoever embraces Islam has the jizyah waived for him,” even though the jizyah is never obligatory for a Muslim.

Furthermore, Safwān ibn Muhriz said:

“I said to Ibn ʿUmar, ‘Tell me about the traveller’s prayer.’ He said, ‘Do you fear that lies will be attributed to me?’ I said, ‘No.’ He said, ‘Two rakʿahs. Whoever opposes the Sunnah has disbelieved.’”

This is a well-known report, transmitted by Abū al-Tayyāh from Muwarriq al-ʿIjlī, from him, and is widely cited in collections of transmitted reports. Another wording reads:

“The traveller’s prayer is two rakʿahs, and whoever opposes the Sunnah has disbelieved.”

Some transmitters traced it back to the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him). Thus, he made clear that the traveller’s prayer is two rakʿahs, and that this belongs to the Sunnah: whoever opposes it by holding a contrary belief has disbelieved.

These proofs demonstrate the weakness of the view that one may shorten the prayer only on an obligatory journey. Some hold that the prayer is not shortened on a disliked or unlawful journey, but is shortened on a permissible one. This, too, is a view reported from Ahmad. As for shortening the prayer on a recreational journey, two views are reported from Ahmad.

Regarding an unlawful journey, the position of the three imams—Mālik, al-Shāfiʿī and Ahmad—is that the prayer is not shortened on it. Abū Hanīfah and groups among the earlier and later generations, however, held that one shortens the prayer on

---

## Page 12178

journeys of every kind. This is also the view of Ibn Hazm and others. Abū Hanīfah, Ibn Hazm and others hold that shortening the prayer is obligatory on every journey, even an unlawful one, just as all agree that dry ablution is obligatory when water is unavailable on an unlawful journey. Ibn ʿAqīl, in some passages, favoured shortening the prayer and not fasting on an unlawful journey.

The evidence supports those who hold that shortening the prayer and not fasting are sanctioned for travel generally, without distinguishing one kind of journey from another. This is the correct view, for the Quran and Sunnah mention travel without qualification. Allah Most High says:

“But whoever among you is ill or on a journey must make up an equal number of other days.”

Likewise, He says in the verse concerning dry ablution:

“If you are ill or on a journey…”

—and the rest of the verse. Likewise, the texts cited earlier indicate that the traveller prays two rakʿahs. No one has ever transmitted from the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) that he distinguished one kind of journey from another, despite his knowing that travel may be either unlawful or permissible. Had this ruling been restricted to a particular kind of journey, explaining that would have been obligatory. Had he explained it, the Muslim community would have transmitted his explanation. I know of nothing reported from the Companions on this matter.

Allah and His Messenger have made various rulings contingent on travel, as in His words concerning dry ablution:

“If you are ill or on a journey…”

And His words concerning fasting:

“But whoever among you is ill or on a journey…”

And His words:

“When you travel through the land, there is no blame upon you for shortening the prayer if you fear that those who disbelieve may attack you.”

And the Prophet’s statement (peace and blessings be upon him):

“The traveller may wipe for three days and nights.”

And his statement:

---

## Page 12179

“It is not lawful for a woman who believes in Allah and the Last Day to travel except with her husband or a man whom she is permanently forbidden to marry.”

And his statement:

“Allah has relieved the traveller of fasting and half the prayer.”

Nowhere in the texts of the Quran and Sunnah is travel ever restricted to one kind rather than another. How, then, could the ruling apply to only one of the two kinds of travel without Allah and His Messenger explaining this—when, in fact, their explanation encompasses both?

The same applies to dividing travel into long and short journeys, divorce after consummation into irrevocable and revocable divorce, and oaths into those that admit of expiation and those that do not, and similar cases. In these matters, Allah and His Messenger have attached the ruling to the general category shared by both, yet some people have divided it into two kinds: one to which that ruling applies and another to which it does not, without any evidence for this from the Quran or Sunnah, whether explicit or inferred.

Those who hold that these rulings do not apply to unlawful travel rely chiefly on Allah Most High’s words concerning carrion:

“But whoever is compelled by necessity, neither desiring it nor exceeding the limit, incurs no sin.”

A group of Quran commentators have held that “one who desires it” refers to someone who rebels against the ruler and whom it is lawful to fight, and that “one who exceeds the limit” refers to someone who attacks the Muslims—that is, those who wage war against them by engaging in highway robbery. They argue: “If it is established that carrion is not lawful for them, then other concessions are all the more so.” They also say: “If someone whose journey is sinful is compelled by necessity, we instruct him to repent and eat; we do not permit him to destroy himself.” This position is well known among the followers of al-Shāfiʿī and Ahmad.

---

## Page 12180

As for Ahmad and Mālik, they permitted him to eat carrion, but not to shorten the prayer or break the fast.

They also argue: “Unlawful travel is sinful, and the concessions granted to the traveller assist him in it; assisting someone in sin is not permissible.” These arguments are weak.

As for the verse, most Quran commentators have said that “one who desires it” means someone who seeks forbidden food despite being able to obtain lawful food, while “one who exceeds the limit” means someone who exceeds the amount he needs. This interpretation, rather than the first, is correct. Allah revealed this in the Meccan surahs al-Anʿām and al-Nahl, and in the Medinan surahs, to explain which foods are lawful and which are forbidden. Necessity is not confined to travel. Even when it arises during travel, unlawful travel is not confined to highway robbery and rebellion against the ruler.

During the lifetime of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, there was no ruler against whom to rebel. Nor must someone who rebels necessarily be a traveller. Likewise, the rebels whom Allah commanded to be fought in the Quran need not be travellers. Those concerning whom the verse was first revealed were not travellers; rather, they were residents of al-ʿAwālī who fought one another with sandals and palm branches. How, then, can the verse be interpreted as referring to something not confined to travel, when this interpretation does not encompass every unlawful journey? If what the verse mentions were as they claim, it would not correspond to unlawful travel: it could occur without travel, and unlawful travel could occur without it.

Moreover, His words “neither desiring it” are a circumstantial qualifier of “is compelled by necessity,” and must therefore apply at the time of

---

## Page 12181

his necessity and his eating, when he eats neither desiring it nor exceeding the limit. For He says:

“There is no sin upon him.”

It is evident that sin is negated only with regard to eating—the act itself—not the mere need for it. Thus, the verse means: whoever is compelled by necessity and eats, neither desiring it nor exceeding the limit. This makes clear that the intended meaning is that he neither seeks what is forbidden in his eating nor exceeds the limit.

Allah Most High pairs wrongdoing with transgression. Wrongdoing consists of conduct that is inherently unjust, whereas transgression means exceeding what is permitted. Likewise, He pairs sin with transgression in His words:

“Cooperate in righteousness and piety, but do not cooperate in sin and transgression.”

Sin denotes evil in general, while transgression means exceeding what is permitted. Wrongdoing therefore falls within the category of sin. Allah Most High says:

“They did not divide until after knowledge had come to them, out of mutual wrongdoing.”

And He Most High says:

“But if anyone fears partiality or sin on the part of a testator and brings about reconciliation between them, there is no sin upon him.”

Here, sin encompasses deliberate wrongdoing against the heirs, whereas partiality includes bias against them, whether deliberate or unintentional. Many Quran commentators, however, have said that partiality means an unintentional error, while sin means deliberate wrongdoing. This is because, once sin—which is deliberate—is mentioned separately, what remains under partiality is unintentional error.

The term transgression concerns overstepping limits, as Allah Most High says:

“These are the limits set by Allah, and whoever oversteps Allah’s limits has wronged himself.”

There are other passages to the same effect. Similar to this are His words:

“Our Lord, forgive us our sins and our excesses in our affairs.”

Excess means going beyond the permitted limit, whereas sins are acts that are inherently evil and sinful.

As for their assertion that this assists in disobedience, it is mistaken, because the traveller is commanded

---

## Page 12182

to pray two units of prayer, just as he is commanded to pray with dry ablution. If he lacks water on an unlawful journey, he must perform dry ablution and pray. Any units beyond the two are neither an act of obedience nor something any traveller is commanded to perform. If a traveller performs them, he has done something forbidden. Praying two units is thus like a traveller’s performing the Friday prayer behind a settled resident: would he pray anything other than two units, even if his journey is sinful, and even if he would pray four when praying alone?

Likewise, his fasting while travelling is neither an act of righteousness nor something he is commanded to do, for it is authentically reported that the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, said:

“Fasting while travelling is not an act of righteousness.”

His fasting while resident is more beloved to Allah than his fasting on an unlawful journey. If he wished to offer voluntary prayers on his mount during an unlawful journey, he would not be prevented from doing so. If he were unsure of the direction of prayer, would he not determine it as best he could and pray? If his clothes were taken away, would he not pray naked?

If it is said that he can do nothing else in these cases, the answer is that the traveller, likewise, has only been commanded to pray two units, and what is prescribed for him is not to fast. Scholars have differed over whether fasting would discharge his obligation, whereas they agree that fasting after Ramadan would discharge it.

There is no precautionary course in this matter. One group holds that if someone prays four units or fasts during Ramadan on an unlawful journey, this does not discharge his obligation—just as, in their view, it would not do so on a permissible journey. Another group holds that only praying four units and fasting during Ramadan will discharge his obligations. Likewise,

---

## Page 12183

eating carrion is obligatory for someone compelled by necessity, whether travelling or resident, and whether that necessity arose from a lawful or unlawful cause. If he threw his wealth into the sea and was compelled to eat carrion, he would be obliged to eat it. If he undertook an unlawful journey that exhausted him until he could no longer stand, he would pray sitting. Likewise, if he engaged in unlawful fighting and his wounds left him unable to stand, he would pray sitting.

If it is asked, “May someone engaged in unlawful fighting offer the prayer of fear?” the answer is: He must pray and refrain from fighting. If he refuses to abandon the unlawful fighting, we do not permit him to abandon prayer. Rather, praying as someone in fear does is better than abandoning prayer altogether. Must he then repeat the prayer? This is disputed. If he can perform it within its prescribed time without these invalidating actions, he must do so, for he is commanded to perform it. But if the time elapses without his doing so, its validity and acceptance thereafter are disputed.

The second point of disagreement is that ʿUthmān regarded as a traveller only someone who carried provisions and waterskins, not someone staying in a place where he had no need of them—for example, a merchant, a resident, or a tax collector staying somewhere they did not need such supplies. ʿUthmān did not set a fixed measure for travel; rather, he did not regard someone in this category as a traveller. It has likewise been said that he did not consider himself and those with him to be travellers at Minā once Minā had become built up.

Ibn Abī Shaybah reported that Ibn Sīrīn said: “They used to say: ‘The journey on which prayers are shortened is one for which provisions and waterskins are carried.’” The basis of this view—and Allah knows best—is that prayers are shortened only

---

## Page 12184

during travel, not while staying in one place. A man staying somewhere where food and drink are available is not a traveller but a resident, unlike a traveller who needs to carry food and drink, for the latter experiences the hardship of travel. The proponent of this view seems to have held that the concession is granted only because of hardship, and that hardship affects only those who need to carry food and drink. Statements distinguishing between different kinds of travel have also been transmitted from others.

Ibn Abī Shaybah narrated from ʿAlī ibn Mushir, from Abū Ishāq al-Shaybānī, from Qays ibn Muslim, from Tāriq ibn Shihāb, from ʿAbd Allāh ibn Masʿūd, who said:

“Do not let this countryside of yours mislead you concerning your prayers, for it is part of your city.”

His words “part of your city” indicate that he treated the countryside as part of the city because it belonged to it.

ʿAbd al-Razzāq narrated from Maʿmar, from al-Aʿmash, from Ibrāhīm al-Taymī, from his father, who said:

“I was with Hudhayfah in al-Madāʾin and asked his permission to visit my family in Kufa. He gave me permission on condition that I neither break my fast nor shorten my prayers to two rakʿahs until I returned to him.”

The distance between the two places was a little over sixty miles.

It was also reported from Hudhayfah that one should not shorten prayers when travelling to al-Sawād, the cultivated countryside. The distance between Kufa and al-Sawād was ninety miles.

It was reported from Muʿādh ibn Jabal and ʿUqbah ibn ʿĀmir:

“None of you should range with livestock over mountain ridges or through valley bottoms and then claim that you are travellers. No, certainly not! Prayers are shortened only on journeys away from one’s dwellings, from one region to another.”

---

## Page 12185

I say:

These scholars did not specify a fixed distance for shortening prayers, whether measured by time or distance. Rather, they did not regard this kind of movement as travel, just as ʿUthmān regarded travel as a journey requiring provisions and waterskins to be carried. If they meant what ʿUthmān meant—that a person continues moving within an area where provisions and waterskins are [not] (1) carried, and is therefore like a resident—then they agreed with ʿUthmān. Yet Ibn Masʿūd disagreed with ʿUthmān over his performing the prayers in full at Minā.

If, however, they meant that the districts belonging to a city are treated as part of it, as al-Sawād is in relation to Kufa, and that a traveller is only someone who leaves one administrative district for another—as in the report of Muʿādh, “from one region to another”—then this is the apparent meaning. Hence Ibn Masʿūd said of al-Sawād:

“For it is part of your city.”

Just as the orchards and farms surrounding a city belong to it, they treated that countryside in the same way, however far it extended, without setting a distance limit. This is comparable to the makhālīf (administrative districts): places where deputies are appointed to represent the chief governor in the major city. The report of Muʿādh states: “Whoever leaves one administrative district for another.”

This is supported by what Muhammad ibn Bashshār narrated:

Abū ʿĀmir al-ʿAqadī narrated to us: Shuʿbah narrated to us: I heard Qays ibn ʿImrān ibn ʿUmayr relate from his father, from his grandfather, that he set out with ʿAbd Allāh ibn Masʿūd, riding behind him on his mule, for a journey of four farsakhs, and Ibn Masʿūd prayed two rakʿahs for Zuhr.

Shuʿbah said: “Qays ibn ʿImrān told me this while his father, ʿImrān ibn ʿUmayr, was present. ʿUmayr was a client of Ibn Masʿūd.”

> **Footnotes:**
> (1) The text in square brackets is absent from the printed edition, and I did not find it in Safeguarding Majmū‘ al-Fatāwā against Omissions and Textual Corruptions.
> Usāma ibn al-Zahrā’ — coordinator of the book for al-Maktaba al-Shāmila.

---

## Page 12186

This shows that Ibn Masʿūd did not define travel by a long distance. Rather, he considered another factor, such as administrative districts. This is not determined by distance or time, but by the breadth or narrowness of administrative jurisdictions. For example, someone in Damascus would be a traveller upon journeying beyond its administrative districts.

Those who held these views appear to have considered that concessions were granted to travellers only because of the hardship they experience on a journey and their need for such concessions. They knew that someone moving from place to place within a single city is not a traveller. The same applies to someone going out to the surrounding area, just as the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, would go to Qubāʾ every Saturday, riding or walking, without shortening his prayers. Likewise, the Muslims used to come regularly from al-ʿAwālī for the Friday prayer without shortening their prayers. In their view, someone moving about within a single administrative district had the same status.

Against them, however, one may cite the people of Mecca shortening their prayers with the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, at ʿArafah, Muzdalifah and Minā, although these places belong to Mecca and are affiliated with it. Indeed, they are more closely attached to Mecca than al-Sawād is to Kufa, and nearer to it than al-Sawād is to Kufa. For between the Gate of Banū Shaybah and the imam’s station at ʿArafah, beside the rocks at the foot of the Mount of Mercy… He means this distance and this journey, and they are travellers.

If it is said, “The place to which they travel is not a place where people reside,” the response is: “On the contrary, the village of Namirah was there, though the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, did not stay in it. There were markets there, and nearby was ʿUranah, whose valley connects with ʿArafah.” Moreover, there is no difference between travelling

---

## Page 12187

to a place where people reside and to one where they do not, provided that the traveller does not intend to reside there. The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and the Muslims travelled to Mecca, a place where residence is possible, yet remained travellers during their military campaigns, Hajj and umrah. The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, shortened his prayers within Mecca itself in the year of the Conquest and said:

“O people of Mecca, offer your prayers in full, for we are travellers.”

Umar likewise did this after him, as Mālik reported with an authentic chain of transmission. Neither the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, nor Abū Bakr nor Umar did this at Minā. Whoever attributes that to them is mistaken.

This differs from the Prophet’s going to Qubā every Saturday, riding or walking, and his going out to pray over the martyrs—for he prayed over them shortly before his death. It also differs from his going to al-Baqī and from the people of al-Awālī coming to Medina to attend the Friday prayer. None of this constitutes travel, since the name Medina encompasses all these places. The people fall into only two categories: the Bedouin and the people of Medina. Moreover, a person would go and return to his family that same day without making the preparations required for travel. He would carry neither provisions nor waterskins, whether for the journey or for the place he was going to.

For this reason, someone who goes to the outskirts of his own city is not called a traveller. Likewise, according to most scholars, the Friday prayer is obligatory for those living around a city, with the relevant distance reckoned by the audibility of the call to prayer and by a measure of one parasang. If that constituted travel, the Friday prayer would not be obligatory for someone who had to undertake a journey to attend it. Since the Friday prayer is not obligatory for a traveller, how could someone be obliged to travel for it?

---

## Page 12188

Accordingly, a traveller is not deemed such because he covers a fixed distance or travels for a fixed number of days, but because the activity he undertakes constitutes travel. A person may count as a traveller over a short distance, yet not over a longer one.

For example, someone who rides a swift horse for the distance of one barīd (postal stage), then immediately returns to his town, is not a traveller. But if he covers this distance over a day and a night, needing to carry provisions and waterskins, he is a traveller, as were the people of Mecca when they travelled to Arafat. If a man rode a swift horse to Arafat and returned to Mecca that same day, however, he would not be a traveller.

Evidence for this is the statement of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace:

“A traveller may wipe over his footwear for three days and nights, and a resident for one day and night.”

If someone covered one barīd in three days, he would be a traveller for three days and nights, and the traveller’s allowance for wiping must therefore apply to him. But if he covered that barīd in half a day, he would not be a traveller. Thus, what the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, took into account was travelling for three days, whether the journey was swift or slow, and whether the days were long or short.

Those who fixed the measure at three days or two calculated this according to travel by camel or on foot. They made one fixed distance a threshold applying equally to everyone: even if someone covered it in a day, they deemed him a traveller, while if he covered less than that distance in ten days, they did not deem him a traveller. This is contrary to the words of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace.

---

## Page 12189

Moreover, when the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, went to Qubā’, al-‘Awālī and Uhud, and when his Companions came from those places to Medina, they travelled through inhabited areas, among buildings and enclosed date-palm groves. These were places of residence, not places of travel. A traveller must emerge into the open country, for the word safar (travel) itself indicates this. The expression safarat al-mar’a ‘an wajhihā means that a woman has uncovered her face. Thus, unless someone emerges from among the dwellings into the open country, where he is exposed to view, he is not a traveller. Allah Most High says:

“Among the Bedouins around you are hypocrites, and among the people of Medina are those entrenched in hypocrisy.”

And He Most High says:

“It was not for the people of Medina and the Bedouins around them to remain behind the Messenger of Allah, nor to prefer their own lives to his.”

He thus divided the people into two groups: the people of Medina and the Bedouins. The Bedouins were tent-dwellers, whereas the people of Medina lived in mud-brick dwellings. Thus, anyone living in a mud-brick dwelling belonged to the people of Medina. Medina had no wall distinguishing its interior from its exterior; rather, it consisted of separate quarters.

A quarter was called a dār (abode). Such a quarter was a small village containing dwellings, surrounded by date palms and burial grounds; its buildings were not contiguous. Thus, in the village of Banū Mālik ibn al-Najjār, their properties and date palms surrounded their houses. The quarter of Banū ‘Adī ibn al-Najjār was likewise, as were those of Banū Māzin ibn al-Najjār, Banū Sālim, Banū Sā‘idah, Banū al-Hārith ibn al-Khazraj, Banū ‘Amr ibn ‘Awf, and Banū ‘Abd

---

## Page 12190

al-Ashhal, and likewise all the other clans of the Ansār. As the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“The best quarters of the Ansār are those of Banū al-Najjār, then Banū ‘Abd al-Ashhal, then Banū al-Hārith, then Banū Sā‘idah. And in every quarter of the Ansār there is good.”

The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, had settled among Banū Mālik ibn al-Najjār, and there he built his mosque. The site had been an enclosed garden belonging to some of Banū al-Najjār, containing date palms, ruins and graves. He ordered the palms to be cut down, the graves to be dug up and the ruins to be levelled, and he built his mosque there. The other quarters of the Ansār lay around it.

Ibn Hazm said that there was no urban centre there. He said that this was something no one was unaware of; rather, it had been transmitted by large numbers of people from similarly large numbers. All of this constituted a single city, just as Allah divided the people into two groups: the people of Medina and the Bedouins around them.

Thus, anyone who was not a Bedouin belonged to the people of Medina. He did not distinguish an interior and an exterior, a wall and outlying suburbs for Medina, as such distinctions are made for walled cities. The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, designated Medina’s sanctuary as an area measuring one barīd by one barīd. Medina lies between two lava fields; a lava field is land whose soil consists of black stones. He said:

“The area between its two lava fields is a sanctuary.”

Everything between its two lava fields therefore belongs to Medina and is a sanctuary. This is a distance of one barīd, yet someone travelling within it is not a traveller. Even if a Meccan who goes out to ‘Arafāt is a traveller, ‘Arafah, Muzdalifah and Minā are tracts of open country outside Mecca, unlike al-‘Awālī

---

## Page 12191

of Medina. This also shows that no fixed distance is decisive. Someone travelling within a large city, even for two or three days, would not be considered a traveller, whereas someone setting out from a small village and travelling for the same length of time would be. Thus, a person must intend to reach a destination, travelling from one place to another. If the stretch between the two places is uninhabited open country through which he carries food and water, he is a traveller, even if food and water are available at his destination.

‘Uthmān applied the same ruling to the destination as to the route leading to it, requiring food and water to be unavailable there. Most of the learned Companions disagreed with him, and their position is stronger. The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, shortened his prayers in Mecca in the year of its conquest, although food and water were available there. Even if Minā is a village where food and water are available, there is open country between it and Mecca, and anyone crossing it is a traveller, just as when crossing the open country between Mecca and other places.

‘Uthmān, however, interpreted the Prophet’s shortening of prayer in Mecca as being due to fear: when he conquered Mecca, the disbelievers were numerous, and news had reached him that Hawāzin had mustered against him. ‘Uthmān permitted anyone in the presence of an enemy to shorten the prayer. This is similar to what is reported from ‘Uthmān, namely that he—meaning the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace—only instructed them to perform ‘umrah followed by Hajj because they were afraid.

‘Alī, ‘Imrān ibn Husayn, Ibn ‘Umar, Ibn ‘Abbās and other Companions disagreed with him, and their position is stronger. During the Farewell Pilgrimage, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, was secure, fearing none but Allah, yet he instructed his Companions to change their Hajj

---

## Page 12192

to ‘umrah and to shorten their prayers. Reducing the number of prayer units depends solely on travel. When fear and travel coincide, however, it is permissible both to reduce the number of prayer units and to shorten the units themselves.

The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and ‘Umar after him, said when praying in Mecca: “People of Mecca, complete your prayers, for we are travellers.” He made clear that their being travellers alone warranted their praying two units. Thus, he linked this ruling to travel, not to fear. This establishes that fear is never a prerequisite for reducing the number of prayer units.

The statements of the Companions, or most of them, on this subject indicate that they did not define travel by a fixed distance or duration applicable to everyone. Rather, they answered according to the questioner’s circumstances: whoever they considered a traveller, they accorded the rulings of travel; otherwise, they did not. This explains why their statements differed regarding duration and distance.

Wakī‘ narrated from al-Thawrī, from Mansūr ibn al-Mu‘tamir, from Mujāhid, from Ibn ‘Abbās, who said: “If you travel for a day until the night prayer, then shorten your prayers if you go beyond that.”

Al-Hajjāj ibn Minhāl also narrated it, saying: Abū ‘Awānah narrated to us from Mansūr ibn al-Mu‘tamir, from Mujāhid, from Ibn ‘Abbās, who said: “A traveller does not shorten his prayers on a journey lasting a day until the night prayer, but only on a journey longer than that.”

Wakī‘ narrated from Shu‘bah, from Shubayl, from Abū Jamrah al-Duba‘ī, who said: “I asked Ibn ‘Abbās, ‘May I shorten my prayers when travelling to al-Aylah?’ He asked, ‘Do you go and return within a day?’ I replied, ‘Yes.’ He said, ‘No, only for a full day’s journey.’”

Here, then, he prohibited shortening the prayer when someone returned to his family within a day—a journey of one postal stage—and permitted it for a day’s journey.

---

## Page 12193

In the first report, however, he forbade him to shorten his prayers unless the journey lasted more than a day. Something similar to the first report has also been narrated from his freedman ‘Ikrimah, who said: “When you leave your family, shorten your prayers; when you return to your family, perform them in full.” Al-Awzā‘ī is reported to have said: “Prayers may only be shortened on a full day’s journey.”

Wakī‘ narrated from Hishām ibn Rabī‘ah ibn al-Ghāz al-Jurashī, from ‘Atā’ ibn Abī Rabāh, who said: “I asked Ibn ‘Abbās, ‘May I shorten my prayers when travelling to ‘Arafah?’ He replied, ‘No, but you may when travelling to al-Tā’if or ‘Usfān, for that is forty-eight miles.’”

Ibn ‘Uyaynah narrated from ‘Amr ibn Dīnār, from ‘Atā’, who said: “I asked Ibn ‘Abbās, ‘May I shorten my prayers when travelling to Minā or ‘Arafah?’ He replied, ‘No, but you may when travelling to al-Tā’if, Jeddah or ‘Usfān. If you arrive where you have livestock or family, perform your prayers in full.’” Ahmad and al-Shāfi‘ī relied on this report.

Ibn Hazm said: “The journey from ‘Usfān to Mecca, as travelled by the Rightly Guided Caliphs, covers thirty-two miles.” He also said: “Trustworthy people have informed us that the distance from Jeddah to Mecca is forty miles.”

I say: His prohibition against shortening prayers when travelling to Minā and ‘Arafah may have concerned someone who went there on an errand and returned to Mecca the same day. This would reconcile it with the reports previously narrated from him. This is supported by the fact that Ibn ‘Abbās could not have been unaware that the people of Mecca used to shorten their prayers behind the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, Abū Bakr and ‘Umar during Hajj, when they went out to ‘Arafah, Muzdalifah and Minā. Ibn ‘Abbās was among the most knowledgeable people concerning the Sunnah, so something like this could not have escaped him. His Meccan companions used to shorten their prayers during Hajj

---

## Page 12194

when going to ‘Arafah and Muzdalifah, among them Tāwūs and others.

Ibn ‘Uyaynah himself, who narrated this report from Ibn ‘Abbās, used to shorten his prayers when going to ‘Arafah during Hajj. Companions of Ibn ‘Abbās, such as Tāwūs, would say: “Do you think that the people—meaning the people of Mecca—prayed during Hajj differently from the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace?” This is conclusive proof. It is well known that the people of Mecca who performed Hajj with him were very numerous. They went out with him to Minā and prayed behind him. Throughout the days spent at Minā, he led only shortened prayers, and everyone prayed behind him: the people of Mecca and all the other Muslims. He did not command any of them to perform their prayers in full, nor has anyone transmitted such a command, whether through an authentic or a weak chain of transmission.

After him, Abū Bakr and ‘Umar likewise led the people of Mecca and others in prayer during Hajj without commanding anyone to perform the prayers in full. Yet it is authentically reported that when ‘Umar ibn al-Khattāb led the prayer in Mecca, he said: “People of Mecca, perform your prayers in full, for we are travellers.” This is also reported from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, as addressed to the people of Mecca in the year of the conquest, not during the Farewell Hajj. During the Farewell Hajj, he did not pray in Mecca, but rather at his lodgings. Abū Dāwūd and others narrated this report, though its chain of transmission is open to criticism.

The point is that anyone who reflects on the Prophet’s leading the people of Mecca and others in prayer at ‘Arafah, Muzdalifah and Minā, and on the fact that no Muslim has ever reported that he commanded them to perform their prayers in full, will know with certainty that they shortened their prayers behind him. This is common knowledge that could not have escaped Ibn ‘Abbās or anyone else. Hence, no Companion is known to have instructed the people of Mecca to

---

## Page 12195

perform their prayers in full behind the imam when he prays two rak‘ahs. This indicates that Ibn ‘Abbās gave that answer only to someone asking about travelling to Minā or ‘Arafah without staying there, but returning the same day. In his view, such a person does not shorten his prayers, for he explained that someone who sets out and returns the same day does not shorten them; only someone who travels for a day does so. He did not say “a day’s journey”; rather, he took into account whether the travel occupied a day. Numerous reports from him establish that he permitted shortening prayers when travelling to ‘Usfān.

Ibn Hazm stated that it is thirty-two miles away, while others say four postal stages, or forty-eight miles. Those who fixed the distance at forty-eight miles rely on statements of Ibn ‘Abbās and Ibn ‘Umar, yet most reports from them contradict this. Even if their statements were the only evidence available, it would not be permissible to accept some of their statements and disregard others. Rather, they must either be reconciled or further evidence must be sought. How much more so when reports from the Companions convey various other positions?

Thus, those among the followers of Mālik, al-Shāfi‘ī and Ahmad who set the distance at sixteen parasangs rely on only two approaches. Some say: “I have found no one who permitted shortening prayers for a journey shorter than this; therefore, this constitutes a consensus.” This is al-Shāfi‘ī’s approach, and it is also reported from al-Layth ibn Sa‘d. These two imams explained their grounds: they knew of no one who advocated a shorter distance. Others, however, did know of people who advocated a shorter distance.

The second approach is to say: “This is the position of Ibn ‘Umar and Ibn ‘Abbās, and no Companion disagreed with them; it therefore constitutes a consensus.” This is false, for it has been reported from both of them

---

## Page 12196

this position as well as others. Moreover, positions contrary to it are reliably reported from other Companions.

There is a third approach, adopted by some followers of al-Shāfi‘ī and Ahmad: that this prescribed distance is reported from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. Thus, Ibn Khuzaymah relates in Mukhtasar al-Mukhtasar, from Ibn ‘Abbās, that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“People of Mecca, do not shorten your prayers for a journey of less than four postal stages, from Mecca to ‘Usfān.”

Those versed in hadith know that this is falsely attributed to the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace; rather, it is a statement of Ibn ‘Abbās. Do you suppose that the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, specified the distance for shortening prayers only for the people of Mecca, but not for the people of Medina—the home of the Sunnah, migration and support—or for the rest of the Muslims? How could he have said this when it is established through mass-transmitted reports that the people of Mecca prayed behind him at ‘Arafah, Muzdalifah and Minā? The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, never defined travel by any distance, whether one postal stage or otherwise, nor by any duration.

Mālik is reported to have specified four postal stages, like al-Layth, al-Shāfi‘ī and Ahmad. This is the position for which he is well known. He said: “If it is a region without milestones, they should not shorten their prayers for less than a day and a night’s journey with baggage.” He said: “This is my preferred measure for shortening the prayer.”

It has also been related from him that prayers may only be shortened for a journey of forty-five miles or more. Another report from him specifies forty-two miles or more, and another specifies forty miles or more. Ismā‘īl ibn Abī Uways narrated from him:

---

## Page 12197

“Prayers may only be shortened for a journey of forty-six ordinary miles.”

Judge Ismā‘īl ibn Ishāq mentioned these reports in his book al-Mabsūt. Mālik held that the people of Mecca specifically could shorten their prayers during Hajj specifically when going to Minā or beyond; Minā is four miles away. Ibn al-Qāsim narrated that Mālik said concerning someone, such as a shepherd or another person, who travelled three miles and, acting on an interpretation, broke his fast in Ramadan: “He is required only to make up the fast, and nothing else.” Al-Shāfi‘ī is reported to have held that prayers may not be shortened for a journey of less than forty-six Hāshimī miles.

The reports from Ibn ‘Umar vary.

Muhammad ibn al-Muthannā narrated: ‘Abd al-Rahmān ibn Mahdī related to us: Sufyān al-Thawrī related to us: I heard Jabalah ibn Suhaym say: I heard Ibn ‘Umar say: “If I travelled a mile, I would shorten the prayer.”

Ibn Abī Shaybah narrated: Wakī‘ related to us: Mis‘ar related to us, from Muhārib ibn Dithār: I heard Ibn ‘Umar say: “I travel for an hour of the day and shorten”—meaning the prayer. Muhārib, the judge of Kufa, was among the finest of the Successors and one of the leading scholars. Mis‘ar was also one of the leading scholars.

Ibn Abī Shaybah narrated: ‘Alī ibn Mus-hir related to us, from Abū Ishāq al-Shaybānī, from Muhammad ibn Zayd ibn Khulaydah, from Ibn ‘Umar, who said: “The prayer may be shortened for a journey of three miles.”

Ibn Hazm said: “Muhammad ibn Zayd is a man of the tribe of Tayyi’, whom Muhammad ibn Abī Tālib appointed judge in Kufa. He is well known and one of the senior Successors.”

Mālik narrated from Nāfi‘, from Ibn ‘Umar, that he shortened his prayers on a journey to Dhāt al-Nusub.

---

## Page 12198

He said: “I used to travel a postal stage with Ibn ‘Umar, and he would not shorten his prayers.” ‘Abd al-Razzāq said: “Dhāt al-Nusub is eighteen miles from Medina.” Thus Nāfi‘ reports that he shortened his prayers over six parasangs, yet would travel a postal stage—which is four parasangs—without shortening them.

A similar report about him is given in the account related by Ghundar: Shu‘bah related to us, from Habīb ibn ‘Abd al-Rahmān, from Hafs ibn ‘Āsim ibn ‘Umar ibn al-Khattāb, who said: “I went with ‘Abd Allāh ibn ‘Umar ibn al-Khattāb to Dhāt al-Nusub, eighteen miles from Medina. When he reached it, he shortened his prayers.” Ma‘mar narrated from Ayyūb, from Nāfi‘, from Ibn ‘Umar, that he used to shorten his prayers on a journey of four postal stages.

The preceding reports indicate that he shortened his prayers over this distance and shorter distances. Wakī‘ narrated from Sa‘īd ibn ‘Ubayd al-Tā’ī, from ‘Alī ibn Rabī‘ah al-Wālibī al-Asadī, who said:

“I asked Ibn ‘Umar about shortening the prayer. He said: ‘On Hajj, ‘umrah, or a military expedition?’

“I replied: ‘No, but one of us may have an estate in the Sawād.’

“He said: ‘Do you know al-Suwaydā’?’

“I replied: ‘I have heard of it, but have not seen it.’

“He said: ‘It is three nights away, or two—or one for a swift traveller. When we go there, we shorten our prayers.’”

Ibn Hazm said: “From Medina to al-Suwaydā’ is seventy-two miles, or twenty-four parasangs.”

I say: Taken together with the preceding reports, this shows that Ibn ‘Umar did not mention that distance to establish a fixed limit;

---

## Page 12199

rather, he was showing that shortening the prayer was permissible in such circumstances. For he had heard that the people of Kufa did not shorten their prayers in the Sawād, and Ibn ‘Umar answered him by affirming its permissibility.

As for the report transmitted through Ibn Jurayj:

“Nāfi‘ informed me that the nearest destination to which Ibn ‘Umar travelled and shortened his prayers was an estate he owned at Khaybar, a journey of three moderate stages. He did not shorten his prayers over any shorter distance.”

Likewise, Hammād ibn Salamah narrated from Ayyūb ibn Humayd—both of them from Nāfi‘, from Ibn ‘Umar:

“He used to shorten his prayers between Medina and Khaybar, a distance comparable to that between Basra and al-Ahwāz. He did not shorten them over any shorter distance.”

Ibn Hazm said: “The distance between Medina and Khaybar is like that between Basra and al-Ahwāz: ninety-six miles.”

He said: “This is among the matters concerning which conflicting reports have been transmitted from Ibn ‘Umar, and also from Nāfi‘ reporting from Ibn ‘Umar.”

I say: This denial—that he did not shorten his prayers over any shorter distance—is unquestionably mistaken. This is not a report of a statement he made, such that one might say his considered judgment had changed. Rather, it denies that he shortened his prayers over any shorter distance.

(Yet authentic reports through Nāfi‘ and others establish that he did shorten his prayers over shorter distances. Thus, this may be an error. As for whoever narrated from Ayyūb—if it is assumed that Nāfi‘ narrated this, then, when relating it, he may have forgotten that Ibn ‘Umar shortened his prayers over shorter distances. For it is established through Nāfi‘ that he did so.)(*)

> **Footnotes:**
> (*) Shaykh Nāsir ibn Hamad al-Fahd stated (p. 199):
> It appears to me that the passage contains a scribal error. Perhaps the correct reading—and Allah knows best—is:
> “... This may be an error by someone who narrated from Ayyūb. If it is assumed that Nāfi‘ narrated this, then he had forgotten at the time he related it ...”

---

## Page 12200

Hammād ibn Zayd narrated:

“Anas ibn Sīrīn narrated to us, saying: ‘I went with Anas ibn Mālik to his estate, which was five parasangs away. He led us in the afternoon prayer aboard a boat carrying us along the Tigris. Seated on a mat, he prayed two rak‘ahs and then pronounced the salutation. He then led us in another two rak‘ahs and pronounced the salutation.’”

This shows that he went only to the estate mentioned; he was not travelling elsewhere, such that one could say it lay along his route. Thus, he shortened his prayers over a distance of five parasangs, equivalent to one and a quarter barīds.

In Sahīh Muslim, it is related:

“Ibn Abī Shaybah and Ibn Bashshār narrated to us, both from Ghundar, from Shu‘bah, from Yahyā ibn Yazīd al-Hunā’ī, who said: ‘I asked Anas ibn Mālik about shortening the prayer. He replied: “When the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, travelled a distance of three miles or three parasangs—Shu‘bah was uncertain—he would pray two rak‘ahs.”’”

Anas did not understand this as covering that distance within a longer journey, because the questioner asked him about shortening the prayer: his question concerned the distance over which prayers may be shortened, not the first prayer one shortens.

Moreover, no one has said that the first prayer may only be shortened after travelling three miles or more. Thus, if that were what was intended, this would not answer the question; nor has anyone maintained such a view. This indicates that Anas meant that anyone travelling this distance may shorten his prayers.

Furthermore, what he related about the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, describes an action of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. He did not clarify whether that distance constituted the journey itself or was the distance he had covered during the journey. For if

---

## Page 12201

he meant that this was his entire journey, then it is explicit evidence. If, however, this was merely the distance he had covered during a journey, Anas ibn Mālik inferred from it that prayers may be shortened on a journey of that length. His reasoning was that prayers are shortened only when travelling; had covering this distance not constituted travel, the Prophet would not have shortened his prayers.

This accords with the view that one may not shorten one’s prayers until one has actually covered a distance that constitutes travel: merely intending to cover such a distance is insufficient. This is the position of Ibn Hazm, Dāwūd and his followers. Ibn Hazm sets the minimum distance for shortening prayers at one mile. However, Dāwūd and his followers maintain that prayers may be shortened only when travelling for Hajj, ‘umrah or a military expedition, whereas Ibn Hazm holds that they may be shortened on any journey.

Ibn Hazm also holds that one may not break the fast until one has covered this distance. His fellow adherents maintain that one may break the fast on any journey, unlike shortening prayers. This is because, in their view, there is no general textual ruling from the Lawgiver concerning shortening prayers, but only the Prophet’s practice of shortening them while travelling. They found no report of anyone shortening prayers over a distance of less than one mile, whereas a distance of one mile was reported from Ibn ‘Umar.

Ibn Hazm says that travel means going beyond one’s place of residence. Yet it is known that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, went out to al-Baqī‘ to bury the dead and went out into the open to relieve himself, with people accompanying him; they neither shortened their prayers nor broke their fasts. Such outings were therefore excluded from being considered travel. They found no distance of less than one mile described as travel, for Ibn ‘Umar said:

“If I went out a mile, I would shorten my prayers.”

Thus, once it was established that this distance

---

## Page 12202

was regarded as travel, and we found no greater distance described as travel, we took this as the threshold. He said: “Anyone less than one mile beyond the last houses of his village is subject to the rulings for a non-traveller: he neither shortens his prayers nor breaks his fast. Once he has covered a mile, his journey qualifies him to shorten his prayers and break his fast, and he may do so from that point onward. Likewise, when returning, once he is less than a mile away, he must perform his prayers in full, since he is not on a journey that permits shortening them.”

I say:

These scholars held that travel has a defined limit in the language. They said: “The shortest distance we have heard described as travel is one mile.” The others held that its limit is defined by the revealed law. Both views are weak. The Lawgiver has set no such limit. Nor has anyone reported that the authorities on language said: “What distinguishes travel from something not called travel is a specified distance.” Indeed, defining travel by distance is itself unfounded in both the revealed law and the language.

Moreover, even if travel were defined by a distance of one mile, if this means a mile measured from the boundaries of one’s own village, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, used to go more than a mile from his place of residence in the Hijāz without shortening his prayers or breaking his fast. If, instead, he means a mile from the whole settled area encompassed by the name of a town, he may be told: “Then his going out to the burial grounds and to relieve himself provides no evidence for your position, because those places were not beyond the outermost boundary of Medina.”

In short, he used to go out to al-‘Awālī and Uhud, just as he went out to the burial grounds and to relieve himself, and some of those places were more than a mile away. The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and his Companions used to go more than a mile outside Medina and come into it from places more than a mile away without shortening their prayers

---

## Page 12203

—as when they went out to Qubā’, al-‘Awālī and Uhud, or came in from these places for Friday prayer and other purposes.

Many of Medina’s dwellings were more than a mile from his mosque, for the sanctuary of Medina measures one barīd (a unit of distance) by one barīd. Because of the distance, two of his Companions would take turns coming in, one on one day and the other on the next. Thus, ‘Umar ibn al-Khattāb and his Ansārī companion would each come in on alternate days.

Ibn ‘Umar’s statement:

“If I went out a mile, I would shorten my prayers,”

is like his statement:

“I travel for a short part of the day and shorten my prayers.”

By this, he may mean the portion he has covered of his intended journey. His meaning would then be: “I do not delay shortening my prayers until I have covered a long distance.” This is the position of the vast majority of scholars, apart from those who say that someone who sets out during the day may not shorten his prayers until nightfall. Against them, the scholars have cited the fact that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, prayed four rak‘ahs for the noon prayer in Medina and two rak‘ahs for the afternoon prayer at Dhū al-Hulayfah. Anas’s report could also be understood in this sense, but his own practice points to the first interpretation.

Alternatively, Ibn ‘Umar may mean that whoever travels may shorten his prayers, even if his intended journey is only this distance, provided that he is in open country and thus travelling, rather than merely moving between dwellings. Everyone agrees that someone doing the latter is not a traveller. If such a person is deemed a traveller, then, supposing he travelled ten cubits less than a mile, he would still be a traveller. Thus, defining travel by distance

---

## Page 12204

has no basis in revealed law, language, custom or reason. People generally do not know the measured distances across the land, so something needed by Muslims generally must not be made dependent on something they do not know. No one surveyed the land during the lifetime of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, nor did the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, determine its distances in miles or parasangs.

A man may leave his village for the open country to gather firewood and remain away for two or three days, thus being a traveller even if the distance is less than a mile. This differs from someone who goes out and returns the same day, who is not thereby a traveller: the former takes food and water with him, unlike the latter. Thus, covering a short distance over a long period constitutes travel, whereas covering a long distance over a short period does not.

Travel, then, is determined by the activity that gives it that name. An activity necessarily takes time. When the activity and its duration are prolonged, so that a person needs the food and water that a traveller requires, he is called a traveller even if the distance is not great.
