When the activity and its duration are so brief that he needs neither food nor water, it is not called travel, even if the distance is great.

The fundamental consideration, therefore, is the activity called travel, and an activity necessarily takes time. What must be considered is the activity that constitutes travel, and this can only take place out in the open, away from settled places. This is something people recognize through their customary practices. It has no fixed definition in revealed law or language; rather, whatever they call travel is travel.

---

## Page 12205

Section:

As for “residence,” it is the opposite of travel. People fall into two categories: residents and travellers. Accordingly, the rulings governing people in the Quran and Sunnah fall into one of these two categories: those applicable to residents and those applicable to travellers. Allah Most High says:

“On the day you travel and on the day you stay.”

Thus, He distinguishes between a day of travel and a day of residence. Allah Most High has made fasting obligatory and said:

“But whoever among you is ill or on a journey, then an equal number of other days.”

Whoever is neither ill nor travelling is therefore a healthy resident. Hence the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Allah has relieved the traveller of fasting and half the prayer.”

Whoever has not been relieved of fasting and half the prayer is therefore a resident.

During his Hajj, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, stayed in Mecca for four days, then spent six days at Minā, Muzdalifah and Arafāt, shortening the prayers along with his Companions. This indicates that they were travellers. During the campaign for the conquest of Mecca, he stayed for nineteen days, shortening the prayers, and he stayed at Tabūk for twenty days, shortening the prayers.

It is clear from ordinary experience that what he was doing in Mecca and Tabūk could not have been completed in three or four days, such that one might say:

---

## Page 12206

he kept saying, “I shall leave today; I shall leave tomorrow.” Rather, he conquered Mecca while its inhabitants and those of the surrounding area were unbelievers at war with him. It was the greatest city he conquered; through its conquest, his enemies were humbled and the Arabs embraced Islam. He dispatched detachments to the surrounding regions and awaited their return. Such matters clearly could not be concluded within four days. Thus, he stayed to attend to matters which he knew could not be concluded within four days. The same applies to his stay at Tabūk.

Furthermore, whoever sets a limit on residence—whether three, four, ten, twelve or fifteen days—has advanced a position unsupported by any evidence from Islamic law. These are competing numerical limits.

These positions entail dividing people into three categories: a traveller; a settled resident, meaning someone who intends to remain in a place, whose presence counts towards constituting a valid Friday congregation and for whom Friday prayer is obligatory; and a third category, a resident who is not settled. The settled resident must, without dispute, pray in full, for he is the resident who stands in contrast to the traveller.

As for the third category, the resident who is not settled, they require him to pray in full and fast, and they also require him to attend Friday prayer. Yet they say, “His presence does not count towards constituting a valid Friday congregation,” and, “Only settled residents count towards constituting a valid Friday congregation.”

This division—that is, dividing residents into settled and non-settled residents—has no basis in evidence from Islamic law. Nor is there any evidence that Friday prayer is obligatory for someone whose presence does not count towards constituting a valid congregation for it. Rather, anyone for whom it is obligatory counts towards constituting it. They said this only because they posited a resident who must pray in full and fast, yet found that he was not settled, so they could not

---

## Page 12207

say that his presence counts towards constituting a valid Friday congregation, for only settled residents count towards constituting it. Yet requiring such a person to attend Friday prayer, fast and pray in full is precisely what lacks evidence. Indeed, it is contrary to Islamic law, for this was the situation of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, in Mecca during the conquest and the Farewell Pilgrimage, and at Tabūk. Indeed, this is the situation of all pilgrims who come to Mecca to perform their rites and then return home. A person may arrive in Mecca on the fourth of Dhū al-Hijjah, a day or several days earlier, or later. They are all travellers, obliged neither to attend Friday prayer nor to pray in full. The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, arrived on the morning of the fourth of Dhū al-Hijjah and prayed two rakʿahs. But what grounds do they have for claiming that, had he arrived on the morning of the third or second, he would have prayed in full and ordered his Companions to do likewise? Nothing in his words or actions indicates this.

Had this been a dividing line between residents and travellers, he would have explained it to the Muslims, as Allah Most High says:

“Allah would not let a people go astray after He has guided them until He has made clear to them what they must avoid.”

Distinguishing between a resident and a traveller on the basis of an intention to stay a specified number of days has no recognized basis in Islamic law, language or custom. The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, permitted an Emigrant to remain in Mecca for three days after completing his pilgrimage rites. Shortening the prayers during this time is permissible according to the scholars. Yet he called this residence and permitted the Emigrant to remain for that period. If the Emigrant wished to remain longer after completing the rites, he would not be

---

## Page 12208

permitted to do so. Nothing in this indicates that this period marks the distinction between a traveller and a resident. Rather, an Emigrant is prohibited from remaining in Mecca for more than three days after completing the pilgrimage rites. Thus, three days constitute a period for which a concession is granted in matters otherwise prohibited in principle. The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“It is not lawful for a woman who believes in Allah and the Last Day to mourn a deceased person for more than three days, except for her husband.”

He also said:

“It is not lawful for a Muslim to shun his brother for more than three days.”

The number of divorces after which a woman becomes forbidden to her husband was likewise set at three. Once he has divorced her three times, she becomes forbidden to him until she marries another husband. This is because divorce is in principle disliked, so as much of it as need requires was permitted. Beyond that, she becomes forbidden to him until the stated condition is fulfilled.

Furthermore, if an Emigrant arrived in Mecca a month before the pilgrimage season, he could remain until that season. If he was permitted only a stay that counted as travel, then his stay until the pilgrimage season would count as travel, during which prayers would be shortened.

Moreover, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and his Companions arrived on the morning of the fourth of Dhū al-Hijjah. Had they remained in Mecca for three days after completing the pilgrimage rites, they would have been permitted to do so. Remaining for more than three days, however, would not have been permissible for them, although others were permitted to remain longer. The Emigrants stayed in Mecca with the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, for nearly twenty days in the year of the conquest. Their stay did not make them residents who no longer counted as travellers, nor was their stay prohibited, because they were staying in order to complete

---

## Page 12209

the jihad campaign, and they left Mecca for the Battle of Hunayn. This differs from the case of someone who comes only to perform the pilgrimage rites, for he has no need to remain for more than three days. It is thus clear that this limit has no bearing on shortening prayers or on defining the duration of travel.

Among those who set the limit at four days, some cited the permitted stay of an Emigrant, excluding the days of arrival and departure from the count. Others based it on the premise that anyone arriving in a town is, in principle, a resident who must pray in full. However, an exception allowing four days was established by the Prophet’s stay during his pilgrimage, when he remained for that period and shortened his prayers. Regarding the campaigns of the conquest and Tabūk, they said that he had not resolved to stay for a specified period, since, in the year of the conquest, he intended to proceed to fight at Hunayn.

This argument rests on the premise that anyone arriving in a town ceases to count as a traveller. That premise is not conceded; rather, it contradicts the revealed texts, scholarly consensus, and customary usage. A merchant who arrives to buy or sell goods and then leaves is regarded by people as a traveller. He may spend several days buying and selling the goods, and people set no fixed limit for this.

Those who said that one may shorten prayers for up to fifteen days argued: “This is the longest period that has been proposed; anyone staying longer is a resident by consensus.” Yet the matter is not as they claimed. Ahmad instructed that prayers be performed in full for a stay exceeding four days as a precaution. Reports from him differ as to whether someone intending a stay encompassing twenty-one prayers should pray in full or shorten them. This reflects differing judgments concerning the Prophet’s prayer on the fourth, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. For if he prayed Fajr at the place where he had spent the night, which was

---

## Page 12210

Dhū Tuwā, then he prayed only twenty prayers in Mecca; but if he prayed Fajr in Mecca, he prayed twenty-one prayers there. The correct view is that he prayed Fajr that day at Dhū Tuwā and entered Mecca in the forenoon, as explicitly stated in hadiths.

In the report transmitted by al-Athram, Ahmad said: “If he resolves to stay longer than that, he must pray in full.” He cited as evidence that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, arrived on the morning of the fourth, saying:

“He stayed on the fourth, fifth, sixth and seventh, and prayed Fajr at al-Abtah on the eighth. He shortened his prayers during those days, having already resolved to stay for that period. Thus, if someone resolves to stay for as long as the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, stayed, he may shorten his prayers; but if he resolves to stay longer, he must pray in full.”

Al-Athram said: “I asked him, ‘Why, then, should he not shorten his prayers when staying longer than that?’ He replied, ‘Because the scholars differed, so he takes the more cautious course and prays in full.’”

He also said: “Abū ‘Abd Allāh was asked, ‘If a man keeps saying, “I shall leave today; I shall leave tomorrow,” may he shorten his prayers?’ He replied, ‘That is a different matter: this man has not made a firm decision.’”

Thus, Ahmad did not mention any evidence that praying in full is obligatory; he merely adopted the precautionary course, and this does not establish an obligation. Moreover, there is the opposing view of those who hold that shortening prayers is obligatory and remains a binding duty even beyond that period.

Al-Athram also narrated: Al-Fadl ibn Dukayn narrated to us, saying: Mis‘ar narrated to us, from Habīb ibn Abī Thābit, from ‘Abd al-Rahmān ibn al-Miswar, who said:

“We stayed with Sa‘d in Oman—or Amman—for two months. He would pray two rak‘ahs while we prayed four. We mentioned this to him, and he said:

---

## Page 12211

‘We know better.’”

Al-Athram said: Sulaymān ibn Harb narrated to us, saying: Hammād narrated to us, from Ayyūb, from Nāfi‘, that Ibn ‘Umar stayed in Azerbaijan for six months, praying two rak‘ahs, as snow had prevented him from entering.

Some scholars said: “Snow occurring during such a period is known not to melt within four days; thus, he had resolved to stay for more than four days.”

Al-Athram said: Muslim ibn Ibrāhīm narrated to us, saying: Hishām narrated to us, saying: Yahyā narrated to us, from Hafs ibn ‘Ubayd Allāh, that Anas ibn Mālik stayed in Syria for two years, shortening his prayers.

Al-Athram said: Al-Fadl ibn Dukayn narrated to us, saying: Hishām narrated to us, saying: Ibn Shihāb narrated to us, from Sālim, who said: “When Ibn ‘Umar stayed in Mecca, he would shorten his prayers unless he prayed with the imam, even if he stayed for two months—unless he firmly resolved to reside there.”

Ibn ‘Umar would arrive long before the Hajj season; sometimes he would even enter ihrām for Hajj at the beginning of Dhū al-Hijjah. He was one of the Emigrants, so he was not permitted to remain for more than three days after completing his pilgrimage rites. Hence, he left instructions that, upon his death, he should be buried at Sarif, since it lies outside the Sacred Precinct, so that he would not be buried in the land from which he had emigrated.

Al-Athram said: Sulaymān ibn Harb narrated to us, saying: Hammād ibn Zayd narrated to us, from Ayyūb, from Nāfi‘, who said: “Ibn ‘Umar would pray only two rak‘ahs in Mecca, except when he would yarfa‘ al-maqām.” Thus, on one occasion he stayed for twelve [days], praying two rak‘ahs while intending to leave. This shows that he would pray two rak‘ahs before the Hajj season, although he intended to remain until the season began. Ibn ‘Umar performed Hajj frequently and would often come to Mecca long before the Hajj season.

Al-Athram said: Ibn

---

## Page 12212

al-Tabbā‘ narrated to us, saying: Al-Qāsim ibn Mūsā al-Faqīr narrated to us, from ‘Abd al-Rahmān ibn Thābit ibn Thawbān, from his father, from Makhūl, from Ibn Muhayrīz:

Abū Ayyūb al-Ansārī, Abū Sirmah al-Ansārī, and ‘Uqbah ibn ‘Āmir spent the winter in Byzantine territory. They fasted Ramadan, observed its night prayers, and prayed the prayers in full.

Al-Athram said: Qabīsah narrated to us, saying: Sufyān narrated to us, from Mansūr, from Abū Wā’il, who said: Masrūq went to al-Silsilah and shortened his prayers. He remained there for years, shortening his prayers, and continued doing so until he returned.

He was asked, “Abū ‘Ā’ishah, what makes you do this?”

He replied, “Following the Sunnah.”

Section

Those who did not consider it disliked for a traveller to pray four rak‘ahs believed that the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, had done so, or that some of his Companions had done so during his lifetime and he had approved it. They believed that a traveller’s praying either two or four rak‘ahs was comparable to fasting or not fasting during Ramadan. Numerous authentic hadiths report that they would travel with the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, some fasting and others not fasting. The scholars unanimously agree that these reports are authentic. As for the reports they cited about praying four rak‘ahs, some scholars considered them authentic, and al-Shāfi‘ī and some of Ahmad’s followers relied on them as evidence.

After mentioning the Prophet’s words, peace and blessings be upon him,

“It is a charity Allah has bestowed upon you, so accept His charity,”

al-Shāfi‘ī said: This indicates that shortening prayers while travelling without fear is a charity from Allah, and charity

---

## Page 12213

is a concession, not an obligation from Allah to shorten the prayer. That a traveller may shorten his prayers while travelling without fear, if he wishes, is indicated by ‘Ā’ishah’s statement:

“The Messenger of Allah, peace and blessings be upon him, did both: he prayed in full while travelling and shortened his prayers.”

I say: Al-Dāraqutnī and others narrated this hadith through Abū ‘Āsim, who said: ‘Umar ibn Sa‘īd narrated to us, from ‘Atā’ ibn Abī Rabāh, from ‘Ā’ishah:

“The Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, would shorten his prayers while travelling and pray them in full, refrain from fasting and fast.”

Al-Dāraqutnī said: This chain of transmission is authentic.

Al-Bayhaqī said: There are corroborating reports for this through Dalham ibn Sālih, al-Mughīrah ibn Ziyād, and Talhah ibn ‘Umar, all of whom are weak narrators.

He narrated Dalham’s report through ‘Ubayd Allāh ibn Mūsā, who said: Dalham ibn Sālih al-Kindī narrated to us, from ‘Atā’, from ‘Ā’ishah, who said:

“When we set out for Mecca, we would pray four rak‘ahs with the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, until we returned.”

He also narrated al-Mughīrah’s report—the best known of these—from ‘Atā’, from ‘Ā’ishah:

“The Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, would shorten his prayers while travelling and pray them in full.”

He narrated Talhah ibn ‘Umar’s report from ‘Atā’, from ‘Ā’ishah, who said:

“The Messenger of Allah, peace and blessings be upon him, did all of these: he prayed in full and shortened his prayers, and he fasted while travelling and refrained from fasting.”

Al-Bayhaqī said: ‘Umar ibn Dharr, a reliable Kufan narrator, said: ‘Atā’ ibn Abī Rabāh informed us that ‘Ā’ishah used to pray the obligatory prayers as four rak‘ahs while travelling. He narrated this with his chain of transmission, then said: “This appears to agree with Dalham ibn Sālih’s report, although

---

## Page 12214

Dalham’s report contains an additional link in its chain of transmission.”

I say: As for the reliable narrator’s report from ‘Atā’, from ‘Ā’ishah, that “she used to pray four rak‘ahs,” this is firmly established from her and well known through the reports of ‘Urwah and others from her. If only these weak narrators traced it back to the Prophet, while the reliable narrators attributed it only to ‘Ā’ishah, this indicates the weakness of the narrator who traced it back to the Prophet; it does not corroborate the version attributed to him.

Ibn Hazm said concerning this hadith: “Al-Mughīrah ibn Ziyād alone narrated it; no one else transmitted it. Ahmad ibn Hanbal said of him: ‘He is weak; every hadith he traces back to the Prophet is rejected.’”

I say: It has indeed been narrated through other routes, but these too are weak. ‘Abd Allāh ibn Ahmad ibn Hanbal mentioned that his father was asked about this hadith and said: “This is a rejected hadith.” It is just as Imam Ahmad said, although some of his followers cited it as evidence, agreeing with others who did so, such as al-Shāfi‘ī. There is no doubt that this hadith is falsely attributed to the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him. Some people, however, say that its wording is:

“He would shorten his prayers while travelling, while she would pray them in full; he would refrain from fasting, while she would fast.”

This means that she was the one who prayed in full and fasted. This is closer to what has been reported from her through other routes, although this too is falsely attributed to her.

Al-Bayhaqī said: “It has strong corroboration with an authentic chain of transmission.” It was narrated through al-Dāraqutnī, through Muhammad ibn Yūsuf, who said: Al-‘Alā’ ibn Zuhayr narrated to us, from ‘Abd al-Rahmān ibn al-Aswad, from his father, from ‘Ā’ishah, who said:

“I went out with the Messenger of Allah, peace and blessings be upon him, in

---

## Page 12215

Ramadan. The Messenger of Allah, peace and blessings be upon him, refrained from fasting while I fasted, and he shortened his prayers while I prayed them in full. I said, ‘O Messenger of Allah, may my father and mother be sacrificed for you! You refrained from fasting while I fasted, and you shortened your prayers while I prayed them in full.’ He said, ‘You have done well, ‘Ā’ishah.’”

Al-Bayhaqī also narrated it through another route, from al-Qāsim ibn al-Hakam, who said: Al-‘Alā’ ibn Zuhayr narrated to us, from ‘Abd al-Rahmān ibn al-Aswad, from ‘Ā’ishah, without mentioning his father. Al-Dāraqutnī said: “The first chain is connected and is a sound chain of transmission. ‘Abd al-Rahmān lived during ‘Ā’ishah’s lifetime and visited her when he was approaching puberty.”

Al-Bayhaqī narrated it through a third route, from Abū Bakr al-Naysābūrī, who said: ‘Abbās al-Dūrī narrated to us: Abū Nu‘aym narrated to us: Al-‘Alā’ ibn Zuhayr narrated to us: ‘Abd al-Rahmān ibn al-Aswad narrated to us, from ‘Ā’ishah, who reported that she travelled with the Messenger of Allah, peace and blessings be upon him, from Medina to Mecca to perform ‘umrah. Upon arriving, she said: “O Messenger of Allah, may my father and mother be sacrificed for you! You shortened your prayers while I prayed them in full, and you refrained from fasting while I fasted.” He replied, “You have done well, ‘Ā’ishah.” She said, “He did not reproach me.”

Abū Bakr al-Naysābūrī said: “This is how Abū Nu‘aym narrated it: from ‘Abd al-Rahmān, from ‘Ā’ishah. Whoever included ‘from his father’ in this hadith was mistaken.”

I say: Abū Bakr al-Naysābūrī was a leading authority in jurisprudence and hadith. He devoted particular attention to hadiths concerning jurisprudence and their variant wordings. Like the renowned imams of hadith, he was closer to the approach of the scholars of hadith and religious learning, which is free of partisan attachment to any jurist’s opinion. For this reason, he preferred this route of transmission. Similarly, among the compilers of

---

## Page 12216

the well-known Sunan collections, none narrated it except al-Nasā’ī. His wording reports from ‘Ā’ishah that she travelled with the Messenger of Allah, peace and blessings be upon him, from Medina to Mecca to perform ‘umrah. Upon arriving, she said: “O Messenger of Allah, may my father and mother be sacrificed for you! You shortened your prayers while I prayed them in full, and you refrained from fasting while I fasted.” He replied, “You have done well, ‘Ā’ishah.” She added, “He did not reproach me.”

This contrasts with someone who sets out to defend a particular person’s opinion and advances evidence in its support that, were he free of that purpose, he would not strain to adduce and would instead judge invalid.

The correct view is what Abū Bakr stated: this hadith does not have a connected chain. ‘Abd al-Rahmān only visited ‘Ā’ishah when he was a boy and did not accurately retain what she said. Abū Muhammad ibn Hazm said of it:

“This hadith was narrated solely by al-‘Alā’ ibn Zuhayr al-Azdī; no one else narrated it. He is an unknown narrator, and this hadith is unquestionably erroneous.”

For it states that she set out with the Messenger of Allah, peace and blessings be upon him, to perform ‘umrah in Ramadan. Yet it is well known, by agreement among the scholars, that the Messenger of Allah, peace and blessings be upon him, never performed ‘umrah in Ramadan, nor did he leave Medina to perform ‘umrah in Ramadan. Indeed, he never even set out for Mecca in Ramadan except in the year of the conquest. On that occasion, he was travelling during Ramadan and conquered Mecca in the month of Ramadan in the year 8 AH, by agreement among the scholars. During that journey, some of his Companions fasted and others did not, but he only ever led them in prayers of two rak‘ahs. None of his Companions reported that he prayed four rak‘ahs while travelling. The aforementioned hadith is erroneous, as we shall explain, if Allah Most High wills.

---

## Page 12217

In the year of the conquest of Mecca, he did not perform ‘umrah. Rather, abundant reports, accepted by those knowledgeable about his life, establish that he performed only four ‘umrahs after the Hijrah: three in Dhū al-Qa‘dah and the fourth together with his Hajj.

The first was the ‘umrah of al-Hudaybiyah, when the polytheists prevented him from proceeding. He therefore left the state of ritual consecration at al-Hudaybiyah because he had been prevented from continuing, without entering Mecca. This was in Dhū al-Qa‘dah. The following year, he performed the ‘umrah of al-Qadiyyah, also in Dhū al-Qa‘dah. Then, after distributing the spoils of Hunayn at al-Ji‘rānah, he performed ‘umrah from al-Ji‘rānah, likewise in Dhū al-Qa‘dah. The fourth was together with his Hajj. Neither he nor anyone who performed Hajj with him performed ‘umrah after that Hajj, except ‘Ā’ishah. She had begun menstruating, so he instructed her to enter the state of consecration for Hajj. Afterwards, he sent her with her brother ‘Abd al-Rahmān to perform ‘umrah from al-Tan‘īm. For this reason, the mosques built there were called the Mosques of ‘Ā’ishah. During the Prophet’s lifetime, peace and blessings be upon him, none of the Companions undertook an ‘umrah from Mecca, either before or after the conquest, except ‘Ā’ishah.

All of this is established by authentic hadiths transmitted through so many chains as to constitute mass transmission. Among them is the report in the two Sahīh collections from Anas:

“The Messenger of Allah, peace and blessings be upon him, performed four ‘umrahs, all in Dhū al-Qa‘dah except the one accompanying his Hajj: an ‘umrah from al-Hudaybiyah in Dhū al-Qa‘dah; an ‘umrah the following year in Dhū al-Qa‘dah; an ‘umrah from al-Ji‘rānah in Dhū al-Qa‘dah, where he distributed the spoils of Hunayn; and an ‘umrah together with his Hajj.”

This is Muslim’s wording. Al-Bukhārī’s wording is:

“He performed four ‘umrahs: the ‘umrah of al-Hudaybiyah in Dhū al-Qa‘dah, when the polytheists prevented him from proceeding; and an ‘umrah the following year in

---

## Page 12218

Dhū al-Qa‘dah, when he concluded a treaty with them; the ‘umrah of Hunayn from al-Ji‘rānah, where he distributed the spoils of Hunayn; and an ‘umrah together with his Hajj.”

In the two Sahīh collections, al-Barā’ ibn ‘Āzib said:

“The Messenger of Allah, peace and blessings be upon him, performed ‘umrah twice in Dhū al-Qa‘dah before performing Hajj.”

This is al-Bukhārī’s wording. He meant the ‘umrahs that he completed, namely those of al-Qadiyyah and al-Ji‘rānah. As for the ‘umrah of al-Hudaybiyah, he was unable to complete it because the polytheists prevented him from proceeding. On that occasion, Allah revealed the verse concerning being prevented from completing the pilgrimage rites, as the scholars unanimously agree.

It is also established in an authentic report that when ‘Ā’ishah was told, “Ibn ‘Umar said that the Messenger of Allah, peace and blessings be upon him, performed ‘umrah in Rajab,” she said:

“May Allah forgive Abū ‘Abd al-Rahmān! The Messenger of Allah, peace and blessings be upon him, never performed ‘umrah without Ibn ‘Umar accompanying him, and he never performed ‘umrah in Rajab.”

In another report, ‘Ā’ishah said:

“The Messenger of Allah, peace and blessings be upon him, performed ‘umrah only in Dhū al-Qa‘dah.”

The same was reported from Ibn ‘Abbās. Ibn Mājah narrated both reports.

Abū Dāwūd narrated that she said:

“The Messenger of Allah, peace and blessings be upon him, performed two ‘umrahs: one in Dhū al-Qa‘dah and one in Shawwāl.”

If this report from her is authentic, perhaps he began his journey in Shawwāl. She never said that he performed ‘umrah in Ramadan; thus, that claim is known to be an outright error.

If it is established through authentic hadiths that he performed ‘umrah only in Dhū al-Qa‘dah, and it is also established that he travelled from Medina to Mecca and entered it only three

---

## Page 12219

times: for the ‘umrah of al-Qadiyyah, then the campaign of the Conquest, and then the Farewell Hajj—and this is undisputed among scholars of hadith, the Prophet’s biography, and the circumstances of the Messenger of Allah, peace and blessings be upon him—and he travelled to Mecca in Ramadan only for the campaign of the Conquest, then each of these two facts is conclusive evidence that this hadith, which states that she performed ‘umrah with him in Ramadan and said, “I performed the prayers in full and fasted,” to which he replied, “You have done well,” is entirely erroneous.

(It is therefore known with certainty to be false. Anyone aware of its status may not narrate it from the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, because he said: “Whoever narrates a hadith from me, believing it to be a lie, is one of the liars.” However, those scholars who narrated it and do not regard such conduct as permissible did not know that it was a lie [and incurred no sin]) (*).

If it is said, “Then the words ‘in Ramadan’ are erroneous, while the rest of the hadith could be true,” the response is: Rather, all its chains of transmission indicate that this occurred in Ramadan, for she said: “I said, ‘You broke your fast while I fasted, and you shortened your prayers while I performed them in full.’ He replied, ‘You have done well, O ‘Ā’ishah.’” Such a statement would only be made concerning obligatory fasting. As for travel outside Ramadan, nothing like this would be mentioned, since it is well known that not fasting then is permissible.

Furthermore, al-Bayhaqī and others narrated through an established chain of transmission from al-Sha‘bī, from ‘Ā’ishah, who said:

“The prayers were prescribed as two rak‘ahs each, except Maghrib, which was prescribed as three. Whenever the Messenger of Allah, peace and blessings be upon him, travelled, he would perform the prayer

> **Footnotes:**
> (*) Shaykh Nāsir ibn Hamad al-Fahd said (p. 199):
> The words in square brackets are an addition by the compiler, may Allah have mercy on him, and make no sense here. The shaykh means that the upright scholars who transmitted this false hadith did not know that it was a lie. His statement thus describes their state of knowledge regarding it, not the ruling applicable to them, as the context indicates (n). Allah knows best.

---

## Page 12220

as originally prescribed. When he was resident, he would add two rak‘ahs to each pair, except for Maghrib, because it is the odd-numbered prayer of the day, and Fajr, because its recitation is prolonged.”

Thus, ‘Ā’ishah reported that whenever he travelled, he performed the prayers as originally prescribed: two rak‘ahs each. Had he sometimes prayed four, she would have reported that. This contradicts that fabricated report attributed to ‘Ā’ishah.

Moreover, ‘Ā’ishah was young during the Prophet’s lifetime, peace and blessings be upon him. When the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, died, she was less than twenty years old. She was nine when he consummated his marriage to her in Medina, and he remained in Medina for only ten years. If he consummated the marriage at the beginning of the Hijrah, she would have been nearly twenty; if we suppose that he consummated it later, she would have been younger than that at the time of his death.

Furthermore, even if she had been older, she was learning Islam and its laws from the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him. How, then, could she conceivably fast and pray while travelling with him in a manner contrary to his practice and that of all the other Muslims and his other wives, yet not tell him until she reached Mecca? Could such conduct be imagined of ‘Ā’ishah, the Mother of the Believers? Why would she have done this on that particular journey, but not on her other journeys with him? How could she have felt comfortable acting contrary to his practice without asking his permission?

It is authentically reported from her in the two Sahīh collections, through chains of transmission whose authenticity the scholars unanimously acknowledge, that she said:

“When Allah first prescribed the prayers, He prescribed them as two rak‘ahs each. He then increased them to their full length for those who are resident, while the prayers during travel remained as originally prescribed.”

---

## Page 12221

This report is transmitted by al-Zuhrī from ʿUrwah, from ʿĀ’ishah, and by al-Zuhrī’s trustworthy students. It is also transmitted by Sālih ibn Kaysān from ʿUrwah, from ʿĀ’ishah, through narrators such as Rabīʿah, and by al-Shaʿbī from ʿĀ’ishah. Hadith scholars unanimously agree that this report is authentically established from her.

How, then, could she venture to pray the full prayer while travelling with the Messenger of Allah, peace and blessings be upon him, before seeking his permission, when she saw that he and the Muslims accompanying him prayed only two rakʿahs? Moreover, when she prayed the full prayer after the Prophet’s death, peace and blessings be upon him, she did not argue that she had done so during his lifetime. Nor did ʿUrwah, her sister’s son and the person most familiar with her, mention any such thing. Rather, she justified her practice on the basis of her own legal reasoning, as al-Naysābūrī, al-Bayhaqī and others reported through sound chains of transmission from Wahb ibn Jarīr:

“Shuʿbah narrated to us, from Hishām ibn ʿUrwah, from his father, that ʿĀ’ishah used to pray four rakʿahs when travelling. I said to her, ‘Why not pray two rakʿahs?’ She replied, ‘My nephew, it causes me no hardship.’”

Furthermore, there is the authentic report: “Sālih ibn Kaysān related that ʿUrwah ibn al-Zubayr told him, on the authority of ʿĀ’ishah, that when prayer was first prescribed, it consisted of two rakʿahs both when resident and when travelling. Prayer during travel remained two rakʿahs, while prayer when resident was increased to four.

Sālih said: ‘ʿUmar ibn ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz related this and said, “ʿUrwah told me that ʿĀ’ishah prayed four rakʿahs when travelling.”’ He continued: ‘One day I found ʿUrwah in his company

---

## Page 12222

and asked him, “What was it you told me about ʿĀ’ishah?” He then repeated what he had told me. ʿUmar said, “Did you not tell me that she used to pray four rakʿahs when travelling?” He replied, “Yes.”’”

In the two Sahīh collections, Sufyān ibn ʿUyaynah narrates from al-Zuhrī, from ʿUrwah, from ʿĀ’ishah, who said:

“When prayer was first prescribed, it consisted of two rakʿahs for each prayer. Prayer when resident was then increased, while prayer during travel was left unchanged.”

Al-Zuhrī said: “I asked, ‘Why, then, did ʿĀ’ishah pray the full prayer?’ He replied, ‘She acted on her own interpretation, as ʿUthmān did.’”

Thus, ʿUrwah reports that she justified praying the full prayer by saying, “It causes me no hardship,” and he states that she acted on her own interpretation, as ʿUthmān did. This shows that her practice of praying the full prayer rested on an interpretation arising from her own legal reasoning. Had the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, approved her praying the full prayer, or had he himself prayed it in full, she would have been following the Sunnah of the Messenger of Allah, peace and blessings be upon him. The same would apply to ʿUthmān. It would not have been a matter of interpretation based on independent legal reasoning.

Now, this hadith is the strongest hadith evidence relied upon by those who advocate praying the full prayer when travelling, yet it has been shown to be false. What, then, of the still more unfounded claim that the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, would sometimes pray the full prayer when travelling and sometimes shorten it? This contradicts what is established through mass transmission concerning his Sunnah, which his Companions unanimously transmitted from him and conveyed to his community. Not a single Companion ever reported that he prayed four rakʿahs when travelling. Rather, reports from them have reached the level of mass transmission that he and his Companions used to pray two rakʿahs when travelling.

As for the report narrated by Zayd al-ʿAmmī from Anas ibn Mālik, who said, “We

---

## Page 12223

Companions of the Messenger of Allah, peace and blessings be upon him, used to travel: some of us fasted and others did not; some prayed the full prayer and others shortened it. Those who fasted did not criticize those who did not, nor did those who prayed the full prayer criticize those who shortened it.” This is undoubtedly a lie. The scholars unanimously agree that Zayd al-ʿAmmī is an abandoned narrator. The authentic report from Anas concerns fasting alone.

This is further demonstrated by the fact that, when travelling with the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, they did not pray individually; rather, they prayed behind him. Fasting was different: a person might fast or refrain from fasting. Thus, this hadith is false. Even if al-Bayhaqī narrated it, this is among the matters for which he was criticized. Scholars observed that he did not examine the reports supporting his opponents as thoroughly as he examined those supporting his own position, and that he cited reports as evidence which, had his opponents cited them, he would have exposed as weak and criticized.

Despite his knowledge and religious devotion, what led him into this was the same thing that led others like him astray: seeking to make the reports from the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, agree with the position of one scholar rather than another. Whoever follows this path will have his arguments refuted, and a measure of unjustified partisanship will become apparent in him.

This is what happens when someone gathers reports and, in many instances, interprets them through demonstrably unsound interpretations to make them agree with the position he defends. Such is the practice of Abū Jaʿfar, the author of Sharh al-Āthār, although he narrates more reports than al-Bayhaqī. Al-Bayhaqī, however, scrutinizes reports and distinguishes the sound from the unsound more thoroughly than al-Tahāwī.

As for the hadith stating that “he, peace and blessings be upon him, used to shorten the prayer and pray it in full

---

## Page 12224

and refrain from fasting and fast,” it has been said that its wording contains a scribal error and actually reads: “He used to shorten the prayer while she prayed it in full”—with the feminine prefix tā—“and he would refrain from fasting while she fasted.” This would give it the same meaning as the other hadith, whose chain of transmission is stronger. That report is well known from ʿAbd al-Rahmān ibn al-Aswad, but it has not been reliably preserved from ʿĀ’ishah. As for attributing this other report to ʿAtā’, that is certainly an erroneous attribution. What is established from ʿAtā’ is that “ʿĀ’ishah used to pray four rakʿahs when travelling,” as others have also narrated.

Had ʿĀ’ishah known of a sunnah from the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, concerning this, she would have cited it as evidence. Had this been a known practice of his, she would not have known more about it than his male Companions, who regularly prayed behind him when travelling. This is not something about which ʿĀ’ishah would have known more than the men—as she would about his night prayers or his ritual bathing after intercourse without ejaculation—let alone something known to her alone. Rather, his Companions knew more than ʿĀ’ishah about his conduct when travelling, since she did not accompany him on every journey. For it is authentically reported that she said:

“Whenever the Messenger of Allah, peace and blessings be upon him, intended to travel, he would draw lots among his wives and take with him whichever wife’s lot was drawn.”

Thus, he took her on his journeys only occasionally, and she would remain secluded in her screened compartment. It is also authentically reported that when Shurayh ibn Hāni’ asked her about “wiping over leather socks,” she said: “Ask ʿAlī, for he used to travel with the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him.” Yet wiping over leather socks is something the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, might do

---

## Page 12225

in his home while not travelling, where she might see him without the men being present. The obligatory prayers were different: whether resident or travelling, the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, always offered them as the imam leading his Companions, unless he had an excuse such as illness or being away to attend to some need. Thus, he was absent when he went to reconcile the people of Qubā’, and on one journey he was absent while purifying himself, so they appointed ʿAbd al-Rahmān ibn ʿAwf to lead them in the dawn prayer. When the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, arrived, he commended their action and affirmed that it was correct.

If he had offered the prayers in full, it would have been with the men praying behind him, so they would certainly have known of it. They would also have had every reason and incentive to report it, since it would have differed from his customary practice on his journeys. Had he done so occasionally, they would have been strongly motivated to report it, just as they reported his wiping over leather socks when he did so, even though he usually performed ablution by washing his feet. Likewise, they reported his occasionally combining two prayers, even though he usually offered each prayer within its own prescribed time.

Moreover, a departure from his customary practice would have been more apparent than the difference between one portion of time and another. People do not perceive the passage of time as readily as they perceive the differences they witness in the grounds for an exemption. The latter can be seen with the eye and requires neither reflection nor inference, unlike determining that the time for the noon or sunset prayer has ended, which requires careful attention.

For this reason, a group of scholars held that, outside ʿArafah and Muzdalifah, his combining prayers consisted only of performing the second prayer early and delaying the first until the end of its time. And it has

---

## Page 12226

been reported that he combined them in this manner. This, then, is something about which uncertainty may arise, unlike his praying four rakʿahs while travelling, had he done so. There would have been no uncertainty or dispute about that; rather, the Muslims would have reported it. Whoever considers it possible that he prayed four rakʿahs while travelling—although none of the Companions reported it, and it is known only through a single narrator judged weak, reporting from another narrator, from ʿĀ’ishah, whose reliably established reports do not agree with it but contradict it—would accept as true a report through a similar chain that the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, once prayed four rakʿahs for the dawn prayer. Such a person should likewise accept all reports of this kind: those transmitted by a lone narrator concerning matters that people would have had every reason and incentive to report, and which, had they been true, would certainly have been reported and become widely known.

This is comparable in weakness to the report that he said to the people of Mecca at ʿArafah, Muzdalifah and Minā:

“Complete your prayers, for we are travellers.”

This is also reported from ʿUmar, but only through a weak chain, even though it is known that, had it been true, people would have had every reason and incentive to report it.

An example is what Abū Dāwūd al-Tayālisī narrated: Hammād ibn Salamah narrated to us, from ʿAlī ibn Zayd, from Abū Nadrah, who said:

“A man asked ʿImrān ibn al-Husayn about the prayer of the Messenger of Allah, peace and blessings be upon him, while travelling. He replied:

‘This young man is asking me about the prayers of the Messenger of Allah, peace and blessings be upon him, while travelling, so remember what I tell you about them. I never travelled with the Messenger of Allah, peace and blessings be upon him, on any journey

---

## Page 12227

without his praying two rakʿahs until he returned. I was present with the Messenger of Allah, peace and blessings be upon him, at Hunayn and al-Tā’if, and he prayed two rakʿahs. Then I performed Hajj and ʿumrah with him, and he prayed two rakʿahs. He then said: “People of Mecca, complete your prayers, for we are travellers.” Then I performed Hajj and ʿumrah with Abū Bakr, and he prayed two rakʿahs at a time. He then said: “People of Mecca, complete your prayers, for we are travellers.” Then I performed Hajj and ʿumrah with ʿUmar, and he prayed two rakʿahs and said: “Complete your prayers, for we are travellers.” Then I performed Hajj and ʿumrah with ʿUthmān, and he prayed two rakʿahs at a time. Later, however, ʿUthmān offered the prayer in full.’”

The statement in this hadith that the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, never prayed anything but two rakʿahs while travelling agrees with all the other narrations. All the Companions reported only that the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, prayed two rakʿahs while travelling.

As for the statement attributed to him:

“People of Mecca, complete your prayers, for we are travellers,”

he said this in Mecca in the year of the Conquest, not during his Hajj. This is simply an error that occurred in this narration. Ibrāhīm ibn Humayd also narrated this hadith from Hammād through his chain of transmission, and al-Bayhaqī related it through him. Its wording is:

“The Messenger of Allah, peace and blessings be upon him, never went on a journey without praying two rakʿahs until he returned. He would say: ‘People of Mecca, stand up and pray two rakʿahs, for we are travellers.’ He went on campaign to al-Tā’if and Hunayn and prayed two rakʿahs. He came to al-Jiʿrānah and performed ʿumrah from there. I performed Hajj with Abū Bakr

---

## Page 12228

and performed ʿumrah, and he would pray two rakʿahs. I also performed Hajj with ʿUmar ibn al-Khattāb, and he would pray two rakʿahs.”

Thus, he mentioned this statement only in connection with the year of the Conquest, before the campaign of Hunayn and al-Tā’if, and did not attribute it to Abū Bakr or ʿUmar. Abū Dāwūd also related it explicitly in his Sunan through Ibn ʿUlayyah:

ʿAlī ibn Zayd narrated to us, from Abū Nadrah, from ʿImrān ibn Husayn, who said:

“I attended ʿArafah with the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, and witnessed the Conquest with him. He stayed in Mecca for eighteen nights, praying two rakʿahs and saying: ‘People of this city, pray four rakʿahs, for we are travellers.’”

This occurred during the campaign of the Conquest, in Mecca itself, not at Minā.

Likewise, it is established that during Hajj, ʿUmar led the people of Mecca in two rakʿahs. After concluding the prayer with the salām, he said:

“Complete your prayers, people of Mecca, for we are travellers.”

Further evidence clarifying this is that none of the Companions reported this statement from the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him—neither those who described his prayer nor those who described his pilgrimage rites and Hajj—despite the abundant reasons and incentives to transmit it. Moreover, the leading jurists of the two Sacred Cities held that the people of Mecca should shorten their prayers at ʿArafah, Muzdalifah and Minā. Could they have known that the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, had taught otherwise? Or could they have been ignorant of something so widely known that no one who performed Hajj with the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, would have been unaware of it?

In the two Sahīh collections, Hārithah ibn Khuzāʿah is reported to have said:

“We prayed two rakʿahs with the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, at Minā, when we were at our most numerous and most secure.”

This Hārithah

---

## Page 12229

was from Khuzāʿah, whose people lived around Mecca. In the two Sahīh collections, ʿAbd Allāh ibn Zayd reported:

“ʿUthmān led us in four rakʿahs at Minā. When ʿAbd Allāh ibn Masʿūd was told of this, he said, ‘To Allah we belong, and to Him we return,’ then said: ‘I prayed two rakʿahs with the Messenger of Allah, peace and blessings be upon him, at Minā; I prayed two rakʿahs with Abū Bakr at Minā; and I prayed two rakʿahs with ʿUmar at Minā. Would that, of these four rakʿahs, my share were two accepted rakʿahs!’”

It has been said that ʿUthmān, may Allah be pleased with him, offered the prayer in full because he had married in Mecca and thus become a resident. In the Musnad, ʿAbd al-Rahmān ibn Abī Dhubāb reported:

“ʿUthmān prayed four rakʿahs at Minā, and the people objected. He said: ‘People, I have married in Mecca since my arrival, and I heard the Messenger of Allah, peace and blessings be upon him, say: “Whoever marries in a town should offer the prayer of a resident in Mecca for three days and shorten the fourth.”’”

He should indeed shorten his prayers, as the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, did, and he cannot remain there longer than that. For ʿUthmān was one of the Emigrants, and they were forbidden to reside in Mecca.

In the two Sahīh collections, it is reported that the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, permitted an Emigrant to stay in Mecca for three days after completing his pilgrimage rites. Whenever ʿUthmān performed ʿumrah, he would order his mount to be prepared and ride away immediately afterward, so that he would not remain in Mecca.

---

## Page 12230

So how could he conceivably have believed that he had become permanently settled in Mecca? Unless it is argued that he regarded marriage as establishing residence rather than permanent settlement. The reply would be: it is well known that someone staying in Mecca for three days shortens his prayers, as the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, did; and he could not stay there any longer than that. Yet marriage itself might be an impediment to shortening the prayer. This, too, is unlikely, for the people of Mecca used to shorten their prayers behind the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, and his successors at Minā. Moreover, the Umayyad rulers after ʿUthmān offered the prayers in full, following his example. Had his justification been peculiar to him, they would not have done so.

It has also been said that he feared the Bedouins would think that the prayer consisted of four rakʿahs. This explanation, too, is weak: the Bedouins were less knowledgeable in the Prophet’s time, peace and blessings be upon him, than in ʿUthmān’s time, yet he did not offer the prayer in full. Furthermore, they could see Muslims praying four rakʿahs when not travelling.

Besides, their supposing that the Sunnah for a traveller’s prayer is four rakʿahs would be an error on their part. It is therefore not permissible to depart from the Sunnah only to bring about something equally mistaken through that departure. ʿUrwah said that ʿĀʾishah had adopted an interpretation just as ʿUthmān had, and ʿĀʾishah stated that offering the prayer in full caused her no hardship.

---

## Page 12231

or perhaps they shared the view of those who regarded shortening the prayer as a concession for the hardship of travel. They considered that, now that they had become more prosperous, they no longer experienced the hardship formerly borne by those who prayed four rakʿahs. This is comparable to what is reported from ʿUthmān concerning his prohibition of mutʿah—here meaning the conversion of Hajj into ʿumrah: it had been permitted because they needed it at that time, but that need had ceased.

---

## Page 12232

Chapter: The Friday Prayer

Shaykh al-Islām—may Allah have mercy on him—said:

In the name of Allah, the Most Compassionate, the Most Merciful.

We seek His help. May Allah bless our master Muhammad and all his family and Companions.

From Ahmad ibn ʿAbd al-Halīm ibn ʿAbd al-Salām Ibn Taymiyyah to all believers and Muslims whom this letter reaches, among the people of Bahrain and elsewhere, and especially to those of knowledge and religious devotion. Peace be upon you, and Allah’s mercy and blessings.

To proceed:

I praise Allah before you: there is no deity but Him. He is worthy of praise and has power over all things. I ask Him to bless the chosen one of His creation, Muhammad, His servant and Messenger and the last of His prophets, whom He sent with clear proofs, guidance, and the religion of truth, to make it prevail over all other religions. Allah suffices as a witness. May Allah bless him and his family and grant him abundant peace.

---

## Page 12233

To proceed:

A delegation arrived from the vicinity of your land and told us much the same as we had already heard about the people of your region: that they hold firmly to the Sunnah and the united Muslim community, observe Allah’s law as conveyed by His Messenger, and shun the practices of pre-Islamic ignorance to which many of the Bedouins still cling. These include shedding one another’s blood, plundering one another’s wealth, severing family ties, casting off the bond of Islam, granting inheritance to males but not females, letting garments trail, and invoking the rallying cries of pre-Islamic ignorance—saying, “O sons of so-and-so!” or “O so-and-so!” They also include supporting one’s tribe in wrongdoing and neglecting what Allah has prescribed concerning marriage, such as the waiting period and similar obligations.

They also shun the deviant notions that Satan has made appealing to a group of those people, through which they have departed from the beliefs of the earliest forerunners among the Emigrants and the Helpers. They have contravened what Allah prescribed for them concerning seeking forgiveness for those who preceded them, as He, Exalted is He, says:

“And those who come after them say, ‘Our Lord, forgive us and our brothers who preceded us in faith, and do not place in our hearts any resentment toward those who believe. Our Lord, You are indeed Most Kind, Most Merciful.’”

They have reviled the Companions of Allah’s Messenger, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, with abuse that could not come from anyone in whose heart faith has taken firm root.

All praise is therefore due to Allah, who has spared us and you the afflictions with which He has tested many of His creatures, and has greatly favoured us above many of those He created. We ask Allah, the Magnificent, the Bestower of favours, the Originator of the heavens and the earth, to perfect His blessing upon us and you, to guide us and you to the words and deeds He loves and is pleased with, and to make us among those who follow the earliest forerunners in righteousness.

---

## Page 12234

Nor is this unprecedented: the people of Bahrain have remained people of Islam and virtue since the time of Allah’s Messenger, may Allah bless him and grant him peace.

{Their delegation from ‘Abd al-Qays came to Allah’s Messenger, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, with al-Ashajj among them. Allah’s Messenger, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said to them, “Welcome to the delegation, without disgrace or regret!”

They said, “O Messenger of Allah, this tribe of unbelievers from Mudar stands between us and you, and we can reach you only during a sacred month. Give us clear, decisive instructions that we may follow and convey to those we have left behind.”

He said, “I command you to believe in Allah. Do you know what belief in Allah is? To testify that none has the right to be worshipped except Allah and that Muhammad is Allah’s Messenger, to establish prayer, to give zakat, to fast Ramadan, and to pay one-fifth of whatever spoils you acquire.”}

Hajj had not yet been made obligatory at that time.

{He said to al-Ashajj, “You possess two qualities that Allah loves: forbearance and deliberation.”

He asked, “Are these two qualities I have cultivated, or two with which I was created?”

He replied, “Two qualities with which you were created.”

He said, “Praise be to Allah, who created me with two qualities that He loves.”}

They then established Friday prayer in their land. The first Friday prayer held in Islam after the Friday prayer in Medina was held at Juwāthā, a village in Bahrain. They remained steadfast in Islam when Allah’s Messenger, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, died and some of the Arabs apostatized, and they were led in battle by their governor, al-‘Alā’ ibn

---

## Page 12235

al-Hadramī—a righteous man—against the apostates. Historical narratives contain fine accounts of them. May Allah, glorified and exalted be He, grant their later generations the same guidance He granted their earliest generations. Indeed, He is the One who grants this and has power over it.

Some of the delegation told us that they used to hold Friday prayer in part of your land, but that some people from Iraq had issued them a ruling to discontinue it. We asked the speaker to describe the place. He said, “There is a mosque built of clay, surrounded by many people who live there permanently. They do not leave the place in winter or summer unless someone forcibly expels them. Rather, they, their fathers, and their grandfathers have been settled there just as permanently as other villagers. Their houses, however, are not built of clay, but of palm stalks and similar materials.”

Know—may Allah have mercy on you—that Friday prayer is to be held in such circumstances. Whenever people are permanently settled in a cluster of buildings which they do not leave in winter or summer, Friday prayer is to be held there, provided that the buildings are constructed from whatever materials they customarily use: clay, wood, reeds, palm stalks, palm leaves, or anything else. The components and materials of the buildings have no bearing on this. What matters is that the people are permanently settled, unlike those living in tents and encampments, who generally seek out places where rain has fallen, move from one area to another, and take their dwellings with them when they move. This is the position of the majority of scholars.

---

## Page 12236

The majority of scholars cited the account from your own land as evidence against Abū Hanīfa’s view that Friday prayer is not to be held in villages. They cited the report transmitted from Ibn ‘Abbās—may Allah be pleased with them both:

“The first Friday prayer held in Islam after the Friday prayer in Medina was in Bahrain, in a village called Jawāthā, one of the villages of Bahrain.”

They also cited the account of Abū Hurayra—may Allah be pleased with him—who was ‘Umar’s governor of Bahrain. He wrote to the Commander of the Faithful, ‘Umar—may Allah be pleased with him—seeking his permission to hold Friday prayer in the villages of Bahrain. ‘Umar wrote back: “Hold Friday prayer wherever you are.”

Perhaps those who told you that Friday prayer should not be held were following the opinion of those who do not permit it in villages. Or perhaps they misunderstood the jurists’ statement in their abridged manuals: “It is to be held only in a village whose buildings are adjoining or close together, such that they fall under a single name.” They thus assumed that construction necessarily meant solid masonry made of clay, lime, stone, or sun-dried brick. This is a mistake on their part. Rather, scholars have explicitly stated that what counts as construction is whatever those permanently settled inhabitants customarily build with, whether reeds, wood, or similar materials.

Accordingly, the leading scholars distinguished between tent-dwelling Bedouins and settled residents on the grounds that the former move about and do not settle permanently in one place, unlike the latter. Some of the early Muslims used to build themselves houses of reeds. “The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, roofed his mosque with date-palm stalks, and the mosque would leak

---

## Page 12237

when it rained. They said, ‘O Messenger of Allah, if only we built something for you’—meaning a solidly constructed building. He replied, ‘Rather, a shelter like the shelter of Moses.’”

Several scholars have explicitly addressed your very question—namely, houses made of date-palm stalks or fronds. They include scholars of Imam Ahmad’s school, such as al-Qādī Abū Ya‘lā, Abū al-Hasan al-Āmidī, Ibn ‘Aqīl, and others. They stated that Friday prayer is to be held among people whose houses are built of fired brick, clay, stone, wood, reeds, date-palm stalks, or palm fronds.

Likewise, several scholars of al-Shāfi‘ī’s school—may Allah be pleased with them—from both Khurāsān, such as the author of al-Wasīt, I believe, and Iraq stated that Friday prayer is to be held in settlements of palm-frond houses. Al-Māwardī disagreed with them in al-Hāwī, stating that Friday prayer is not to be held in settlements of houses made of reeds or date-palm stalks, but rather in those with sturdy wooden houses.

This distinction is weak. It runs contrary to the position of the majority, to analogical reasoning, and to what is indicated by the transmitted reports and the statements of the leading scholars. For Abū Hurayra, while in Bahrain, wrote to ‘Umar ibn al-Khattāb—may Allah be pleased with them both—asking him about Friday prayer. ‘Umar ibn al-Khattāb wrote back: “Hold Friday prayer wherever you are.” Imam Ahmad based his position on this report from ‘Umar.

It is also reported from Nāfi‘ that Ibn ‘Umar—may Allah be pleased with them both—used to pass by the watering places that

---

## Page 12238

lay between Mecca and Medina, where the inhabitants held Friday prayer, and he did not object to them. Thus, ‘Umar instructed the people of Bahrain to hold Friday prayer wherever they had settled permanently, knowing that some houses were made of date-palm stalks, and he did not stipulate any particular type of construction. Likewise, Ibn ‘Umar approved of the inhabitants of the settlements between Mecca and Medina holding Friday prayer. It is known that their houses were not built of clay, but rather of date-palm stalks or palm fronds.

Imam Ahmad said:
“Friday prayer is not obligatory for the Bedouins because they move from place to place.”

He thus identified their movement from place to place as the reason for their exemption. Accordingly, anyone who is permanently settled and does not move away of his own accord is counted among village dwellers. Such people differ from tent dwellers in two respects:

First: Tent dwellers generally do not settle permanently in any particular place. Even if some groups among them do settle in one place, they remain liable to move away. This is unlike these permanent settlers, who till the land and sow crops, and who move away only as the inhabitants of clay-built houses do: either because a need arises or because an overpowering authority displaces them, as kings do with farmers.

Second: Tent dwellers take their homes with them when they move, so their homes are movable rather than immovable property. This is unlike houses made of wood, reeds, or date-palm stalks: their owners do not transport these materials to build with them in the place to which they move. Rather, they build

---

## Page 12239

in each place using materials found nearby. This, however, is not the place for an exhaustive examination of the evidence on this matter. This practice—holding Friday prayer in villages—first began in your region, so do not discontinue this prescribed observance in your land, for Allah will bring together for you every kind of good.

Know, then—may Allah have mercy upon you and grant us and you the good of this world and the Hereafter—that Allah sent Muhammad, peace and blessings be upon him, with the truth and revealed the Book to him. He was sent to people with divergent desires, divided hearts, and conflicting opinions. Through him, Allah brought them together, united their hearts, and protected them from Satan’s schemes. Then He, glorified and exalted, made clear that this principle—communal unity—is a pillar of His religion.

He, glorified be He, said:

“O you who believe, fear Allah as He should be feared, and do not die except as Muslims.”

“Hold firmly, all together, to the rope of Allah and do not become divided. Remember Allah’s favor upon you: you were enemies, and He united your hearts, so that by His favor you became brothers. You were on the brink of a pit of Fire, and He saved you from it. Thus Allah makes His signs clear to you, so that you may be guided.”

“Let there be among you a community calling to goodness, enjoining what is right and forbidding what is wrong. It is they who will prosper.”

“Do not be like those who became divided and differed after clear proofs had come to them. They will suffer a tremendous punishment.”

“On the Day when some faces will turn white and some faces will turn black

---

## Page 12240

—as for those whose faces turn black, they will be told: ‘Did you disbelieve after having believed? Then taste the punishment for your disbelief.’”

“As for those whose faces turn white, they will be within Allah’s mercy, abiding therein forever.”

Ibn ‘Abbās, may Allah be pleased with them both, said: “The faces of the people of the Sunnah will turn white, and the faces of the people of innovation will turn black.” Consider, then—may Allah have mercy upon you—how Allah called for unity and forbade division. In another verse, He said:

“Indeed, those who divided their religion and became sects—you have nothing to do with them.”

Thus He dissociated His Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, from those who divided their religion and became sects. Likewise, He forbade us to divide and differ, saying:

“Do not be like those who became divided and differed after clear proofs had come to them.”

The Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, disapproved of disputation that leads to disagreement and division. He came out to a group of his Companions who were disputing about divine decree. His face looked as though pomegranate seeds had been crushed upon it, and he said:

“Is this what you were commanded to do? Is this what you were called to—to set one part of Allah’s Book against another? Those before you were destroyed for this very reason: they set one part of Allah’s Book against another.”

‘Abd Allāh ibn ‘Amr, may Allah be pleased with them both, said: “I have never felt more fortunate than I did for not having been present at that gathering.” Abū Dāwūd related this hadith in his Sunan, as did others, and the core of the account is found in the two Sahīh collections.

The well-known hadith reported from him, peace and blessings be upon him, in the Sunan collections and elsewhere states that he, peace and blessings be upon him, said: “My community will split into seventy-three sects

---

## Page 12241

, all of them in the Fire except one.” It was asked: “O Messenger of Allah, which one is it?” He replied: “Those who follow what I and my Companions follow today.” Another narration states: “It is the united community.” Yet another states: “Allah’s Hand is over the united community.” Thus he described the saved group as those who hold firmly to his Sunnah and as the united community.

When the scholars among the Companions, the Successors and those who came after them disputed a matter, they followed Allah’s command in His words:

“If you disagree over anything, refer it to Allah and the Messenger, if you believe in Allah and the Last Day. That is better and best in outcome.”

They would discuss a question in a spirit of consultation and sincere mutual counsel. Their views might differ on theoretical and practical matters while their affection, bonds and religious brotherhood remained intact. However, anyone who opposes the clear Book, the widely transmitted Sunnah, or the consensus of the community’s early generations, in a manner for which there is no excuse, is treated as the people of innovation are treated.

Thus ‘Ā’ishah, the Mother of the Believers, may Allah be pleased with her, disagreed with Ibn ‘Abbās and other Companions over whether Muhammad, peace and blessings be upon him, saw his Lord. She said: “Whoever claims that Muhammad saw his Lord has uttered a tremendous lie against Allah Most High.” The majority of the community follows Ibn ‘Abbās’s view, yet they do not regard those who deny this and agree with the Mother of the Believers, may Allah be pleased with her, as innovators. Likewise, she denied that the dead hear the living calling to them when she was told that the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, had said: “

---

## Page 12242

You hear what I say no better than they do.” She said: “What he actually said was: ‘They now know that what I told them is true.’” Nevertheless, there is no doubt that the dead hear the sound of sandals, as has been established from the Messenger of Allah, peace and blessings be upon him.

“No man passes the grave of someone he knew in this world and greets him without Allah restoring his soul to him so that he may return the greeting.”

This has been authentically reported from the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, alongside other hadiths to this effect. The Mother of the Believers adopted an interpretation; may Allah be pleased with her.

Likewise, regarding the Ascension, Mu‘āwiyah is reported to have said that it took place only with his soul. Others disagree with Mu‘āwiyah, may Allah be pleased with him. There are many such examples.

As for disagreement over “legal rulings,” there are too many instances to enumerate. If two Muslims were to cut ties whenever they disagreed about something, no bond or brotherhood would remain among Muslims. Abū Bakr and ‘Umar, may Allah be pleased with them both, were the foremost Muslims, yet they disputed certain matters while intending nothing but good. On the day of Banū Qurayzah, the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, said to his Companions:

“‘None of you is to pray ‘Asr except at Banū Qurayzah.’ The time for ‘Asr came while they were on the road. Some said: ‘We will not pray except at Banū Qurayzah,’ and they missed the time for ‘Asr. Others said: ‘He did not intend for us to delay the prayer,’ so they prayed on the road. He reproached neither group.”

Both al-Bukhārī and Muslim recorded this in their Sahīh collections from the hadith of Ibn ‘Umar. Although this concerns legal rulings, any matter that does not

---

## Page 12243

involve essential principles is treated in the same way as legal rulings. The Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, said:

“Shall I tell you of something higher in rank than fasting, prayer, charity, enjoining what is right and forbidding what is wrong?” They replied, “Yes, O Messenger of Allah.” He said, “Mending relations between people, for discord is the razor. I do not mean that it shaves off hair; rather, it shaves away religion.”

Abū Dāwūd narrated this from al-Zubayr ibn al-‘Awwām, may Allah be pleased with him. It is also authentically reported that the Prophet said:

“It is not lawful for a Muslim to shun his brother for more than three days: they meet, and each turns away from the other. The better of the two is the one who greets the other first.”

It is true, however, that he authentically is reported to have shunned Ka‘b ibn Mālik and his two companions—may Allah be pleased with them—when they stayed behind from the expedition to Tabūk, their disobedience became apparent, and there were fears that they might fall into hypocrisy. He therefore shunned them and commanded the Muslims to do likewise, even ordering them to live apart from their wives without divorcing them. The shunning lasted fifty nights, until the acceptance of their repentance was revealed from heaven.

Likewise, ‘Umar, may Allah be pleased with him, commanded the Muslims to shun Sabīgh ibn ‘Isl al-Tamīmī when he saw that he was among those who pursue the ambiguous passages of the Book. This continued until a year had passed and his sincerity in repentance became clear; ‘Umar then commanded the Muslims to resume relations with him. On this and similar grounds,

---

## Page 12244

the Muslims considered it appropriate to shun those who showed signs of deviation by openly professing and promoting religious innovations or openly committing major sins. As for someone who conceals a sin or secretly holds an innovation that does not amount to disbelief, such a person is not to be shunned. Rather, it is the one who calls others to innovation who is to be shunned, for shunning is a form of punishment, and only someone who openly manifests disobedience in word or deed is punished. As for someone who outwardly shows us good, we accept what he presents outwardly and entrust his inner state to Allah Most High. At worst, he is like the hypocrites whose outward professions the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, accepted, while entrusting their inner states to Allah, when they came to him in the year of Tabūk, swearing oaths and offering excuses.

For this reason, Imām Ahmad and most of the imams before and after him, such as Mālik and others, would neither accept the narrations of someone who called others to an innovation nor sit with him, unlike someone who remained silent about it. The compilers of authentic hadith collections included reports from many people accused of innovation who remained silent about it, but did not include reports from those who called others to innovations.

What prompted these remarks was that your delegation told us of instances of division and disagreement among you. They even mentioned that matters had come close to fighting. There is no power nor strength except through Allah, the Most High, the Magnificent. It is Allah whom we ask to unite our hearts and yours, mend our relations, guide us to the paths of peace, and bring us out

---

## Page 12245

from darkness into light, keep us away from shameful deeds, both open and hidden, bless our hearing, sight, spouses, and offspring for as long as He keeps us alive, and make us grateful for His blessings, praising Him for them and accepting them, and perfect His blessings upon us.

They mentioned that the cause of this was disagreement over “the question of whether the unbelievers will see their Lord.” We had not imagined that matters concerning this issue would reach such a point, for it is a minor matter.

He then gave the answer, which has already appeared in the “Book of Names and Attributes.”

---

## Page 12246

Shaykh al-Islām—may Allah sanctify his soul—also said:

Section

People have differed over “the Friday prayer and the two Eid prayers”: is residence a condition for them, or may they be performed while travelling? There are three views:

First: Residence is a condition for both, so neither is prescribed while travelling. This is the view of the majority and the position of Abū Hanīfah, Mālik, and Ahmad, according to the stronger of the two opinions reported from him.

Second: Residence is a condition for the Friday prayer, but not for the Eid prayer. This is the view of al-Shāfiʿī and of Ahmad according to the second opinion reported from him.

Third: Residence is not a condition for either, as some of the Zāhirīs maintain. Their principal evidence is the unrestricted wording of the command, His words, “When the call is made,” and similar passages. They claimed that nothing in Islamic law requires restricting this to residents.

Those who distinguished between the Friday and Eid prayers said: The Eid prayer is either voluntary or obligatory on

---

## Page 12247

a communal basis, and it does not discharge another obligation as the Friday prayer discharges the noon prayer. Voluntary prayers are prescribed for residents and travellers alike, such as the midmorning prayer, night prayers, and the regular Sunnah prayers associated with the obligatory prayers. The same applies to communal obligations, such as funeral prayers.

The correct view, without doubt, is the first: neither prayer is prescribed for travellers. The Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, undertook many journeys. He performed three pilgrimages of ʿumrah besides the ʿumrah accompanying his Hajj, performed the Farewell Pilgrimage with many thousands accompanying him, and took part in more than twenty military expeditions. Yet no one ever reported that he performed either the Friday prayer or the Eid prayer while travelling. Rather, throughout his journeys he would pray two units at a time, and on Fridays he would pray two units just as on other days.

No one ever reported that, while travelling, he delivered a sermon before the prayer on Friday—whether standing on his feet or mounted on his riding animal, as he would do when delivering the Eid sermon, or from a pulpit, as he would when delivering the Friday sermon. He did sometimes deliver sermons during his journeys as occasions arose, and these were transmitted, as in the hadith of ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿAmr (1). Yet no one ever reported that he delivered a sermon before the prayer on Friday while travelling. Indeed, no one even reported that he recited aloud on Friday. Clearly, had he departed from his usual practice by reciting aloud and delivering a sermon, they would have reported it.

On the Day of ʿArafah, he delivered a sermon to them, then came down and led them in two units of prayer. No one reported that he recited aloud, and that sermon was not for the Friday prayer;

> **Footnotes:**
> (1) A blank in the original.
> Shaykh Nāsir ibn Hamad al-Fahd writes (p. 200):
> Perhaps the blank was intended for the report narrated, with similar wording, by Ahmad (2/161, 191), Muslim (1844), al-Nasā’ī (4191), Ibn Mājah (3956), and others, through al-A‘mash, from Zayd ibn Wahb, from ‘Abd al-Rahmān ibn ‘Abd Rabb al-Ka‘bah, who said:
> 
> I came to ‘Abd Allāh ibn ‘Amr ibn al-‘Ās while he was sitting in the shade of the Ka‘bah, and heard him say: “While we were travelling with the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, he stopped to camp. Some of us were pitching their tents, others were tending their grazing animals, and others were competing in archery. Then his herald called out, ‘Gather for prayer!’ We gathered, and the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, stood and addressed us, saying: ‘No prophet before me failed to guide his community to what he knew was good for them …’” The hadith continues.

---

## Page 12248

for had it been for the Friday prayer, he would have delivered sermons on other Fridays as well. Rather, it was delivered for the pilgrimage rites. Hence, Muslim scholars unanimously agree that a sermon is to be delivered at ʿArafah even when the day is not a Friday. These mass-transmitted reports thus establish that the sermon is delivered for the Day of ʿArafah, even when it does not fall on a Friday, and not for the Friday prayer.

Likewise, neither he nor any of his Rightly Guided Caliphs performed the Eid prayer at Minā. He entered Makkah in the year of the Conquest, arriving during Ramadan, and was there for Eid al-Fitr. Yet he did not perform the Eid prayer there on that day, nor has any Muslim reported that he did. Clearly, had he led the Eid prayer in Makkah, with so many Muslims accompanying him—more than ten thousand—this would have been among the events people had the strongest interest and incentive to transmit. Likewise, the Battle of Badr took place during Ramadan, and Eid came while he was travelling, yet he did not perform an Eid prayer while travelling.

Furthermore, no one in Madinah performed the Eid prayer except with him, just as they did not perform the Friday prayer except with him. There were many mosques in Madinah: each quarter of the Ansār had a mosque and an imam who led its people in prayer. These imams led them in the five daily prayers, but did not lead them in either the Friday prayer or the Eid prayer.

It is therefore clear that they regarded the Eid prayer as belonging to the same category as the Friday prayer, not as an unrestricted voluntary prayer or a funeral prayer. The claim that the Eid prayer is voluntary is not conceded.

---

## Page 12249

and even if this were conceded, one would reply: this prayer has distinctive features that no other prayer shares.
