The established Sunnah was for all the Muslims to gather behind the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and his caliphs after him. They did not do this for other voluntary prayers. He would lead them out into the open country, proclaim Allah’s greatness during the prayer, and deliver a sermon afterward. This is prescribed on every Eid as a regular religious observance.

Seeking rain, however, was not confined to prayer. Sometimes he would seek rain through supplication alone while in Madinah; at other times, he would go out into the open country and seek rain, with or without prayer. Indeed, some scholars, such as Abū Hanīfah, did not recognize a prayer for seeking rain. Since seeking rain is prescribed without either prayer or a sermon, and for individuals as well, it cannot be placed in the same category as Eid, which invariably involves prayer and a sermon. Eid is a regular religious observance, not one prescribed in response to an occasional circumstance, as are the eclipse prayer and seeking rain.

Furthermore, when Alī ibn Abī Tālib appointed someone to lead the weak in the Eid prayer in the congregational mosque, he instructed him to pray four units, just as someone who does not perform the Friday prayer prays four units. Before Alī, people had known no practice of performing the Eid prayer except with the imam in the open country. Since the practice of the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and his caliphs included no Eid prayer except with the imam, it is invalid to equate it with prayers that they performed both individually and in congregation.

Furthermore, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, did not prescribe it for women; rather, he commanded them to go out on Eid. He even commanded that menstruating women be brought out. They said to him: “If not

---

## Page 12250

even an outer garment is available to a woman?” He said: “Let her sister provide her with an outer garment of her own.” This emphatically urges women to go out on Eid, even though, regarding Friday prayer and congregational prayer, he said: “Their homes are better for them.” This is because they could pray at home on Friday as on other days, offering the noon prayer. Had the Eid prayer been prescribed for them at home, there would have been no need to urge them so emphatically to go out. Moreover, had that been permissible, women would have done so during his lifetime, just as they offered voluntary prayers. Since no one has reported that any woman—or any man—performed the Eid prayer at home during his lifetime, whereas women went out to the prayer ground at his command, it is clear that this was not part of what he prescribed.

Furthermore, Alī ibn Abī Tālib, may Allah be pleased with him, was told: “There are weak people in the city who cannot go out with you. If only you would appoint someone to lead them in prayer.” So he appointed someone who led them in prayer. Had individuals been able to perform it on their own, there would have been no need for this appointment, for which there was no precedent in the Sunnah. What the Commander of the Faithful, Alī, did thus indicates a distinction between those able to go out to the prayer ground and those unable to do so. Those who are able go out; women are able to go out, so they go out rather than pray on their own. The same applies to travelers who are in town: since they can pray with the imam, they do not pray separately with an imam of their own. This differs from Friday prayer: if they do not perform it, they pray separately; and when they are in their homes, they pray behind an imam, just as they do in the open country. As for someone who is

---

## Page 12251

ill or imprisoned on Eid and normally performs the Eid prayer, he cannot go out. Such people are in the same position as those for whom ʿAlī appointed someone to lead the prayer. They pray in congregation or individually, offering four units of prayer, as they do on Friday, without the additional takbīrs, audible recitation, or either call to prayer—the adhān or iqāmah. Since the Eid prayer has neither an adhān nor an iqāmah, neither is prescribed for the prayer offered in its place. This differs from Friday prayer, since both Friday prayer and the noon prayer have an adhān and an iqāmah.

Anyone who misses Friday prayer offers the noon prayer, because the noon prayer is obligatory and is waived only for those who perform Friday prayer. Thus, everyone upon whom prayer is obligatory must offer either Friday prayer or the noon prayer on Friday. For this reason, women, travelers, and others who do not perform Friday prayer offer the noon prayer.

As for Eid, no prayer is prescribed for that occasion other than the Eid prayer, and it is prescribed only with the imam. Women and travelers who can perform it with the imam do so, and this is prescribed for them. Friday prayer is different: they may choose either to perform it with the imam or to offer the noon prayer. With Eid, however, if they miss it, they miss it without a substitute. Thus, the Eid prayer is more emphatically prescribed for travelers and women than Friday prayer: Friday prayer has a substitute, whereas the Eid prayer does not.

Moreover, each of the two Eids occurs only once a year, whereas Friday prayer recurs fifty times or more each year. Missing some Friday prayers is therefore not like missing the Eid prayer. Someone who regards the Eid prayer as an individual obligation might reasonably hold it obligatory for those who

---

## Page 12252

are in the town, including travelers and women, as was the practice. For all Muslims, men and women alike, used to attend the Eid prayer with the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. The view that it is an individual obligation is stronger than the view that it is a communal obligation. As for the view that it is voluntary, this is very weak indeed. The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, commanded it and consistently observed it, as did his successors and the Muslims after him. No Muslim land has ever been known to abandon the Eid prayer, which is among the greatest outward symbols of Islam.

Allah Most High says:
“And that you glorify Allah for having guided you.”

This and similar commands to proclaim Allah’s greatness on the two Eids imply, with even greater force, a command to perform the prayer that includes both the regular and additional takbīrs. If the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, did not permit women to miss it, how could men be permitted to do so?

Anyone who says that it is a communal obligation may be told: this applies only to matters whose intended benefit is achieved when some people undertake them, such as burying the dead or overcoming the enemy. Eid does not involve a particular benefit that some people can secure on behalf of the rest. Rather, a greater gathering is prescribed for the Eid prayer than for Friday prayer. The Prophet commanded women to attend it, whereas they were not commanded to attend Friday prayer, but merely permitted to do so. He said:
“Your prayers in your homes are better for you.”

Moreover, how many people are needed to achieve this benefit? Whatever number is stipulated would be arbitrary, whether one, two, or three. If forty are stipulated, this is an analogy with Friday prayer, which is an individual obligation. Thus, no one may stay away from the Eid prayer

---

## Page 12253

unless unable to attend, even if exempt from Friday prayer on account of travel or being female. And Allah knows best.

Likewise, it may be argued that Friday prayer is obligatory for travelers who are in a town, even though they are not required to perform their prayers in full. Just as they must perform the prayer in full when following an imam who does so, they must also attend Friday prayer as followers of the residents. Similarly, those who recognize a third category between a permanently settled resident and a traveler—namely, a resident who has not settled permanently—require such a person to attend as a follower, saying: “Friday prayer is obligatory for him, but his presence does not count toward establishing its congregation.”

It has been explained elsewhere that the Book of Allah and the Sunnah of His Messenger recognize only residents and travelers. A resident is someone who has settled permanently; anyone else is a traveler who shortens the prayer. These travelers are required to attend Friday prayer, because His words,

“When the call is made for prayer,”

and similar passages include them, and they have no excuse. In a Muslim town, no one should fail to attend Friday prayer except someone unable to do so, such as a sick person or a prisoner. These travelers are able to attend. However, travelers do not establish a Friday congregation themselves; rather, when the townspeople establish it, they pray with them. There are stronger grounds for this than for requiring them to perform the prayer in full behind a resident imam.

There is also a strong case for Friday prayer being obligatory for a slave, either unconditionally or when his master gives him permission. A traveler in a town does not pray on his mount, even though he shortens the prayer; the same principle applies to Friday prayer. As for his refraining from fasting, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, entered Mecca during Ramadan, and neither he nor the Muslims were fasting. It has not been reported that they were instructed to begin

---

## Page 12254

fasting. Refraining from fasting is thus like shortening the prayer: both are permitted to a traveler during stopovers within a journey. Praying on a mount, by contrast, is prescribed only while on the move. Moreover, Allah has linked refraining from fasting and shortening the prayer to travel as such. This differs from praying on a mount, for which there is no wording about performing the prayer in full; rather, there is an action, which does not establish a general rule. It is therefore akin to combining two prayers, which is permitted whenever there is a valid excuse. Likewise, an obligatory prayer may be performed on a mount during travel when an excuse prevents dismounting. Someone offering voluntary prayers needs to be able to continue doing so, which is not possible if he must dismount while traveling. If voluntary prayer is permissible while seated despite being able to stand, then it is all the more permissible for a traveler to offer it on his mount.

They would take some of the children along for Eid, just as Ibn ‘Abbās attended Eid with the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, before he had reached puberty.

As for someone unable to attend the prayer with the imam, he is entitled to do what he can. In everything it commands, the Sharia distinguishes between those who are able and those who are unable. Someone who is able may not perform a commanded act without fulfilling its conditions, whereas a condition that someone cannot fulfill is waived for him. Thus, someone able to pray facing the qiblah, standing and in a state of ritual purity, may not pray otherwise. Someone unable to do so, however, prays according to his circumstances, in whatever manner he can: without clothing, facing a direction other than the qiblah, and using dry ablution, if that is all he can do. The same applies on Eid: if he cannot go out with the imam, that requirement is waived for him, and he is permitted to

---

## Page 12255

do what he can, so that he may perform as much worship as he is able on this day. He therefore prays four rak‘ahs, two of which take the place of the sermon that did not accompany his prayer, just as the Friday sermon takes the place of two rak‘ahs. The Eid takbīrs are prescribed only in the two-rak‘ah prayer accompanied by a sermon, and the same applies to reciting aloud. Just as the imam recites aloud in the two-rak‘ah Friday prayer, while someone praying four rak‘ahs does not, so too on Eid someone praying four rak‘ahs does not recite aloud.

Thus, a prisoner, a sick person, and someone who set out to pray but missed the prayer with the imam pray on Eid, unlike someone who deliberately neglected it. This is a major principle established by the Sunnah concerning the distinction between the Friday and Eid prayers.

Three different views are reported from Ahmad concerning someone who misses the Eid prayer: whether he should pray four rak‘ahs, two rak‘ahs, or be given the choice between them.

---

## Page 12256

He was also asked:

A group of people residing in a village number fewer than forty. Which prayer must they perform: the Friday prayer or the noon prayer?

He replied:

If there are fewer than forty men in the village, they perform the noon prayer according to most scholars, including al-Shāfi‘ī and Ahmad, according to the best-known view reported from him. Abū Hanīfah holds the same view. However, al-Shāfi‘ī, Ahmad, and most scholars say that if they number forty, they perform the Friday prayer.

---

## Page 12257

Shaykh al-Islām—may Allah have mercy on him—was also asked:

Regarding prayer after the first call to prayer on Friday: did the Prophet (may Allah bless him and grant him peace), or any of the Companions, Successors, or imams, perform it? Is it explicitly prescribed in any of the schools of the universally recognized imams? And is the Prophet’s saying (may Allah bless him and grant him peace), “Between every two calls to prayer there is a prayer,” specific to Friday, or does it apply at all times?

He replied—may Allah be pleased with him:

All praise is due to Allah, Lord of the worlds.

The Prophet (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) did not offer any prayer before the Friday prayer after the call to prayer, nor has anyone reported that he did so. During his lifetime, the call to prayer was not given until he had sat down on the pulpit. Bilāl would give the call, then the Prophet (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) would deliver the two sermons. Bilāl would then give the call to commence the prayer, and the Prophet (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) would lead the people in prayer. Thus, neither he nor any of the Muslims praying with him could have offered a prayer after the call to prayer. Nor has anyone reported that he prayed at home before going out on Friday, nor did he verbally prescribe a prayer

---

## Page 12258

with a fixed number of rakʿahs before the Friday prayer. Rather, his words (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) encourage prayer when a man comes to the mosque on Friday, without specifying a time, as in his saying:

“Whoever sets out early and arrives early, walks rather than rides, and prays as much as has been decreed for him.”

This is also the practice reported from the Companions. When they came to the mosque on Friday, they would begin praying upon entering and offer as much prayer as they could. Some would pray ten rakʿahs, some twelve, some eight, and some fewer than that.

For this reason, the great majority of the imams agreed that there is no sunnah prayer before the Friday prayer with a specified time and a fixed number of rakʿahs. Such a prescription can only be established through the Prophet’s words or actions (may Allah bless him and grant him peace), and he prescribed nothing of the kind, either by word or deed. This is the position of Mālik, al-Shāfiʿī and most of his followers, and the well-known position in Ahmad’s school.

A group of scholars held that there is a sunnah prayer before it. Some specified two rakʿahs, as did a group of the followers of al-Shāfiʿī and Ahmad. Others specified four, as has been reported from the followers of Abū Hanīfah and a group of Ahmad’s followers. Reports from Imam Ahmad have also been cited in support of this view.

Among these scholars, some cite a weak hadith as evidence, while others say that it is

---

## Page 12259

a shortened midday prayer, so the sunnah prayer associated with the midday prayer also applies to it. This is mistaken for two reasons.

First, by the agreement of the Muslims, Friday prayer has distinctive rulings that set it apart from the daily midday prayer, even if it is called a shortened midday prayer. Friday prayer must be performed within its prescribed time and cannot be made up afterward, whereas the midday prayer can be made up. Friday prayer also requires a certain number of worshippers, permanent residence, the imam’s permission, and other conditions, none of which is required for the midday prayer. The rulings of Friday prayer therefore cannot be derived from those of the midday prayer when Friday prayer has distinctive rulings that set it apart. Since Friday prayer shares some rulings with the midday prayer but differs from it in others, the disputed issue cannot be assigned to either category without evidence. There is no stronger basis for treating the sunnah prayer as a point of similarity than as a point of difference.

The second reason is this:

Even supposing that it is a shortened midday prayer, the Prophet (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) did not offer the sunnah prayer associated with the shortened midday prayer while travelling, either before or after it. He offered it only when he performed the midday prayer in full, as four rakʿahs. Since his practice regarding the shortened midday prayer differed from his practice regarding the full prayer, what they have cited is evidence against them, not in their favour. Moreover, the reason warranting the omission of part of the obligatory prayer warrants, all the more, the omission of its regular sunnah prayer. As one of the Companions said:

“If I were going to offer voluntary prayers, I would have performed the obligatory prayer in full.”

For if it were recommended for a traveller to pray four rakʿahs, it would be more appropriate for him to offer the midday prayer as four rakʿahs than to offer two obligatory rakʿahs and two sunnah rakʿahs.

---

## Page 12260

This is because the mass-transmitted Sunnah of the Messenger of Allah (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) establishes that, when travelling, he prayed only two rakʿahs for the midday, afternoon and night prayers. Likewise, when he led the people on the Farewell Pilgrimage, he led them in only two rakʿahs at Minā and elsewhere. Abū Bakr after him likewise prayed only two rakʿahs, as did ʿUmar after him. Anyone who reports that the Prophet (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) prayed four rakʿahs for the midday, afternoon or night prayer while travelling is mistaken. The hadith narrated from ʿĀʾishah concerning this is weak to begin with, quite apart from the distortion that has occurred in its wording.

The wording of the hadith is that she said to the Prophet (may Allah bless him and grant him peace):

“You did not fast, whereas I fasted? And you shortened the prayer, whereas I performed it in full?”

He replied: “You did right, ʿĀʾishah.”

Despite its weakness and the evidence establishing its falsity, this report was transmitted in a form stating that ʿĀʾishah reported that the Prophet (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) would both fast and refrain from fasting, and would both shorten the prayer and perform it in full. Some of the imams therefore thought that the hadith contained her report of both practices from the Messenger of Allah (may Allah bless him and grant him peace). This is discussed at length in its proper place.

The point here is that the Sunnah for a traveller is to pray two rakʿahs. The imams agree that this is preferable, apart from a less well-supported opinion of al-Shāfiʿī. Most of the imams regard it as disliked for a traveller to pray four rakʿahs. This is the position of Abū Hanīfah, Mālik and Ahmad, according to the more explicit of the two reports from Ahmad. Some of these scholars hold that praying four rakʿahs is not permissible

---

## Page 12261

as Abū Hanīfah holds. Others say that it is permissible but disliked, as Mālik and Ahmad hold.

The response is this: if it were beloved to Allah for a traveller to pray two rakʿahs followed by another two, He would have recommended that he perform the obligatory prayer as four rakʿahs. Drawing near to Him through part of the obligatory midday prayer is better than drawing near to Him through voluntary prayer accompanying it. This is why He has made four rakʿahs obligatory for a resident. If a resident wished to pray two obligatory rakʿahs and two voluntary rakʿahs, that would not be permissible for him. Allah Most High never requires one thing of him and forbids him another unless what He commands is better than what He forbids. Thus, performing the midday prayer as four rakʿahs is better in Allah’s sight than performing it as two rakʿahs accompanied by two voluntary rakʿahs.

Since He, glorified be He, has not recommended that a traveller pray four rakʿahs in the better of these two ways in His sight, all the more would He not recommend praying four in the way He regards as less preferable. This sound reasoning thus establishes that the practice of the Messenger of Allah (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) is the most perfect, that his guidance is the best guidance, and that it is better for a traveller to confine himself to the two obligatory rakʿahs than to accompany them with two Sunnah rakʿahs.

This also makes clear that, if the Friday prayer is a shortened midday prayer, it is not Sunnah to accompany it with the Sunnah prayer associated with a resident’s midday prayer. Rather, it should be treated like a traveller’s shortened midday prayer.

When travelling, the Prophet (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) would perform the two Sunnah rakʿahs of the dawn prayer and Witr. He would pray on his mount in whichever direction it carried him, and would perform Witr on it, except that he

---

## Page 12262

would not perform the obligatory prayer on it. This is because the dawn prayer is not shortened when travelling, so its Sunnah prayer remains unchanged, unlike the prayers that are shortened during travel. Witr is an independent prayer, like the rest of the night prayer, which is the best prayer after the obligatory prayers. In some respects, the Sunnah prayer before dawn also comes under night prayer. For this reason, the Prophet (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) would perform it when travelling: it is independent, and the reason for offering it remains in force.

The correct position is to say:

There is no regular Sunnah prayer with a prescribed number of rakʿahs before the Friday prayer, even if there had been two calls to prayer in his time. It is authentically reported from him in the Sahīh that he said:

“Between every two calls to prayer there is a prayer; between every two calls to prayer there is a prayer; between every two calls to prayer there is a prayer.”

Then, the third time, he added, “For whoever wishes,” lest people adopt it as an established Sunnah.

This authentic hadith indicates that prayer is prescribed before the afternoon prayer, before the night prayer, and before the sunset prayer, but that it is not a regular Sunnah prayer. It is also established that his Companions would pray between the two calls for the sunset prayer while he watched them. He neither forbade them nor instructed them to do so, nor did he do so himself. This indicates that it is permissible.

Some have cited his words as evidence for praying before the Friday prayer:

“Between every two calls to prayer there is a prayer.”

Others have countered this by saying: The call to prayer given from the pulpits did not exist during the time of the Messenger of Allah (may Allah bless him and grant him peace). Rather, ʿUthmān ordered it when the population increased during his time, and the people could no longer hear the call given when he came out and sat on the pulpit.

It may reasonably be said that when ʿUthmān instituted this call to prayer

---

## Page 12263

and the Muslims agreed upon it, it became a call to prayer sanctioned by Islamic law. Prayer between it and the second call would then be permissible and commendable, but not a regular Sunnah prayer, just like prayer before the sunset prayer. Thus, neither those who offer it nor those who omit it should be censured. This is the most balanced view, and Imam Ahmad’s statements support it.

Omitting it may then be preferable if people who lack knowledge think that it is a regular Sunnah prayer or obligatory. It should be omitted so that people understand that it is neither a regular Sunnah prayer nor obligatory. This is especially so if people observe it consistently: it should sometimes be omitted so that it does not resemble an obligatory prayer. Similarly, most scholars recommended not invariably reciting Sūrat al-Sajdah (The Prostration) on Fridays, although it is established in an authentic hadith that the Prophet (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) did so. If such constant observance is discouraged, then it is all the more appropriate not to observe constantly something the Prophet (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) did not institute.

If a man sometimes offers this prayer between the two calls as an unrestricted voluntary prayer, or as a prayer between the two calls, just as he prays before the afternoon and night prayers—not as a regular Sunnah prayer—then this is permissible.

If a man is among people who offer it, and they heed him, such that if he omitted it and explained the Sunnah to them, they would not object but would instead come to understand the Sunnah, then omitting it is commendable. But if they do not heed him, and he considers that offering it would win their hearts over to something more beneficial, or avert disputes and ill feeling because he is unable to explain the truth to them and gain their acceptance of it

---

## Page 12264

or for similar reasons, then offering it is also commendable. Thus, the same act may sometimes be recommended and at other times be better omitted, depending on which course offers the greater benefit according to the evidence of Islamic law.

A Muslim may refrain from a recommended act if performing it would cause harm outweighing its benefit. Thus, the Prophet (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) refrained from rebuilding the House upon Abraham’s foundations. He said to ‘Ā’ishah:

“Were it not that your people have only recently left the pre-Islamic age of ignorance, I would have demolished the Ka‘bah, brought it down to ground level, and given it two doors: one through which people would enter and another through which they would leave.”

This hadith is recorded in the two Sahīh collections. The Prophet (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) therefore refrained from what he considered the better of the two courses because of an overriding consideration: Quraysh had only recently embraced Islam, and doing this would have alienated them. The harm therefore outweighed the benefit.

For this reason, leading scholars, including Ahmad, recommended that an imam forgo what he considers preferable when doing so would foster harmony among those praying behind him. For example, he may consider it preferable to separate the witr prayer by concluding the even-numbered units with the closing salutation and then offering the single unit of witr, while leading people who accept only an uninterrupted witr prayer. If he cannot bring them to adopt the preferable practice, the benefit of accommodating them by offering witr without a break outweighs the benefit of separating it while they dislike praying behind him. Likewise, if he considers it preferable to recite the basmalah (“In the name of Allah”) quietly or aloud, while those praying behind him hold the opposite view

---

## Page 12265

, and he adopts the practice he considers less preferable for the sake of agreement and harmony, whose benefit outweighs that of the preferable practice, then doing so is permissible and commendable.

Likewise, departing from the preferable practice in order to explain the Sunnah and teach it to those unfamiliar with it is commendable. For example, one may recite the opening supplication, the prayer for refuge in Allah, or the basmalah aloud so that people learn that reciting these in prayer is commendable and prescribed. Thus, it is recorded in the Sahīh that ‘Umar ibn al-Khattāb recited the opening supplication aloud. He would pronounce the opening takbīr and say:

“Glory and praise be to You, O Allah. Blessed is Your name, exalted is Your majesty, and there is no god but You.”

Al-Aswad ibn Yazīd said: “I prayed behind ‘Umar more than seventy times. He would pronounce the opening takbīr and then say these words.” Muslim recorded this in his Sahīh. This is why this opening supplication became widespread, until most people adopted it.

Likewise, Ibn ‘Umar and Ibn ‘Abbās would recite the prayer for refuge in Allah aloud, and several Companions would recite the basmalah aloud. According to the majority of leading scholars, who do not consider reciting it aloud a regular Sunnah practice, this was done to teach people that reciting it in prayer is part of the Sunnah. Similarly, it is recorded in the Sahīh that Ibn ‘Abbās offered a funeral prayer and recited the Opening Chapter of the Quran aloud, explaining that he had done so to teach people that it is part of the Sunnah.

This is because there are two views concerning the funeral prayer. Some hold that it involves no Quran recitation at all, as many of the early generations maintained. This is the position of Abū Hanīfah and Mālik.

---

## Page 12266

Others regard Quran recitation in the funeral prayer as a Sunnah practice, as do al-Shāfiʿī and Ahmad, on the basis of this hadith of Ibn ʿAbbās and other reports. Among these scholars, some hold that recitation is obligatory, as it is in the regular prayer. Others hold that it is a recommended Sunnah practice, not an obligation.

This is the most balanced of the three views, for the early generations followed both practices, and both were well known among them. They offered funeral prayers both with and without Quran recitation, just as they sometimes recited the basmalah aloud in prayer and sometimes did not; sometimes recited an opening supplication and sometimes omitted it; sometimes raised their hands at the three junctures and sometimes did not; sometimes concluded the prayer with two salutations of peace and sometimes with one; sometimes recited quietly behind the imam and sometimes did not recite; and sometimes pronounced four takbīrs in the funeral prayer, sometimes five, and sometimes seven. Some among them followed one practice, while others followed another. All of this is established from the Companions.

It is likewise established that some of them, when giving the adhān, repeated the two testimonies of faith aloud after first reciting them quietly, while others did not. Some pronounced the phrases of the iqāmah, the call to commence prayer, singly, while others pronounced them in pairs. Both forms are established from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace.

---

## Page 12267

Thus, in these matters, although one practice may be preferable to another, whoever follows the less preferable practice has done something permissible. Indeed, following the less preferable practice may become preferable because of an overriding benefit, just as forgoing the preferable practice may sometimes be preferable because of an overriding benefit.

This applies to acts of worship generally: an act belonging to a superior category may, in certain circumstances, be surpassed by another. Prayer as a category is superior to Quran recitation, Quran recitation is superior to remembrance of Allah, and remembrance is superior to supplication. Yet prayer after Fajr and ʿAsr is prohibited, and Quran recitation, remembrance, and supplication are superior to it at those times. Likewise, Quran recitation during bowing and prostration is prohibited, and remembrance in those positions is superior to it. Supplication at the end of the prayer, after the tashahhud, is superior to remembrance.

An otherwise less meritorious act may also be preferable in view of a particular person’s circumstances: he may be unable to perform the superior act, or he may have greater love, desire, and concern for the less meritorious act and derive greater benefit from it. It is then preferable for him because of the greater effort, love, resolve, and benefit accompanying it. Similarly, a sick person may benefit from a medicine he desires more than from one he does not desire, even if the latter belongs to a superior category.

This explains why remembrance may, for some people at certain times, be better than Quran recitation, and Quran recitation may, for others at certain times, be better than prayer, along with other such examples. This is because of the full benefit the person derives from it, not because it belongs to a superior category.

---

## Page 12268

In this subject—“The relative merits of deeds”—unless one understands which deeds are preferable, and that for many deeds this may vary with circumstances, much confusion will arise. Some people, once they believe an act to be recommended and preferable, observe it more diligently than they observe their obligations, until this leads them into following their desires, partisanship, and the zealotry of pre-Islamic ignorance. You find this among those who choose certain practices and regard them as distinguishing marks of their school. Others, when they consider refraining from such an act preferable, are more diligent in refraining from it than in avoiding prohibited acts, until this too leads them into following their desires and the zealotry of pre-Islamic ignorance. You find this among those who regard abstention as a distinguishing mark of their school, and in similar cases. All of this is wrong.

Rather, one must give everything its due, allow the latitude that Allah and His Messenger have allowed, and bring together what Allah and His Messenger have brought together. In doing so, one must take account of the benefits and objectives prescribed by the religion that Allah and His Messenger love. One must know that the best speech is the speech of Allah and the best guidance is the guidance of Muhammad, may Allah bless him and grant him peace; and that Allah sent him as a mercy to all worlds, bringing happiness in this world and the Hereafter in every matter.

One must also possess enough detailed understanding to uphold these general principles. Otherwise, many people believe in them in general but abandon them when it comes to particulars, whether out of ignorance, wrongdoing, or following their desires. We therefore ask Allah to guide us to the straight path, the path of those whom Allah has blessed among the prophets and the steadfastly truthful

---

## Page 12269

and the martyrs and the righteous. How excellent they are as companions!

Section

As for the Sunnah prayer after the Friday prayer, it is established in the two Sahīh collections that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, “used to pray two rak‘ahs after the Friday prayer.” It is likewise established in these two collections that he “used to pray two rak‘ahs before Fajr, two after Zuhr, two after Maghrib, and two after ‘Ishā’.”

As for Zuhr, Ibn ‘Umar’s hadith states that “he used to pray two rak‘ahs before it,” whereas ‘Ā’ishah’s report in the two Sahīh collections states that “he used to pray four before it.” In the Sahīh, Umm Habība reports that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Whoever prays twelve voluntary rak‘ahs over the course of a day and a night, apart from the obligatory prayers, Allah will build for him a house in Paradise.”

These are specified in the Sunan collections as:

“Four before Zuhr and two after it, two after Maghrib, two after ‘Ishā’, and two before Fajr.”

These, then, are the regular Sunnah prayers established in authentic reports through the words and practice of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. They rest upon these three hadiths: those of Ibn ‘Umar, ‘Ā’ishah, and Umm Habība.

The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, used to pray either eleven or thirteen rak‘ahs at night. Thus, the total of his prayers by night and day, obligatory and voluntary, was approximately forty rak‘ahs.

---

## Page 12270

People hold three views concerning these regular Sunnah prayers:

Some prescribe no fixed practice in this regard. This is Mālik’s position, for he recognizes no regular Sunnah prayer apart from Witr and the two rak‘ahs before Fajr. He used to say, “Only the people of Iraq lay down such fixed prescriptions.”

Others prescribe particular prayers on the basis of weak, indeed baseless, hadiths, as found in the schools of the people of Iraq and among some followers of al-Shāfi‘ī and Ahmad who agree with them. Their books contain prescribed prayers and reports concerning them which those knowledgeable about the Sunnah know to have been falsely attributed to the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. Examples include reports that “he prayed four rak‘ahs before ‘Asr,” that “he made up the Sunnah prayer of ‘Asr,” that “he prayed six rak‘ahs before Zuhr,” or “four after it,” or that “he regularly observed the Duhā prayer,” and similar hadiths falsely attributed to the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace.

More serious still is what some authors writing on “heart-softening exhortations and the merits of good deeds” mention concerning weekly and annual prayers. These include the prayers for Sunday, Monday, Tuesday, Wednesday, Thursday, Friday, and Saturday mentioned in the works of Abū Tālib, Abū Hāmid, ‘Abd al-Qādir, and others. They also include the “thousandfold” prayer, al-Alfiyya, at the beginning of Rajab and in mid-Sha‘bān; the “twelvefold” prayer, al-Ithnā ‘Ashariyya, on the first Friday night of Rajab; and the prayer on the night of the twenty-seventh of

---

## Page 12271

Rajab, other prayers mentioned for the three months, prayers on the nights of the two Eids, the prayer on the day of ‘Āshūrā’, and similar prayers attributed to the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, despite the agreement of those knowledgeable about his hadith that these attributions are false. Yet these reports reached some people of knowledge and religious devotion, who thought them authentic and acted upon them. They are rewarded for their good intentions and earnest efforts, not for departing from the Sunnah. As for someone to whom the Sunnah has become clear, yet who believes something else to be better than it, he is misguided, an innovator—indeed, a disbeliever.

The balanced and just position is that which accords with the authentic Sunnah established from him, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. It is established that he used to pray two rak‘ahs after the Friday prayer. In Sahīh Muslim, he is reported to have said:

“Whoever among you intends to pray after the Friday prayer should pray four rak‘ahs.”

It has also been reported that a number of the Companions prayed six rak‘ahs, combining these two practices. The Sunnah is to separate the obligatory prayer from the voluntary prayer, whether on Friday or otherwise. As established in an authentic report:

“He, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, forbade joining one prayer directly to another without separating them by standing up or speaking.”

One should therefore not do what many people do: proceed directly from the closing salutation to the two Sunnah rak‘ahs, for this contravenes the prohibition of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace.

---

## Page 12272

And part of the wisdom in this is to distinguish obligatory prayer from other prayers, just as acts of worship are distinguished from other acts. This is why it is recommended to hasten the breaking of the fast, delay the predawn meal, and eat before the prayer on Eid al-Fitr, and why fasting a day or two immediately before Ramadan is forbidden. All of this serves to distinguish prescribed fasting from fasting that is not prescribed, and worship from other acts. The same applies to distinguishing the Friday prayer, which Allah has made obligatory, from other prayers.

Moreover, many adherents of innovation, such as the Rāfidah and others, do not intend to perform the Friday prayer; rather, they intend to perform Zuhr. They make it appear that they have given the closing salutation when they have not. They thus pray Zuhr while an observer supposes that they are praying the Sunnah prayer. Distinguishing obligatory prayer from voluntary prayer therefore helps prevent this innovation. There are many similar examples. And Allah, Glorified is He, knows best.

---

## Page 12273

He was asked:

A man sets out for the Friday prayer when the prayer has already begun. Should he run to reach it, or come calmly, even if he misses it?

He answered:

Praise be to Allah. If he fears missing the Friday prayer, he should hasten so that he catches at least one rakʿah. But if he can catch it while walking calmly, that is better; indeed, that is the Sunnah. And Allah knows best.

He was asked:

Must Sūrat al-Sajdah (The Prostration) always be recited in prayer on Friday?

He answered:

Praise be to Allah. Reciting the surah beginning “Alif Lām Mīm. The revelation,” which contains a verse of prostration, or any other surah containing a verse of prostration, is not obligatory in the Friday Fajr prayer, by agreement of the imams. Whoever believes this to be obligatory, or censures someone for omitting it, is misguided and mistaken, and must

---

## Page 12274

repent of this, by agreement of the imams. The scholars differed only over whether this recitation is recommended or disliked. Mālik considers it disliked to recite a passage containing a prostration verse in prayers recited aloud. The correct view, however, is that it is not disliked, as held by Abū Hanīfah, al-Shāfi‘ī and Ahmad. For it is established in an authentic report that “the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, prostrated during the ‘Ishā’ prayer when reciting ‘When the sky is split apart.’” It is also established in the two Sahīh collections that “he used to recite ‘Alif Lām Mīm. The revelation’ and ‘Has there come’ in the Fajr prayer on Friday.”

Mālik also considers it disliked to single out a particular surah for recitation. Al-Shāfi‘ī and Ahmad, however, recommend the recitations established in the Sunnah, such as al-Jumu‘ah (Friday) and al-Munāfiqūn (The Hypocrites) in the Friday prayer; al-Dhāriyāt (The Winnowing Winds) and Iqtarabat (The Hour Has Drawn Near) in the Eid prayer; and ‘Alif Lām Mīm. The revelation’ and ‘Has there come’ in the Friday Fajr prayer.

There are, however, two useful points here:

First, by agreement of the imams, it is not recommended to recite another surah containing a prostration verse in its place. The recommendation concerns these two surahs, not the prostration itself; the prostration verse is incidental to their recitation. These two surahs mention creation and resurrection, which take place on Friday.

Second, this recitation should not be maintained so invariably that those who lack knowledge imagine it to be obligatory and regard anyone who omits it as doing wrong. Rather, it should be omitted occasionally, since it is not obligatory. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 12275

He was asked about reciting Surah al-Sajdah (The Prostration) on Friday: “Is the prostration what is intended, so that reciting part of the surah or prostrating during another surah would suffice, or is the surah itself what is intended?”

He answered:

All praise is due to Allah. Rather, what is intended is the recitation of the two surahs, “Alif Lām Mīm. The revelation” and “Has there come upon man,” because they mention the creation of Adam, the coming of the Hour, and related matters, for these events occur on Friday. The prostration itself is not what is intended. Thus, deliberately choosing another surah containing a prostration verse would be disliked.

The Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, used to recite both surahs, so the Sunnah is to recite them in full. This should not be done invariably, lest someone without knowledge think it obligatory. Rather, other passages of the Quran should sometimes be recited instead.

Al-Shāfi‘ī and Ahmad recommend reciting these two surahs. Mālik and Abū Hanīfah, however, consider deliberately choosing them for recitation disliked.

---

## Page 12276

He was asked:

“If someone catches one rak‘ah of the Friday prayer, then stands to complete what he has missed, should he recite aloud or not?”

He answered:

Rather, he should recite quietly, not aloud. When a latecomer stands to complete what he has missed, he is praying alone during that portion and is subject to the rulings governing someone praying alone. During the portion he catches with the imam, however, he is subject to the rulings governing a follower. Hence, if he makes an error during the portion he is completing, he performs the prostrations of forgetfulness.

Accordingly, a latecomer recites aloud only where someone praying alone would do so. Scholars who hold that someone praying alone should recite aloud in Maghrib, ‘Ishā’, and Fajr likewise hold that a latecomer should recite aloud when completing the first two rak‘ahs. Those who hold that someone praying alone should not recite aloud likewise hold that a latecomer should not do so.

No one performs the Friday prayer alone, so there can be no question of someone praying alone reciting aloud in it. A latecomer is like someone praying alone and therefore does not recite aloud. Nevertheless, he is counted as having caught the Friday prayer through his participation in it as a follower. What is subordinate need not fulfil the conditions required of what it follows. Thus, the required number of worshippers and similar conditions do not apply to the portion that the latecomer completes.

However, the established Sunnah is that whoever catches one rak‘ah of a prayer has caught that prayer. Thus, he has caught the Friday prayer, just as someone who catches one rak‘ah of ‘Asr before the sun sets,

---

## Page 12277

and someone who catches one rak‘ah of Fajr before sunrise have both caught the prayer, even if the remainder is performed outside its prescribed time. And Allah knows best.

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked about the Friday prayer in the citadel’s congregational mosque:

“Is it permissible, given that another Friday sermon is delivered in the city, while the citadel has its own walls and its gates are closed?”

He answered:

Yes, the Friday prayer may be held there, because it constitutes a separate city, as in the case of Misr and Cairo. Even if it did not constitute a separate city, most scholars permit holding the Friday prayer in two places in a large city when there is a need. Thus, when Baghdad was built with its two sides, a Friday prayer was established on the eastern side and another on the western side. Most scholars permitted this. They drew an analogy with the fact that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, [did not pray] (1) in his city except in one place: he would take the Muslims out and offer the Eid prayer in the open country. This remained the practice during the caliphates of Abū Bakr, ‘Umar, and ‘Uthmān.

When ‘Alī ibn Abī Tālib became caliph and settled in Kufa, which had a large population, they said:

“O Commander of the Faithful, there are elderly and weak people in the city for whom going out to the open country is difficult.”

So he appointed

> **Footnotes:**
> (1) Thus in the original.
> Shaykh Nāsir ibn Hamad al-Fahd states (p. 201):
> The omitted wording appears to be: “that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, [did not pray] in his city except in [one] place; he would take the Muslims out…”—or words to this effect. And Allah Most High knows best.

---

## Page 12278

a man to lead the people in the Eid prayer in the mosque, while he, ‘Alī ibn Abī Tālib, led the people out in the open country. This had not been done before, and ‘Alī was one of the Rightly Guided Caliphs. The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Adhere to my Sunnah and the Sunnah of the Rightly Guided Caliphs after me.”

Whoever adheres to the Sunnah of the Rightly Guided Caliphs has therefore obeyed Allah and His Messenger. In these lands and in these times, necessity calls for more than one Friday congregation, since there is no single congregational mosque large enough to accommodate everyone, and they cannot gather for a single Friday prayer without great hardship.

There is also a third consideration: the citadel may be treated as a village outside the city. The majority of scholars, including Mālik, al-Shāfi‘ī and Ahmad, hold that Friday prayers are to be held in villages. This is because it is reported in the Sahīh collection that Ibn ‘Abbās said:

“The first Friday prayer held in Islam after the Friday prayer in Medina was the Friday prayer” at Juwāthā, “one of the villages of Bahrain.”

This occurred during the lifetime of the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, when the delegation of ‘Abd al-Qays came to him. Likewise, ‘Umar ibn al-Khattāb wrote to the Muslims instructing them to hold Friday prayers wherever they were. ‘Abd Allāh ibn ‘Umar would pass by the settlements around watering places between Mecca and Medina, where people were holding Friday prayers, and he did not object.

As for the statement of ‘Alī, may Allah be pleased with him, “There is no Friday prayer or Eid prayer except in a central town,” even if no one had disagreed with him, it could be understood to mean that every village is a central town, just as a central town may be called a village. Allah called Mecca a village; indeed, He called it

---

## Page 12279

“the Mother of Towns.” He also called places greater than Mecca villages, as in His words:

“How many a village, mightier than your village which drove you out, have We destroyed, leaving them no helper.”

He also called ancient Egypt a village, saying:

“Ask the village where we were and the caravan with which we came.”

There are many similar examples in the Quran. And Allah knows best.

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

Two men disagreed about Eid when it falls on a Friday. One said, “He must offer the Eid prayer and not offer the Friday prayer,” while the other said, “He should offer the Friday prayer.” What is correct concerning this?

He replied:

Praise be to Allah. When Friday and Eid fall on the same day, the scholars hold three views:

The first is that Friday prayer remains obligatory for those who attended the Eid prayer, just as it is on all other Fridays, because of the general texts establishing the obligation of Friday prayer.

The second is that the obligation is waived for people living in the countryside, such as the inhabitants of al-‘Awālī and outlying settlements, because ‘Uthmān ibn ‘Affān permitted them to miss the Friday prayer after leading them in the Eid prayer.

---

## Page 12280

The third view—and it is the correct one—is that whoever attends the Eid prayer is exempt from the obligation to attend Friday prayer. However, the imam must hold Friday prayer so that those who wish to attend, and those who did not attend the Eid prayer, may do so.

This is what is reported from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and his Companions, including ‘Umar, ‘Uthmān, Ibn Mas‘ūd, Ibn ‘Abbās, Ibn al-Zubayr, and others. No disagreement among the Companions is known on this matter.

Those who held the first two views had not received the relevant reports from the Sunnah of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. When the two festivals coincided during his lifetime, he offered the Eid prayer and then permitted people to miss Friday prayer. According to one wording, he said:

“O people, you have attained good. Whoever wishes to attend Friday prayer, let him attend, for we shall be holding it.”

Moreover, attending the Eid prayer fulfils the purpose of gathering together. If a person does not attend Friday prayer, he then offers the noon prayer, so the noon prayer is offered at its proper time, while the Eid prayer fulfils the purpose of Friday prayer. Making Friday prayer obligatory upon people would impose hardship on them and diminish the purpose of their Eid and the joy and relaxation prescribed for them on that day. If they were prevented from enjoying these things, Eid would defeat its own purpose.

Furthermore, Friday is a festival, as are Eid al-Fitr and Eid al-Adhā. It is the Lawgiver’s practice, when two acts of worship of the same kind coincide, to include one within the other, just as He includes ablution within a ritual bath, and one ritual bath within another. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 12281

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked about a man who said:

“If Eid falls on a Friday and a person offers the Eid prayer, he may offer Friday prayer if he wishes; otherwise, he need not.”

Was he correct or mistaken in saying this?

He replied:

All praise is due to Allah, Lord of the worlds. May Allah bestow abundant blessings and peace upon our master Muhammad, his family, and his Companions.

When Friday and Eid coincide, the jurists hold three views:

The first is that Friday prayer remains obligatory upon those who have offered the Eid prayer and those who have not. This is the view of Mālik and others.

The second is that the obligation to attend Friday prayer is waived for those living in the rural districts outside the town. This accords with the report that ‘Uthmān ibn ‘Affān, may Allah be pleased with him, offered the Eid prayer and then permitted the villagers to miss Friday prayer. Al-Shāfi‘ī followed this view.

The third is that whoever offers the Eid prayer is exempt from the obligation to attend Friday prayer. However, it is appropriate

---

## Page 12282

for the imam to hold Friday prayer so that whoever wishes may attend. As reported in the Sunan collections:

“Eid and Friday coincided during the lifetime of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. He offered the Eid prayer, then granted permission not to attend Friday prayer.”

In another wording, he offered the Eid prayer and addressed the people, saying:

“O people, you have received much good. Whoever among you wishes to attend Friday prayer may do so, for we shall be holding it.”

This hadith is narrated in the Sunan collections through two routes of transmission: he offered the Eid prayer, then gave the people the choice of whether to attend Friday prayer.

There is also a third report on this matter in the Sunan collections: Eid and Friday coincided during Ibn al-Zubayr’s time, so he combined them at the beginning of the day and thereafter prayed only ‘Asr. He mentioned that ‘Umar ibn al-Khattāb, may Allah be pleased with him, had done the same. This was mentioned to Ibn ‘Abbās, may Allah be pleased with him, who said, “He has followed the Sunnah correctly.”

What is thus transmitted is established from the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, his caliphs, and his Companions. It is also the view of those imams whom these reports reached, such as Ahmad and others. Those who disagreed had not received the Prophetic reports and accounts from the early generations concerning this matter. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 12283

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked about a sermon between two prayers, each obligatory at its appointed time, at a time whose precise identity is unclear, and about the conditions applicable to it, as with other religious observances: the noon prayer and the Sunnah prayers, the appointed time and the direction of prayer, and likewise the call to prayer.

He replied:

Praise be to Allah.

This question may refer to several situations, some agreed upon and others disputed.

One is when Eid and Friday coincide. Whoever holds that the Eid prayer is obligatory would say that the Friday sermon is a sermon between two prayers, both obligatory, unlike the Eid sermon, which he does not regard as obligatory.

Alternatively, it may refer to two Friday prayers held in a place where two Friday prayers cannot both be valid. The first is valid and the second invalid if both were authorized by the imam. If it is unclear which took place first, both are invalid, and they offer the noon prayer instead. Thus, the sermon preceding the second prayer is a sermon between two prayers, both obligatory, if the imam has authorized each of them

---

## Page 12284

and they believe that Friday prayer is not held in their locality, while each of the two congregations believes its own Friday prayer to be obligatory.

The questioner may also mean the dawn prayer and the Friday prayer: the dawn prayer is obligatory at its appointed time, and the Friday prayer is obligatory at its appointed time. Between them comes a sermon—the Friday sermon.

Other examples include the sermons delivered during Hajj. The sermon at Arafah comes between the prayer at Arafah and the sunset prayer, both of which are obligatory. The sermon on the Day of Sacrifice comes between the dawn and noon prayers, both of which are obligatory.

He was asked:

Is there a hadith concerning the recitation of Sūrat al-Kahf after the afternoon prayer on Friday?

He replied:

Praise be to Allah. There are reports concerning the recitation of Sūrat al-Kahf on Friday, mentioned by scholars of hadith and jurisprudence. However, these refer generally to Friday without specifying a particular time. I have not heard that this is specifically prescribed after the afternoon prayer. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 12285

He was also asked:

Is it permissible to spread a prayer rug in the blessed Rawdah (Garden)?

He replied:

No one may spread anything out to reserve a place for himself while he is absent, thereby preventing others from using it. This is an unlawful appropriation of that space and prevents Muslims from performing the prayer that Allah Most High has commanded. The Sunnah is to come early in person. Whoever reserves a place in advance with a prayer rug is acting unjustly and must be stopped. Such rugs must be removed, and people must be allowed to use the spaces they occupy.

Moreover, the very practice of spreading such rugs is a religious innovation, especially in the Prophet’s Mosque, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. The Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and his Companions used to pray on the ground. The palm-leaf mat on which the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, prayed was small, not the size of a prayer rug.

I said: Ibn Hazm related in al-Muhallā, on the authority of ‘Atā’ ibn Abī Rabāh, that

---

## Page 12286

it is not permissible to pray in a mosque except directly on the ground. When ‘Abd al-Rahmān ibn Mahdī arrived from Iraq and spread out a rug in the mosque, Mālik ibn Anas ordered him imprisoned as a disciplinary punishment. When asked to reconsider the matter, he explained that doing this in such a mosque was a religious innovation whose perpetrator should be disciplined. People must denounce this practice and prevent it, especially those authorities responsible for the mosque: they are duty-bound to remove these prayer rugs. If their owners were punished by having the rugs given away in charity, that would be permissible as a matter of scholarly judgment. End quote.

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was also asked:

Concerning the muezzin’s saying on Friday, when the imam enters the mosque:

“O Allah, bless Muhammad and the family of Muhammad, and grant them peace. May Allah be pleased with all the Companions of the Messenger of Allah.”

And concerning the imam’s supplication after ascending the pulpit, and the muezzin’s saying after the second call to prayer:

“Abū Hurayrah, may Allah be pleased with him, reported that the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said: ‘If you say to your companion, “Be quiet,” while the imam is delivering the sermon on Friday, you have engaged in idle speech.’”

Are these practices prescribed by the Sunnah, recommended, or disliked in the Friday prayer?

---

## Page 12287

He replied:

Praise be to Allah. This is not part of the Sunnah of the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, nor has any of the leading Muslim scholars recommended it. However, those who have publicly recited the hadith have done so to instruct people to listen attentively, and this is a kind of sermon. As for the imam’s supplication after ascending the pulpit and the muezzins’ raising their voices in invoking blessings upon the Prophet, the scholars have not mentioned this. Those who practise it do so without any basis in Islamic law. As for the muezzins’ raising their voices during the sermon, whether invoking blessings or saying anything else, this is disliked by agreement of the imams.

He was also asked:

Concerning a muezzin who says, when the preacher enters the congregational mosque:

“Indeed, Allah and His angels send blessings upon the Prophet.”

A man said, “This is a religious innovation.” What, then, is required of him?

He replied:

The muezzin’s reciting this aloud—like his audibly invoking blessings upon the Prophet and asking Allah to be pleased with the Companions when the preacher ascends the pulpit, or audibly supplicating for the preacher and the imam, and similar practices—did not occur during the time of the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, or his Rightly Guided Caliphs. Nor has any of the imams recommended it. Still worse is saying such things aloud during the sermon. All of this is religious innovation, and Allah knows best.

---

## Page 12288

Chapter: The Two Eid Prayers

Shaykh al-Islām was asked:

Is any particular recitation required in the two Eid prayers? What should a person say between successive takbīrs (declarations that Allah is greatest)?

He replied:

Praise be to Allah. Whatever he recites is permissible, just as it is in other similar prayers. However, it is good to recite Sūrat Qāf and Sūrat al-Qamar, or other passages mentioned in the transmitted reports.

Between the takbīrs, he should praise and extol Allah, invoke blessings upon the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and offer whatever supplications he wishes. The scholars have transmitted something to this effect from ʿAbd Allāh ibn Masʿūd.

It is good if he says:

“Glory be to Allah, praise be to Allah, there is no god but Allah, and Allah is greatest. O Allah, bless Muhammad and the family of Muhammad. O Allah, forgive me and have mercy on me.”

The same applies if he says:

“Allah is supremely great, abundant praise belongs to Allah, and glory be to Allah morning and evening,”

or something similar.

---

## Page 12289

and no fixed wording for this has been transmitted from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, or the Companions. Allah knows best.

He was asked:

What is the wording of the takbīr on the two Eids, and when should it be said?

He replied:

Praise be to Allah.

The soundest view concerning takbīr, held by the majority of the early Muslims and jurists among the Companions and the leading scholars, is that it should be said after every prayer from dawn on the Day of ʿArafah until the last of the days of Tashrīq. It is also prescribed for everyone to proclaim the takbīr aloud when going out to the Eid prayer. This is agreed upon by the four imams.

The wording of the takbīr transmitted from most of the Companions, and also reported as traced back to the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, is:

“Allah is greatest, Allah is greatest. There is no god but Allah. Allah is greatest, Allah is greatest, and all praise belongs to Allah.”

If one says “Allah is greatest” three times, that is permissible. Some jurists say “Allah is greatest” three times only; others say it three times and then add:

“There is no god but Allah alone, with no partner. To Him belongs sovereignty, and to Him belongs all praise, and He has power over all things.”

As for the takbīrs within the prayer, the person praying behind the imam follows him in saying them. Most of the Companions, may Allah be pleased with them, and the leading scholars say seven takbīrs in the first rakʿah and five in the second.

---

## Page 12290

And if he wishes to say between two takbīrs:

“Glory be to Allah, all praise belongs to Allah, there is no god but Allah, and Allah is greatest. O Allah, forgive me and have mercy on me.”

That is good, as this has been reported from some of the early Muslims. Allah knows best.

He was asked:

Is the takbīr more strongly required on Eid al-Fitr than on Eid al-Adhā? Please explain this to us; may you be rewarded.

He replied:

The takbīr is prescribed on Eid al-Adhā by agreement. It is also prescribed on Eid al-Fitr according to Mālik, al-Shāfiʿī and Ahmad. Al-Tahāwī also cited this as the position of Abū Hanīfah and his disciples, although their well-known position is otherwise. Nevertheless, proclaiming the takbīr on this occasion is what has been transmitted from the Companions, may Allah be pleased with them. It is more emphatically prescribed on this occasion insofar as Allah commanded it in His words:

“So that you may complete the prescribed period and glorify Allah for having guided you, and that you may be grateful.”

The time for this takbīr begins with the sighting of the crescent and ends when the Eid observance concludes, which, according to the correct view, is when the imam finishes the sermon. As for the takbīr on Eid al-Adhā, it is more emphatically prescribed insofar as it is prescribed after the prayers

---

## Page 12291

and its prescription is agreed upon, and because place and time come together on Eid al-Adhā. Eid al-Adhā is superior to Eid al-Fitr; hence, its acts of worship are sacrifice together with prayer, whereas those of Eid al-Fitr are charity together with prayer.

Sacrifice is superior to charity because it combines bodily and financial worship: slaughtering is both a bodily and a financial act of worship, whereas charity and giving gifts are financial acts of worship. Moreover, the charity given at Eid al-Fitr is an adjunct to fasting, for the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, prescribed it to purify the fasting person from idle and obscene speech and to provide food for the poor. It is therefore prescribed to give it before the prayer, as Allah Most High says:

“He has certainly succeeded who purifies himself, remembers the name of his Lord and prays.”

Sacrifice, by contrast, is prescribed on the day itself as an independent act of worship. It is therefore prescribed after the prayer, as Allah Most High says:

“So pray to your Lord and sacrifice. Indeed, your enemy is the one cut off.”

The prayer offered by people in the towns corresponds to the pilgrims’ stoning of Jamrat al-ʿAqabah, and their slaughtering in the towns corresponds to the pilgrims’ slaughtering of their sacrificial animals. A hadith recorded in the Sunan collections states:

“The best of days in Allah’s sight is the Day of Sacrifice, followed by the Day of Rest.”

Another hadith recorded in the Sunan collections, which al-Tirmidhī graded authentic, states:

“The Day of ʿArafah, the Day of Sacrifice and the days of Minā are our festival, we people of Islam. They are days of eating, drinking and remembering Allah.”

Thus, the correct scholarly view is that people in the towns proclaim the takbīr from dawn on the Day of ʿArafah until the end of the days of Tashrīq, on the basis of this hadith and another hadith narrated by al-Dāraqutnī from Jābir, from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. It is also the consensus of the leading Companions. Allah knows best.

---

## Page 12292

Shaykh al-Islām also said:

Section

Allah Most High says:

“And that you complete the prescribed number and glorify Allah for having guided you, and that you may be grateful.”

The particle lām (“that” or “so that”) is connected either to an explicitly stated verb, giving the meaning:

“Allah intends ease for you … and that you complete the prescribed number.”

This is like His saying:

“Allah intends to make clear to you.”

Or it is connected to an omitted verb, meaning: He prescribed this so that you may complete the prescribed number.

The latter interpretation is more common, because He says:

“And that you may be grateful.”

The first interpretation would require the wording to mean, “And He intends that perhaps you will be grateful,” which is a weak construction.

However, the first interpretation may be supported by His words in the verse concerning ablution:

“Allah does not intend to place any hardship upon you, but He intends to purify you and complete His favour upon you, that you may be grateful.”

The verse concerning fasting and the verse concerning purification correspond in both wording and meaning. Thus, His words:

“Allah intends ease for you and does not intend hardship for you”

correspond to His words:

“Allah does not intend to place any hardship upon you,”

and His words:

---

## Page 12293

“but He intends to purify you and complete His favour upon you”

correspond to His words:

“and that you complete the prescribed number and glorify Allah for having guided you, and that you may be grateful.”

The point here is that Allah, Glorified is He, wills, by way of religious prescription, that we proclaim His greatness (takbīr) for having guided us. Hence some of the early Muslims, such as Zayd ibn Aslam, said that this refers to the takbīr of Eid. The Muslim community unanimously agrees that the Eid prayer is distinguished by additional takbīrs. The reference to takbīr may also encompass the Eid prayer itself, just as prayer is called glorification, standing, prostration, and Quran recitation. Similarly, the two prayers offered together are included in the remembrance of Allah in His words:

“When you depart from Arafat, remember Allah at al-Mashʿar al-Harām.”

And the sermon and prayer are meant by His words:

“Hasten to the remembrance of Allah and leave off trade.”

This would mean that, because the prayer is called takbīr, it is distinguished by additional takbīrs, just as the dawn prayer, being called Quran recitation, is distinguished by additional recitation. Its longer recitation was made a substitute for the two additional units in a four-unit prayer. Likewise, “the night prayer,” being called standing in His words:

“Stand in prayer at night,”

is distinguished by prolonged standing. Thus the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, would prolong his standing, bowing, and prostration at night more than he did during the day.

For this reason, some of the early Muslims said that prolonging prayer at night is better, and that increasing the number of bowings and prostrations during the day

---

## Page 12294

is better. Additional takbīr is also prescribed in the Eid sermon, beyond that of Friday sermons. According to our school and most scholars, takbīr is prescribed from the onset of Eid until its conclusion, when the prayer and sermon have ended. But should someone praying behind the imam cease the takbīr upon arriving at the prayer ground, since he is then occupied with awaiting the prayer? Or should he cease when the prayer begins, since he will then be occupied with the prayer and sermon? Or should he continue until the sermon ends? Differing views on this are reported from Ahmad and others. The correct view is that it continues until the conclusion of Eid.

Concerning Hajj, Allah Most High says:

“So that they may witness benefits for themselves and mention Allah’s name on appointed days for the livestock He has provided for them.”

One interpretation is that the appointed days are the days of sacrifice, and that mentioning Allah’s name means invoking His name over the Eid sacrifices and the sacrificial offerings brought for pilgrimage. This is Mālik’s view according to one report.

Another interpretation is that they are the ten days. This is the well-known view reported from Ahmad, as well as the view of al-Shāfiʿī and others. According to our school, mentioning Allah’s name during these days means remembering Him through takbīr throughout the ten days. Another view is that it means remembering Him upon seeing the sacrificial offerings; I believe this is reported from al-Shāfiʿī.

Sahīh al-Bukhārī relates that Ibn ʿUmar and Ibn ʿAbbās would go out to the marketplace during the ten days and proclaim Allah’s greatness, and the people would do likewise in response to their takbīr. An authentic hadith collection also relates from Anas that, on the morning of ʿArafah, as they travelled from Minā to ʿArafah, some would proclaim Allah’s greatness without anyone objecting, while others would recite the talbiyah (pilgrimage invocation) without anyone objecting. And among

---

## Page 12295

the examples of hadiths traced back to the Prophet is his saying:

“So, during these days, frequently declare that there is no god but Allah, proclaim His greatness, and praise Him.”

According to our school, mentioning Allah’s name for what He has provided them is like His saying, “for having guided you,” and His saying:

“When you depart from ʿArafāt, remember Allah at the Sacred Monument, and remember Him as He has guided you.”

It is also like His saying:

“Just as We have sent among you a Messenger from yourselves, reciting Our verses to you and purifying you”—up to His words—“So remember Me; I will remember you.”

According to the other view, it is like His saying:

“So eat of what they catch for you, and mention Allah’s name over it.”

And His saying:

“So mention Allah’s name over them as they stand in rows.”

This is supported by His words, “of livestock,” which indicate that mā (“what”) is a relative pronoun, not a nominalizing particle. The meaning is thus: “over the livestock He has provided them.” Likewise, He says:

“For every community We have appointed a sacrificial rite, so that they may mention Allah’s name over the livestock He has provided them.”

According to our view, Allah’s name is mentioned over them both at slaughter and while they are being driven along, by reciting the talbiyah beside them and proclaiming Allah’s greatness. This is supported by the fact that, had He intended merely invoking His name at slaughter, there would be nothing distinctive about the Eid sacrifice in this respect. His name is mentioned whenever an animal is slaughtered, with no distinction between the Eid sacrifice and other slaughtered animals: whatever is obligatory in the one is obligatory in the other, and whatever is not obligatory in the one is not obligatory in the other.

---

## Page 12296

Moreover, there would be no particular significance to His saying:

“And proclaim the pilgrimage to the people; they will come to you on foot and on every lean camel”—up to His words—“so that they may witness benefits for themselves and mention Allah’s name.”

He thus made their coming to the sacred sites serve the purpose of witnessing benefits for themselves and mentioning Allah’s name during specified days. Had He intended only the Eid sacrifice, the sacred sites would have no distinctive connection with this, since the Eid sacrifice is prescribed throughout the earth. This argument, however, also raises an objection to our view that Allah’s name is to be mentioned throughout the ten days in all towns. It may be asked: why, then, did He associate this specifically with coming to the sacred sites?

Someone who holds that Allah should be remembered upon seeing the pilgrimage offerings might cite this as evidence, since these offerings are driven to Mecca. Yet according to him, an offering may be slaughtered whenever it arrives. What purpose, then, would there be in restricting this to the specified days?

This may be answered in two ways.

First, slaughter at the sacred sites is the primary practice, while the other towns follow Mecca. This is why the Festival of Sacrifice is the greater festival, and the Day of Sacrifice is the day of the greater pilgrimage: it combines the festival associated with the place and that associated with the time.

Second, Allah is remembered there for His provision of both Eid sacrifices and pilgrimage offerings, unlike places outside Mecca, where there are only Eid sacrifices. The Eid sacrifice is restricted to the specified days. For according to our school, pilgrimage offerings have an appointed time: someone who brings an offering with him may slaughter it only when leaving the consecrated state, and he may not leave that state until he has slaughtered his offering, as Allah Most High says:

“Until the sacrificial offering reaches its appointed place.”

The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, instructed his Companions during the Pilgrimage of

---

## Page 12297

Farewell to leave the consecrated state, except for those who had brought sacrificial offerings: they were not to leave it until they had slaughtered them. This is undisputed when someone arrives with his offering during the ten days. If he arrives with it before the ten days, however, there are two transmitted views.

If it is asked: Since the Quran and Sunnah command remembrance of Allah during the specified days, why was proclaiming His greatness after the prayers not prescribed during them, as it was prescribed during the festival days?

The answer is:

For the Festival of Breaking the Fast, proclaiming Allah’s greatness was prescribed from the night before the festival until its conclusion, but not after the prayers. This is because proclaiming His greatness after the prayers is more emphatically prescribed, and was therefore reserved for the greater festival. The festival days are five—the days of gathering—as the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“The Day of Arafah, the Day of Sacrifice, and the days of Minā are our festival, we people of Islam, and they are days of eating and drinking.”

Allah Most High also says:

“And remember Allah during the numbered days.”

According to the well-known view in our school, as well as the view of al-Shāfiʿī and others, these are the days of Tashrīq. Another view holds that they are the days of slaughter. According to the first view, remembering Allah during these days includes proclaiming His greatness after the prayers and when stoning the pillars, as the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Going between al-Safā and al-Marwah and stoning the pillars were prescribed only to establish the remembrance of Allah.”

The remembrance mentioned in these verses is unrestricted, although the Sunnah prescribes proclaiming Allah’s greatness at the Festival of Sacrifice—in its prayer and sermon, after its prayers, and when stoning its pillars. As for the remembrance in the verse on fasting, what is meant is proclaiming Allah’s greatness for His guidance. Thus, this is

---

## Page 12298

remembrance of Allah and a proclamation of His greatness for guidance, whereas there it is for provision. It is authentically reported that when the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, came within sight of Khaybar, he said:

“Allah is the Greatest! Khaybar is ruined! When we descend upon a people’s territory, evil is the morning of those who were warned.”

He would proclaim Allah’s greatness upon reaching elevated places—for example, when mounting his riding animal, ascending a rise in the ground, or climbing al-Safā and al-Marwah. Jābir said:
