“When we were with the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, we would proclaim Allah’s greatness whenever we ascended and glorify Him whenever we descended; prayer was therefore arranged according to this pattern.”

Reported by Abū Dāwūd.

The proclamation of Allah’s greatness is repeated at the beginning and end of the call to prayer, which is an exalted remembrance. It is also prescribed during prayer, when rising and lowering oneself, and when commencing it, as he said:

“One enters its consecrated state by proclaiming Allah’s greatness.”

It has also been narrated that “proclaiming Allah’s greatness extinguishes fire.” Thus, proclaiming Allah’s greatness is also prescribed to repel enemies—the devils among humans and jinn, as well as fire, which is an enemy to us. All this shows that proclaiming Allah’s greatness is prescribed on momentous occasions, whether because of the size of the gathering, the greatness of the deed, the intensity of the circumstances, or other such weighty matters. This makes clear that Allah is greater, so that His majesty prevails in people’s hearts over the greatness of those momentous things. Thus, all devotion is for Allah, and His servants proclaim His greatness.

They thereby attain two aims: the aim of worship, through their hearts magnifying Allah; and the aim of seeking His help, through the submission to His majesty of all else they seek. For this reason, proclaiming Allah’s greatness is prescribed for guidance, provision, and victory, for these three

---

## Page 12299

are the greatest things a servant seeks and encompass all that serves his welfare. Guidance is greater than provision and victory, since the benefit of provision and victory may be confined to this world, whereas guidance certainly benefits one in the Hereafter. Guidance is also the purpose served by provision and victory. It was therefore singled out for an explicit proclamation of Allah’s greatness, because it is the greatest blessing bestowed by the True One. The other two are lesser, so greater latitude was allowed in their case through the general mention of Allah’s name.

In sum, proclaiming Allah’s greatness is prescribed in connection with everything momentous, whether a place, a time, a circumstance, or people. This makes clear that Allah is greater, so that His majesty may prevail in people’s hearts over the majesty of all else, and His eminence may surpass every other eminence.

He, exalted be He, said, as His Messenger, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, narrated from Him:

“Greatness is My lower garment and majesty is My cloak. Whoever contends with Me for either of them, I will punish.”

When He, glorified be He, said:

“So that you may complete the prescribed period and proclaim Allah’s greatness for having guided you, and that you may be grateful,”

He mentioned proclaiming His greatness and giving thanks, as in His words:

“So remember Me; I will remember you. And be grateful to Me and do not be ungrateful to Me.”

Gratitude may be expressed in words, which is praise, or through deeds, as He, exalted be He, said:

“Work, O family of David, in gratitude.”

Thus praise was joined to the proclamation of Allah’s greatness on the Eids, with the words:

“Allah is the Greatest, Allah is the Greatest. There is no god but Allah. Allah is the Greatest, Allah is the Greatest, and all praise is due to Allah.”

This is because both proclaiming Allah’s greatness and giving thanks are called for on these occasions. Hence, it has been narrated in a report that one says:

“Allah is the Greatest for having guided us, and all praise is due to Allah for what He has bestowed upon us.”

This combines proclaiming Allah’s greatness with praise expressing gratitude, just as He combined

---

## Page 12300

praise extolling Him with the proclamation of His greatness in His words:

“And say, ‘All praise is due to Allah, who has not taken a son, who has no partner in His dominion, and who needs no protector out of weakness.’ And proclaim His greatness abundantly.”

Thus He commanded that He be praised and His greatness proclaimed. It is well known that the finest speech after the Quran consists of four statements:

“Glory be to Allah; all praise is due to Allah; there is no god but Allah; and Allah is the Greatest.”

They form two pairs: glorification is paired with praise. Hence the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Two statements are light on the tongue, heavy in the balance, and beloved to the Most Merciful: ‘Glory be to Allah and praise be to Him; glory be to Allah, the Magnificent.’”

Al-Bukhārī and Muslim both recorded this in their Sahīh collections on the authority of Abū Hurayrah. He, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, also said, as Muslim narrated on the authority of Abū Dharr:

“The finest speech is what Allah has chosen for His angels: ‘Glory be to Allah and praise be to Him.’”

And in the Quran:

“We glorify You with praise.”

“So glorify your Lord with praise and seek His forgiveness. Indeed, He is ever Accepting of repentance.”

“The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, would therefore say while bowing, ‘Glory be to You, O Allah, our Lord, and praise be to You. O Allah, forgive me,’ putting the Quran into practice.”

This is how it is reported in the authentic hadith collections on the authority of Ā’ishah. Thus his words, “Glory be to You, O Allah, and praise be to You,” were an enactment of the command, “So glorify your Lord with praise.” He, exalted be He, also said:

“So be patient; indeed, Allah’s promise is true. Seek forgiveness for your sin, and glorify your Lord with praise in the evening and the morning.”

---

## Page 12301

and He said:

“So glorify Allah when you reach the evening and when you reach the morning.”

“And to Him belongs all praise in the heavens and the earth.”

Reports coupling the two are numerous.

As for the declaration that there is no god but Allah, it is paired with the proclamation of His greatness, as in the words of the adhan:

“Allah is the Greatest, Allah is the Greatest. I bear witness that there is no god but Allah. I bear witness that Muhammad is the Messenger of Allah.”

Then, after calling people to prayer:

“Allah is the Greatest, Allah is the Greatest. There is no god but Allah.”

Thus, its beginning and end contain proclamations of Allah’s greatness and testimony to faith. These are remembrance of Allah Most High, while its middle calls people to prayer and success. Prayer is the deed, and success is its reward. Yet the proclamations of Allah’s greatness come in pairs, while the testimonies come singly: for every two proclamations there is one testimony.

Its beginning contains twice as much as its ending: at the beginning of the adhan, Allah’s greatness is proclaimed four times and the testimonies are recited twice, both testimonies explicitly using the words “I bear witness.” At its end, Allah’s greatness is proclaimed only twice, followed by the declaration that there is no god but Allah, without the words of bearing witness or the other testimony.

This—and Allah knows best—is comparable to the relationship between the first two units of prayer and the last two. The first two are distinguished by the recitation of an additional surah and by reciting aloud. Thus, they are distinguished in both the amount of recitation and its manner, just as the first half of the adhan is distinguished in both the amount of remembrance and its manner. Here, however, the distinction in manner is that the declaration of Allah’s oneness is accompanied by the words “I bear witness.”

For this reason, in the iqāmah (call to commence prayer), according to those who prefer its phrases to be recited singly—which was Bilāl’s form of the iqāmah—the additional repetitions are omitted, just as one lowers

---

## Page 12302

one’s voice when giving the iqāmah, for these additional repetitions are of the same nature as the original phrases. Omitting them thus resembles omitting the last two units of prayer when travelling. None of the fundamental phrases, however, is omitted. Likewise, the Prophet’s practice—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—in night prayer, the eclipse prayer and other acts of worship was to make the beginning longer than the end, for reasons that call for this.

Just as he combined proclaiming Allah’s greatness with declaring that there is no god but Allah in the adhan, he combined them when proclaiming Allah’s greatness on elevated ground. Thus, on al-Safā and al-Marwah, and whenever he ascended a height during a military expedition, Hajj or ʿumrah, he would proclaim Allah’s greatness three times and say:

“There is no god but Allah alone, with no partner. His is the dominion and His is all praise, and He has power over all things. There is no god but Allah alone. He fulfilled His promise, granted victory to His servant, gave might to His army, and defeated the confederates alone.”

He would do this three times. This is reported in the authentic hadith collections. Likewise, when mounted, he proclaimed Allah’s greatness three times and declared that there is no god but Allah three times, thus combining the two.

Similarly, the hadith of ʿAdī ibn Hātim, reported by Ahmad and al-Tirmidhī, relates that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said to him:

“O ʿAdī, what makes you turn away? Does it make you turn away that people should say, ‘There is no god but Allah’? Do you know of any god besides Allah? O ʿAdī, what makes you turn away? Does it make you turn away that people should say, ‘Allah is the Greatest’? Is there anything greater than Allah?”

Thus, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, paired the declaration that there is no god but Allah with the proclamation of Allah’s greatness.

---

## Page 12303

Sahīh Muslim also contains the hadith of Abū Mālik al-Ashʿarī, who reported that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Purification is half of faith. ‘Praise be to Allah’ fills the Scale. ‘Glory be to Allah’ and ‘Praise be to Allah’—the two fill, or he said, it fills—the space between heaven and earth. Prayer is light, charity is proof, patience is radiance, and the Quran is evidence for you or against you. Everyone goes out in the morning and sells his soul, either setting it free or bringing it to ruin.”

He thus stated that it fills the space between heaven and earth, which is greater than filling the Scale.

In the hadith of Talhah ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn Kurayz recorded in al-Muwattaʾ, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“The best supplication is the supplication on the Day of ʿArafah, and the best thing that I and the prophets before me have said is: ‘There is no god but Allah alone, with no partner. His is the dominion and His is all praise, and He has power over all things.’”

Thus, in this hadith he brought together “the best supplication and the best praise, for remembrance is of two kinds: supplication and praise. He said: ‘The best supplication is the supplication on the Day of ʿArafah,’ and ‘The best thing I have said is this declaration.’” He did not say, “The best thing I have said on the Day of ʿArafah is this declaration.” Rather, it is the best thing he has said without qualification. Likewise, a hadith narrated by Ibn Abī al-Dunyā states:

“The best remembrance is ‘There is no god but Allah,’ and the best supplication is ‘Praise be to Allah.’”

Furthermore, an authentic hadith from Abū Hurayrah reports that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Faith has seventy-odd branches. The highest is saying, ‘There is no god but Allah,’ and the lowest is removing something harmful from the road.”

He thus explicitly stated that the highest of the branches of

---

## Page 12304

faith is this declaration. Furthermore, Sahīh Muslim records:

“The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said: ‘Ubayy, do you know which verse in the Book of Allah is the greatest?’ He replied: ‘Allah—there is no god but Him, the Ever-Living, the Sustainer of all.’ The Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, then said: ‘May you rejoice in your knowledge, Abū al-Mundhir!’”

In this authentic hadith, he stated that this is the greatest verse in the Quran, and in the other, that this declaration is the highest of the branches of faith. This is the utmost excellence, for the whole religion is encompassed by the Quran and faith. Since it is the greatest in the Quran and the highest in faith, its supreme merit is established.

Moreover, affirming Allah’s oneness is the foundation of faith. It is the declaration that distinguishes the people of Paradise from the people of the Fire, and it is the price of Paradise. No one’s Islam is valid without it. Whoever’s last words are ‘There is no god but Allah’ will enter Paradise. Every sermon that contains no testimony of faith is like a severed hand. It thus occupies the position of a root, while praising and glorifying Allah occupy that of branches.

Furthermore, it is prescribed as an expression of magnification, to be proclaimed aloud, and on momentous occasions—for example, in the call to prayer, in which voices are raised, and when ascending to high places, because of the height and elevation involved. The declaration ‘Allah is the Greatest’ is proclaimed aloud in the prayers, and it is prescribed on the festivals.

---

## Page 12305

Jābir said:

“When we were with the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, we would proclaim ‘Allah is the Greatest’ whenever we ascended, and glorify Allah whenever we descended. Prayer was thus structured according to this pattern.”

Reported by Abū Dāwūd and others. This makes clear that proclaiming Allah’s greatness is prescribed in connection with elevation, whether in place or action, as in prayer and the call to prayer, while glorifying Him is prescribed in connection with lowering, whether in place or action, as in prostration and bowing. Hence, the Sunnah is to glorify Allah quietly on the occasions when it is prescribed. It is not prescribed to be voiced aloud and publicly proclaimed to the same extent as declaring Allah’s greatness and His oneness. Clearly, these additional features prescribed for a form of remembrance reflect the greater emphasis placed on its observance.

As for the hadith of Abū Dharr:

“The best words are those Allah has chosen for His angels: ‘Glory be to Allah, and praise be to Him,’”

it would seem—and Allah knows best—that this refers to words uttered on occasions when raising the voice is not prescribed, as in bowing, prostration and the like. It does not necessarily follow that these words are the best in absolute terms. The evidence for this is that reciting the Quran is better than other forms of remembrance, yet the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, prohibited its recitation while bowing and prostrating. He said:

“I have been forbidden to recite the Quran while bowing or prostrating. When bowing, magnify the Lord; when prostrating, strive earnestly in supplication, for then your supplications are likely to be answered.”

Here is a principle we should understand: something that is generally superior need not be superior in every circumstance or for every person. Rather,

---

## Page 12306

the less meritorious act, in the circumstances for which it is prescribed, is better than an act that is superior in general. Thus, glorifying Allah while bowing and prostrating is better than reciting the Quran, declaring His oneness or proclaiming His greatness. Likewise, reciting the testimony of faith at the end of prayer and supplicating thereafter are better than reciting the Quran. This is comparable to the Prophet’s saying, may Allah bless him and grant him peace:

“The people should be led in prayer by the one most proficient in reciting the Book of Allah. If they are equal in recitation, then by the one most knowledgeable about the Sunnah. If they are equal in knowledge of the Sunnah, then by the one who emigrated earliest. If they are equal in emigration, then by the oldest in age—or the earliest to embrace Islam.”

He then followed this with:

“No one should lead another man in prayer within the latter’s sphere of authority, nor sit in his place of honour, without his permission.”

He thus set out the order of precedence for leading prayer, then explained that those whose office gives them authority, such as regular prayer leaders—military commanders in earlier times, mosque imams and the like—take precedence over others, even if those others are superior to them. Similarly, gold is superior to iron and lime, yet these minerals may take precedence over gold when they, rather than gold, are needed. This is clear.

Likewise, most people are incapable of performing the best deeds. Were they instructed to perform them, they would do so in a manner from which they derived no benefit, or only a lesser benefit. For each such person, a deed that suits him and benefits him is therefore better than one that does not. Hence, for many people, remembering Allah is better than reciting the Quran, because remembrance nurtures faith in a person, while the Quran imparts knowledge, and knowledge comes after faith.

Allah Most High says:

“Allah will raise those of you who believe and those who have been given

---

## Page 12307

knowledge by degrees.”

The Quran requires understanding and reflection, which a person may be incapable of. Such people, however, fall into error when they believe that remembrance of Allah is superior without qualification. This is not so; rather, reciting the Quran is inherently superior to remembrance, by the consensus of the Muslims. The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“The best words after the Quran are four expressions, which are themselves found in the Quran: Glory be to Allah; praise be to Allah; there is no god but Allah; and Allah is the Greatest.”

Narrated by Muslim.

A man said to him, “I cannot commit any of the Quran to memory, so teach me something that will suffice for me in my prayer.”

He replied, “Say: Glory be to Allah; praise be to Allah; there is no god but Allah; and Allah is the Greatest.”

For this reason, the scholars held that remembrance in prayer is a substitute for recitation, to which one may resort only when unable to recite. Its relation to recitation is like that of purification with earth to ablution, fasting two months to freeing a slave, and fasting to offering a pilgrimage sacrifice.

The hadith recorded in al-Tirmidhī states:

“The servants have never drawn near to Allah with anything better than that which has come forth from Him.”

This means the Quran. In the hadith of Ibn ‘Abbās narrated by Abū Dāwūd and al-Tirmidhī, and graded authentic by the latter, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Allah has people of His own among mankind.”

He was asked, “Who are they, Messenger of Allah?”

He replied, “Those devoted to the Quran: they are Allah’s own people and His chosen ones.”

The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, would give those devoted to the Quran precedence in various situations. Thus, on the day of Uhud, he gave them precedence in burial. He permitted two or three men to be buried in a single grave and said, “Place those who knew the most Quran nearest the qiblah, the direction of prayer.”

---

## Page 12308

The Prophet’s reply in the hadith of Abū Dharr, when he was asked, “Which words are best?” and answered, “Glory and praise be to Allah,” was given in response to a question. He may have known of some particular circumstance affecting the questioner. Likewise, when he said:

“The best words that I and the prophets before me have said are: ‘There is no god but Allah’”—and so on—

he meant words of remembrance, not Quranic recitation. For reciting the Quran is, generally speaking, superior to remembrance as a category, although these words may sometimes be superior to recitation. Thus, uttering the two testimonies of faith when entering Islam, renewing one’s profession of it, or on occasions that call for them—such as after ablution, at the conclusion of prayer, in the call to prayer, and on other such occasions—is superior to recitation.

The same applies to repeating the words of the caller to prayer. If someone is reciting the Quran and hears the caller, repeating the words of the call is better for him at that moment than continuing his recitation. Indeed, it is recommended that he interrupt his recitation for this purpose, because this is the appointed time for that act of worship: its opportunity passes when its time passes, whereas the opportunity to recite remains.

We therefore say that there are three situations. In the first, saying the words quietly is recommended and raising the voice is disliked, because one is in a lowered position, as when bowing or prostrating. Here, glorifying Allah is better than declaring His oneness and proclaiming His greatness. The same applies in the bottoms of valleys.

As for situations in which the Sunnah is to raise the voice and proclaim the words openly, such as when ascending high ground or giving the call to prayer, the Sunnah is to declare Allah’s oneness and proclaim His greatness. As for situations in which both are prescribed, this may apply.

---

## Page 12309

Section

Once it is understood that praising Allah accompanies glorifying Him, and that declaring His oneness accompanies proclaiming His greatness, the proclamations of Allah’s greatness at the two Eids bring together these paired forms of remembrance: proclaiming His greatness is combined with declaring His oneness, and with praising Him. For He says:

“And that you may proclaim Allah’s greatness for having guided you, and that you may be grateful.”

His guidance calls for proclaiming His greatness in gratitude for it, so its companion—declaring His oneness—is joined to it. His blessing calls for gratitude, so praising Him is also joined to it.

Similarly, mounting an animal combines taking an elevated position with receiving a blessing. The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, would therefore combine both forms of remembrance when mounting. For Allah, glorified be He, says:

“So that you may settle upon their backs, then remember your Lord’s blessing when you have settled upon them, and say, ‘Glory be to Him who has subjected this to us, for we could not have brought it under our control. And surely to our Lord we shall return.’”

He thus commanded that His blessing be remembered by praising Him for it, and commanded glorification, which accompanies praise.

“When a mount was brought to the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, he placed his foot in the stirrup and said, ‘In the name of Allah.’ When he had settled on its back, he said, ‘Praise be to Allah.’ Then he said, ‘Glory be to Him who has subjected this to us, for we could not have brought it under our control. And surely to our Lord we shall return.’ Then he praised Allah three times and proclaimed His greatness three times, then said, ‘There is no god but You

---

## Page 12310

, glory be to You! I have wronged myself, so forgive me.’ Then he laughed and said, ‘I laughed because of the Lord’s laughter when His servant says this. Allah says, “My servant knows that none but Me forgives sins.”’”

He then recited the remembrance associated with elevated places: proclaiming Allah’s greatness together with declaring His oneness. He concluded by seeking forgiveness, for this is coupled with affirming Allah’s oneness, just as Allah has placed seeking forgiveness alongside affirming His oneness in several passages, such as His saying:

“Know, then, that there is no god but Allah, and seek forgiveness for your sin.”

And His saying:

“Worship none but Allah. Indeed, I am to you from Him a warner and a bearer of good tidings. And seek your Lord’s forgiveness.”

And His saying:

“So remain steadfast toward Him and seek His forgiveness.”

Thus, his remembrance while riding included the four phrases that constitute the enduring good deeds, together with seeking forgiveness. Likewise, the remembrance recited at the Eids encompasses both magnifying Allah and acknowledging His blessing, and therefore combines proclaiming His greatness with praising Him. Thus we proclaim Allah’s greatness for having guided us, and praise Allah for what He has bestowed upon us.

It is reported that Ibn ‘Umar would proclaim Allah’s greatness three times and say:

“There is no god but Allah alone, with no partner. His is the dominion and His is all praise, and He has power over all things.”

He thus made it resemble the remembrance associated with elevated places by repeating the proclamation of Allah’s greatness three times and joining it with the declaration of His oneness. This is the practice preferred by al-Shāfi‘ī. Ahmad, Abū Hanīfah and others, however, preferred the form they transmitted from a group

---

## Page 12311

of the Companions. Al-Dāraqutnī also transmitted it in a hadith from Jābir, traced back to the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, who said:

“Allah is the Greatest, Allah is the Greatest. There is no god but Allah. Allah is the Greatest, Allah is the Greatest, and to Allah belongs all praise.”

They thus recite the proclamation of Allah’s greatness in two pairs, joining one pair with the declaration of His oneness and the other with praise, making it resemble the remembrance recited in the adhān. Indeed, it more closely resembles this because it is connected with prayer and is recited at the Eids, when people gather in a general assembly, just as the adhān calls people to gather. It therefore resembles the adhān in being a proclamation of Allah’s greatness associated with a gathering rather than a place, and in being connected with prayer rather than elevated ground. Its repetition is therefore prescribed, just as repetition of the proclamation of Allah’s greatness is prescribed in the adhān, with the proclamation uttered in pairs each time. All the transmitted forms are good. Some people recite it three times on the first occasion and twice on the second; a group of people follows this practice.

Our principle in this matter—the soundest of principles—is that whenever the various forms of worship, whether words or actions, are transmitted through reports that may validly be relied upon, none of them is disliked; rather, all are prescribed. This is what we have said concerning the different forms of prayer in times of fear; the two forms of the adhān, with or without repeating the two testimonies of faith first quietly and then aloud; and the two forms of the iqāmah, with paired or single utterances. We have likewise said this concerning the various forms of the testimonies recited while seated in prayer, the opening supplications, the formulas for seeking refuge with Allah, the Quranic recitations, the additional proclamations of Allah’s greatness in the Eid prayer, the funeral prayer, the prostrations for forgetfulness, the qunūt supplication before or after bowing, and the formula of praise with the conjunction “and” retained

---

## Page 12312

or omitted, and other such variations. However, some of these transmitted forms may be recommended and preferred over others when evidence establishes their superiority, without the others being disliked.

It is well known that a person accountable under Islamic law cannot combine two alternative forms of an act of worship at the same time. He cannot recite two forms of the testimony while seated in prayer together, or two Quranic readings together, or perform two forms of the prayer in times of fear together. If he performs the act twice, that is prohibited. Combining these forms is therefore sometimes forbidden and sometimes disliked.

Pay no heed to those who may recommend combining some of these forms. For example, I have seen someone piece together the formulas for sending blessings upon the Prophet that were transmitted from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and recommend reciting that composite supplication. He also commented on the hadith of Abū Bakr al-Siddīq, reported by both al-Bukhārī and Muslim, in which he said to the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace:

“Teach me a supplication to recite in my prayer.” He replied: “Say: ‘O Allah, I have committed grave wrongs against myself (in another narration: many wrongs), and none forgives sins but You. So grant me forgiveness from Yourself and have mercy upon me. You are indeed the All-Forgiving, the Most Merciful.’”

This person said that it is recommended to say, “many grave wrongs,” and makes similar recommendations in comparable cases. This view is weak. First, this is not Sunnah; rather, it is contrary to the prescribed practice. The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, did not say all these wordings together. Rather, he would say one on one occasion and the other on another—if both are authentically established from him. Combining them is therefore not Sunnah, but an innovation, even if it is permissible.

---

## Page 12313

Second, combining the different wordings of a single supplication or formula of remembrance as an act of devotion is like combining all the variant readings transmitted by the Quran reciters—not for study and memorization, but for recitation and reflection—despite their differing meanings. An example would be to recite in prayer:

“In their hearts is a disease, and Allah has increased their disease. They will have a painful punishment because they used to lie” (“because they used to deny”).

“Our Lord, lengthen the distances between our journeys”—bāʿid (“lengthen the distances between our journeys”—baʿʿid).

“And Allah is not unaware of what you do” (“of what they do”).

“And He relieves them of their burden” (“their burdens”).

“And your feet up to the ankles”—wa-arjulakum (“and your feet”—wa-arjulikum).

“And do not approach them until they become pure” (“until they purify themselves”).

“And it is not lawful for you to take back anything you have given them, unless the two fear” (“unless it is feared concerning the two”).

“Or you have touched women”—lāmastum (“or you have touched”—lamastum).

It is well known that this is an objectionable, reprehensible innovation.

Third, the same applies to prescribed formulas of remembrance. If a man were to piece together a testimony to recite while seated in prayer from the transmitted versions, combining Ibn Masʿūd’s hadith and … (1) and its mention of prayers with the “pure things” in ʿUmar’s testimony and the “blessed things” in Ibn ʿAbbās’s, so that he said, “All greetings, prayers, good things, blessed things and pure things belong to Allah,” this would be neither prescribed nor recommended for him. Other such combinations have even less claim to being recommended.

> **Footnotes:**
> (1) A blank in the original.
> Shaykh Nāsir ibn Hamad al-Fahd states (p. 201):
> “It appears that the blank should read [wa-tahiyyātihi (and its salutations)], referring to the hadith narrated by Ibn Mas‘ūd, may Allah be pleased with him, concerning the tashahhud (testimony recited while seated in prayer). And Allah knows best.”

---

## Page 12314

Fourth, this is done only by someone intent on multiplying letters and words, even though doing so may diminish or alter the meaning. If he reflected on what he was saying, he would realize that each transmitted wording achieves the intended purpose, although some achieve it more fully. For when he says, “much wrongdoing,” whatever is much is also great in meaning, and whatever is great is also much in meaning.

When he says:

“O Allah, bestow blessings upon Muhammad and upon the family of Muhammad,”

or:

“O Allah, bestow blessings upon Muhammad, his wives and his offspring,”

his wives and offspring are undoubtedly included in his family—or they constitute his family. Thus, if he combines the two and says, “upon the family of Muhammad and upon his wives and offspring,” he has not reflected on the prescribed wording.

In short, if one formula of remembrance agrees with the other in its essential meaning, they are like two Quranic readings with the same meaning. If their meanings differ, they are like two Quranic readings with differing meanings. In either case, combining them on a single occasion is not prescribed. As for combining two forms of the prayer in times of fear, the testimony recited while seated in prayer, the call to commence prayer, or similar practices, this is prohibited by the consensus of the Muslims.

Since these verbal or physical acts of worship must be performed in one of their forms, just as the Quran must be recited according to one of its readings, it does not follow that everyone who adopts a particular form does so only because he considers it the best, or there may not be

---

## Page 12315

any superior form among them. Rather, this is like the routes to Mecca: the people of each region make the pilgrimage by their own route. They do not choose it because it is superior, such that their pilgrimage would be better than that of others, but because they must take some route. They therefore take this one either because it is easier for them or for some other reason, even if all the routes are equal.

One should therefore distinguish between choosing a prescribed form because one considers it intrinsically superior and choosing one because some choice is unavoidable, with ease or some other consideration tipping the balance in its favour.

Each of the early generations would recite, pray, supplicate and remember Allah in a prescribed manner, and his companions and the people of his locality would learn that manner from him. These forms might be equal, or some might be superior to others. Then, among later generations, someone would seek to attribute that choice to the superiority of the chosen form, while another would oppose him on this point. From this arose ruinous, misleading passions. The two forms might be equal in the sight of Allah and His Messenger, yet each group considers its own way superior, loves those who agree with it, and turns away from those who practise the other form. Thus they give preference where Allah has made things equal, and treat things as equal where Allah has distinguished between them in merit.

This is one avenue of the division and disagreement that have afflicted the Muslim community, which the Quran and Sunnah have forbidden. The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, forbade this very kind of disagreement in an authentic hadith—as I have explained in al-Sirāt al-Mustaqīm (The Straight Path)—when he said:

“Recite as you have learned.”

---

## Page 12316

What is required, then, is that none of these forms be considered superior to another without evidence from Islamic law. The mere selection of one form because performing the act of worship requires a choice must not be treated as grounds for its superiority. If Allah obliges me to free a slave or perform a congregational prayer, this necessarily requires me to free a particular slave and pray with a particular congregation. Neither need be better than another; indeed, neither may be superior in any respect. Their merit must therefore be examined separately.

Even if the religious evidence establishes that one form is superior, someone who practises a permissible alternative must not be censured, nor should others be turned against him on that account. Nor should its superiority be exaggerated beyond the degree assigned to it by Islamic law, for the difference in merit may be slight.

There is, however, a related question: whether these forms are equal in merit or some are superior, is it better for a person to adhere consistently to one form, or to practise one sometimes and another at other times, as the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, used to do? Some people consistently practise one form out of preference or a belief that it is superior, and consider their constant adherence to that form better. For most, however, such consistency stems from habit and conformity to the habits of their companions and those who follow their way, rather than a belief in its superiority.

The correct view is this:

Varying one’s practice in this way, following the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace—for this entails adherence to the Sunnah and the community, reviving his Sunnah, uniting the hearts of the Muslim community, and benefiting from the distinctive quality of each form—is better than consistently practising a particular form to which the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, did not consistently adhere, for several reasons:

---

## Page 12317

First:
This is what it means to follow the Sunnah and Islamic law. For if the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, practised one form at times and another at other times, without consistently adhering to either, then following his example in this is the prescribed way of emulating and following him: doing what he did, in the manner in which he did it, because he did it.

Second:
This unites the hearts of the Muslim community, brings them into harmony, and removes much of the division, discord and pursuit of whims among them. This secures a great benefit and averts a great harm. The Quran and Sunnah urge us to secure the former and avert the latter. Allah Most High says:

“And hold firmly to the rope of Allah all together and do not become divided.”

He also says:

“And do not be like those who became divided and differed after clear proofs had come to them.”

And He says:

“Indeed, those who have divided their religion and become sects—you have nothing to do with them.”

Third:
This prevents a permissible practice established by the Sunnah from being made to resemble an obligation. Consistent adherence to something recommended or permissible makes it resemble an obligation. Thus, if most of those who consistently adhere to certain permissible or recommended forms were to adopt another form, the change would provoke greater aversion in their own hearts and in those of others than neglecting many obligatory duties would. This is because habit has made what is permissible seem obligatory.

Fourth:
This secures the benefit of each of these forms, for every form necessarily has a distinctive quality, even if it is less preferable. How much more so, then, if it is

---

## Page 12318

equally preferable? We have already explained that the less preferable may become preferable in certain circumstances.

Fifth:
This lifts many of the burdens and shackles that Satan has imposed upon the Muslim community without any scripture from Allah or any trace of knowledge. For a person may consistently adhere to a permissible practice, preferring it over others to such an extent that he loves those who agree with him in it, but does not love those who do not. Indeed, he may even hate them, reproaching them for abandoning it, so that this leads to the neglect of rights due to him and duties incumbent upon him. This makes that practice a burden he cannot put down and a shackle around his neck, preventing him from doing some of what he has been commanded to do, and perhaps causing him to fall into some of what he has been forbidden to do.

What I have described happens frequently. What begins as consistent adherence to that practice gives rise to belief and love that are not sanctioned by Islamic law. It then develops into unjustified praise and blame, commands and prohibitions. This, in turn, develops into a kind of allegiance and enmity not sanctioned by Islamic law, resembling the conduct of the pre-Islamic age of ignorance, such as that of the Aws and Khazraj during that age, and the conduct of … (1).

It then develops into giving and withholding: on that basis, he spends his wealth in gifts, payments and other ways, without any entitlement under Islamic law, and withholds from those whom the Lawgiver has commanded

> **Footnotes:**
> (1) A lacuna in the original.

---

## Page 12319

him to give to, whether as an obligation or a recommendation. This then escalates into warfare and fighting, as has happened in some eastern lands. It all begins with preferring what Islamic law has not given preference to. Consistent adherence to it, even without believing it superior, fosters a belief in its superiority and a corresponding preference for it. Such consistent adherence is therefore either prohibited or less preferable, whereas varying one’s practice among the prescribed forms, just as the Messenger of Allah, peace and blessings be upon him, varied his practice, is better and more complete.

Sixth:

Consistently adhering to one form to the exclusion of others entails abandoning some prescribed practices. This causes them to be forgotten and disregarded, until people come to believe that they are not part of the religion. Thus, many ordinary people become convinced that these practices are not part of the religion, while the learned among them act contrary to their knowledge. Their scholars know that these practices belong to the religion, yet refrain from explaining this, either out of fear of people or to exchange Allah’s revelations for the paltry gains of status and wealth, as the People of the Book did.

We have seen people who are accustomed to hearing only an iqāmah (the call to commence prayer) with its phrases recited singly, or only one with its phrases recited in pairs. When they hear the other form, they recoil from it and reject it, as though they had heard a call to prayer other than that of the Muslims. The same applies to those accustomed to qunūt (supplication during prayer) before bowing, or after it. Abandoning some prescribed practices causes enmity and hatred to arise within the Muslim community.

Allah Most High says:

“And from those who say, ‘We are Christians,’ We took their covenant; but they forgot a portion of that of which they were reminded. So We caused among them animosity and hatred until the Day of Resurrection.”

---

## Page 12320

Thus He, glorified be He, explains that their forgetting a portion of what they had been reminded of caused enmity and hatred to arise among them. When a person follows all the prescribed practices of the sunnah, observing one prescribed form at one time and another at another time, the sunnah is preserved in both knowledge and practice, and the harm feared from abandoning this is averted. The essential point here is that, although confining oneself to one form is permissible, preserving the other form is part of the religion, so that its permissibility and prescribed status remain known. Practising it on occasion preserves the revealed law, whereas abandoning it may cause it to be lost and forgotten.

Seventh:

Allah commands justice and good conduct. Justice means treating like cases alike. He has forbidden injustice for Himself and made it forbidden among His servants. Among the greatest forms of justice is justice in religious matters. Although justice in worldly matters involving lives and property—such as legal retribution and inheritance—is obligatory, and failing to uphold it is injustice, justice in religious matters is still greater. This means dealing justly with the prescribed practices of the religion and with its adherents.

If the Lawgiver has accorded equal standing to two deeds or two people who perform them, then favoring one over the other is a grave injustice. If He has given one precedence over the other, treating them as equal is likewise unjust. Favoring one over another, or treating them as equal, on the basis of conjecture and personal desires is characteristic of the religion of the disbelievers. For the adherents of every religion and creed favor their own religion, whether through conjecture or desire, whether in belief or conduct. This is what leads them to hold fast to it and disparage other religions.

---

## Page 12321

Thus, if the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, prescribed those forms through either his words or his practice, and in many cases did not favor one over another, then treating them equally is just, while favoring one over another is unjust. Many forms of practice whose relative merits are disputed by groups within the Muslim community are not unequal in merit at all; rather, they are equal. The distinctive merit of one may counterbalance the distinctive merit of the other. Yet you find someone asking, “Which is better, this or that?” This is an ill-founded question, for asking which one is superior presupposes that a difference in merit has been established. Who has established that one surpasses the other, such that we should seek to identify the superior one?

What should be asked is: “Are these two equal in merit, or does one surpass the other? If one surpasses the other, is that superiority unconditional, or does it depend on the circumstances, so that one is better at one time and the other is better at another?”

Yet, despite the matter being as you see, most answers arise from personal desire and false, mistaken suppositions. One of the greatest causes of this is continually observing a practice when such continual observance has not been prescribed. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 12322

He was also asked—may Allah Most High have mercy on him:

“Do greetings exchanged on Eid, including common expressions such as ‘May your Eid be blessed’ and the like, have any basis in Islamic law? If so, what should be said? Please give us a ruling, and may you be rewarded.”

He replied:

As for exchanging greetings on the day of Eid, saying to one another upon meeting after the Eid prayer, “May Allah accept from us and from you,” “May Allah bring this occasion around again for you,” and similar expressions, it has been reported that a number of the Companions used to do this. The imams, including Ahmad and others, permitted it.

However, Ahmad said: “I do not initiate such greetings with anyone, but if someone greets me first, I respond.”

This is because returning a greeting is obligatory. Initiating Eid greetings, however, is neither a Sunnah that one is instructed to observe nor something forbidden. Whoever does so has a precedent to follow, and whoever refrains likewise has a precedent to follow. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 12323

Chapter: The Eclipse Prayer

Shaykh al-Islām was asked:

“Concerning the statement of almanac compilers that the moon will be eclipsed on the fourteenth of this month and the sun on the twenty-ninth: are they to be believed in this? When these eclipses occur, should one perform the eclipse prayer or glorify Allah? If one prays, how should the prayer be performed? Please mention the scholars’ views on this.”

He replied:

Praise be to Allah. Lunar and solar eclipses have appointed times, just as the appearance of the crescent moon has an appointed time. This is part of the regular order Allah has established for night and day, winter and summer, and everything else that follows the courses of the sun and moon. These are among the signs of Allah Most High.

As the Most High says:

“It is He who created the night and the day, and the sun and the moon—each floating in an orbit.”

And the Most High says:

“It is He who made the sun a radiance and the moon a light, and determined phases for it so that you may know the number of years and the reckoning of time. Allah did not create all this except in truth.”

And the Most High says:

---

## Page 12324

“The sun and the moon follow a precise reckoning.”

And the Most High says:

“He causes the dawn to break, and has made the night for rest, and the sun and the moon for reckoning. That is the determination of the Almighty, the All-Knowing.”

And the Most High says:

“They ask you about the crescent moons. Say, ‘They mark appointed times for people and for Hajj.’”

And the Most High says:

“Indeed, the number of months with Allah is twelve, as ordained in Allah’s Record on the day He created the heavens and the earth; four of them are sacred. That is the upright religion.”

And the Most High says:

“And a sign for them is the night: We strip the daylight from it, and suddenly they are in darkness.”

“And the sun runs towards its appointed resting place. That is the determination of the Almighty, the All-Knowing.”

“And the moon—We have determined phases for it until it returns like an old date stalk.”

“It is not for the sun to overtake the moon, nor does the night outstrip the day. Each floats in an orbit.”

Just as Allah Most High has established the regular order whereby the crescent moon first appears only on the thirtieth or thirty-first night of the month, and a month consists only of thirty or twenty-nine days—so that anyone who supposes a month can be longer or shorter is mistaken—likewise, Allah has established the regular order whereby a solar eclipse occurs only when the moon is hidden from view, and a lunar eclipse occurs only when the moon is full. The full moon occurs on the bright nights whose days it is recommended to fast: the thirteenth, fourteenth and fifteenth nights. A lunar eclipse occurs only on these nights.

---

## Page 12325

The crescent moon is hidden from view at the end of the month for either one night or two, as on the twenty-ninth and thirtieth nights. A solar eclipse occurs only while the moon is hidden. Solar and lunar eclipses have their customary nights; whoever knows these knows when the eclipses occur. Similarly, someone who knows how much of the month has passed knows that the crescent moon will appear on a particular night or the night before it. However, knowledge of the crescent moon’s regular pattern is common knowledge shared by everyone, whereas knowledge of the regular pattern of solar and lunar eclipses belongs only to those who know how to calculate the movements of the sun and moon. A calculator’s prediction of an eclipse is neither knowledge of the unseen nor one of the astrological predictions in which he is wrong more often than he is right. Such astrological predictions are assertions made without established knowledge and rest on no sound foundation.

In Sunan Abī Dāwūd, it is reported that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Whoever acquires a branch of astrology has acquired a branch of magic; the more he acquires of the former, the more he acquires of the latter.”

In Sahīh Muslim, it is reported that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Whoever goes to a fortune-teller, asks him about something and believes him, Allah will not accept his prayer for forty days.”

Soothsayers have greater knowledge of what they say than astrologers have of their predictions. Yet it is authentically reported that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, forbade visiting and questioning them. How much more, then, does this apply to an astrologer? We have discussed this at length elsewhere, outside this response.

---

## Page 12326

As for what is known through calculation, it is like knowing the times of the seasons, such as the beginning of spring, summer, autumn and winter, when the sun aligns with the beginnings of the zodiacal signs. When they say, “The sun has entered such-and-such a sign,” they mean that it has come into alignment with it. Any jurist who says that a solar eclipse can occur outside the period when the moon is hidden from view has erred and spoken without knowledge.

The statement reported from al-Wāqidī—that Ibrāhīm, the son of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, died on the tenth day of the month, and that this was the day on which the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, performed the eclipse prayer—is mistaken. Al-Wāqidī’s reports cannot be relied upon as evidence even when he supplies chains of transmission, let alone when he relates something without a chain tracing it to anyone. This applies even to reports not known to be erroneous; this particular report, however, is known to be erroneous. Whoever considers this possible has pursued something of which he has no knowledge, and whoever argues about it has argued about something of which he has no knowledge.

As for the coincidence of the Eid prayer and the eclipse prayer mentioned by some jurists, they mentioned this within their discussion of cases in which the eclipse prayer coincides with other prayers. They considered its coincidence with the witr prayer and the noon prayer, and included the Eid prayer without considering whether such a coincidence could occur in the ordinary course of events. Their hypothetical treatment of this case therefore does not establish that it actually occurs. Rather, the knowledge gained concerns its ruling on the assumption that it occurs—just as they posit cases known never to occur in order to formulate principles precisely and train minds to grasp them accurately.

---

## Page 12327

As for believing or disbelieving someone who reports such matters, one may neither believe him unless his truthfulness is known nor disbelieve him unless his falsehood is known. As the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“When the People of the Book relate something to you, neither believe them nor disbelieve them, for they may tell you something true and you disbelieve them, or tell you something false and you believe them.”

Although it is possible to know when solar and lunar eclipses will occur, the particular person reporting this may or may not possess that knowledge, and may or may not be trustworthy in what he reports. Judgment must be suspended concerning the report of an unknown person whose knowledge and truthfulness cannot be relied upon, but who is not known to be lying. Even if someone reported the time of prayer, his report would not be accepted if his reliability were unknown. However, when those versed in astronomical calculation agree in their reports, they hardly ever err. Even so, their reports do not establish knowledge authoritative under Islamic law, for the solar and lunar eclipse prayers are performed only when we witness the eclipse.

If a person considers such a report possibly true, or believes it most likely to be true, and therefore intends to perform the eclipse prayer at that time and prepares to watch for the eclipse, this would encourage him to hasten to obey Allah Most High and worship Him. Muslims agree that prayer is prescribed during an eclipse. Reports of this practice from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, have been transmitted through numerous independent chains, and the compilers of the Sahīh, Sunan and Musnad collections have narrated them through many routes. It is also widely reported that he led the Muslims in the eclipse prayer on the day his son Ibrāhīm died.

---

## Page 12328

And it seems that some people thought the sun had become eclipsed because Ibrāhīm had died. The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, therefore addressed them, saying:

“The sun and the moon are two of Allah’s signs. They do not become eclipsed because of anyone’s death or life. When you see them eclipsed, hasten to prayer.”

Another version in the Sahīh reads:

“Rather, they are two of Allah’s signs through which He strikes fear into His servants.”

This makes clear that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, regarded the two eclipses as a cause for punishment to befall people. Allah strikes fear into His servants only through what they fear when they disobey Him and His messengers, and people fear only what harms them. Were there no possibility of harm befalling people during an eclipse, it would not serve to frighten them. Allah Most High says:

“And We gave Thamūd the she-camel as a clear sign, but they wronged her. We send signs only as a warning.”

The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, commanded actions that dispel fear: prayer, supplication, seeking forgiveness, charity and freeing slaves, until what afflicts people is removed. He led the Muslims in a lengthy prayer during the eclipse.

Various forms of the eclipse prayer have been reported. However, the form widely reported among those knowledgeable about the Sunnah of Allah’s Messenger, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and narrated by al-Bukhārī and Muslim through several routes—and which most scholars, including Mālik, al-Shāfi'ī and Ahmad, considered preferable—is that he led them in two units of prayer, with two bowings in each unit. He recites at length, then bows for a long time, though less than the time spent reciting. He then stands and recites at length, though less than in the first recitation, then bows for less time than in the preceding bowing

---

## Page 12329

, then makes two lengthy prostrations.

It is also established in the Sahīh that he recited aloud during this prayer. The aim is for the prayer to continue throughout the eclipse until it clears. If one finishes praying before it clears, one should remember Allah and supplicate to Him until it clears. An eclipse may last longer or shorter depending on how much of the sun is obscured: all of it may be eclipsed, or half or a third. When the eclipse is extensive, one should prolong the prayer, reciting al-Baqarah or a similar surah in the first unit, and reciting a shorter passage after the second bowing.

Authentic hadiths from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, establish everything we have mentioned. Among them is the report in the two Sahīhs from Abū Mas'ūd al-Ansārī, who said:

“The sun was eclipsed on the day Ibrāhīm, the son of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, died. People said, ‘The sun has been eclipsed because Ibrāhīm has died.’ The Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said, ‘The sun and the moon are two of Allah’s signs. They do not become eclipsed because of anyone’s death or life. When you see that, hasten to the remembrance of Allah and to prayer.’”

Also in the Sahīh, Abū Mūsā reported that he, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“These signs that Allah sends do not occur because of anyone’s death or life. Rather, Allah strikes fear into His servants through them. When you see any such thing, hasten to remember Him

---

## Page 12330

and supplicate to Him and seek His forgiveness.”

In the two Sahīhs, Jābir reports that he, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“The sun and the moon are two of Allah’s signs. They do not become eclipsed because of anyone’s death. When you see any such thing, pray until it clears.”

In a narration from Ibn Mas‘ūd:

“When you see any of these signs, pray and supplicate until what has befallen you is lifted.”

In a narration from ‘Ā’ishah:

“Pray until Allah relieves you of what has befallen you.”

In the two Sahīhs, ‘Ā’ishah reported:

“The sun was eclipsed during the lifetime of the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. The Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, went out to the mosque, stood and proclaimed Allah’s greatness, and arranged the people in rows behind him. The Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, recited a lengthy passage, then proclaimed Allah’s greatness and bowed for a long time. He then raised his head and said, ‘Allah hears those who praise Him. Our Lord, to You belongs all praise.’

“He then stood and recited a lengthy passage, though shorter than the first. He then proclaimed Allah’s greatness and bowed for a long time, though less than the first bowing. He then said, ‘Allah hears those who praise Him. Our Lord, to You belongs all praise.’ He then prostrated and did the same in the second unit, completing four bowings and four prostrations. The sun had cleared before he finished the prayer.”

His prolonging the prostrations is also reported in an authentic hadith, as is his reciting aloud. Although silent recitation has also been reported, the report of reciting aloud is more authentic. As for prolonging the prostrations, there is no

---

## Page 12331

disagreement among the hadith reports concerning it. However, each report contains additional details absent from the other. All the authentic hadiths agree and do not contradict one another. And Allah knows best.

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked about rain, thunder, and earthquakes according to the scholars of the revealed Law and according to the philosophers.

He replied:

All praise is due to Allah, Lord of all worlds.

As for rain, Allah creates it in the sky from clouds, and it descends from the clouds, as He, Most High, says:

“Have you considered the water you drink? Is it you who bring it down from the rain clouds, or is it We who bring it down?”

He, Most High, also says:

“And We sent down abundant, pouring water from the rain clouds.”

And He, Most High, says:

“Then you see the rain emerge from within it.”

That is, from within the clouds.

His expression “from the sky,” which occurs in several places, means “from above.” The word “sky” is a generic designation for whatever is above. Depending on the accompanying context, it may refer specifically to what is above the Throne, to the celestial spheres, or to the roof of a house.

The substance from which rain is created is sometimes the air in the atmosphere and sometimes the vapour rising from the earth. This is what Muslim scholars have stated, and the philosophers agree with it.

---

## Page 12332

Section

As for thunder and lightning, a hadith traced back to the Prophet, recorded by al-Tirmidhī and others, relates that he was asked about thunder and replied:

“An angel entrusted with the clouds, carrying whips of fire with which he drives the clouds wherever Allah wills.”

In al-Kharā’itī’s Makārim al-Akhlāq (Noble Character), it is reported that ‘Alī was asked about thunder and replied, “An angel.” When asked about lightning, he replied, “Whips in the hands of the angels.” Another report from him reads: “Whips of iron in his hand.” Other reports to the same effect have also been transmitted.

Statements have also been reported from some of the early Muslims that do not conflict with this, such as the view that thunder is the collision of masses of cloud caused by the compression of air within them. This does not contradict the foregoing, for ra‘d (thunder) is the verbal noun of ra‘ada, yar‘adu (to thunder). The one producing thunder may likewise be called ra‘d, just as a just person may be called ‘adl (justice).

Movement produces sound, and it is the angels who move the clouds and convey them from place to place. Every movement in the higher and lower worlds is brought about by the angels. A human being’s voice results from the striking together of parts of his body—his lips, tongue, and teeth

---

## Page 12333

and his uvula and throat. Yet he may at the same time be glorifying the Lord, enjoining what is right and forbidding what is wrong.

Thunder, then, is a sound that drives the clouds along. Likewise, lightning has been described as the gleam of water or the flash of fire. Its being the flash of fire or the gleam of water does not preclude the flashing object from being a whip in the angel’s hand. For the fire flashing in the angel’s hand is like a whip, like that of one who drives the rain along. The angel drives the clouds along just as a driver urges his mounts onward.

Earthquakes are among the signs by which Allah strikes fear into His servants, just as He does through eclipses and other signs. Events have causes and wise purposes; their being signs by which Allah strikes fear into His servants is one such wise purpose.

As for their causes, one is the compression of vapour within the earth, just as air and water are compressed in a confined space. When compressed, it seeks an outlet, splitting and shaking the nearby earth.

As for some people’s claim that a bull moves its head and thereby moves the earth, this is ignorance, even if it has been reported from some people. Its falsehood is evident: if that were so, the whole earth would shake, whereas that is not the case. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 12334

The Book of Funerals

He—may Allah Most High have mercy on him—was asked:

Concerning Muslims who live alongside Christians: Is it permissible for a Muslim to visit a Christian when he is ill and to follow his funeral procession when he dies? Does a Muslim who does so incur any sin?

He replied:

Praise be to Allah, Lord of all worlds. He must not follow his funeral procession. As for visiting him when he is ill, there is nothing wrong with that, for it may serve to win him over to Islam. If he dies as a disbeliever, however, Hellfire becomes his due; hence no funeral prayer is offered for him. And Allah knows best.

He was also asked:

Concerning the bile of slaughtered animals and other animals, whether lawful or forbidden to eat: Is it permissible to use their bile as a remedy or not?

---

## Page 12335

He replied:

Praise be to Allah. If the slaughtered animal is lawful to eat, its bile may be used as a remedy; otherwise, it may not.

He was asked:

Is it permissible to use wine as a remedy?

He replied:

Using wine as a remedy is forbidden by an explicit statement of the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. This is the position of the overwhelming majority of scholars. It is established in the Sahīh:

“He was asked about wine prepared for medicinal use and said: ‘It is a disease, not a remedy.’”

The Sunan collections report:

“He forbade the use of foul substances as remedies.”

Ibn Masʿūd said:

“Allah has not placed your cure in what He has forbidden you.”

Ibn Hibbān narrated in his Sahīh that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Allah has not placed the cure for my community in what He has forbidden them.”

The Sunan collections also report:

“He was asked about a frog being used in a remedy. He forbade killing it and said: ‘Its croaking is glorification of Allah.’”

This is not comparable to someone compelled by necessity to eat carrion. Eating it certainly achieves the intended purpose, and the person has no alternative. Eating it is obligatory: whoever is compelled by necessity to eat carrion but refrains until he dies will enter Hellfire. Here, however, it is not known

---

## Page 12336

that a cure will result, nor is this particular remedy indispensable. Rather, Allah Most High restores His servant to health through various means. Seeking treatment is not obligatory according to the majority of scholars, so the two cases cannot be treated as analogous. Allah knows best.

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

Regarding using wine as a remedy and the view of those who permit it: what is the meaning of the Prophet’s words, may Allah bless him and grant him peace:

“It is a disease, not a remedy”?

What evidence do those who permit it in cases of necessity have? They also say that the hadith,

“Allah has not placed the cure for my community in what He has forbidden them,”

is weak. Permitting its medicinal use contradicts the hadith, so what evidence do those who hold this view have?

He replied:

Using wine as a remedy is forbidden according to the overwhelming majority of leading scholars, including Mālik, Ahmad and Abū Hanīfah. This is also one of the two views within the school of al-Shāfiʿī, for it is established in the Sahīh that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace,

“was asked about wine prepared for medicinal use and said: ‘It is a disease, not a remedy.’”

The Sunan of Abū Dāwūd reports that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace,

“forbade the use of foul substances as remedies.”

Wine is the mother of all foul things. Al-Bukhārī and others related that Ibn Masʿūd said:

“Allah has not placed the cure for my community in what He has forbidden them.”

It was also narrated by Abū Hātim ibn Hibbān

---

## Page 12337

in his Sahīh, tracing it back to the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace.

Those who permit treatment with forbidden substances draw an analogy with the permissibility of forbidden foods, such as carrion and blood, for someone in dire necessity. This analogy is weak for several reasons.

First, someone in dire necessity is certain to achieve his purpose by consuming these forbidden foods: eating them keeps him alive and relieves his desperate need. By contrast, a cure through foul substances—or indeed through other remedies—is not certain. How many people seek treatment without being cured! For this reason, scholars have permitted using wine to dislodge something stuck in the throat, because it achieves the intended result and is the only available means of doing so. Drinking it to relieve thirst is different, and they have disagreed over it, saying that it does not quench thirst.

Second, someone in dire necessity has no way to relieve that necessity except by eating these substances. In medical treatment, however, consuming this foul substance is not the only means of recovery. There are many kinds of medicines, and recovery may occur without medicines, through supplication and recitation for healing. This is the greater of the two forms of treatment. Hippocrates even said:

“Our medicine, compared with the medicine of the temple masters, is like old women’s medicine compared with ours.”

Recovery may also occur without any deliberately undertaken means, through the natural powers Allah places in the body and other such things.

---

## Page 12338

Third, eating carrion is obligatory for someone in dire necessity, according to the apparent position of the imams and others. As Masrūq said: “Whoever is compelled by necessity to eat carrion but refuses to eat until he dies will enter the Fire.” Medical treatment, however, is not obligatory according to the overwhelming majority of imams.

Only a small minority have considered it obligatory, including some followers of al-Shāfi‘ī and Ahmad. Indeed, scholars have disagreed over which is better: seeking treatment or patiently enduring illness. This is because of the authentic hadith narrated by Ibn ‘Abbās about:

“A young woman who suffered seizures and asked the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, to supplicate for her. He said: ‘If you wish, you may remain patient and Paradise will be yours; or, if you wish, I will ask Allah to heal you.’ She replied: ‘Rather, I will remain patient. But I become uncovered, so ask Allah that I not become uncovered.’ He therefore supplicated for her not to become uncovered.”

It is also because many of the Companions and their successors did not seek treatment; some even chose illness, such as Ubayy ibn Ka‘b and Abū Dharr. Yet they were not censured for forgoing treatment.

Since eating carrion is obligatory while seeking treatment is not, drawing an analogy between them is invalid. For an obligatory act may permit what is permitted in a non-obligatory act, because the benefit of fulfilling the obligation outweighs the harm of the prohibited act. The Lawgiver takes both harms and benefits into account; when they coincide, He gives precedence to the greater benefit over the lesser harm. Thus, in obligatory jihad He permitted things He did not permit otherwise, even allowing the enemy to be bombarded with catapults, though this might lead to the killing of

---

## Page 12339

women and children, although deliberately killing them is forbidden. There are many similar examples in Islamic law. And Allah knows best.

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

A man was prescribed pig fat for an illness he had. Is this permissible for him or not?

He replied:

Treating an illness by consuming pig fat is not permissible. As for applying it to the body as a treatment and then washing it off, this depends on whether direct contact with impure substances outside prayer is permissible. There is a well-known disagreement concerning this, but the correct view is that it is permissible when there is a need, just as a man may use his hand to cleanse himself after relieving himself or to remove an impure substance.

Whatever is permitted on account of need may be used for medical treatment. Thus, wearing silk as a treatment is permissible according to the more correct of the two scholarly views. However, whatever is permitted only out of dire necessity, such as unclean foods, may not be used for medical treatment, just as drinking wine for treatment is not permissible—especially according to those who say that they used to use the fat of dead animals to coat ships and grease hides

---

## Page 12340

and burn it in lamps, and that the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—approved of their doing so. He only prohibited them from taking payment for it. For this reason, those who do not consider the hides of dead animals to be purified by tanning nevertheless permit their use for dry materials, according to the more correct of the two views, and for liquids that the hides do not render impure.

He was asked:

Is using wine, pork, and other prohibited substances for medical treatment permissible out of necessity? Does the following verse permit what has been mentioned, or not?

“He has already explained to you what He has forbidden you, except that to which you are compelled by necessity.”

He replied:

Using these substances for medical treatment is not permissible. Indeed, it is established in the Sahīh:

“The Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—was asked about using wine for medical treatment. He said: ‘It is a disease, not a remedy.’”

And in the Sunan collections:

“He forbade treatment with unclean substances and said: ‘Allah has not placed my community’s cure in what He has forbidden them.’”

This is not a case of necessity, since recovery through such substances is not certain, whereas satisfying hunger by eating prohibited meat is certain. Moreover, recovery is not confined to a single means; rather, it can be achieved through various kinds

---

## Page 12341

of medicines and by other means, unlike severe hunger, which can be relieved only by eating.

He was asked:

If physicians tell a sick person, “There is no remedy for you other than eating dog meat or pork,” is he permitted to eat it, despite the Most High’s words:

“He makes lawful for them what is wholesome and forbids them what is foul,”

and the Prophet’s saying—may Allah bless him and grant him peace:

“Allah has not placed my community’s cure in what He has forbidden them”?

If he is prescribed wine or nabīdh, is he permitted to drink it despite these texts, or not?

He replied:

It is not permissible to use wine or other unclean substances for medical treatment, on account of the report narrated by Wā’il ibn Hujr:

“Tāriq ibn Suwayd al-Ju‘fī asked the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—about wine, and he forbade him to use it. He said, ‘I only make it for medicinal purposes.’ He replied, ‘It is not a remedy; rather, it is a disease.’”

Reported by Imam Ahmad and by Muslim in his Sahīh.

Abū al-Dardā’ narrated that the Messenger of Allah—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—said:

“Allah has sent down the remedy and sent down the disease, and has provided a remedy for every disease. So seek treatment, but do not treat yourselves with anything forbidden.”

Reported by Abū Dāwūd. Abū Hurayrah narrated:

“The Messenger of Allah—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—forbade treatment with unclean substances.”

One wording adds: “meaning poison.” Reported by

---

## Page 12342

Ahmad, Ibn Mājah and al-Tirmidhī.

‘Abd al-Rahmān ibn ‘Uthmān narrated:

“A physician mentioned a medicine in the presence of the Messenger of Allah—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—and mentioned that a frog was included in it. The Messenger of Allah—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—forbade killing frogs.”

Reported by Ahmad, Abū Dāwūd and al-Nasā’ī.

‘Abd Allāh ibn Mas‘ūd said concerning intoxication:

“Allah has not placed your cure in what He has forbidden you.”

Al-Bukhārī cited this in his Sahīh. Abū Hātim ibn Hibbān also narrated it in his Sahīh as a saying traced back to the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace. These texts and others like them explicitly prohibit treatment with unclean substances and expressly forbid treatment with wine, for it is the mother of all foul things and encompasses every sin.

The term khamr applies to every intoxicant, as established by reports from the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace. Muslim narrated in his Sahīh, from Ibn ‘Umar, that the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—said:

“Every intoxicant is khamr, and all khamr is forbidden.”

Another version reads:

“Every intoxicant is forbidden.”

In the two Sahīhs, Abū Mūsā al-Ash‘arī narrated:

“I said, ‘O Messenger of Allah, give us a ruling concerning two drinks we used to make in Yemen: al-bit‘, made from honey left to ferment until it becomes strong, and al-mizr, made from sorghum and barley left to ferment until it becomes strong.’ The Messenger of Allah—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—had been endowed with concise, comprehensive speech, and he said, ‘Every intoxicant is forbidden.’”

Likewise, in the two Sahīhs, ‘Ā’ishah narrated:

“The Messenger of Allah

---

## Page 12343

—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—was asked about al-bit‘, a fermented honey drink which the people of Yemen used to drink. He said, ‘Every drink that intoxicates is forbidden.’”

Muslim in his Sahīh, al-Nasā’ī and others also narrated from Jābir:

“A man from Hubshān in Yemen asked the Messenger of Allah—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—about a drink made from sorghum that they drank in their land, called al-mizr. He asked, ‘Is it intoxicating?’ The man replied, ‘Yes.’ He said, ‘Every intoxicant is forbidden. Allah has pledged to give whoever drinks intoxicants a drink of the mire of corruption.’” The hadith continues.

These widely transmitted hadiths explicitly establish that every intoxicant is forbidden and is khamr, whatever it is made from. It is not permissible to use any of these substances as medicine.

As for physicians’ claim that a person cannot recover from a particular illness except through one specific medicine, this is an ignorant person’s claim. No one with any knowledge of medicine would say this, let alone someone who knows Allah and His Messenger. Recovery is not tied to a particular cause that ordinarily brings it about in the way that satiety has a particular cause that ordinarily produces it. Allah heals some people without medicine, while He heals others through physical remedies, whether lawful or unlawful. A remedy may also be administered without bringing about recovery because a necessary condition is absent or an impediment is present.

This differs from eating, which is a cause of satiety. Hence Allah has permitted a person in dire necessity to eat unclean foods when extreme hunger compels him to do so: these foods dispel his hunger, and nothing else available will do so; otherwise, he will die or fall ill from hunger. Thus, when they became the only means

---

## Page 12344

of achieving that end, Allah permitted them, unlike unclean medicines.

Indeed, it has even been said that seeking healing through unclean medicines indicates a sickness in one’s heart—that is, in one’s faith. For if a person were among the believing members of Muhammad’s community, Allah would not have placed his healing in what He had forbidden him. Hence, when someone is compelled by necessity to eat carrion or the like, eating it becomes obligatory according to the well-known position in the schools of the four imams.
