Medical treatment, however, is not obligatory according to most scholars, even when lawful remedies are used. They differed over whether it is better to seek treatment or to forgo it out of reliance upon Allah.

This is further clarified by the fact that when Allah forbade carrion, blood, pork and other such things, He permitted them only to those compelled by necessity, neither seeking them out nor exceeding the limits. In another verse, He says:

“But whoever is forced by severe hunger, with no inclination to sin—then indeed, Allah is Forgiving and Merciful.”

It is known that a person seeking medical treatment is not compelled by necessity to use these things; thus, they have not become lawful for him.

As for what is permitted on grounds of need, and not only dire necessity—such as wearing silk—it is established in the Sahīh:

“The Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—permitted al-Zubayr and ‘Abd al-Rahmān ibn ‘Awf to wear silk because of itching from which they both suffered.”

This is permissible according to the more correct of the scholars’ two views, because wearing silk is forbidden only when one has no need of it. Hence, it is permitted for women because of their need to adorn themselves with it, and they are permitted to use it to cover themselves without restriction. The need to use it for medical treatment warrants the same permission—indeed, all the more so. Such clothing was prohibited because it involves

---

## Page 12345

extravagance, arrogance and boasting, none of which is present when it is needed. The same applies to wearing it for protection from the cold, or when one has nothing else with which to cover oneself.

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

Does the purified Sacred Law deny what the devils among the jinn do to human beings: possessing and deranging them, their flashes darting over them, and assailing them? Can this be treated through magical contrivances, protective amulets, incantations, adjurations, healing recitations, invocations for protection, and charms?

Some people have said that they cannot be controlled, because the jinn return to the realities when flourishing bodies fall into ruin; that these seals which people carry—whether Syriac, Hebrew, non-Arabic or Arabic—have no evidence supporting them, but are fabricated sayings and baseless superstitions; and that no human being possesses the strength or power of control necessary to perform the things we have mentioned concerning those who have undertaken this practice throughout the ages.

He replied:

Praise be to Allah. The existence of the jinn is established by the Book of Allah, the Sunnah of His Messenger, and the agreement of the early generations of this community and its imams. Likewise, the entry of a jinn into a human body is established by the agreement of the imams of Ahl al-Sunnah wa-l-Jamā‘ah. Allah Most High says:

“Those who consume usury will not stand except as one whom Satan has driven to madness by his touch.”

And in the

---

## Page 12346

authentic hadith collection, it is reported that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Satan flows through the son of Adam as blood flows.”

‘Abd Allāh, son of Imam Ahmad ibn Hanbal, said: “I said to my father, ‘Some people say that a jinn does not enter the body of a possessed person.’ He replied, ‘My son, they are lying. Here it is, speaking through his tongue.’”

What he described is well known. A jinn possesses a man, who then speaks a language whose meaning he does not understand. His body may be struck with blows so severe that, were a camel struck with them, they would leave a tremendous mark upon it. Yet the possessed person is unaware of the blows or the words he utters. The jinn may drag a possessed person or someone who is not possessed, pull the mat on which he sits, shift equipment, move things from place to place, and perform other such acts. Those who witness these things gain indubitable knowledge that what speaks through the human being’s tongue and moves these bodies is a kind of being other than a human.

None of the imams of the Muslims denies that a jinn can enter the body of a possessed person or anyone else. Whoever denies this and claims that the Sacred Law rejects it has falsely attributed that claim to the Sacred Law. There is nothing in the scriptural evidence that negates it.

As for treating the possessed with healing recitations and invocations for protection, this falls into

---

## Page 12347

two categories:

If the healing recitations and invocations for protection have known meanings and contain words that Islam permits a person to utter when supplicating to Allah, remembering Him, addressing His creation, or the like, then they may be recited over the possessed person for healing and protection. For it is authentically reported of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace:

“He permitted healing recitations so long as they involved no associating partners with Allah.”

He also said:

“Whoever among you can benefit his brother should do so.”

If, however, they contain forbidden words, such as words involving associating partners with Allah, or words of unknown meaning that might contain disbelief, no one may use them for healing recitations, incantations, or adjurations—even if they cause the jinn to leave the possessed person. For whatever Allah and His Messenger have forbidden brings more harm than benefit. This includes sīmiyā and other forms of magic. A magician who practices sīmiyā may attain some of his aims through it, just as a thief attains some of his aims through theft, a liar through lying and betrayal, and a polytheist through associating partners with Allah and disbelief. Yet even if these people attain some of their aims through such forbidden practices, those practices bring upon them harm in this world and the Hereafter far greater than whatever they have gained by attaining their aims.

Allah sent the messengers to secure and perfect benefits, and to eliminate and reduce harms. Thus, in everything Allah and His Messenger have commanded, the good outweighs the evil and the benefit outweighs the harm, even if people dislike it.

As Allah Most High says:

“Fighting has been prescribed for you, though you dislike it. Yet you may dislike something that is good

---

## Page 12348

for you”—to the end of the verse. He thus commanded jihad, although people naturally dislike it, because its benefits outweigh the pain it causes them. This is like someone who drinks unpleasant medicine to recover his health: the benefit of regaining his health outweighs the discomfort of drinking the medicine. Likewise, a merchant travels far from his homeland, stays awake at night, experiences fear, and endures these hardships because the benefit of the profit he gains outweighs them.

In the two Sahīh collections, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, is reported to have said:

“Paradise is surrounded by hardships, and the Fire is surrounded by desires.”

Allah Most High says concerning the magician:

“The magician will never succeed, wherever he may go.”

And He Most High says:

“Yet the two would not teach anyone without first saying, ‘We are only a trial, so do not disbelieve’”—up to His words—“How wretched was the price for which they sold themselves, if only they knew!”

He, glorified be He, thus makes clear that these people know that the magician will have no share in the Hereafter. They seek through this only to attain some of their worldly aims.

“Had they believed and been mindful of Allah, a reward from Allah would have been better, if only they knew!”

Had they believed and been mindful of Allah by doing what He commanded and refraining from what He forbade, what He would grant them in return, in this world and the Hereafter, would have been better for them than what they obtain through magic.

Allah Most High says:

“We certainly help Our messengers and those who believe, both in the life of this world and on the Day when the witnesses stand forth.”

And He says:

“Whoever does righteous deeds, male or female, while being a believer, We will surely grant them a good life.”

And He says:

“As for those who emigrated for Allah’s sake after suffering injustice, We will surely grant them a good home in this world.”

---

## Page 12349

—both verses.

And He says:

“Among them are those who say, ‘Our Lord, grant us good in this world and good in the Hereafter, and protect us from the punishment of the Fire.’”

“Those will have a share of what they have earned.”

There are very many hadiths concerning the rewards Allah grants His believing servant for righteous deeds, both in this world and in the Hereafter. A servant may not ward off every harm by whatever means he pleases, nor secure every benefit by whatever means he pleases. Rather, he must seek benefit and avert harm only through means consistent with mindfulness of Allah. If the incantations, adjurations, supplications, seclusion, keeping vigil, and similar practices he engages in are among those Allah and His Messenger have permitted, there is nothing wrong with them. But if they are among those Allah and His Messenger have forbidden, he must not engage in them.

Whoever denies the reality of jinn, devils, magic, and the various things they accomplish—such as invoking the heavenly bodies, drawing forth active celestial forces through receptive terrestrial forces, and the descent of devils upon every sinful liar, for what they call the spirits of the heavenly bodies are the devils that descend upon them—and denies that jinn enter human bodies or appear when summoned through incantations, adjurations, and similar means, as actually occurs, has denied what he does not fully comprehend.

Whoever holds that a person may employ whichever of these practices he considers effective without weighing them against the law of Islam—doing what Allah has permitted and refraining from

---

## Page 12350

what Allah has forbidden—has fallen into what Allah and His Messenger have forbidden, whether unbelief, wickedness, or disobedience. Rather, everyone must do what Allah and His Messenger have commanded and refrain from what Allah and His Messenger have forbidden.

Among the forms of seeking refuge prescribed by the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, are the following. It is authentically reported from him in the Sahīh that he said:

“Whoever recites the Verse of the Throne when retiring to bed will remain under the protection of a guardian from Allah, and no devil will approach him until morning.”

The Sunan collections report that he used to teach his Companions to say:

“I seek refuge in the perfect words of Allah from His anger and punishment, from the evil of His servants, from the incitements of the devils, and from their presence.”

“When the devils came to him with a flame of fire, he instructed that refuge be sought with these words: ‘I seek refuge in the perfect words of Allah, which neither the righteous nor the wicked can overstep, from the evil of what He has created and brought into being; from the evil of what descends from heaven and what ascends into it; from the evil of what He has brought forth upon the earth and what emerges from it; from the trials of night and day; and from the evil of every visitor by night, except one who comes bringing good, O Most Merciful.’”

The scholars have compiled ample remembrances and supplications for a servant to recite in the morning, in the evening, when going to sleep, when afraid of something, and on similar occasions. Whoever follows such a path has followed the path of Allah’s allies, who shall have no fear, nor shall they grieve. But whoever enters upon the path of the followers of jibt and tāghūt—a path involving associating partners with Allah and sorcery—has lost both this world and the Hereafter. For this, censure was pronounced by

---

## Page 12351

Allah upon those He censured among the People of the Book who altered their Scripture, when He said:

“And when a Messenger from Allah came to them, confirming what was with them, a group of those who had been given the Scripture cast the Book of Allah behind their backs, as though they did not know.”

“And they followed what the devils recited over…”—up to His words—“How wretched was that for which they sold themselves, if only they knew.”

And Allah, Glorified and Exalted, knows best.

He also said elsewhere, may Allah have mercy on him:

Section

As for the nature of the jinn and their speech not having become clear to him, their existence is not denied on account of his lack of knowledge. Their existence is established in many ways besides the evidence of the Quran and Sunnah. Some people have seen them; others have seen those who have seen them, and this has become established for them through reports and certain knowledge. Some people have spoken to them, and they have spoken in return. Others command them, forbid them, and exercise authority over them. This occurs among both the righteous and the unrighteous. If I were to recount what happened to me and my companions with them, the discussion would be lengthy; and likewise

---

## Page 12352

what happened to others. In scholarly responses, however, reliance is placed on what people know in common, not on what is known only to the respondent, unless he is answering someone who believes what he reports.

He was asked about someone who says, “O Azirrān! O Kiyān!” Is it established that these names occur in the Sunnah and that uttering them is not forbidden?

He replied:

Praise be to Allah. No one has transmitted these expressions from the Companions through either an authentic or a weak chain of transmission, nor from the early generations of the Muslim community or its leading scholars. These expressions have no meaning in the Arabic language. No one may use any name of unknown meaning in recitations for protection or healing, let alone in supplication. Even if one knew their meaning and knew it to be sound, it would still be disliked to supplicate Allah using non-Arabic names.

He was asked about someone afflicted with an illness who, when his pain becomes severe, appeals to Allah Most High for help and weeps. Does his appeal for help conflict with the patience he is commanded to observe? Or is it

---

## Page 12353

an act of humble entreaty and seeking refuge?

He replied:

His supplication to Allah, his appeal to Him for help, and his complaint to Him do not conflict with the patience he is commanded to observe. What conflicts with it in this situation is complaining to a created being. Jacob, peace be upon him, said:

“So patience is most fitting.”

He also said:

“I complain of my anguish and sorrow only to Allah.”

It is reported that Tāwūs disliked a sick person’s groaning, saying that it was a form of complaint. This was read to Ahmad ibn Hanbal during his final illness, and he did not groan thereafter until he died.

It is also reported that al-Sarī al-Saqatī regarded a sick person’s utterance of “Ah!” as remembrance of Allah. This applies when it is between the person and Allah. This resembles what is reported of ʿUmar ibn al-Khattāb, may Allah be pleased with him: during the dawn prayer, he recited:

“I complain of my anguish and sorrow only to Allah.”

Then he wept until his sobbing could be heard from the back rows. Thus, groaning and weeping out of fear of Allah, humbly entreating Him, and complaining to Allah, Mighty and Majestic, are good. What is blameworthy, however, remains blameworthy. Allah knows best.

He was asked about a man afflicted with illness who took up residence in a house among healthy people. Some of them said, “We cannot live beside you, and you should not live among healthy people.” Is it permissible to evict him?

---

## Page 12354

He replied:

Yes, they may prevent him from living among healthy people, for the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, said:

“An owner of sick camels must not bring them to an owner of healthy camels.”

Thus, he forbade the owner of sick camels from bringing them to the owner of healthy camels, despite also saying:

“There is no contagion and no evil omen.”

It is likewise reported that when a man with leprosy came to pledge allegiance to him, he sent word accepting his pledge and did not permit him to enter Medina.

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

Does anyone receive a reward for offering the funeral prayer for someone who did not pray? Is a person sinful for refraining from offering it when he knows that the deceased did not pray? Likewise, if someone drank wine and did not pray, may a person who knew his condition offer the funeral prayer for him or not?

He replied:

Anyone who outwardly professes Islam is subject to the outward legal rulings of Islam concerning marriage, mutual inheritance, washing his body, offering the funeral prayer for him, burying him in Muslim cemeteries, and similar matters. However, if someone’s hypocrisy and heresy are known, anyone who knows this about him may not offer the funeral prayer for him, even if he outwardly professes Islam. Allah forbade His Prophet from offering the funeral prayer for the hypocrites, saying:

“Never offer the funeral prayer for any of them who dies, nor stand at his grave. They disbelieved in Allah and His Messenger

---

## Page 12355

and died defiantly disobedient.” And He said:

“It is all the same for them whether you ask forgiveness for them or not: Allah will never forgive them.”

As for those who openly commit sins while retaining faith, such as those who commit major sins, some Muslims must offer the funeral prayer for them. Whoever refrains from offering the funeral prayer for one of them to deter others like him from similar conduct acts commendably in following this Sunnah. The Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, refrained from offering the funeral prayer for someone who killed himself, someone who misappropriated war booty, and someone who died in debt without leaving the means to repay it. Likewise, many of the early generations refrained from offering the funeral prayer for adherents of religious innovations.

(Jundub ibn ‘Abd Allāh al-Bajalī’s son once told him, “I could not sleep last night because of indigestion from overeating.” He replied, “Indeed, had you died, I would not have offered the funeral prayer for you”) (*).

It is as though he were saying, “You killed yourself by overeating.” This is akin to shunning those who openly commit major sins until they repent. When doing so serves an overriding benefit of this kind, it is commendable. Likewise, if someone offers the funeral prayer for one of them, hoping that Allah will have mercy on him, and refraining would serve no overriding benefit, that too is commendable. If he were to refrain publicly while supplicating for the deceased privately, thereby combining both benefits, securing both would be preferable to forfeiting either one.

It is permissible to seek forgiveness for any Muslim who is not known to be a hypocrite and to offer the funeral prayer for him. Indeed, this is prescribed and enjoined, as Allah Most High said:

“Ask forgiveness for your sin, and for the believing men and women.”

Anyone who openly commits major sins may be punished by being shunned

> **Footnotes:**
> (*) Shaykh Nāsir ibn Hamad al-Fahd stated (p. 201):
> The shaykh—may Allah have mercy on him—identified the person elsewhere (24/291) as Samura ibn Jundub, which is correct. The account appears in Ibn Abī ‘Āsim’s al-Zuhd (1/199). Perhaps the name given here is a slip of the pen. Allah knows best.

---

## Page 12356

and by other means, even by someone whose shunning him would serve an overriding benefit for him. Thus, the benefits sought by Islamic law are achieved as far as possible. And Allah knows best.

He was asked:

“Should the funeral prayer be offered for a man who prays occasionally but frequently neglects prayer, or who does not pray at all?”

He answered:

Muslims have always offered the funeral prayer for such a person. Indeed, Muslims offer the funeral prayer even for hypocrites who conceal their hypocrisy; their bodies are washed, and the rulings of Islam apply to them, as was the case with the hypocrites during the lifetime of the Messenger of Allah, peace and blessings be upon him.

If, however, someone knows that a person is a hypocrite, he may not offer the funeral prayer for him, just as the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, forbade offering the funeral prayer for anyone whose hypocrisy was known. As for someone whose condition is uncertain, it is permissible to offer the funeral prayer for him if he outwardly professes Islam. Thus, the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, offered the funeral prayer for those for whom he had not been forbidden to pray, and among them were some whose hypocrisy was unknown.

As Allah Most High said:

“Among the Bedouins around you are hypocrites, and among the people of Medina are some hardened in hypocrisy. You do not know them; We know them.”

For such people, it is not permissible to forbid

---

## Page 12357

offering the funeral prayer for them. Yet the funeral prayer offered by the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, and the believers does not benefit a hypocrite. Thus, when the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, clothed Ibn Ubayy in his shirt, he said, “How can my shirt avail him against Allah?”

Allah Most High said:

“It is all the same for them whether you ask forgiveness for them or do not ask forgiveness for them: Allah will never forgive them.”

As for someone who sometimes neglects prayer, and others like him who openly commit sins, people of knowledge and religious commitment should shun him and refrain from offering his funeral prayer if doing so benefits the Muslims by encouraging them to be diligent in offering the prayer over him. This is just as the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, refrained from offering the funeral prayer for someone who had killed himself, someone guilty of misappropriation, and a debtor who had no means of repayment. Such a person is worse than they are.

Shaykh al-Islām—may Allah have mercy on him—said:

Section

It is established that the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, would refrain from offering the funeral prayer for someone who owed a debt unless he left sufficient means to repay it. This was before the Prophet had the means to discharge the debt on his behalf. Once he had the means, he would pay it himself from his own resources, so the debtor thereby left provision for repayment.

---

## Page 12358

This was so despite his saying, as reported from him by Abū Mūsā:

“The gravest sin with which a servant can meet Allah, after the major sins He has forbidden, is for a man to die owing a debt and leaving nothing with which to repay it.”

Narrated by Ahmad. This establishes that leaving an unpaid debt ranks after the major sins. If he refrained from offering the funeral prayer for a debtor who left no means of repayment, then refraining from offering it for someone who committed major sins is all the more warranted. This includes someone who killed himself and someone guilty of misappropriation, for he did not offer the funeral prayer for either of them. This may be cited as evidence that people of virtue may refrain from offering the funeral prayer for those who openly commit major sins and those who call others to religious innovations, although offering the funeral prayer for them is permissible in general.

As for his saying:

“A martyr is forgiven everything except debt,”

he meant that the creditor must be paid what he is owed.

He was asked:

A man had a slave who ran away and then returned. Upon returning, the slave took his knife and killed himself. Does his master bear any sin? Is it permissible to offer the funeral prayer for him?

He answered:

Praise be to Allah. He had no right to kill himself, even if his master had wronged and mistreated him. Rather, if he could not free himself from the injustice, his duty was

---

## Page 12359

to remain patient until Allah granted relief. If his master wronged him to the point that he did this—for example, by stinting on his upkeep, overworking him, striking him unjustly, seeking to commit an indecent act with him, or the like—then his master bears sin in proportion to the wrongdoing attributable to him.

“The Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, did not offer the funeral prayer for someone who had killed himself. He said to his Companions, ‘Offer the funeral prayer for him.’”

Thus, people in general may offer the funeral prayer for him. As for religious leaders whose example is followed, if they refrain from offering the funeral prayer for him to deter others, following the Prophet’s example, then this is right. Allah knows best.

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

A man claiming to be a spiritual master saw a serpent. One of those present rose to kill it, but he prevented him and took hold of it with his own hand as a display of a miraculous gift granted to him. The serpent bit him, and he died. Is it permissible to offer the funeral prayer for him or not?

He answered:

Praise be to Allah, Lord of all worlds. People of knowledge and religious devotion should refrain from offering the funeral prayer for this man and others like him, although people in general may offer it for him, just as “the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, refrained from offering the funeral prayer for someone who had killed himself and for

---

## Page 12360

one who had embezzled from the spoils of war, and said, ‘Offer the funeral prayer for your companion.’”

They said to Samurah ibn Jundub, “Your son did not sleep last night.” He asked, “Because of indigestion from overeating?” They replied, “Yes.” He said, “Had he died, I would not have offered the funeral prayer for him.” Samurah thus explained that, had his son died from overeating, he would not have offered the funeral prayer for him, because he would have killed himself by eating excessively.

There is even greater reason, then, for people of knowledge and religious devotion to refrain from offering the funeral prayer for this man, who prevented the serpent from being killed and held it in his hand until it killed him, for he killed himself. Indeed, had someone else done this to him, that person would have been liable to execution in retaliation.

If it is said that he thought it would not kill him, then his action is comparable to that of someone who ate until he suffered indigestion: he too did not intend to kill himself. When someone commits an injurious act that does not ordinarily cause death, this constitutes quasi-intentional homicide. Handling serpents is a form of wrongful injury, since it is not a permissible act. This man intended nothing by his action except to display something extraordinary, and he had nothing with him to prevent a breach of the ordinary course of events.

How much more so when most of these people are liars and deceivers who disregard Allah Most High’s commands and prohibitions! They lead people away from obedience to the Most Merciful and into obedience to Satan, corrupting their reason, their religion, and their worldly lives. They turn a rational person into someone bereft of his senses like a madman, or frenzied like a deluded devil. They lead people away from the revealed law with which Allah sent His Messenger, peace and blessings be upon him, into innovations opposed to it. Thus, they twist their hair

---

## Page 12361

and uncover their heads, instead of following the Sunnah of Allah’s Messenger, peace and blessings be upon him, of grooming the hair and covering the head. They gather for whistling and clapping instead of following the way prescribed by Allah and His Messenger of gathering for the five daily prayers and other acts of worship. Their prayers lack essential pillars and obligatory elements, yet they gather to perform their reprehensible innovations in the most complete manner.

They prepare lādhan, rosewater, and saffron for handling serpents and entering fire, employing various physical tricks and satanic states instead of the lawful paths and spiritual states bestowed by the Most Merciful that Allah has granted His God-fearing allies. They corrupt some women and boys instead of observing what Allah has commanded of chastity, lowering the gaze, guarding one’s private parts, and restraining the tongue.

Anyone who openly practises religious innovation must be reproved. One prescribed form of such reproof is to shun him until he repents. This includes religious people refraining from offering the funeral prayer for him, to deter those who imitate his way and call others to it. Mālik ibn Anas, Ahmad ibn Hanbal, and other leading scholars have prescribed such measures. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 12362

He was asked:

A man went to sea for trade and drowned. Did he die a martyr?

He answered:

Yes, he died a martyr, provided that undertaking the voyage was not sinful. It is authentically reported that the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, said:

“One who drowns and one who dies of an abdominal illness are martyrs. One who dies in a fire is a martyr; one who dies of plague is a martyr; a woman who dies during her postpartum period is a martyr; and one who dies beneath a collapsed structure is a martyr.”

Others have also been mentioned as martyrs. Travelling by sea for trade is permissible when safety is the most likely outcome. Otherwise, a person may not undertake a sea voyage for trade. If he does, he has helped bring about his own death, and such a person is not to be called a martyr. And Allah knows best.

He was asked:

What about raising one’s voice during a funeral procession?

He answered:

Praise be to Allah. It is not recommended to raise one’s voice while accompanying a funeral procession, whether through recitation

---

## Page 12363

or remembrance of Allah, or anything else. This is the position of the four imams and what is transmitted from the early generations—the Companions and their successors. I know of no dissenting view on this. Indeed, it is reported that the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, “forbade accompanying a funeral with raised voices or fire.” Narrated by Abū Dāwūd.

Abdullāh ibn Umar, may Allah be pleased with them both, heard a man saying during a funeral procession, “Ask forgiveness for your brother.” Ibn Umar replied, “May Allah grant no forgiveness after this.”

Qays ibn Abbād—one of the leading successors and a companion of Alī ibn Abī Tālib, may Allah be pleased with him—said: “They used to regard lowering the voice as recommended during funeral processions, during remembrance of Allah, and during battle.” Scholars versed in hadith and transmitted reports agree that this practice of raising voices did not exist during the three most virtuous generations.

As for the questioner’s statement that this has become a matter of consensus among the people, that is not so. There have always been Muslims who disapprove of it, and many funeral processions have continued to take place without it in various Muslim cities. The fact that the inhabitants of one, two, or ten towns have become accustomed to it does not constitute consensus. Indeed, the inhabitants of the Prophet’s city, peace and blessings be upon him—where the Quran and Sunnah were revealed, the home of emigration, support, faith, and knowledge—did not practise it. Indeed, even if they had agreed on something in a period such as that of Mālik and his teachers, but had not transmitted it from

---

## Page 12364

the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, or his caliphs, their consensus would not constitute binding proof according to the majority of Muslims. After the time of Mālik and his companions, their consensus does not constitute binding proof by agreement of the Muslims. How, then, could the consensus of the inhabitants of other cities do so?

As for the claim, “This resembles Jewish and Christian funerals,” that is not so. Rather, the People of the Book customarily raise their voices during funeral processions, and the terms of their covenant of protection stipulated that they must not do so. Moreover, we have only been forbidden to imitate them in matters that are not part of the way of our earliest predecessors. When we follow the way of our earliest predecessors, we are acting correctly, even if others share some of those practices with us, just as they share our practice of burial in the earth and other practices.

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

“A Christian woman whose husband is Muslim has died while carrying a seven-month-old fetus. Should she be buried among the Muslims or among the Christians?”

He answered:

“She should be buried neither in Muslim cemeteries nor in Christian cemeteries, because a Muslim and a non-Muslim are together here. A non-Muslim is not to be buried among Muslims, nor a Muslim among non-Muslims. Rather, she should be buried separately, with her back towards the qiblah, because

---

## Page 12365

the child’s face is towards her back. If she is buried in this position, the Muslim child will be facing the qiblah. By agreement of the scholars, the child is Muslim by virtue of his father being Muslim, even if his mother is a non-Muslim.”

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

“To the mufti of the people, a surviving representative of the noble predecessors, Taqī al-Dīn, one of the remaining scholars qualified to exercise independent legal reasoning—may Allah reward him and be gracious to him: Is there any authentic report from the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, or his Companions concerning prompting the deceased in his grave after his burial is complete? If there is no such report, is it permissible to do this or not?”

He answered:

“This prompting has been reported from a group of the Companions, including Abū Umāmah al-Bāhilī and others, who instructed that it be done. A hadith concerning it has also been narrated from the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, but it cannot be judged authentic.

Many of the Companions did not practise this. For this reason, Imam Ahmad and other scholars said that there is nothing wrong with such prompting: they permitted it but did not enjoin it. Some followers of al-Shāfiʿī and Ahmad considered it recommended, while some scholars among the followers of Mālik and others considered it disliked.

---

## Page 12366

The Sunan collections report:

“The Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, would stand at the grave of one of his Companions after his burial and say: ‘Ask Allah to grant him steadfastness, for he is now being questioned.’”

It is also established in the two Sahih collections that the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, said:

“Prompt those among you who are dying to say: ‘There is no god but Allah.’”

Thus, prompting a dying person is a prescribed Sunnah. It is established that the person in the grave is questioned and tested, and that supplicating for him is enjoined. For this reason, it has been said that prompting benefits him, since the deceased hears when called. It is established in the Sahih that the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, said:

“He hears the sound of their sandals.”

He also said:

“You do not hear what I say any better than they do.”

He instructed us to greet the dead, saying:

“No man passes the grave of a man whom he knew in this world and greets him without Allah restoring the deceased’s soul to him so that he may return the greeting.”

And Allah knows best.”

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

“Is prompting the deceased after his burial obligatory or not? Does the reward of recitation reach the deceased?”

He answered:

“Prompting him after his death is not obligatory, by scholarly consensus, nor was it part of

---

## Page 12367

the well-known practice of the Muslims during the time of the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, and his caliphs. Rather, it is reported from some of the Companions, such as Abū Umāmah and Wāthilah ibn al-Asqa'. Some imams, such as Imam Ahmad, permitted it, while some of his followers and those of al-Shāfi'ī considered it recommended. Other scholars regarded it as disliked, believing it to be an innovation.

There are thus three views on it: that it is recommended, disliked, or permissible. The last is the most balanced view. What is recommended, however, and what the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, commanded and encouraged, is supplication for the deceased.

As for reciting the Quran at the grave, Abū Hanīfah and Mālik regarded it as disliked, as did Ahmad according to one of the two reports from him. According to the other report, he did not regard it as disliked. He permitted it only because he had learned that Ibn 'Umar had instructed that the opening and closing verses of Surah al-Baqarah be recited at his grave. Recitation of Surah al-Baqarah has also been reported from some of the Companions. Thus, recitation at the time of burial has some basis in transmitted reports. As for recitation after that, no report has been transmitted concerning it. And Allah knows best.”

---

## Page 12368

He was asked:
“Is prompting the deceased, whether adult or child, prescribed or not?”

He replied:
“As for prompting the deceased, a number of the scholars of the Shāfi‘ī school from Khurāsān mentioned it and regarded it favorably, including al-Mutawallī, al-Rāfi‘ī, and others. Nothing concerning it has been transmitted from al-Shāfi‘ī himself. Some of the Companions practised it, including Abū Umāmah al-Bāhilī, Wāthilah ibn al-Asqa‘, and others. Some of Ahmad’s followers considered it recommended. The sound conclusion is that it is permissible, but not a regular Sunnah practice. And Allah knows best.”

He was asked:
“Regarding a complete recitation of the Quran held for the deceased, and reciters who are paid: does the reward for their recitation reach the deceased? Does the reward for the food served on that occasion reach the deceased, or not? And if

---

## Page 12369

the deceased’s child borrows money to give charity, with repayment deferred until he has the means, does its reward reach the deceased?”

He replied:
“Hiring people to recite the Quran and dedicate its reward to the deceased is not prescribed, nor has any scholar recommended it. For the recitation whose reward reaches the deceased is that which is performed for Allah. Thus, if someone has been hired to recite for Allah, and the person hiring him has not given charity on behalf of the deceased but has instead hired someone to recite as an act of worship to Allah, Mighty and Majestic, its reward does not reach the deceased. However, if charity is given on behalf of the deceased to people who recite the Quran or to others, it benefits him by the agreement of the Muslims. Likewise, if someone recites the Quran seeking Allah’s reward and dedicates its reward to the deceased, it benefits him. And Allah knows best.”

He was asked:
“Is placing a copy of the Quran beside a grave and lighting a lantern there, without anyone reading from it, disliked or not?”

He replied:
“As for placing copies of the Quran beside graves and lighting lanterns there, this is disliked and forbidden, even if arrangements are made to read from them there. How much more so if they are not read! For the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

‘May Allah curse women who visit graves and those who establish mosques and lamps over them.’

Thus, lighting lamps, whether lanterns

---

## Page 12370

or otherwise, over graves is categorically forbidden, because it is one of the two acts whose perpetrators the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, cursed. Similarly, he said:

“Two men must not go out to relieve themselves with their private parts exposed, conversing with one another, for Allah abhors that.”

Reported by Abū Dāwūd and others. It is well known that exposing one’s private parts is forbidden on its own, as is conversing in that situation. Likewise, Allah Most High says:

“And those who do not invoke another god besides Allah, nor kill a soul whom Allah has forbidden to be killed except by right, nor commit unlawful sexual intercourse. Whoever does so will incur a penalty.”

“His punishment will be multiplied on the Day of Resurrection, and he will remain therein forever, humiliated.”

Thus, He threatened punishment for a combination of acts, each of which is forbidden. This is because attaching blame to the combination entails that each individual act contributes to that blame. If any of them were permissible, it would not contribute to the blame. A forbidden act does not become more emphatically forbidden by combining it with a particular permissible act.

The leading scholars differed over reciting the Quran at a grave. Abū Hanīfa and Mālik considered it disliked, as did Ahmad according to most reports of his position. According to another report, Ahmad permitted it, as did a group of Abū Hanīfa’s followers and others. As for placing copies of the Quran by graves for those who intend to read the Quran there

---

## Page 12371

and recite it, this is a reprehensible innovation that none of the early generations practised. Indeed, it falls within the meaning of “establishing places of worship over graves.” Numerous reports from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, prohibit this. He even said:

“May Allah curse the Jews and Christians: they took the graves of their prophets as places of worship.”

He was warning against what they had done. ʿĀ’isha said: “Were it not for that, his grave would have been exposed, but he disliked its being made a place of worship.”

He also said:

“Those who came before you used to take graves as places of worship. Beware! Do not take graves as places of worship, for I forbid you to do so.”

There is no disagreement among the early generations and the leading scholars concerning the prohibition against taking graves as places of worship. It is well known that mosques were built for prayer, remembrance of Allah, and recitation of the Quran. Thus, if a grave is used for any of these purposes, it falls within that prohibition. If this applies when people actually read from the copies of the Quran placed there, what then of placing them where they are not read, and neither the living nor the dead benefit from them? There is no disagreement concerning the prohibition of this. Had the deceased benefited from anything of this kind, the early generations would have done it, for they knew best what Allah loves and is pleased with, and were quickest to do it and seek it out.

---

## Page 12372

He was asked:

“Is it permissible to move the deceased? Do the souls of the dead meet one another? Does the soul of the deceased descend into the grave? Does the deceased recognize those who visit him?”

He answered:

Praise be to Allah. The deceased should not be exhumed except for a need, such as when something in the original burial place harms him. He may then be moved elsewhere, as some of the Companions did in such circumstances.

When the souls of the living are taken, they meet the souls of the dead. The dead ask the newcomer about the condition of the living, saying, “What happened to so-and-so?” They reply, “So-and-so has married; so-and-so is doing well.” They ask, “What happened to so-and-so?” He replies, “Has he not come to you?” They say, “No. He was taken to his mother—the Abyss.”

The souls of the dead meet one another: those higher up descend to those below, but those below do not ascend to those above. The soul looks down upon the grave and is sometimes returned to the burial niche, as the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“There is no man who passes by the grave of a man

---

## Page 12373

whom he knew in this world and greets him, but Allah restores his soul to him so that he may return the greeting.”

The deceased may recognize those who visit him. Hence, the Sunnah is to say:

“Peace be upon you, inhabitants of this abode, a believing people. We shall, if Allah wills, join you. May Allah have mercy on those among us and you who have gone before and those who will follow.”

And Allah knows best.

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

A group of people have a burial ground in an isolated place, where someone was killed. They have established another burial ground for themselves. Is it permissible to transfer their dead to the newly established burial ground or not?

He answered:

The deceased must not be exhumed for the reason mentioned. And Allah knows best.

He was asked about the assertion made by some people:

“Allah has angels who transfer the dead from Muslim cemeteries to Jewish and Christian cemeteries, and from Jewish and Christian cemeteries to Muslim cemeteries.”

What they mean is that someone who met an evil end as known to Allah, although he outwardly died a Muslim, or someone who was

---

## Page 12374

one of the People of the Book and was granted a good end, dying as a Muslim in Allah’s knowledge while outwardly appearing to die a disbeliever—such people are transferred. Has any report been transmitted concerning this? Is there any evidence for it?

He answered:

Praise be to Allah. As for bodies, they are not transferred from their graves. However, we know that some who outwardly profess Islam are hypocrites—whether Jews, Christians, or apostates and deniers. Whoever is like this will be with his counterparts on the Day of Resurrection, as Allah Most High says:

“Gather those who committed wrong, their kind, and what they used to worship.”

That is, those who resemble them and their counterparts.

Among those who outwardly died as disbelievers, there may be someone who believed in Allah before the death rattle began, with no believer present, and whose family concealed this for reasons of inheritance or otherwise. Such a person will be with the believers, even though buried among the disbelievers.

As for the angels transferring them, I have heard no report to that effect.

---

## Page 12375

He—may Allah Most High have mercy on him—was asked:

Regarding the words of Allah Most High:

“And that man will have nothing but what he strives for,”

and the words of the Prophet (peace and blessings of Allah be upon him):

“When the son of Adam dies, his deeds come to an end except for three: ongoing charity, knowledge from which benefit is derived, or a righteous child who supplicates for him.”

Does this imply that, after he dies, no benefit from acts of righteousness reaches him?

He answered:

Praise be to Allah, Lord of all worlds. Neither the verse nor the hadith states that the deceased does not benefit from others’ supplications for him or from righteous deeds performed on his behalf. Rather, the leading scholars of Islam agree that the deceased benefits from these things. This is a matter necessarily known to be part of Islam, established by the Quran, the Sunnah, and scholarly consensus. Whoever disputes this is among the people of religious innovation.

Allah Most High says:

“Those who bear the Throne and those around it glorify their Lord with praise, believe in Him, and seek forgiveness for those who believe: ‘Our Lord, You encompass all things in mercy and knowledge, so forgive those who repent and follow Your way, and protect them from the punishment of Hell.’”

“‘Our Lord, admit them to the Gardens of everlasting residence which You have promised them, along with the righteous among their parents, spouses, and descendants. Indeed, You are the Almighty, the All-Wise.’”

“‘And protect them

---

## Page 12376

from evil consequences. Whoever You protect from evil consequences that Day, You have indeed shown mercy.’”

He, glory be to Him, has thus stated that the angels pray for the believers to be forgiven, protected from punishment, and admitted to Paradise. Yet the angels’ supplication is not an act performed by the servant himself.

Allah Most High says:

“Seek forgiveness for your sin, and for the believing men and believing women.”

Abraham, Allah’s intimate friend (peace be upon him), said:

“My Lord, forgive me, my parents, and the believers on the Day the Reckoning takes place.”

Noah (peace be upon him) said:

“My Lord, forgive me, my parents, whoever enters my house as a believer, and the believing men and believing women.”

Thus, Allah has mentioned the messengers’ seeking forgiveness for the believers, both by commanding them to do so and by recounting their doing so.

Among the practices of the Sunnah established through mass transmission, the denial of which constitutes disbelief, are the Muslims’ funeral prayer for the deceased and their supplication for him during that prayer. The same applies to the intercession of the Prophet (peace and blessings of Allah be upon him) on the Day of Resurrection: the reports concerning it are mass-transmitted. Indeed, none but the people of religious innovation have denied his intercession for those who commit major sins. On the contrary, it is established that he intercedes for those who commit major sins, and his intercession consists of his supplicating and petitioning Allah, Blessed and Most High.

This and similar evidence is found in the Quran and the mass-transmitted Sunnah. Whoever denies such matters is a disbeliever once the proof has been established against him.

There are many authentic hadiths on this subject, such as the report in the authentic hadith collections from Ibn ‘Abbās (may Allah be pleased with him and his father): “A man said to the Prophet (peace and blessings of Allah be upon him), ‘My mother has died. Will it benefit her if I give charity on her behalf?’

He replied, ‘Yes.’

---

## Page 12377

He said, ‘I have a grove—that is, an orchard—and I call you to witness that I have given it in charity on her behalf.’”

In the two Sahīh collections, ‘Ā’ishah (may Allah be pleased with her) reported that a man said to the Prophet (peace and blessings of Allah be upon him), “My mother died suddenly without making a bequest. I think that, had she been able to speak, she would have given charity. Will she receive a reward if I give charity on her behalf?” He replied, “Yes.”

In Sahīh Muslim, Abū Hurayrah (may Allah be pleased with him) reported that a man said to the Prophet (peace and blessings of Allah be upon him), “My father died without making a bequest. Will it benefit him if I give charity on his behalf?” He replied, “Yes.”

‘Abdullāh ibn ‘Amr ibn al-‘Ās reported that al-‘Ās ibn Wā’il had vowed during the pre-Islamic period of ignorance to slaughter one hundred sacrificial animals. Hishām ibn al-‘Ās slaughtered his share of fifty, and ‘Amr asked the Prophet (peace and blessings of Allah be upon him) about this. He replied, “As for your father, had he affirmed Allah’s oneness, and you had then fasted or given charity on his behalf, that would have benefited him.”

In Sunan al-Dāraqutnī, a man asked the Prophet (peace and blessings of Allah be upon him), “O Messenger of Allah, I used to honor both my parents during their lives. How can I honor them after their death?” The Prophet (peace and blessings of Allah be upon him) replied, “Among the ways to continue honoring them is to pray on their behalf along with your own prayers, fast on their behalf along with your own fasting, and give charity on their behalf along with your own charity.”

At the beginning of his book, Muslim related from Abū Ishāq al-Tālaqānī, who said:

---

## Page 12378

“I said to ‘Abdullāh ibn al-Mubārak, ‘O Abū ‘Abd al-Rahmān, what about the reported hadith, “Continuing to honor your parents after their death means praying on their behalf along with your own prayers and fasting on their behalf along with your own fasting”?’

“‘Abdullāh said, ‘O Abū Ishāq, from whom is this narrated?’ I told him, ‘This is narrated by Shihāb ibn Khirāsh.’ He said, ‘He is trustworthy.’ I said, ‘From whom?’ He replied, ‘From al-Hajjāj ibn Dīnār.’ He said, ‘He is trustworthy. From whom?’ I said, ‘From the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings of Allah be upon him).’ He said, ‘O Abū Ishāq, between al-Hajjāj and the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings of Allah be upon him) lie vast deserts in which riding beasts would perish. But there is no disagreement concerning charity.’”

The matter is just as ‘Abdullāh ibn al-Mubārak stated, for this hadith has a disconnected chain of transmission. The imams agree that charity benefits the deceased, as do other acts of worship involving wealth, such as freeing slaves. They differed only concerning bodily acts of worship, such as prayer, fasting, and Quran recitation.

Nevertheless, in the two Sahīh collections, ‘Ā’ishah (may Allah be pleased with her) reported that the Prophet (peace and blessings of Allah be upon him) said:

“If someone dies owing fasts, his next of kin should fast on his behalf.”

Also in the two Sahīh collections, Ibn ‘Abbās (may Allah be pleased with him) reported:

“A woman said, ‘O Messenger of Allah, my mother died owing fasts she had vowed to observe.’ He said, ‘Tell me, if your mother had owed a debt and you had paid it, would that have discharged it on her behalf?’ She replied, ‘Yes.’ He said, ‘Then fast on your mother’s behalf.’”

---

## Page 12379

It is also reported from him in the Sahīh:

“A woman came to the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings of Allah be upon him) and said, ‘My sister died owing two consecutive months of fasting.’ He said, ‘Tell me, if your sister had owed a debt, would you have paid it?’ She replied, ‘Yes.’ He said, ‘Then Allah’s right takes precedence.’”

In Sahīh Muslim, ‘Abdullāh ibn Buraydah ibn al-Husayb narrated from his father:

“A woman came to the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings of Allah be upon him) and said, ‘My mother died owing a month of fasting. Would my fasting on her behalf discharge her obligation?’ He said, ‘Yes.’”

These authentic hadiths explicitly establish that fasts which the deceased had vowed to observe may be performed on their behalf, and that this was likened to paying a debt. The imams differed on this matter. Those whom these authentic, explicit hadiths reached did not oppose them; only those whom they had not reached held a contrary view. The hadith of ‘Amr has already been cited, stating that when people fast on behalf of a Muslim, it benefits him. As for Hajj, the imams generally hold that performing it on behalf of the deceased discharges their obligation; there is only an isolated dissenting view.

In the two Sahīh collections, Ibn ‘Abbās (may Allah be pleased with them both) reported:

“A woman from Juhaynah came to the Prophet (peace and blessings of Allah be upon him) and said, ‘My mother vowed to perform Hajj, but died without having performed it. Should I perform Hajj on her behalf?’ He said, ‘Perform Hajj on her behalf. Tell me, if your mother had owed a debt, would you have paid it on her behalf? Settle what is owed to Allah, for Allah has a greater right

---

## Page 12380

to be repaid.’” In al-Bukhārī’s version:

“My sister vowed to perform Hajj.”

In Sahīh Muslim, Buraydah reported:

“A woman said, ‘O Messenger of Allah, my mother died without having performed Hajj. Would my performing Hajj on her behalf suffice—or discharge her obligation?’ He said, ‘Yes.’”

These authentic hadiths establish “that he commanded the performance of the obligatory Hajj and a vowed Hajj on behalf of the deceased,” just as he commanded fasting. The person instructed was sometimes a child of the deceased and sometimes a brother. The Prophet (peace and blessings of Allah be upon him) likened this to a debt owed by the deceased, and anyone may validly repay such a debt. This therefore indicates that anyone may perform these acts on behalf of the deceased; this is not restricted to their children, as the explicit mention of a brother confirms.

What is established by the Quran, the Sunnah and scholarly consensus thus constitutes detailed, clearly explained knowledge. It is therefore clear that this does not contradict His words:

“And that man will have nothing except what he strives for,”

or the statement:

“When the son of Adam dies, his deeds cease except for three.”

Rather, both are true.

As for the hadith, he said:

“His deeds cease except for three: ongoing charity, knowledge from which people benefit, or a righteous child who supplicates for him.”

The child and his supplication for his parent are specifically mentioned because one’s child is among the fruits of one’s efforts, as Allah said:

“His wealth will not avail him, nor what he earned.”

They explained that “what he earned” refers to his child. Likewise, the Prophet (peace and blessings of Allah be upon him) said: “Indeed,

---

## Page 12381

the best food a man eats comes from his own earnings, and his child is part of what he has earned.” Since his efforts brought the child into existence, the child’s deeds count among the fruits of his own efforts, unlike the deeds of a brother, paternal uncle, father, or others. He also benefits from their supplications—indeed, even from the supplications of strangers—but these are not part of his own deeds.

The Prophet (peace and blessings of Allah be upon him) said:

“His deeds cease except for three…”

He did not say that he no longer benefits from the deeds of others. When his child supplicates for him, this is part of his own deeds that have not ceased. When someone else supplicates for him, it is not part of his own deeds, but he nevertheless benefits from it.

As for the verse, people have offered various answers concerning it. Some have said that it applies only to the religious law of those before us; others, that its general scope is restricted; others, that it has been abrogated; and others, that it encompasses both direct efforts and efforts that bring something about indirectly, and that faith is among his own efforts through which he has brought this benefit about. None of this is necessary. Rather, the apparent meaning of the verse is true and does not contradict the other scriptural texts. For He said:

“Man will have nothing except what he strives for.”

This is true: a person is entitled only to his own striving, for that is what he owns and has a right to, just as the earnings he owns are those he himself has earned. Another person’s striving belongs rightfully to that other person, not to him. But this does not prevent him from benefiting from another’s striving, just as a person benefits from another’s earnings.

Whoever offers the funeral prayer receives a qīrāt of reward. The person praying is thus rewarded for his own striving, namely his prayer, while the deceased also receives mercy through the living person’s prayer for him, as he said:

“There is no

---

## Page 12382

Muslim who dies and for whom a congregation of a hundred Muslims prays—it is also narrated as forty, and as three rows—interceding for him, without their intercession being accepted—or he said, ‘without his being forgiven.’” Allah Most High thus rewards the person who strives for his own striving, while showing mercy to the deceased through the efforts of this living person: his supplication for him, his charity on his behalf, his fasting on his behalf, and his pilgrimage on his behalf.

It is established in an authentic hadith that the Prophet (peace and blessings of Allah be upon him) said:

“There is no man who supplicates for his brother but Allah appoints an angel over him. Whenever he supplicates for his brother, the angel appointed over him says, ‘Amen, and may you be granted the same.’”

This is an example of the striving through which a believer benefits his brother: Allah rewards the one and shows mercy to the other.

“Man will have nothing except what he strives for.”

Not everything from which the deceased or the living benefits, or through which he receives mercy, is part of his own striving. Indeed, the children of believers enter Paradise with their parents without any striving of their own. Thus, what depends exclusively on one’s own striving is narrower in scope than benefit in general, so that a person should not seek reward for deeds other than his own. This is like a debt that someone pays on another’s behalf: the debtor is discharged from liability, but he does not own the funds with which the debt was paid. He should himself be the one to pay it. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 12383

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

“What do the esteemed jurists and imams of religion—may Allah Most High guide them to what pleases Him—say concerning Quran recitation for the deceased: does its reward reach him or not? What about payment for this, food provided by the deceased’s family for those entitled to it, and related matters? Of recitation at the grave and charity on behalf of the deceased, which is the prescribed practice that we have been commanded to observe?

“What about a mosque situated among graves, and praying in it, when it is not known whether the mosque was built before the graves or the graves were there before it? It has three revenue allotments and four hundred asddmwn dating from Roman times; this belongs not to him but to the mosque. The sermon is delivered there every Friday, and prayers are also held there at certain times. Each year it has a gathering attended by many men and women, who bring their votive offerings with them. May the imam take anything from these for the needs of the mosque in the town? Give us your ruling—may Allah have mercy on you and reward you.”

He replied:

“All praise is due to Allah, Lord of all worlds. As for charity on behalf of the deceased, he benefits from it by the agreement of the Muslims. Authentic hadiths to this effect have been reported from the Prophet (peace and blessings of Allah be upon him).

“One example is Saʿd’s statement:

‘O Messenger of Allah, my mother died suddenly, and I believe that, had she been able to speak, she would have given charity. Will it benefit her if I give charity on her behalf?

---

## Page 12384

He replied, “Yes.”’

Likewise, the deceased benefits from Hajj performed on his behalf, the Eid sacrifice offered on his behalf, a slave freed on his behalf, and supplication and requests for forgiveness for him. There is no disagreement among the imams concerning this.

As for fasting, voluntary prayer, and Quran recitation on his behalf, the scholars hold two views:

The first is that he benefits from them. This is the position of Ahmad, Abū Hanīfah, and others, as well as some of al-Shāfiʿī’s followers and others.

The second is that their reward does not reach him. This is the well-known position in the schools of Mālik and al-Shāfiʿī.

As for hiring someone simply to recite and dedicate the reward, this is not valid. The scholars’ disagreement concerns only the permissibility of receiving payment for teaching the Quran, giving the call to prayer, leading prayer, and performing Hajj on another’s behalf, since the person paying receives the benefit of the service.

One view holds that hiring someone for these purposes is valid, as is the well-known position in the schools of Mālik and al-Shāfiʿī.

Another view holds that it is impermissible, because those performing these acts must be eligible to perform acts of devotion: such acts are valid only when performed by a Muslim, not an unbeliever. They may therefore be performed only as a means of drawing near to Allah Most High. If they are performed for material gain, they earn no reward, by agreement, for Allah accepts only deeds done seeking His Face, not those done for worldly gain.

---

## Page 12385

It has also been said that a poor person may receive payment for these acts, but a wealthy person may not. This is the third view in Ahmad’s school, just as Allah has permitted an orphan’s guardian to take from the orphan’s wealth when poor, but to abstain when wealthy. This view is stronger than the others. On this basis, if a poor person performs these acts for Allah and accepts payment only because of need and to help him obey Allah, Allah rewards him according to his intention. He has thus consumed what is lawful and done what is righteous.

If, however, a person recites the Quran only for material gain, they earn no reward for doing so. If there is no reward, nothing reaches the deceased, for it is the reward of the deed that reaches the deceased, not the deed itself. If this money is instead given in charity to someone entitled to receive it, its reward reaches the deceased. It is better still to direct it to someone who will use it to help them recite and teach the Quran, for devoting one’s efforts and wealth to helping Muslims learn, recite, and teach the Quran is among the finest deeds.

As for the deceased’s family preparing food and inviting people to eat it, this is not prescribed; rather, it is a religious innovation. Indeed, Jarīr ibn ʿAbd Allāh said:

“We used to regard gathering at the home of the deceased’s family and their preparing food for people as a form of wailing.”

What is recommended when someone dies is that food be prepared for their family, as stated by

---

## Page 12386

the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, when news of the death of Jaʿfar ibn Abī Tālib reached him:

“Prepare food for Jaʿfar’s family, for something has befallen them that keeps them occupied.”

As for ongoing Quran recitation at graves, this was not known among the early generations.

Scholars have differed over reciting the Quran at a grave. Abū Hanīfa, Mālik, and Ahmad—in most reports from him—disapproved of it. In a later report, however, Ahmad permitted it after learning that ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿUmar had instructed that the opening and closing verses of al-Baqara be recited at his burial. It has also been reported that one of the Ansār instructed that al-Baqara be recited at his grave. This, however, was only at the time of burial; nothing of this kind has been transmitted from them concerning recitation afterward. Thus, a third view distinguishes between recitation at the time of burial and regular recitation afterward, for the latter is a religious innovation with no known basis.

Anyone who says that the deceased benefits from hearing the Quran and is rewarded for it is mistaken, for the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“When a son of Adam dies, his deeds come to an end except for three: ongoing charity, knowledge from which others benefit, or a righteous child who supplicates for him.”

After death, therefore, the deceased receives no reward for listening or for anything else. Even if the deceased hears the sound of their sandals, hears the greeting of someone who greets him, and hears other things, he no longer has any deeds apart from those excepted.

---

## Page 12387

As for building mosques over graves—structures known as “shrines”—this is impermissible. Indeed, the entire Muslim community forbids it, because it is established in the two Sahīh collections that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“May Allah curse the Jews and the Christians: they took the graves of their prophets as places of worship.”

He was warning against what they had done. ‘Ā’isha said: “Were it not for that, his grave would have been left exposed, but this was avoided lest it be taken as a place of worship.”

It is also reported in the Sahīh that he said:

“Those who came before you used to take graves as places of worship. Beware! Do not take graves as places of worship, for I forbid you to do so.”

It is reported in the Sunan collections that he said:

“May Allah curse women who visit graves and those who build mosques over them and place lamps upon them.”

The leading Muslim scholars agree that prayer in shrines is neither obligatory nor recommended. They also agree that prayer in shrines built over graves and similar sites has no special merit over prayer elsewhere, let alone over prayer in mosques. Whoever believes that prayer at these sites is superior to prayer elsewhere, or that it is better than prayer in some mosques, has broken with the Muslim community and departed from the religion.

Rather, the position of the Muslim community is that prayer in these shrines is strictly forbidden. They do, however, disagree about prayer in a cemetery: is it forbidden, disliked, or permissible? Or should a distinction be drawn between a cemetery whose graves have been dug up and an old cemetery? This disagreement arises from attributing the prohibition to impurity, because the soil becomes mixed with pus from the dead.

---

## Page 12388

In this case, however, he forbade it because it involves imitating the polytheists and is the origin of idol worship. Allah Most High says:

“And they said, ‘Never abandon your gods; never abandon Wadd, Suwā‘, Yaghūth, Ya‘ūq, or Nasr.’”

Several Companions and Successors said: These were the names of righteous men among Noah’s people. When they died, people lingered in devotion at their graves, then made statues of them. For this reason, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said, as Mālik reported in al-Muwatta’:

“O Allah, do not let my grave become an idol that is worshipped. Allah’s wrath was severe against people who took the graves of their prophets as places of worship.”

For this reason, the Muslims agree that it is not prescribed to vow offerings of oil, candles, dirhams, or anything else to shrines built over graves, nor to those residing beside them or tending the graves. The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, cursed those who build mosques over graves and place lamps upon them. Whoever makes such a vow has vowed to commit a sin. It is reported in the Sahīh that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Whoever vows to obey Allah must obey Him, and whoever vows to disobey Allah must not disobey Him.”

As for expiation, there are two views. According to Ahmad and others, he must offer the expiation required for an oath, because the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“The expiation for a vow is the expiation for an oath.”

Reported by Muslim. It is also reported in the Sunan collections that he, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Whoever vows to obey Allah must obey Him, and whoever vows to disobey Allah must not disobey Him.”

According to Mālik, al-Shāfi‘ī, and others, nothing is required of him. However, if he gives in charity what he vowed

---

## Page 12389

for the shrines to deserving poor Muslims who use it to help them obey Allah and His Messenger, he has done well, and his reward rests with Allah.

By the agreement of the Muslims, no one may transfer the Muslims’ prayers and sermons from a mosque where they gather to a shrine built over a grave or the like. Rather, this is among the gravest forms of misguidance and wrongdoing: they have abandoned what Allah and His Messenger commanded and done what Allah and His Messenger forbade. They have abandoned the Sunnah and practised innovation; they have abandoned obedience to Allah and His Messenger and committed disobedience to Allah and His Messenger. Rather, Friday and congregational prayers must be restored to the mosque, which is one of the houses of Allah:

“Allah has commanded that they be raised and His name remembered therein. In them, morning and evening, He is glorified by men whom neither trade nor sale distracts from the remembrance of Allah, the establishment of prayer, and the payment of zakat.”

Allah Most High also says:

“The mosques of Allah are maintained only by those who believe in Allah and the Last Day, establish prayer, give zakat, and fear none but Allah. It is they who may be among the rightly guided.”

As for graves, whether in shrines or elsewhere, the Sunnah for those visiting them is to greet the deceased and supplicate for them, as in the funeral prayer. The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, used to teach his Companions to say when visiting graves:

“Peace be upon you, inhabitants of these abodes, believers and Muslims. We shall, if Allah wills, soon join you. May Allah have mercy on those among us and you who have gone before and those who come after.

---

## Page 12390

We ask Allah for well-being for ourselves and for you. O Allah, do not deprive us of the reward for them, do not put us to trial after them, and forgive us and them.”

As for rubbing oneself against a grave, praying beside it, seeking it out to supplicate there in the belief that supplication there is better than elsewhere, making vows to it, and similar practices, none of this belongs to the religion of the Muslims. Rather, these are reprehensible innovations that have been introduced and are branches of associating partners with Allah. Allah knows best and is Most Wise.

He was asked:

When someone recites the Noble Quran, or part of it, is it better to dedicate its reward to his parents and the deceased Muslims, or to keep its reward exclusively for himself?

He answered:

The best acts of worship are those that accord with the guidance of the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and the guidance of the Companions. It is authentically reported that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, used to say in his sermons:

“The best speech is the speech of Allah, and the best guidance is the guidance of Muhammad. The worst matters are those newly introduced, and every innovation is misguidance.”

He also said, may Allah bless him and grant him peace:

“The best generation is my generation, then those who follow them.”

Ibn Masʿūd said:

“Whoever among you wishes to follow an example should follow someone who has

---

## Page 12391

died, for one who is still alive cannot be considered safe from trial. Such were the Companions of Muhammad.”

With this principle established, the practice known among Muslims in the favored generations was to worship Allah through the various prescribed acts of worship, both obligatory and voluntary: prayer, fasting, recitation, remembrance of Allah, and other acts. They would supplicate for believing men and women, as Allah commanded, both the living and the dead among them, in their funeral prayers, when visiting graves, and on other occasions.

It is reported from a number of the early Muslims that a supplication offered upon each completion of the Quran is answered. Thus, when someone supplicates after completing the Quran for himself, his parents, his teachers, and other believing men and women, this falls within what is prescribed. The same applies to supplicating for them during the night prayer and on other occasions when supplications are answered.

It is authentically reported that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, instructed that charity be given on behalf of the deceased and that fasting be performed on their behalf. Giving charity on behalf of the dead is therefore a righteous deed, as is fasting on their behalf in the ways established by the Sunnah.

These and other proofs were cited by those scholars who held that it is permissible to dedicate the reward of financial and bodily acts of worship to deceased Muslims. This is the position of Ahmad, Abū Hanīfah, and a group of the followers of Mālik and al-Shāfiʿī. Thus, if someone dedicates the reward of fasting, prayer, or recitation to a deceased person, that is permissible.

---

## Page 12392

And most of the followers of Mālik and al-Shāfiʿī say that this is prescribed only for financial acts of worship. Nevertheless, it was not the practice of the early Muslims, when they performed voluntary prayers, fasted, performed Hajj, or recited the Quran, to dedicate the reward to their deceased fellow Muslims or to particular individuals among them. Rather, their practice was as described above. People should therefore not depart from the way of the early Muslims, for it is better and more complete. And Allah knows best.

He was asked:

Is the following an authentic hadith or not: “If someone declares ‘There is no god but Allah’ seventy thousand times and dedicates it to the deceased, it will deliver the deceased from the Fire”? If a person repeats this declaration and dedicates it to the deceased, does its reward reach him or not?

He answered:

If a person repeats this declaration in this manner—seventy thousand times, or fewer or more—and its reward is dedicated to the deceased, Allah benefits him thereby. This is neither an authentic nor a weak hadith. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 12393

He was asked:

Does the reward of Quran recitation by the deceased’s family reach him? If someone dedicates to the deceased the reward of glorifying Allah, praising Him, declaring that there is no god but Allah, and proclaiming His greatness, does that reward reach him or not?

He answered:

The reward of his family’s Quran recitation, their glorification of Allah, their proclamation of His greatness, and all their other remembrance of Allah Most High reaches the deceased if they dedicate it to him. And Allah knows best.

He was asked:

According to the school of al-Shāfi'ī, does the reward of a child’s Quran recitation reach the deceased parent or not?

He answered:

As for whether the reward of bodily acts of worship—such as Quran recitation, prayer, and fasting—reaches the deceased, the position of Ahmad, Abū Hanīfa, and a group of the followers of Mālik and al-Shāfi'ī is that it does. Most of the followers of Mālik and al-Shāfi'ī, however, hold that it does not. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 12394

He was also asked—may Allah have mercy on him:

If someone left his parents while they were unbelievers and does not know whether they have embraced Islam, is it permissible for him to supplicate for them?

He answered:

Praise be to Allah. Anyone belonging to a people who were originally unbelievers may not ask forgiveness for his parents unless they have embraced Islam. As Allah Most High says:

“It is not for the Prophet and those who believe to ask forgiveness for the polytheists, even if they are close relatives, after it has become clear to them that they are companions of Hellfire.”

---

## Page 12395

Chapter on Visiting Graves

He was asked—may Allah have mercy on him:

What is prescribed concerning visiting graves?

He answered:

Visiting graves takes two forms: prescribed and innovated.

The prescribed form is like the funeral prayer: its purpose is to supplicate for the deceased, just as that is the purpose of offering the funeral prayer over him. The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, would visit those buried in al-Baqīʿ and the martyrs of Uhud. He taught his Companions to say when visiting graves:

“Peace be upon you, inhabitants of this abode of believers. Allah willing, we shall join you. May Allah have mercy on those among us and you who have gone before and those who come after. We ask Allah for well-being for ourselves and for you. O Allah, do not deprive us of the reward on their account, do not subject us to trials after them, and forgive us and them.”

The same applies to every supplication for believers, whether prophets or others, such as invoking blessings and peace upon the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. As related in an authentic report, he said:

---

## Page 12396

“When you hear the caller to prayer, say what he says, then invoke blessings upon me. Whoever invokes blessings upon me once, Allah will bless him ten times in return. Then ask Allah to grant me al-wasīlah, for it is a rank in Paradise reserved for a single servant of Allah, and I hope to be that servant. Whoever asks Allah to grant me al-wasīlah will be entitled to my intercession on the Day of Resurrection.
