No Muslim sends greetings of peace upon me but Allah restores my soul to me so that I may return his greeting.”

As for the innovated form of visiting, it is the visiting practised by those who associate partners with Allah, like the visits made by Christians who intend to call upon the deceased, seek his assistance, and seek the fulfilment of their needs at his grave. They pray at his grave and supplicate through him. Neither this nor anything like it was practised by any of the Companions, commanded by the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, or recommended by any of the early generations of the Muslim community or its leading scholars. Rather, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, closed “the door to associating partners with Allah.”

An authentic report relates that he said during his final illness:

“May Allah curse the Jews and Christians; they took the graves of their prophets as places of prayer.”

He was warning against what they had done. ‘Ā’ishah, may Allah be pleased with her, said: “Were it not for that, his grave would have been left in the open. But it was considered objectionable that it should be taken as a place of prayer.”

Five days before his death, he said:

“Those before you used to take graves as places of prayer. Be warned: do not take graves as places of prayer, for I forbid you to do so.”

Thus, the first form of visiting is a form of worshipping Allah and doing good to His creation.

---

## Page 12397

and this is akin to the obligatory charity that Allah has commanded.

The second, by contrast, is a form of associating partners with Allah and violating the rights of Allah and His servants. An authentic report relates that when Allah Most High revealed:

“Those who believe and do not mix their faith with wrongdoing,”

this troubled the Prophet’s Companions, who said, “Which of us has not wronged himself?” The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, replied:

“What is meant here is associating partners with Allah. Have you not heard the words of the righteous servant: ‘Indeed, associating partners with Allah is a tremendous wrong’?”

He also said, may Allah bless him and grant him peace:

“O Allah, do not let my grave become an idol that is worshipped.”

Allah Most High has said:

“And they said, ‘Never abandon your gods; never abandon Wadd, Suwā‘, Yaghūth, Ya‘ūq, or Nasr.’”

A group of the early generations said: These were righteous men among the people of Noah. When they died, the people kept vigil at their graves and made likenesses of them. This marked the beginning of idol worship, and it is of the same nature as the religion of the Christians.

The Companions—may Allah be pleased with them—and their Successors did not specifically seek to supplicate at the grave of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, or at anyone else’s grave. Rather, the leading scholars disapproved of standing at the Prophet’s grave to supplicate. They said, “This is an innovation that neither the Companions nor their Successors practised.” Instead, they would greet him and his two Companions with peace and then leave.

---

## Page 12398

And when Abd Allāh ibn Umar entered the mosque, he would say:

“Peace be upon you, O Messenger of Allah. Peace be upon you, O Abū Bakr. Peace be upon you, my father.”

Then he would leave. Mālik and other leading scholars explicitly stated this. Abū Yūsuf and other scholars explicitly stated that no one may ask Allah by virtue of any created being—whether the Prophet, the angels, or anyone else.

The Muslims experienced drought and hardship. They would supplicate to Allah, ask Him for rain, pray against their enemies, seek His help in attaining victory, and seek to draw near to Him through the supplications of the righteous. As the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Are you granted victory and provision except through the weak among you—through their supplications, prayers, and sincerity?”

They did not deliberately seek to supplicate at the grave of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, or at the grave of any righteous person, nor to offer prayers there, ask him to fulfil their needs, or adjure Allah by him—for example, by saying, “I ask You by the right of so-and-so.” Rather, all these are newly introduced innovations. The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“The best generation is the generation among whom I was sent, then those who follow them.”

The Muslims have agreed that the Companions of the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, are the best generation of this nation.

---

## Page 12399

The shaykh was asked about visiting graves.

He replied:

Returning to the grave after burial is not recommended. Rather, what is recommended at burial is to stand by the deceased’s grave and supplicate that he be granted steadfastness. Abū Dāwūd narrated in his Sunan that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace,

“would stand by the grave after burying one of his Companions and say: ‘Ask that he be granted steadfastness, for he is now being questioned.’”

This is included in the meaning of Allah’s words:

“And never pray over any of them who dies, nor stand at his grave.”

When Allah forbade His Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, to offer the funeral prayer over the hypocrites or stand by their graves, the implication by contrast was that the funeral prayer should be offered over a believer before burial and that one should stand by his grave after burial. Thus, the prescribed practice of visiting the deceased to supplicate for them and seek forgiveness for them is a form of this prescribed standing by the grave.

---

## Page 12400

He was asked:

When the living visit the dead, are the dead aware of their visit? And do they know when someone dies, whether a relative of theirs or someone else?

He replied:

Praise be to Allah. Yes, reports have come down concerning their meeting one another, questioning one another, and the deeds of the living being presented to the dead. Thus, Ibn al-Mubārak narrated that Abū Ayyūb al-Ansārī said:

“When the believer’s soul is taken, the recipients of mercy among Allah’s servants welcome it as people welcome a bearer of glad tidings in this world. They turn to him and question him, but some say to others: ‘Give your brother time to rest, for he has been in severe distress.’”

He continued: “They then turn to him and ask: ‘What happened to so-and-so? And what happened to such-and-such a woman? Has she married?’” The report continues.

As for the deceased’s awareness of a living person who visits him and greets him with peace, Ibn ʿAbbās narrated that the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“No one passes by the grave of a fellow believer whom he knew in this world and greets him with peace without the deceased recognizing him and returning his greeting.”

Ibn al-Mubārak said: “This is established from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace.” It was also graded authentic by ʿAbd al-Haqq, the author of al-Ahkām.

---

## Page 12401

As for what Allah has revealed concerning the martyr’s life and provision, and what is related in authentic hadith about their souls entering Paradise, some groups have held that this applies exclusively to martyrs, to the exclusion of the eminently truthful and others.

The correct view, held by the leading scholars and the vast majority of Ahl al-Sunnah, is that life, provision, and the souls’ entry into Paradise are not exclusive to martyrs, as the established texts indicate. The martyr is singled out for mention because someone might suppose that he will die and consequently shrink from jihad. Allah therefore revealed this to remove what might deter people from undertaking jihad and facing martyrdom. Similarly, He forbade killing children for fear of poverty because that is what occurs in practice, although killing them is also forbidden when there is no fear of poverty.

---

## Page 12402

Shaykh al-Islām, mufti to the people, the scholar who practised what he taught, the ascetic and scrupulously pious champion of the Sunnah and suppressor of innovation, Taqī al-Dīn Abū al-ʿAbbās Ahmad ibn ʿAbd al-Halīm ibn ʿAbd al-Salām ibn Taymiyya al-Harrānī—may Allah Most High have mercy on him—was asked:

Regarding the hadith reported from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, in which he, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Allah has cursed women who visit graves and those who build mosques over them and place lamps upon them.”

Is it abrogated by his saying, may Allah bless him and grant him peace:

“I previously forbade you to visit graves. Now visit them, for they remind you of the Hereafter”?

Or is it not? Is the first hadith authentic? Is visiting graves forbidden, disliked, or recommended for women? If it is considered disliked, does this mean that it is prohibited, or merely discouraged?

Is it authentically established that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said, “Whoever visits my grave will be entitled to my intercession”? Are any hadiths concerning the merit of visiting the Prophet’s grave, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, authentic?

He replied:

All praise is due to Allah, Lord of all worlds. As for visiting graves, it is established in an authentic hadith that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, initially issued a general prohibition against it, then granted permission

---

## Page 12403

to do so. He said:

“I previously forbade you to visit graves. Now visit them, for they remind you of the Hereafter.”

He also said, may Allah bless him and grant him peace:

“I asked my Lord for permission to visit my mother’s grave, and He granted me permission. I asked Him for permission to seek forgiveness for her, but He did not grant me permission. So visit graves, for they remind you of the Hereafter.”

There are two issues here: one is agreed upon, while the other is disputed.

As for the first, visits to graves are of two kinds: those prescribed by Islam and those based on religious innovation.

The prescribed visit consists of greeting the deceased with peace and supplicating for him, much like offering the funeral prayer for him. It is established in an authentic hadith that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, used to teach his Companions to say when visiting graves:

“Peace be upon you, inhabitants of these abodes, Muslims and believers. Allah willing, we shall join you. May Allah have mercy on those among us and you who have gone before and those who are yet to follow. We ask Allah for well-being for ourselves and for you. O Allah, do not deprive us of our reward on their account, do not subject us to trials after them, and forgive us and them.”

Parts of this supplication are reported in different hadiths, and it has been transmitted in several wordings, just as the tashahhud (the testimony recited while seated in prayer) and other supplications have been transmitted in different wordings. This is the kind of visit the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, used to make when he went out to visit the graves of the people of al-Baqīʿ.

As for visits based on religious innovation, they are of the same kind as the visits made by Jews, Christians, and adherents of

---

## Page 12404

religious innovation, who take the graves of prophets and righteous people as places of worship. It is widely reported from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, in the authentic hadith collections and elsewhere, that he said as he approached death:

“May Allah curse the Jews and Christians; they took the graves of their prophets as places of worship.”

He was warning against what they had done. ʿĀʾishah, may Allah be pleased with her, said: “Were it not for that, his grave would have been left exposed, but it was considered objectionable that it should be made a place of worship.”

It is also authentically established that he, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Those before you used to take graves as places of worship. Do not take graves as places of worship, for I forbid you to do so.”

Visits based on religious innovation include deliberately going to the grave of a prophet or righteous person to pray there, to supplicate there or through him, to ask him to fulfil one’s needs or to ask Allah Most High to fulfil them at his grave, to seek his aid, to adjure Allah Most High by him, and similar practices. These are religious innovations that none of the Companions or those who followed them in righteousness practised. Neither the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, nor any of his rightly guided caliphs prescribed them. Rather, the leading scholars of the Muslims prohibited them.

The hadith that some people transmit, “When you ask Allah, ask Him by virtue of my standing,” is a fabrication that none of the Muslim scholars transmitted, nor is it found in any of the books of hadith. It is like the saying they attribute to him: “If any of you placed his confidence in a stone, Allah would cause him to benefit through it.” This too is a fabrication.

---

## Page 12405

Several scholars have explicitly stated that Allah must not be adjured by any created being, whether a prophet or anyone else. Among these statements is what Abū al-Husayn al-Qudūrī recorded in his Commentary on al-Karkhī, from Bishr ibn al-Walīd, who said: I heard Abū Yūsuf say that Abū Hanīfah said:

“No one should supplicate Allah except through Him. I disapprove of saying, ‘By the seats of might in Your Throne,’ and ‘By the right of Your creation.’”

This was also Abū Yūsuf’s view. Abū Yūsuf said:

“‘By the seats of might in His Throne’ refers to Allah Most High, so I do not disapprove of this. But I disapprove of saying, ‘By the right of so-and-so,’ ‘By the right of Your prophets and messengers,’ and ‘By the right of the House and the Sacred Monument.’”

Al-Qudūrī, the commentator on the book, said:

“Asking by His creation is impermissible, because created beings have no right over the Creator; therefore, it is impermissible”—meaning that this is agreed upon.

I say:

As for seeking his intercession with Allah Most High—that is, asking him to intercede—and drawing near to Allah through his supplication and intercession, through faith in him, love for him and obedience to him, and turning to Allah Most High by these means, this is prescribed by the agreement of the Muslims, as authentic hadiths attest.

It is authentically reported in Sahīh al-Bukhārī, from Abū Humayd al-Sā‘idī, may Allah be pleased with him, that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Let me not find any of you coming on the Day of Resurrection and saying, ‘O Messenger of Allah, come to my aid!’ whereupon I will say, ‘I have no power

---

## Page 12406

to help you against Allah. I have conveyed the message to you.’”

It is also authentically reported that he, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“O Fātimah, daughter of Muhammad, I cannot avail you at all against Allah. O ‘Abbās, uncle of the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, I cannot avail you at all against Allah. O Safiyyah, aunt of the Messenger of Allah, I cannot avail you at all against Allah. Ask me for whatever you wish of my wealth.”

He said this to his closest relatives. It is also related that he said:

“Yet you have ties of kinship with me, which I shall duly maintain.”

Thus he, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, explained what accords with the Book of Allah: his duty is only to convey the message clearly. Recompense through reward and punishment, however, rests with Allah Most High. As He says:

“Say, ‘Obey Allah and obey the Messenger.’ But if you turn away, he is responsible only for the duty laid upon him, and you are responsible for the duty laid upon you. If you obey him, you will be guided. The Messenger’s duty is only to convey the message clearly.”

He, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, did indeed convey the message clearly. He delivered the message and called Allah to witness concerning his community that he had conveyed it to them. Thus, during the Farewell Pilgrimage, he repeatedly said:

“Have I conveyed the message?”

They replied, “Yes.” He then raised his finger towards heaven and pointed it towards them, saying, “O Allah, bear witness.”

Muslim related this in his Sahīh.

As for answering supplications, relieving distress and fulfilling needs, these belong to Allah alone, glorified and exalted is He; no one shares in them with Him. For this reason, Allah, glorified is He, distinguished in His Book between those matters in which the Messenger has a right

---

## Page 12407

and those that belong to Allah alone, as in His words, Most High:

“Whoever obeys Allah and His Messenger, fears Allah and is mindful of Him—it is they who will triumph.”

He, glorified is He, thus explained that the Messenger is entitled to obedience, for whoever obeys the Messenger has obeyed Allah. As for reverent fear and mindfulness, He reserved these for Himself alone. Likewise, He says:

“If only they had been content with what Allah and His Messenger gave them, and had said, ‘Allah is sufficient for us. Allah will give us of His bounty, and so will His Messenger. Indeed, to Allah alone we turn in hope.’”

He thus attributed giving to both Allah and the Messenger, as in His words, Most High:

“Whatever the Messenger gives you, take it; and whatever he forbids you, refrain from it.”

As for reliance and turning in hope, these are for Allah alone, as in His words, Most High:

“And they said, ‘Allah is sufficient for us.’”

He did not say, “and His Messenger.” He also said:

“Indeed, to Allah alone we turn in hope.”

He did not say, “and to the Messenger.” This accords with His words, Most High:

“So when you have finished, strive, and to your Lord turn in hope.”

Worship, awe, reliance, supplication, hope and fear are therefore for Allah alone; no one shares in them with Him. As for obedience, love and seeking to please, we must obey Allah and His Messenger, love Allah and His Messenger, and seek to please Allah and His Messenger. For obedience to the Messenger is obedience to Allah, pleasing him is pleasing Allah, and loving him is part of loving Allah.

Many misguided people among the disbelievers and the people of religious innovation have altered the religion. Allah Most High appointed the messengers—may Allah’s blessings and peace be upon them—as intermediaries in conveying His commands, prohibitions, promises and warnings. Thus, no one has a path to Allah except by following

---

## Page 12408

the Messenger, doing what he commanded and refraining from what he warned against.

Whoever posits a path to Allah other than following the Messenger, whether for the elite or the common people, disbelieves in Allah and His Messenger. This includes anyone who claims that among the foremost friends of Allah, scholars, philosophers, speculative theologians or kings are those who have a path to Allah Most High other than following His Messenger. In support of this, they cite fabricated reports containing the gravest disbelief and falsehood.

For example, some claim that the Messenger, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, sought permission to enter upon the People of the Portico, but they said, “Go to those to whom you were sent as a messenger.” Others claim that, on the morning following the Night of the Ascension, they told him the secret that Allah had confided to him, and that Allah had informed them of it without the Messenger telling them.

Others claim that they fought against him alongside the disbelievers in some of his military campaigns, saying, “Whomever Allah is with, we are with.” Such claims are among the gravest forms of disbelief and falsehood.

Likewise, some invoke the account of al-Khidr and Moses, peace be upon him, as evidence that certain friends of Allah can dispense with Muhammad, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, just as al-Khidr dispensed with Moses. Others claim that the Seal of the Friends of Allah has a path to Allah by which he can dispense with the Seal of the Prophets. Such claims have become widespread among many who profess asceticism, spiritual poverty, Sufism, speculative theology and philosophy. The disbelief of these people may be of the same kind as that of the Jews and Christians, or it may be

---

## Page 12409

more grievous, or it may be less severe, depending on their circumstances. Allah, glorified be He, has not appointed any prophet or believer as an intermediary for Him in any aspect of His lordship or His exclusive right to worship, such as those acts that belong to Him alone: creating, providing sustenance, answering supplications, granting victory over enemies, fulfilling needs, and relieving distress. Rather, a servant can at most serve as a means, as when he supplicates or intercedes. Allah Most High says:

“Who is there who can intercede with Him except by His permission?”

He also says:

“They intercede only for those with whom He is pleased.”

And He says:

“How many angels there are in the heavens whose intercession avails nothing, except after Allah has given permission for whom He wills and approves.”

Allah Most High also says:

“Say, ‘Call upon those you claim to be gods besides Him. They have no power to remove harm from you or shift it elsewhere.’”

“Those whom they invoke themselves seek a means of drawing near to their Lord, striving to be nearest to Him. They hope for His mercy and fear His punishment. Indeed, your Lord’s punishment is something to beware of.”

A number of the early generations said: Some people used to invoke the angels and prophets, so Allah forbade them to do so in His words:

“It is not for a human being whom Allah has given the Scripture, judgment, and prophethood to then say to people, ‘Be my worshippers rather than Allah’s.’ Rather, he would say, ‘Be devoted to your Lord through your teaching of the Scripture and your study of it.’”

“Nor would he command you to take the angels and prophets as lords. Would he command you to disbelieve after you have become Muslims?”

Thus, He, glorified be He, made clear that taking the angels and prophets as lords is disbelief. Accordingly, people fall into three categories regarding intercession:

---

## Page 12410

the polytheists affirmed a form of intercession that constitutes associating partners with Allah: the intercession of one created being with another, as when the intimates of kings intercede with them because kings need their assistance. They therefore petition kings without their permission, and the kings grant their requests because they need them. Those who affirm such intercession with Allah Most High are polytheists and disbelievers, for no one intercedes with Allah Most High except by His permission, and He has no need of any of His creation. Rather, His answering the supplications of intercessors is an expression of His mercy and kindness. He, glorified be He, is more merciful to His servants than a mother is to her child.

For this reason, Allah Most High says:

“You have no protector or intercessor besides Him.”

He also says:

“Warn with it those who fear being gathered before their Lord: they will have no protector or intercessor besides Him.”

Allah Most High also says:

“Or have they taken intercessors besides Allah? Say, ‘Even though they possess nothing and have no understanding?’”

“Say, ‘All intercession belongs to Allah.’”

Allah Most High also relates the words of the man mentioned in Surah Yā Sīn:

“Should I take gods besides Him? If the Most Merciful intends harm for me, their intercession will be of no benefit to me, nor can they save me.”

“Indeed, I would then be clearly astray.”

“Indeed, I have believed in your Lord, so listen to me.”

As for the Kharijites and Mu'tazilites, they denied our Prophet’s intercession—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—for those of his community who committed major sins. They are misguided innovators who oppose the widely transmitted Sunnah of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and the consensus of the best generations.

The third category consists of Ahl al-Sunnah wa-l-Jamā'ah—the people of the Sunnah and the community. They are the early generations of this community, its leading scholars, and those who followed them in righteousness. They affirmed what Allah affirmed in His Book and in the Sunnah of His Messenger

---

## Page 12411

, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and rejected what Allah rejected in His Book and in the Sunnah of His Messenger. The intercession they affirm is that described in the hadiths, such as the intercession of our Prophet Muhammad, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, on the Day of Resurrection. The people will go to Adam, then Noah, then Abraham, then Moses, then Jesus, and finally come to him, peace be upon him. He said:

“I will go to my Lord, and when I see Him, I will fall down in prostration before Him. I will praise my Lord with words of praise that He will inspire me with, which I cannot now express. He will say, ‘O Muhammad, raise your head. Speak, and you will be heard; ask, and you will be given; intercede, and your intercession will be accepted.’”

Thus, he comes to his Lord, glorified be He, and begins by prostrating before Him and praising Him. Then, when He permits him to intercede, he intercedes. May my father and mother be ransomed for him; may Allah bless him and grant him peace.

As for the intercession denied by the Quran—the kind believed in by the polytheists, the Christians, and those of this community who resemble them—the people of knowledge and faith reject it. This includes asking absent or deceased prophets and righteous people to fulfill their needs, claiming that these figures will fulfill those needs if they so wish. They also claim that their standing with Allah Most High resembles that of kings’ intimates with their kings: they intercede without the kings’ permission and presume upon their privileged standing with them to have their needs met. They thus assign them a position in relation to Allah Most High like that of a king’s partners or children.

Yet Allah Most High has declared His holy Self far above such things, as He says:

“And say, ‘Praise be to Allah, who has never taken a son, who has no partner in His dominion, and who needs no protector out of weakness.’ And proclaim His greatness.”

For this reason, the Prophet said:

---

## Page 12412

may Allah bless him and grant him peace:

“Do not praise me excessively as the Christians praised the son of Mary. I am only a servant, so say: ‘Allah’s servant and His Messenger.’”

This issue is discussed at length elsewhere.

“Innovated grave visitation” is among the causes of associating partners with Allah Most High, invoking His creatures, and introducing a religion that Allah has not authorized. “Religiously sanctioned grave visitation,” by contrast, is a form of kindness to the deceased through supplication for them, just as offering the funeral prayer for them is a kindness. It is an act of worship devoted to Allah Most High through which He benefits both the supplicant and the person for whom supplication is made. Other examples include invoking blessings and peace upon the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, asking Allah to grant him al-wasīlah, and supplicating for all believers, living and dead.

The disputed issue is this: does the permission to visit graves extend to women, thereby abrogating the prohibition applicable to them? Or were they not given permission, so that they remain forbidden to visit? And does the prohibition mean that it is unlawful or merely disliked?

Scholars hold three well-known positions on this matter. All three are found within the schools of al-Shāfi‘ī and Ahmad, as well as others. Three differing reports on this matter have been transmitted from Ahmad. This parallels their disagreement over women accompanying funeral processions, although some permit grave visitation but not accompanying funeral processions. This was the position chosen by a group of Ahmad’s followers and others.

Some scholars held that women were permitted to visit graves and that he had given permission

---

## Page 12413

to them just as he had to men, and understood his words, may Allah bless him and grant him peace,

“So visit them, for they remind you of the Hereafter,”

as an address applying generally to both men and women.

The correct view, however, is that women were not included in the permission to visit graves, for several reasons.

First: his words, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, “So visit them,” employ a masculine form. By its linguistic designation, the masculine form applies only to men, though it may also include women by using the masculine form for both sexes. There are, however, two views on this. According to one, such inclusion requires separate evidence; on this view, including women in this address requires separate evidence. According to the other, an unqualified masculine form is understood to include both sexes. On this view, women’s inclusion would rest on a weak basis of general applicability. A general statement cannot override the widely transmitted specific evidence prohibiting women from visiting graves, as we shall explain, if Allah Most High wills. Indeed, according to the majority of scholars, it cannot even abrogate that evidence, even if the specific text is known to have preceded the general one.

The second reason is this: if women were included in the address, visiting graves would be recommended for them, just as it is recommended for men according to the majority. This is because the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, gave a reason that entails its being recommended, namely his words:

“For they remind you of the Hereafter.”

For this same reason, it is permissible to visit the graves of polytheists. As recorded in the authentic collection, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, visited his mother’s grave and said:

“I asked my Lord for permission to seek forgiveness for my mother, but He did not grant me permission. I asked Him for permission to visit her grave, and He granted me permission. So visit graves, for they remind you of the Hereafter.”

---

## Page 12414

As for his visits to those buried in al-Baqī‘, these also involved seeking forgiveness and supplicating for them, just as the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, taught his community to greet the believers buried in the graves and supplicate for them when visiting them. If women had been permitted to visit graves, it would have been recommended for them just as it is recommended for men, because such visits involve supplicating for the believers and remembering death. Yet we know of no imam who recommended that women visit graves, nor did women in the time of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and his rightly guided caliphs go out to visit graves as men did.

Those who allowed women to visit graves relied on the report that ‘Ā’ishah—may Allah be pleased with her—visited the grave of her brother ‘Abd al-Rahmān, who had died in her absence. She said:

“Had I been present when you died, I would not have visited you.”

This indicates that visiting graves is not recommended for women as it is for men. Had it been so, visiting his grave would have been recommended for her, just as it is recommended for men, whether or not she had been present at his death.

Furthermore, offering the funeral prayer is more strongly enjoined than visiting graves. Nevertheless, it is established in the authentic collection that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, forbade women from following funeral processions, even though this means that they miss the prayer over the deceased. If the prayer and reward involved do not make following funeral processions recommended for women, then what of visiting graves?

---

## Page 12415

The third argument is as follows:

The most that can be said about his words, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, “Visit graves,” is that they constitute a general address. Yet his words, may Allah bless him and grant him peace,

“Whoever offers the funeral prayer will receive one qīrāt of reward, and whoever follows the funeral procession until the deceased is buried will receive two qīrāts,”

are clearly more general in scope than the masculine form of address. The word “whoever” includes both men and women by universal agreement, notwithstanding the dissent of those who do not know what they are saying. Indeed, “whoever” is among the strongest expressions of generality. Nevertheless, authentic hadiths establish that this general wording does not include women, because the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, forbade them from following funeral processions, whether this prohibition establishes unlawfulness or merely discourages the practice. If they are not included in this general wording, then they are all the more clearly excluded from the other. Both practices belong to the same category, for accompanying a funeral procession is akin to visiting graves.

Allah Most High says:

“And never pray over any of them who dies, nor stand at his grave.”

He thus forbade His Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, from praying over the hypocrites or standing at their graves. The contrary implication of this wording and the implications of the stated rationale require that believers be prayed over and that people stand at their graves. As most Quran commentators explain, this means standing there to supplicate for them and seek forgiveness for them, which is the purpose of visiting believers’ graves. If women are not included in the general wording concerning following funeral processions, despite the prayer over the deceased that this entails, then the argument for excluding them from visits to graves, whose purpose ranks below praying over the deceased, is

---

## Page 12416

all the stronger. This differs from cases in which women can offer the funeral prayer without following the procession, as when they pray over the deceased at home. That is comparable to supplicating for him and seeking forgiveness for him at home.

If it is said, “The harm involved in following funeral processions is greater than that involved in visiting graves, because the bereavement is recent, and the women’s voices and appearance cause distress to the deceased and temptation for the living,” the response is:

Accompanying a funeral procession as such outweighs the benefit of visiting graves, because it includes praying over the deceased, which is greater than merely supplicating for him. Moreover, its purpose—carrying and burying the deceased and offering the funeral prayer—is a communal obligation, whereas no aspect of visiting graves is a communal obligation. This obligation is shared by men and women: if a man dies with none but women present, carrying him, burying him and offering the funeral prayer over him become obligatory upon them. As for women washing deceased men, there is disagreement and the ruling requires further distinctions. Likewise, whether dry purification should be performed for a deceased person whose body cannot be washed is a well-known matter of disagreement, with two positions in the school of Ahmad and elsewhere. Thus, if women are forbidden to participate when men undertake these duties, even though such acts are communal obligations and yield greater benefit, then this applies all the more to something that is obligatory upon no one.

The claim that accompanying a funeral procession involves greater harm is not conceded. Rather, allowing a woman to visit graves is likely to lead her to do so repeatedly, thereby increasing the harm and renewing anguished grief and distress to the deceased. It would also be likely to lead men to seek the women out and become infatuated with them, as actually happens in many towns. For there arise, as a result of

---

## Page 12417

women visiting graves, forms of temptation, indecency, and corruption that do not occur at all when they accompany funeral processions. All this shows that women’s visiting graves generally entails greater harm than their accompanying funeral processions. Even if the injunction against accompanying funeral processions merely indicates disapproval, this does not preclude the injunction against visiting graves from indicating prohibition. This is because preventing a woman from accompanying a funeral procession may be impossible owing to her overwhelming grief, just as calming women may also be impossible for the same reason. If a concession is made because this motivating impulse is so strong, it does not follow that a concession must also be made where the impulse is not strong. If Allah Most High pardons His servant for something he cannot refrain from without great hardship, it does not follow that He will pardon him for something he can refrain from without having to endure that hardship.

The fourth argument:

It may be said that it has been reported from the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, through two chains of transmission that he cursed women who visit graves. Abū Hurayrah—may Allah be pleased with him—reported:

“The Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, cursed women who visit graves.”

This was narrated by Imam Ahmad, Ibn Mājah, and al-Tirmidhī, who graded it authentic. Ibn ‘Abbās—may Allah be pleased with them both—reported:

“The Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, cursed women who visit graves and those who establish mosques over them and place lamps upon them.”

This was narrated by Imam Ahmad, Abū Dāwūd, al-Nasā’ī, and al-Tirmidhī, who graded it good; some copies record his grading as authentic. Ibn Mājah also narrated the portion mentioning visits to graves.

---

## Page 12418

If it is objected:

The first hadith was narrated by ‘Umar ibn Abī Salamah, concerning whom ‘Alī ibn al-Madīnī said, “Shu‘bah abandoned him, and he is not particularly strong.” Ibn Sa‘d said, “He narrated many hadiths, but his hadiths are not relied upon as evidence.” Al-Sa‘dī and al-Nasā’ī said, “He is not strong in hadith.”

The second includes Abū Sālih Bādhām, the freedman of Umm Hāni’, whom the scholars have graded weak. Ahmad said, “Ibn Mahdī had abandoned Abū Sālih’s hadiths.” Abū Hātim said, “His hadiths may be recorded, but are not relied upon as evidence.”

Ibn ‘Adī said, “Most of what he narrates concerns Quranic commentary; he has very few reports traced back to the Prophet. I know of none of the earlier scholars who approved of him.”

I reply:

There are several ways to answer this objection. First, each of these two men was declared reliable by a number of scholars, just as others criticized him. As for ‘Umar, Ahmad ibn ‘Abd Allāh al-‘Ijlī said concerning him, “There is nothing wrong with him.” Likewise, Yahyā ibn Ma‘īn said, “There is nothing wrong with him.” Ibn Ma‘īn and Abū Hātim were among the most stringent scholars in declaring narrators reliable.

As for the statement, “Shu‘bah abandoned him,” it means that he did not narrate from him. As Ahmad ibn Hanbal said, “Shu‘bah did not hear anything from ‘Umar ibn Abī Salamah.” Shu‘bah, Yahyā ibn Sa‘īd, ‘Abd al-Rahmān ibn Mahdī, Mālik, and others like them would refrain from narrating hadith from certain people because of some suspicion conveyed to them that did not warrant rejecting

---

## Page 12419

their reports. Thus, when they narrated from someone, their narration amounted to declaring him reliable. Refraining from narrating from someone, however, could be due to a suspicion that did not warrant criticism of his reliability. This is well known in the case of several narrators whose reports appear in the authentic collections.

Likewise, the statement, “He is not strong in hadith,” is a mild expression suggesting that there may have been some deterioration in his memory. In their usage, such an expression implies neither deliberate lying nor excessive error.

As for Abū Sālih, Yahyā ibn Sa‘īd al-Qattān said, “I have not seen any of our fellow scholars abandon Abū Sālih, the freedman of Umm Hāni’, nor have I heard anyone say anything against him. Neither Shu‘bah nor Zā’idah abandoned him.” Thus, Shu‘bah’s narration from him amounts to declaring him reliable, as is known from Shu‘bah’s customary practice. Ibn Mahdī’s abandonment of him does not outweigh this, for Yahyā ibn Sa‘īd was more knowledgeable than Ibn Mahdī about hidden defects in hadith and the narrators. Indeed, hadith scholars agree that Shu‘bah and Yahyā ibn Sa‘īd were more knowledgeable about narrators than Ibn Mahdī and others like him.

As for Abū Hātim’s statement, “His hadiths may be recorded, but are not relied upon as evidence,” Abū Hātim says similar things about many narrators found in the two Sahīh collections. This is because his criteria for declaring narrators reliable are stringent, and what qualifies as authoritative evidence in his terminology differs from what qualifies as such for the majority of scholars.

---

## Page 12420

This is like saying, “I do not know that they regarded him as acceptable.” This implies that, in their estimation, he does not belong to the highest tier. For this reason, al-Bukhārī and Muslim did not include reports from him or others like him. Yet their merely not including a narrator’s reports does not warrant rejecting his hadiths.

Accordingly, when both the critic and the scholar declaring a narrator reliable are leading authorities, criticism is accepted only when its grounds are explained. A declaration of reliability therefore takes precedence over unspecified criticism.

The second point is that the hadiths of narrators like these fall within the category of good hadiths, which the majority of scholars accept as evidence. If scholars such as al-Tirmidhī and others have graded the hadith authentic, and the only criticism concerning it is what has been mentioned, then it is, at the very least, good.

The third point is that it has been transmitted through two distinct routes: one from Ibn ‘Abbās and the other from Abū Hurayrah. The narrators of one route are different from those of the other, so neither received it from the other. Neither chain contains anyone accused of lying; rather, the weakness stems solely from poor memory. Such a report undoubtedly constitutes evidence and is among the finest examples of good hadiths meeting al-Tirmidhī’s criteria. He defined a good hadith as one transmitted through multiple routes, containing no narrator accused of lying, and not anomalous—that is, not contradicting what has been established through the transmission of reliable narrators.

This hadith has multiple routes, contains no narrator accused of lying, and is not contradicted by any reliable narrator. The reason is that only two things are feared concerning a hadith: deliberate lying or a narrator’s error. Thus, if it comes through two routes, with neither narrator having received it

---

## Page 12421

from the other, and its content is not of a kind in which identical lies would ordinarily arise independently, it is known not to be a lie—especially when the narrators are not people given to lying. As for error, its likelihood diminishes with multiple routes of transmission. For this reason, Abū Bakr and ‘Umar, may Allah be pleased with them both, would seek someone to corroborate a lone narrator, fearing that he might have made a mistake. Likewise, concerning the two women, Allah Most High says:

“so that if one of them errs, the other can remind her.”

This would hold even if both routes went back to the same Companion. How much stronger, then, when one narrator relates it from one Companion and the other from another, and the wording of one contains something additional to the wording of the other? All this, and similar considerations, shows that the hadith has a known basis.

If it is objected: Granted that it is authentic, it is nevertheless abrogated, for the former report abrogates it. This is supported by what al-Athram narrated—which Ahmad cited as evidence in his narration—and what Ibrāhīm ibn al-Hārith narrated from ‘Abd Allāh ibn Abī Mulaykah:

“One day, ‘Ā’ishah, may Allah be pleased with her, was returning from the graveyards, and I said to her, ‘Mother of the Believers, had the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, not forbidden visiting graves?’ She replied, ‘Yes, he had forbidden visiting graves, but then he commanded that they be visited.’”

The reply is that this can be answered in several ways. First, it has already been explained that the permission did not extend to women, so they are not included in the abrogating ruling.

---

## Page 12422

Second, the Prophet’s statement, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, is specific to women:

“Allah has cursed women who frequently visit graves”—or “women who visit graves.”

As for his words, “So visit them,” any inclusion of women is only by extension, through a weak claim to general applicability: the wording may be restricted to men, or it may include women. According to the majority of scholars, a general ruling does not abrogate a specific ruling even when it is known to have come later. This is the position of al-Shāfi‘ī and of Ahmad, according to the better-known of the two opinions transmitted from him, and it is the position recognized among his followers. How much more so when it is not known that this general ruling came after the specific one? His statement, “Allah has cursed women who frequently visit graves,” may have come after he permitted men to visit them.

Supporting this is the fact that he coupled it with the condemnation of those who establish mosques and place lamps over graves. He referred to the latter using the masculine form, which includes men, whereas he made the curse upon female visitors specific to women. It is well known that the prohibition on establishing mosques and placing lamps over graves remains in force and has not been abrogated, as the authentic hadiths indicate. The same therefore applies to the other prohibition.

As for the report from ‘Ā’ishah, may Allah be pleased with her, Ahmad cited it as evidence according to one of the two opinions transmitted from him, because his independent juristic reasoning led him to that conclusion. The other opinion transmitted from him contradicts it, and this is the position chosen by al-Khiraqī and other early followers of his.

The report from ‘Ā’ishah, however, provides no proof for that position. The person who challenged her cited the general prohibition, and she answered that this prohibition had been abrogated. It is indeed as she said, may Allah be pleased with her. But the person challenging her did not mention the prohibition specific to women, which contains a curse upon them for visiting graves.

Her words, “He commanded that they be visited,” make this clear. This shows that his command to visit them was one that

---

## Page 12423

entailed recommendation, whereas such recommendation applies exclusively to men. Rather, ‘Ā’ishah explained that his later command abrogated his earlier prohibition, so that prohibition could no longer be invoked as evidence, leaving women subject to the original presumption of permissibility. Had ‘Ā’ishah believed that women were commanded to visit graves, she would have done so as men do, and would not have said to her brother, “I would not have visited you.”

The third response:

This is the response of those followers of Ahmad and al-Shāfi‘ī who regard it as disliked. They say: The hadith containing the curse indicates prohibition, while the hadith granting permission removes that prohibition, leaving the underlying disapproval in place. This is supported by Umm ‘Atiyyah’s statement:

“We were forbidden to follow funeral processions, but this was not strictly imposed upon us.”

Visiting graves is of the same nature as following funeral processions, so both are disliked rather than forbidden.

The fourth response:

This is the response of a group among them, such as Ishāq ibn Rāhawayh. They say: The curse was expressed using the word zawwārāt, meaning women who visit frequently. A single visit in a lifetime does not fall within that description, nor does it make a woman a visitor. They say that ‘Ā’ishah visited once and was not a frequent visitor.

Those who maintain that it is forbidden, however, say: The wording zawwārāt has indeed been reported, but this form may refer to the multiplicity of women, just as one says, “I opened all the doors,” since each door has its own separate opening. An example of this is the statement of Allah Most High:

“Until, when they reach it and its gates are opened…”

---

## Page 12424

and it is well known that each gate is opened only once.

They also argue that there is no clear criterion here distinguishing what is forbidden from what is not, whereas the curse explicitly indicates prohibition.

Some of them maintain that the same applies to following funeral processions. They cite reports containing stern warnings against it, such as the Prophet’s words (peace and blessings be upon him):

“Go back, bearing sin rather than reward, for you tempt the living and distress the dead.”

And his words to Fātimah (may Allah be pleased with her):

“Indeed, had you accompanied them as far as the graveyard, you would not enter Paradise until such and such came to pass.”

These two reports are supported by what is authentically established in the two Sahīh collections:

“He forbade women to follow funeral processions.”

As for Umm ‘Atiyyah’s statement, “But this was not strictly imposed upon us,” she may have meant that he did not emphasize the prohibition, which does not rule out its being forbidden. Or she may herself have supposed that the prohibition did not render it unlawful. The authoritative evidence, however, lies in the Prophet’s words (peace and blessings be upon him), not in someone else’s supposition.

The fifth response:

The Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) explained that men were permitted to visit graves because doing so reminds them of death, softens the heart, and brings tears to the eyes. This is how it is reported in the Musnad of Ahmad. It is well known that if this door is opened to a woman, it leads her to anguish, lamentation, and wailing, because of her weakness, frequent anguish, and limited patience. Moreover, this causes the deceased to be distressed by her weeping and men to be tempted

---

## Page 12425

by her voice and appearance, as stated in another hadith:

“For you tempt the living and distress the dead.”

Since women’s visits are likely to occasion and cause prohibited conduct on their part and on the part of men, and the underlying rationale here cannot be precisely delimited, it is impossible to define an extent of visiting that would not lead to this, or to distinguish one kind from another. One of the principles of Islamic law is that, when the underlying rationale is hidden or not widespread, the ruling is attached to the circumstances in which that rationale is likely to occur. This avenue is therefore prohibited to block the means to wrongdoing, just as looking at concealed adornment is prohibited because of the temptation it entails, and seclusion with a woman whom one is not permanently forbidden to marry is prohibited, along with other such forms of looking. There is no benefit in this sufficient to outweigh this harm: the only benefit is her supplication for the deceased, which she can offer at home.

For this reason, the jurists have said:

If a woman knows that visiting the cemetery would cause her to say or do something impermissible, then, without dispute, she is not permitted to visit.

Section

As for the aforementioned hadith concerning visiting the Prophet’s grave (peace and blessings be upon him), it is weak. There is no hadith concerning visiting the Prophet’s grave (peace and blessings be upon him) that is graded good or authentic. Nor have such reports been narrated by the compilers of the well-known Sunan collections, such as the Sunan of Abū Dāwūd, al-Nasā’ī, Ibn Mājah and al-Tirmidhī, or by the compilers of the well-known Musnad collections, such as the Musnad of Ahmad

---

## Page 12426

and the like, or by the compilers of works such as Mālik’s Muwatta’ and others. Rather, most reports narrated on this subject are lies and fabrications, such as the saying attributed to him (peace and blessings be upon him):

“Whoever visits me and my father Abraham in the same year, I guarantee him Paradise from Allah.”

This hadith is a fabrication and a lie, by scholarly agreement. The same applies to the saying attributed to him:

“Whoever visits me after my death, it is as though he visited me during my lifetime; and whoever visits me after my death, I guarantee him Paradise from Allah.”

None of this has any basis. Although al-Dāraqutnī and al-Bazzār, in his Musnad, narrated some of these reports, their chains of transmission depend on Abd Allāh ibn Umar al-Umarī, or on narrators even weaker than him, whose reports cannot establish a ruling of Islamic law.

Rather, the leading scholars relied in this matter on what Abū Dāwūd narrated in his Sunan, from Abū Hurayrah, that the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) said:

“No man greets me with peace but Allah restores my soul to me so that I may return his greeting.”

Likewise, the Sunan of al-Nasā’ī contains the report that the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) said:

“Allah has appointed angels at my grave to convey to me the greetings of peace from my community.”

Sending blessings and peace upon him is something Allah and His Messenger have commanded; hence the scholars have recommended it. Further clarification of this is that Mālik—may Allah have mercy on him—disliked a man saying:

---

## Page 12427

“I visited the grave of the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him).” Mālik had met people from among the Successors, who were the most knowledgeable about this matter. This indicates that expressions referring to visiting the Prophet’s grave (peace and blessings be upon him) were not familiar to them. Hence, some leading scholars disliked standing facing the grave while supplicating. Indeed, Mālik and others also disliked standing there to supplicate for oneself. He mentioned that this was not the practice of the Companions and the Successors, and that nothing will set the latter generations of this community right except what set its earliest generations right.

Among the reasons scholars have mentioned for his dislike of saying, “I visited the Prophet’s grave,” is that many people had come to use this expression to mean an innovated form of visitation: approaching the deceased to ask of him, invoke him, and appeal to him to fulfil their needs, and similar things that many people do. This is what they mean by the word “visitation.” Such conduct is not prescribed in Islam, by agreement of the leading scholars. Mālik therefore disliked using an ambiguous expression that conveys a corrupt meaning, unlike sending blessings and peace upon him, which Allah has commanded.

As for the word “visitation” when applied to graves in general, this meaning may not be understood from it. Consider his saying:

“So visit graves, for they remind you of the Hereafter.”

Together with his visit to his mother’s grave, this encompasses visiting the graves of disbelievers. Thus, it is not understood to mean visiting the deceased to invoke him, ask of him, seek his aid, or engage in similar practices of those who commit shirk and follow religious innovations. This is unlike the case when the person whose grave is visited is held in religious veneration:

---

## Page 12428

such as the prophets and the righteous, for by visiting their graves people often mean this innovated form of visitation involving shirk. This is why Mālik disliked such wording in cases like this, even if he did not dislike it in another context where this harmful consequence was absent.

No one can transmit anything concerning visiting the Prophet’s grave through an established chain of transmission from the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) or his Companions. Rather, what is authentically reported from him in the two Sahīh collections contradicts the corrupt meaning that ignorant people convey through this wording. Among his sayings (peace and blessings be upon him) are:

“Do not make my grave a place of celebration. Send blessings upon me, for your blessings reach me wherever you may be.”

And:

“May Allah curse the Jews and Christians; they took the graves of their prophets as places of worship.”

He was warning against what they had done. ʿĀ’ishah (may Allah be pleased with her) said: “Were it not for that, his grave would have been left exposed, but it was disliked that it should be made a place of worship.”

He also said (peace and blessings be upon him):

“Those before you used to take graves as places of worship. So do not take graves as places of worship, for I forbid you to do so.”

And he said (peace and blessings be upon him):

“O Allah, do not make my grave an idol that is worshipped.”

There are other similar hadiths in the authentic collections, the Sunan collections, and other authoritative books. How, then, can anyone possessing knowledge and faith turn away from what these texts require—texts whose authenticity the hadith scholars agree upon—and turn instead to reports that contradict their meaning, not one of which any scholar has established as authentic?

Allah, Glorified is He, knows best. May Allah bless Muhammad.

---

## Page 12429

Shaykh al-Islām—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked about women visiting graves: “Is there any hadith from the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) concerning this?”

He replied:

All praise is due to Allah, Lord of all worlds.

It is authentically reported from the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him), in a hadith narrated by Abū Hurayrah (may Allah be pleased with him), that he said:

“Allah has cursed women who visit graves.”

This was reported by Ahmad, Ibn Mājah, and al-Tirmidhī, who graded it as authentic.

Ibn ʿAbbās (may Allah be pleased with him) reported:

“The Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him) cursed women who visit graves and those who establish places of worship over them and place lamps upon them.”

This was reported by the four compilers of the Sunan: Abū Dāwūd, al-Nasā’ī, al-Tirmidhī, and Ibn Mājah. Al-Tirmidhī graded the hadith as good, and Abū Hātim included it in his Sahīh.

According to the stronger of the scholars’ two views, the ruling followed is that he forbade women from visiting graves. For the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) said:

“I had forbidden you to visit graves, but now visit them, for they remind you of the Hereafter.”

If it is said, “The prohibition has therefore been abrogated,” as those holding the other view maintain, the answer is:

This is not a sound argument, because his words, “I had forbidden you to visit

---

## Page 12430

graves, but now visit them,” are addressed to men, not women. The wording is masculine: it either applies exclusively to males or includes others by extension.

If it applies exclusively to men, women are not mentioned. If it includes others, then the wording is general, whereas his statement:

“Allah has cursed women who visit graves”

applies specifically to women, not men. Do you not see that he says:

“Allah has cursed women who visit graves and those who establish places of worship over them and place lamps upon them”?

Allah has cursed those who establish places of worship over graves and place lamps upon them, whether they are male or female. As for those who visit graves, however, he cursed only the women who visit them, not the men. Since this statement is specific, and it is not known to have preceded the permission, it takes precedence over the general wording according to the great majority of scholars. The same applies if it is known to have come after that permission.

This is comparable to his statement (peace and blessings be upon him):

“Whoever offers the funeral prayer receives one qīrāt of reward, and whoever follows the funeral procession until the burial receives two qīrāts.”

This wording is general, yet women are not included, because it is established in the Sahīh that he forbade women from following funeral processions.

ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿUmar narrated:

“We set out with the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him)—meaning that we were accompanying a funeral procession. When we had finished, the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him) departed, and we departed with him. When we were midway along the road, we saw a woman approaching. As she drew near, we realized that she was Fātimah. The Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him) said to her, ‘What has brought you out of your house, Fātimah?’ She replied, ‘I came, O Messenger of Allah, to the family of this

---

## Page 12431

house, and we offered them condolences for their deceased.’ The Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him) said, ‘Perhaps you went with them as far as al-kudā? Indeed, had you gone with them as far as al-kudā, you would not see Paradise until your father’s grandfather sees it.’”

This was related by the compilers of the Sunan collections and by Abū Hātim in his Sahīh. Al-kudā has been explained as referring to graves. And Allah knows best.

He—may Allah have mercy upon him—was also asked:

Does the deceased hear his visitor’s speech and see him in person? Is his soul returned to his body at that time, or does it hover over his grave then and at other times? Do Quran recitation and charity offered by his benefactors and others benefit him, whether funded from wealth inherited from him or from other sources? Is his soul brought together with the souls of his family members and relatives who died before him, whether he is buried near them or far away? Is his soul transferred to his body at that time? Or, if he dies in a distant land and is buried there, is his body transferred to the land where he was born? Is he distressed by his family’s weeping over him?

The scholars—may Allah be pleased with them—are requested to answer these questions individually, clearly and comprehensively, covering what has been reported concerning them in the Quran and Sunnah and transmitted from the Companions—may Allah be pleased with them. They are also requested to explain the positions of the imams and scholars of the various schools, their disagreements, and which of their views carries the greater weight. May you be rewarded, if Allah Most High wills.

---

## Page 12432

He replied:

All praise is due to Allah, Lord of the worlds.

Yes, the deceased can hear, in general, as is established in the two Sahīh collections from the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him), who said:

“He hears the sound of their sandals as they turn away from him.”

It is also established that the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him):

“Left those slain at Badr for three days, then came to them and said, ‘O Abū Jahl ibn Hishām, O Umayyah ibn Khalaf, O ‘Utbah ibn Rabī‘ah, O Shaybah ibn Rabī‘ah! Have you found what your Lord promised you to be true? For I have found what my Lord promised me to be true.’ ‘Umar—may Allah be pleased with him—heard this and said, ‘O Messenger of Allah, how can they hear, and how could they answer, when they have decomposed?’ He replied, ‘By Him in Whose Hand is my soul, you hear what I say no better than they do, but they are unable to answer.’”

He then ordered that they be dragged into the well of Badr. Likewise, the two Sahīh collections relate from ‘Abdullāh ibn ‘Umar:

“The Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) stood by the well of Badr and said, ‘Have you found what your Lord promised you to be true?’ He also said, ‘They now hear what I am saying.’”

It is also established through several reports in the two Sahīh collections that he would instruct people to greet the inhabitants of the graves, saying:

“Say: ‘Peace be upon you, inhabitants of these dwellings, believers and Muslims. We will, if Allah wills, join you. May Allah have mercy upon those among us and you who have gone ahead and those who are to follow. We ask Allah for well-being for ourselves and for you. O Allah, do not deprive us of the reward for them, do not subject us to trials after them, and forgive us and them.’”

This is an address to them, and only someone who hears is addressed. Ibn ‘Abd al-Barr related from the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him)

---

## Page 12433

that he said:

“No man passes the grave of a man whom he knew in this world and greets him with peace without Allah restoring his soul to him so that he may return the greeting.”

It is also related from him in the Sunan collections that he said:

“Send abundant blessings upon me on Friday and the night preceding it, for your blessings are presented to me.” They asked, “O Messenger of Allah, how will our blessings be presented to you when you have decayed?”—meaning, when you have crumbled to dust. He replied, “Allah, Most High, has forbidden the earth to consume the flesh of the prophets.”

It is also related in the Sunan collections that he said:

“Allah has appointed angels at my grave who convey to me the greetings of peace from my community.”

These texts and others like them show that the deceased can, in general, hear the speech of the living. This does not necessarily mean that they hear continuously; rather, they may hear at one time and not at another, just as happens to the living. A living person may sometimes hear someone addressing him, while at other times something may prevent him from hearing. This hearing is a matter of perception and entails no reward or punishment. Nor is it the hearing negated in His words:

“Indeed, you cannot make the dead hear.”

What is meant there is hearing that leads to acceptance and obedience. Allah likens the disbeliever to a dead person who does not respond to someone calling him, and to animals that hear the sound but do not understand its meaning. Although a dead person may hear the words and understand their meaning, he cannot respond to the caller or comply with his commands and prohibitions, and thus does not benefit from those commands and prohibitions. Likewise, the disbeliever does not benefit from commands and prohibitions, even though he hears the address and understands its meaning, as

---

## Page 12434

Allah Most High says:

“Had Allah known any good in them, He would have made them hear.”

As for the deceased’s ability to see, reports concerning this have been transmitted from ‘Ā’ishah and others.

Section

As for the question, “Is his soul returned to his body at that time, or does it hover over his grave then and at other times?”—his soul is returned to his body at that time, as stated in the hadith, and it is also returned at other times.

The souls of the believers are in Paradise, as stated in the hadith related by al-Nasā’ī, Mālik, al-Shāfi‘ī and others:

“The believer’s soul is a bird that feeds among the trees of Paradise until Allah returns it to his body on the day He resurrects him.”

In another wording:

“Then they take shelter in lamps suspended from the Throne.”

Nevertheless, they connect with the body whenever Allah wills. This happens in an instant, like the descent of an angel, the appearance of a ray of light on earth, or a sleeper’s awakening.

Several reports also state that souls remain in the vicinity of graves. Mujāhid said:

“Souls remain in the vicinity of graves for seven days from the day the deceased is buried, without leaving him.”

This happens on occasion. Mālik ibn Anas said:

“It has reached me that souls are free to go wherever they wish.”

And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 12435

Section

As for “Quran recitation and charity” and other righteous deeds, the scholars of Ahl al-Sunnah wa-l-Jamā‘ah do not dispute that the reward of acts of worship involving wealth, such as charity and freeing slaves, reaches the deceased. Likewise, he benefits from supplication, seeking forgiveness for him, the funeral prayer offered for him, and supplication at his grave.

They have differed over whether the reward of bodily acts of worship, such as fasting, prayer, and Quran recitation, reaches him. The correct view is that the reward of all these reaches him. It is established in the two Sahīh collections that the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, said:

“If someone dies owing fasts, his next of kin should fast on his behalf.”

It is also established that “he instructed a woman whose mother had died owing fasts to fast on her mother’s behalf.”

In al-Musnad, it is related that the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, said to ‘Amr ibn al-‘Ās:

“Had your father embraced Islam, and you then given charity, fasted, or freed slaves on his behalf, that would have benefited him.”

This is the position of Ahmad, Abū Hanīfah, and a group of the followers of Mālik and al-Shāfi‘ī.

As for those who cite as evidence the words of Allah Most High:

“And that a person will have nothing but what he strives for,”

they may be answered as follows: It is established through the mass-transmitted Sunnah and the consensus of the Muslim community that prayer is offered

---

## Page 12436

for him, supplication is made for him, and forgiveness is sought for him—all of which are the efforts of others. Likewise, as established above, he benefits from charity given and slaves freed on his behalf, although these too are the efforts of others. Whatever answer they give concerning the cases on which there is consensus also serves as the answer of the other side concerning the disputed cases. Various answers have been given to this.

The sound answer, however, is that Allah Most High did not say, “A person benefits only from his own striving.” Rather, He said:

“A person will have nothing but what he strives for.”

Thus, he owns only his own striving and has no entitlement to anything beyond it. Another person’s striving belongs to that person, just as one owns only one’s own wealth and the benefits one provides, while another person’s wealth and the benefits he provides likewise belong to him. Yet if that other person freely gives these to him, this is permissible. The same applies here: if someone freely gives him the benefit of his striving, Allah causes him to benefit from it, just as He causes him to benefit from that person’s supplication for him and charity on his behalf. He benefits from whatever reaches him from any Muslim, whether a relative or otherwise, just as he benefits from the funeral prayer offered for him and the supplications made for him at his grave.

---

## Page 12437

Section

As for his question, “Will his soul meet the souls of his family and relatives?” there is a report from Abū Ayyūb al-Ansārī and others among the early generations, also related by Abū Hātim in al-Sahīh from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace:

“When the deceased’s soul is taken up, the other souls receive him and ask him about the living. Some of them say to the others, ‘Leave him until he has rested.’ Then they ask him, ‘What has become of so-and-so?’ He replies, ‘He did righteous deeds.’ They ask, ‘What has become of so-and-so?’ He replies, ‘Has he not come to you?’ They say, ‘No,’ then say, ‘He has been taken to the Abyss.’”

Since the deeds of the living are presented to the dead, Abū al-Dardā’ used to say:

“O Allah, I seek refuge in You from doing anything that would disgrace me before ‘Abd Allāh ibn Rawāhah.”

Thus, they gather when he arrives, asking him questions which he answers. Their settled abodes, however, correspond to their ranks with Allah. Whoever is among those brought near to Allah has a higher station than someone among the people of the right. Yet those of higher rank descend to those of lower rank, while those of lower rank do not ascend to those of higher rank. Thus they meet, if Allah wills, just as they meet in this world despite their differing ranks, and they visit one another.

---

## Page 12438

And whether burial places in this world are far apart or close together, souls may meet despite the distance between their graves, or remain apart despite their graves being close together. A believer may be buried beside a disbeliever, yet the soul of the former is in Paradise and that of the latter in the Fire. Two men may be sitting or sleeping in the same place, yet the heart of one enjoys bliss while the heart of the other suffers torment, with no connection between their souls. Souls, as the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said, are:

“Troops assembled: those that recognize one another come together, while those that do not recognize one another remain apart.”

The body is not transported to its birthplace. Rather, it has been reported:

“The deceased is sprinkled with soil from his grave.”

A report of this kind cannot be affirmed with certainty or cited as proof. There is, however, a stronger hadith which states:

“No one dies away from his native town without the distance from his birthplace to the place where his journey ended being measured out for him in Paradise.”

A person will be resurrected from where he died, and his body is visibly present in his grave. What is observed must not be rejected on the strength of groundless conjectures that, moreover, contradict both reason and revelation.

Section

As for the questioner’s words, “Does weeping over him cause him distress?” this is a matter of disagreement among the early and later generations and the scholars. The correct view is

---

## Page 12439

that he is distressed by weeping over him, as stated in the authentic hadiths from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, who said:

“The deceased is tormented by his family’s weeping over him.”

Another wording states:

“Whoever is wailed over is tormented because of the wailing over him.”

An authentic hadith relates that when Abd Allāh ibn Rawāhah lost consciousness, his sister began lamenting, saying, “O my strength! O my helper!” When he regained consciousness, he said, “For everything you said about me, I was asked, ‘Are you really like that?’”

Some among the early and later generations rejected this, believing that it meant punishing a person for someone else’s sin and thus contradicted the words of Allah Most High:

“No bearer of burdens will bear the burden of another.”

They then adopted different approaches to these authentic hadiths. Some held that their narrators, such as Umar ibn al-Khattāb and others, had erred. This was the approach of Ā’ishah, al-Shāfi‘ī, and others.

Others understood this to apply when the deceased had left instructions for such mourning, so that he is punished for having instructed them to do so. This was the view of a group of scholars, including al-Muzanī and others.

Still others understood this to apply when it was his family’s customary practice, so that he is punished for failing to forbid wrongdoing. This was the view preferred by a group of scholars, including my grandfather, Abū al-Barakāt. And all

---

## Page 12440

these views are very weak indeed. Authentic, unambiguous hadiths narrated by the likes of Umar ibn al-Khattāb, his son Abd Allāh, Abū Mūsā al-Ash‘arī, and others cannot be rejected on such grounds. Ā’ishah, the Mother of the Believers, may Allah be pleased with her, has other comparable instances in which she rejected a hadith through interpretation and independent judgment, believing its meaning to be false when that was not in fact the case. Anyone who reflects on this subject will find that whoever rejects this authentic, unambiguous hadith, transmitted by a trustworthy narrator, on such grounds is mistaken.

Ā’ishah, may Allah be pleased with her, narrated two wordings from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace—and she was truthful in what she transmitted. She narrated that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Allah indeed increases the disbeliever’s torment because of his family’s weeping over him.”

This agrees with Umar’s hadith, for if it is possible for Him to increase someone’s torment because of his family’s weeping, it is also possible for Him to torment another person in the first place because of his family’s weeping. For this reason, al-Shāfi‘ī rejected this hadith in Mukhtalif al-Hadīth on account of its meaning, saying:

“The more likely version is her other report: ‘They are weeping over him while he is being tormented in his grave.’”

Among those who accepted the import of this hadith, some supposed that this was a case of a person being punished for someone else’s sin, and that Allah does whatever He wills and decrees whatever He wishes. These people believed that Allah punishes a person for someone else’s sin, so they deemed it possible

---

## Page 12441

for the children of disbelievers to be sent to the Fire for their fathers’ sins. Although some groups who affiliate themselves with the Sunnah have held this view, the Quran and Sunnah indicate that Allah admits no one to the Fire except those who disobey Him. As He says:

“I will surely fill Hell with you and all those among them who follow you.”

Thus, Hell must be filled with the followers of Iblīs. Once it is full, there will be no room in it for anyone else. Whoever does not follow Iblīs will therefore not enter the Fire.

The soundest view concerning the children of disbelievers is to say: “Allah knows best what they would have done,” as the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, answered in the authentic hadith.

Some among Ahl al-Sunnah and others said that they are all in the Fire. This was the view chosen by al-Qādī Abū Ya‘lā and others. He stated that Ahmad had explicitly affirmed it, but this is a mistaken attribution to Ahmad. Another group maintained categorically that they are all in Paradise. This was the view chosen by Abū al-Faraj ibn al-Jawzī and others. They cited as evidence a hadith recounting the Prophet’s vision, may Allah bless him and grant him peace:

“When he saw Abraham, the close friend of Allah, with the children of the believers beside him, he was asked, ‘O Messenger of Allah, and the children of the polytheists?’ He replied, ‘And the children of the polytheists.’”

The correct position concerning them is to say: “Allah knows best what they would have done.” No particular child among them is to be judged destined for either Paradise or the Fire. Several hadiths state that, on the Day of Resurrection, they will be given commands and prohibitions in the gathering grounds of the Resurrection. Whoever obeys will enter Paradise, and whoever

---

## Page 12442

disobeys will enter the Fire. This is the position that Abū al-Hasan al-Ash‘arī reported from Ahl al-Sunnah wa-l-Jamā‘ah.

Divinely imposed obligations cease only upon entering the abode of recompense—Paradise or the Fire. In the gathering grounds of the Resurrection, however, people will be tested, just as they are tested in the intermediate realm, where each is asked: “Who is your Lord? What is your religion? Who is your prophet?”

Allah Most High says:

“On the Day when a shin is uncovered and they are called to prostrate, but will be unable to do so.”

“Their eyes downcast, humiliation will overwhelm them. They had been called to prostrate while they were sound.”
